# DECISIONS.md — Implementation Decision Journal

Every choice the specs don't dictate gets an entry, written at the moment of the decision (not at task close-out). Audits reconcile this file against the specs periodically; entries may be promoted into the specs or flagged as contradictions.

Format:

```
## <date> — <task ID> — <one-line decision>
Decision: <what was chosen>
Why: <rationale, incl. alternatives considered if any>
Nearest spec: <the section closest to this gap, e.g. "main §7.4 — silent on X">
Class (filled by audit): a: fine as-is | b: promote to spec | c: contradicts spec
```

---

## 2026-09-04 — R-GRAPH-ENFORCE — Two more moves the graph did not draw, both of them a piece of code undoing itself
Decision: two edges added beyond the one the card authorised — `scheduled → accepted` and `executing → new`. Neither was in the earlier audit or in `R-GRAPH`'s own findings; both were found by walking every status write in the product rather than trusting a list. (1) Calendar replenishment marks a candidate as taken *before* it tries to give it a day, deliberately, so a crash costs one candidate instead of putting two articles on one subject; when the placement is refused it takes the mark back off (`packages/jobs/src/generation/replenish.ts`). (2) Pressing "improve this page" marks the page as being worked on before the work is queued; if queueing throws, the page is put back exactly where it came from, which for a page nobody had touched yet is `new` (`apps/web/app/api/recommendations/_lib/handlers.ts`). Neither path had a test, which is why neither was noticed.
Why: both are a compensation — a piece of code undoing a move it made a moment earlier — and a graph drawn only forwards had no room for either. Refusing them would not have corrected anything; it would have turned a failed calendar placement into an exception that leaves a candidate stranded on the calendar for ever, and a failed queue write into a page marked "being worked on" that nobody can ever press again. The card says a feature performing a genuinely wrong move is to be reported rather than fixed; these are not that. They restore the state the row was already in, and each mirrors the exact guard the shipping code uses (`from: ['scheduled']`, `from: ['executing']`), so the graph and the guard cannot disagree — the same posture `R-GRAPH` took with "still open". Both paths are now exercised by tests, which is what would have caught them a day earlier.
Nearest spec: main §7.9 (the status list); build plan §6 `R-GRAPH-ENFORCE`; DECISIONS 2026-09-04 R-GRAPH (the four found before these).

## 2026-09-04 — R-GRAPH-ENFORCE — The check lives in the repository, on the statuses a guard names rather than the one a row is in
Decision: the graph is consulted inside the six database functions that write an opportunity's status, not at each of the eleven places that call them, and it is asked about **every** status the function's own guard is willing to move a row out of — not the status the row turns out to be in. A move nobody drew throws immediately, whether or not any row would have matched.
Why: putting it at the call sites is the arrangement that let the last four disagreements exist — a rule every caller has to remember is a rule that holds until the next caller. Checking the guard's whole list rather than the row's actual status makes the refusal a property of the code instead of a property of the data: an undrawn move is refused the first time it is asked for, in a test, rather than the first time a real row happens to be sitting in the wrong place at 3am. It also means a caller cannot get an illegal move past by naming a wide set of statuses and hoping. The cost is that a deliberately broad guard has to be legal from every status it names, which is exactly the property the card asks to be proved.
Nearest spec: main §7.9, §14.3.1; CLAUDE.md invariant 15.

## 2026-09-04 — R-GRAPH-ENFORCE — FLAGGED: dismissing a suggestion already on the calendar does not call off the article
Decision: not fixed here, and reported rather than resolved. The "not interested" button on the Opportunities screen is offered on every open card, including one whose article is already booked for a day or already being written. It moves the suggestion to dismissed and stops there: nothing tells the calendar, and the daily cycle works off the topic row alone and never looks at the suggestion's status. So the merchant is told the suggestion is gone and the article still appears.
Why: this is not new and this card did not create it — the button behaved this way before the graph enforced anything, and the graph never stopped it, so the enforcement neither fixes nor worsens it. Stopping the work would mean cancelling the calendar topic from the dismissal, which is another lane's state machine and, more to the point, a product question the card does not answer: whether saying "not interested" in a suggestion also calls off an article already scheduled from it, or whether the merchant is expected to use the calendar's own veto for that. The card is explicit that a feature doing something genuinely wrong is reported, not fixed. Note the veto path is the other way round and is whole: vetoing a calendar topic stops the article and then dismisses the suggestion.
Nearest spec: main §7.9, §8.7 (veto available any time up to dequeue); ui §5.

## 2026-09-04 — R-GRAPH-ENFORCE — A dismissal stops the improve-this-page run at two points, and the model call is absorbed
Decision: the OPTIMIZE generation job stops if the row is no longer marked as being worked on — checked once before anything is bought, and once more after the model call and before the recommendation is stored. A run that finds the row gone returns "skipped" and writes nothing; no recommendation, no tasks, no "your recommendations are ready" notification.
Why: the founder's decision is that a merchant may dismiss running work and the work is abandoned, absorbing the model call. Before this the job read the opportunity but never looked at its status, so a dismissal mid-run still produced a stored recommendation and a notification for something the merchant had said no to — the row would sit dismissed while the product announced work on it. Two checks rather than one because the model call is where the time goes and is the window a dismissal actually lands in. The same check also stops a redelivered job from paying a second time for one press, which the at-least-once queue makes possible; that was not the reason for it but it is not separable from it.
Nearest spec: main §10.2, §10.4, §14.3.1; DECISIONS 2026-09-04 FOUNDER "A merchant may dismiss work already running".

## 2026-09-04 — T6.3 — The page that keeps the search is the one Google already shows most
Decision: when several of a store's own pages compete for one search, the page named as the primary target is the one holding the largest share of the store's impressions for that search; ties fall to the better average position, then to the page that led in the most weeks, then to the address itself so the answer never depends on row order. Which measurement actually separated the winner from the runner-up is what the merchant is shown as the reason, rather than a stock sentence.
Why: no document states a rule for picking the primary. The three candidates were impressions, clicks and position. Impression share is Google's own revealed preference — the page it puts in front of this store's searchers most often is the one it is closest to settling on, so promoting it is the shortest route out of the split; clicks confound the choice with how good each page's title happens to be, and position alone can crown a page Google almost never shows. The tie-break chain exists so the recommendation is a function of its inputs and nothing else: a merchant who reloads the drawer must not be told to keep a different page.
Nearest spec: main §11 (duplicate/cannibalizing pages — "primary-page designation ... with the reasoning shown"), §7.8 example 5; ui §5.3 item 5.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.3 — A canonical is only ever suggested between two pages of the same kind
Decision: the consolidation recommendation suggests a canonical tag on a competing page only where that page is the same kind of page as the primary — collection to collection, article to article. Where it is not (a product page competing with a collection, say), no canonical is suggested and the merchant is told plainly why not; the primary designation and the internal-link realignment are the whole recommendation for that page.
Why: a canonical tag tells Google two addresses are the same page, and Google then drops one of them. Two collections covering the same ground are the same page. A product and a collection are not, and pointing a product's canonical at a collection asks Google to remove that product from search entirely — a worse outcome than the cannibalization it was meant to cure, arrived at by following our advice. The specs say "canonical suggestion" and stop there, so the choice was between suggesting one everywhere (which produces harmful advice on the most common shape of this problem, a collection and a product competing) and suggesting one only where it is safe. Silence about the rest would have read as an oversight, so the view says why no canonical is offered.
Nearest spec: main §11 (the cannibalization row: "canonical suggestion; never a redirect we execute") — silent on when a canonical is appropriate.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.3 — A blocking FIX is checked again at the moment the merchant presses, not only at the weekly scan
Decision: the improve-this-page request re-reads the account's open rows and refuses — moving the suggestion to `blocked` with the obstacle named — when a blocking FIX is open on the same page, in addition to the stored status the weekly scan writes. The background worker repeats the check before spending anything.
Why: the scan's own check reads the rows open *before* its pass, so an obstacle found in the same pass as the suggestion does not block it until the following week; and any future detector that raises an obstacle outside a scan would not block it at all until the next Monday. The press is the moment the store's daily allowance and its model spend are committed, so it is the moment worth guarding. Moving the row to `blocked` rather than only answering 409 is deliberate: a blocked suggestion is information the merchant needs, and a refusal that leaves the card looking available invites them to press again.
Nearest spec: main §7.9 ("technical blockers precede content"), §11; main §10.2 (the press is the only thing that spends).

## 2026-09-04 — T6.3 — Our own articles are refused the recommendation path, and the pool they should go to does not exist
Decision: an improve-this-page request on a page marked as one of our own published articles is refused rather than answered with a recommendation, and the request the refresh pool would be handed is built and returned by a named function that nothing yet consumes. No opportunity row's action is rewritten, and nothing is inserted anywhere.
Why: the spec is explicit that our own articles do not take the recommendation path — they are rewritten through the full article pipeline instead — and the pool they go into is `T7.2`, inside the learning milestone the founder deferred out of the first release. Building the insertion anyway would mean either changing a row's recommended action (which changes what the merchant's card says and which button it offers, i.e. user-visible behaviour outside this card) or inventing a second, quieter path onto the calendar past the placement rules. Refusing is the honest halfway house: the product stops doing the thing it says it should not do, and the missing half is one named function call away when the pool lands. **This leaves a real gap: a low-CTR or weak-metadata suggestion landing on one of our own articles now has no action a merchant can take.**
Nearest spec: main §10.5 (our own articles), §9.6.5 (the refresh pool); build plan §6 `T7.2` (deferred).

## 2026-09-04 — T6.3 — The FIX view sends keys and numbers, and the FIX recommendation is not stored
Decision: the consolidation recommendation is rebuilt from the opportunity row's own evidence each time it is read, and the view is a list of string-catalogue keys with the numbers they interpolate — no sentence crosses the API, and no new table or column exists.
Why: the recommendation is a pure function of measurements already stored on the row, so storing it a second time would create two answers that can disagree the moment the scan re-measures the signal — and there is no schema wave to put it in. Sending keys rather than sentences is the same rule every "why" in this product follows: the words live in one file, so they can be reviewed and translated without reading code, and no model ever writes something a merchant reads.
Nearest spec: main §7.5 step 6 ("deterministic for FIX/HOLD, templated from evidence"), §7.1; CLAUDE.md invariant 8; ui §5.3 item 5.

## 2026-09-04 — R-GRAPH — The card named three missing moves; there are four, and the fourth is the merchant saying they applied it
Decision: the written state graph now also admits `completed` from every status an opportunity can still be open in — `new`, `accepted`, `scheduled`, `executing`, `blocked` — not only from `executing` as before. The card and the audit that raised it both named three moves (`new`/`accepted` → `executing` and `executing` → `accepted`). A fourth was found by grepping every status write on the improve-this-page path rather than trusting that list: the "I applied this" button (`markOpportunityApplied`) completes the opportunity, and in the ordinary lifecycle it does so from `accepted`, because generation hands the row back to `accepted` before the merchant ever sees the recommendation. The graph called that illegal too.
Why: that helper's own database guard is "the row is still open" and nothing narrower, so the graph mirrors that list verbatim instead of a subset of it. The narrower alternative — admitting only the statuses a merchant can demonstrably be in when they press the button — needs a chain of reasoning about which states are reachable, would have left the graph disagreeing with a shipping guard in at least one place, and would go wrong again the first time somebody made a fifth state reachable. The move is also the one the spec describes for `completed` ("recommendation marked applied by the user"), and it places no condition on what the row was doing beforehand. No code changed: the card is explicit that the code is right and the graph is wrong.
Nearest spec: main §7.9 (the status list and what `completed` means); build plan §6 `R-GRAPH`.

## 2026-09-04 — R-GRAPH — Two graph/code divergences outside this card are left alone and flagged
Decision: not fixed here. (1) The "not interested" button (`dismissOpportunity`) is guarded on any open status, so it will dismiss a row that is `executing`; the graph does not admit `executing` → `dismissed`. (2) The one-line prose summary of the lifecycle repeated above the status list in `packages/core/src/contracts/opportunities.ts` and `packages/db/src/schema/enums.ts` still draws only the calendar's path (`new → accepted → scheduled → executing → completed`), which is now narrower than the graph beneath it.
Why: neither is on the improve-this-page path this card was authorised to reconcile, and the card says in terms to keep it to that size because it is the only thing holding up the last card in its milestone. (1) is a real divergence of the same family and wants its own finding rather than a lane deciding at night whether a merchant may dismiss work already running. (2) is a comment in two files, one of them another lane's and one of them the schema.
Nearest spec: main §7.9; build plan §3 (lane directories).

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — The repair is two calls, not one, because a mended article looks exactly like one that was never broken
Decision: `mendArticleReferences` re-points the broken mentions and writes the repair record; `settlePendingRepairs` republishes anything mended and not yet posted. The drift pass calls the second on every run, including runs that find no drift at all.
Why: found while writing the end-to-end crash test, and it is the sort of hole that would never have shown up in normal use. Mending is what makes the article stop naming a product that has gone — and the instant it is mended, nothing about the store's current state says a repair was ever owed. A worker dying between the mend and the re-post would have left a corrected copy in the app, a stale article on the merchant's blog, and no later pass able to tell the difference. Driving the second half from the open repair record instead of from the store is what makes it survivable; the record says a mend happened and no publication followed, and that outlives any crash. A repair record carrying a revision number is finished and is never touched again.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.1–14.3.4 (effectively-once, checkpoint anything long), §14.3.7 (two-phase publish); CLAUDE.md invariant 18.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — The drift pass is a daily fan-out with no cursor, and reads deletions from the shared change record
Decision: one hourly-free daily sweep queues a pass per live account, keyed on the store's own date. Each pass re-reads current state and asks its four questions; only "was this product deleted" is read from the shared record of what the store told us changed, because nothing removes the local product row.
Why: two of the four triggers are not events at all. A product being unbuyable *for a fortnight* and a range's attributes no longer being the ones an article compares on cannot be announced by any message from Shopify — only looking can find them — so a purely event-driven pass would never fire either. Re-reading state also makes the pass safe to run twice, to miss a day, or to be killed halfway, which a cursor over a change stream is not: losing the cursor loses the deletions. Rejected: subscribing the pass to the existing change-stream drain, which belongs to another lane's directory and whose cursor lives in a job payload this schedule cannot supply.
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (webhooks plus a daily reconciliation, "never webhooks alone"), §7.5 (event-driven cadence); build plan §3 (lane directories).

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — The repair queue is opportunity rows; no new table, and none asked for
Decision: a repair is an `opportunities` row with `signal_type` `broken_product_reference` or `product_change_impact`, `entity_type = 'article'` and `entity_ref` = the article's id, plus its `opportunity_tasks` rows (kind `repair_reference` or `schedule_topic`). Nothing new is stored anywhere. The open-row partial unique index on `(account_id, signal_type, entity_ref)` is what makes a second detection of the same broken article update the card rather than raise a second one.
Why: main §14.1 says outright that "the repair and refresh queues are simply the FIX/REFRESH execution path for those [opportunity] rows", and every column a repair needs already exists — the evidence blob, the impact and confidence, the reason key, the recommended action, the status machine, the merchant-facing task list. Keying on the article rather than on the product is the part that had a real alternative: §14.1 flags *every article* referencing a withdrawn product, logs the repair per article, and hands the merchant a card per article, so three broken articles must be three cards. Keyed on the product they would have collapsed into one row naming one of them. The cost accepted is that two different products going missing from one article produce one card carrying both, which is what a merchant would want to read anyway.
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (drift table and its opportunity sentence), §7.9 (dedupe and lifecycle); CLAUDE.md invariant 10.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — A repair's before-and-after is written into the opportunity's own outcome, under a `repair` key
Decision: when a repair completes, `opportunities.outcome_json` is set to `{"repair": {kind, route, repairedAt, references: [{placeholderKey, fromProductId, fromProductTitle, toProductId, toProductTitle, overlap}], revisionN}}` and `applied_at` is stamped. An article's repair history is the finished repair rows naming it, newest last.
Why: main §14.1 requires "All repairs are logged per article with before/after", and the UI spec asks for a `repaired` badge linking to a repair log and a repair/refresh history on the article screen. There is no repairs table and this card may not add one. Opportunity rows are never deleted (invariant 10: expiry never deletes), each repair is already one row, and a completed row is a permanent record of one repair — so the sequence of them *is* the history, with no second place to keep in step. The `repair` key namespaces it deliberately: main §9.6.10's per-opportunity outcomes will eventually write to the same column on the same rows, and two writers at the top level would overwrite each other. Rejected: `opportunity_tasks.evidence_refs` (a bare text array, no structure and no timestamp) and `refresh_log` (two columns, and it means the 60-day refresh cooldown, not a repair).
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (log per article with before/after), §9.6.10 (`outcome_json`'s other eventual writer); ui §4, §5.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — The only mechanical repair is swapping in a like-for-like product; removing a dead mention is a rewrite
Decision: main §14.1 names two mechanical fixes an auto-repair store gets silently — "dead link removal" and "swapping a deleted product for an in-family equivalent". Only the swap is built as mechanical. Removing a mention routes to the rewrite queue like any other prose change.
Why: under the reference model (founder decision 2026-09-01) a product mention is a marker sitting inside a sentence the writer built around it — "the {{p1}} is the one to buy". Pointing that marker at a close enough product in the same family leaves a sentence that is still true and still reads. Deleting the marker leaves a sentence with a hole in it, on a merchant's own site, published by us without anyone reading it. The spec's phrase predates the reference model, when a dead link was an anchor tag that could be unwrapped. Consequence accepted: a withdrawn product with no close enough sibling costs a day of the calendar rather than being silently excised.
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (repair semantics by delivery mode); DECISIONS 2026-09-01 T4.0; `docs/content-pointers.md` §9.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — A product unbuyable for a fortnight always earns a rewrite, never a swap
Decision: the out-of-stock trigger never takes the mechanical path, on any store, whatever the auto-repair setting says and even where a like-for-like in-stock sibling exists.
Why: `docs/content-pointers.md` §11 states it directly — "a product unavailable long enough that the recommendation itself is wrong is a prose problem, not a value problem, and still earns a refresh". Swapping would have been cheaper and was rejected: the article did not merely name a product that has gone, it *reasoned* its way to recommending that one, and pointing the sentence at a different product leaves the reasoning behind it unexamined. Main §14.1 puts this row in the "repair queue" and §14.1's own repair semantics then route anything needing rewritten prose through the Gate 3 pipeline, so the two documents agree once read together.
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (out-of-stock row, repair semantics by delivery mode); `docs/content-pointers.md` §11.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — An export-mode store's own copy is repaired; only their shop is left alone
Decision: when a store that publishes by downloading has a broken article, we re-point the reference in our copy exactly as we would for an auto-publish store, and raise an action card. The Shopify write seam is never called. A test asserts zero calls to it.
Why: main §14.1's card reads "Article X recommends a discontinued product — **download the repaired version**", which only means anything if a repaired version exists. "Export-mode accounts never get silent edits" is about their site, not about our record: they have granted no write permission and nothing of ours can reach their blog. Repairing our copy is what makes the offer in the card true; leaving it unrepaired would hand them a download of the broken article.
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (repair semantics by delivery mode), §9.5 (delivery modes), §11 (consent); CLAUDE.md invariant 21.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — A repair is accepted the moment it is found, never `new`
Decision: drift opportunities are written with status `accepted`, not `new`.
Why: main §7.9 starts OPTIMIZE and FIX rows at `new` because they are proposals a merchant weighs up. A page on their own site saying something untrue is not a proposal. What varies is who does the work and whether it costs a calendar day, and that is decided by the routing, not by an approval click. This matches the treatment CREATE and REFRESH already get for the same reason — the merchant's lever is the veto, not the acceptance.
Nearest spec: main §7.9 (approval policy: "FIX: automatic only for the mechanical repairs already allowed by `auto_repair`"), §14.1.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — A repair's size is the number of broken mentions in the article
Decision: the raw score a repair is ranked on is the count of references the drift breaks, floored at one. The `impact` band is then the same percentile rank within the action family that every other opportunity gets.
Why: no spec states a formula for a repair's size, and the only job the number has is to order one broken article against another on the Opportunities screen. A guide whose three recommendations have all been withdrawn is more wrong than one whose single passing mention has, and that is the whole of what this measures. Deliberately not a traffic figure: an article nobody reads yet is still wrong, and ranking repairs by traffic would leave the newest broken pages last.
Nearest spec: main §7.6 (impact is a percentile within the action family), §14.1 — no formula stated for either drift signal.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — The axes an article was written around are read back off its own section headings
Decision: the family-axis trigger compares a family's current `differentiation_axes` against the axes recovered from the article's stored section headings, which the writer named "<section>: by <axis>". An article whose shape has no axis-derived section is never flagged by this trigger.
Why: nothing records the shape of a product range at the moment an article was written — there is no snapshot column and this card may not add one — but the headings are that record, because `sectionsFor` builds them from the axes and the article stores them. The alternative was to flag every article whose family had been regrouped since publication, which fires on every ordinary re-grouping and would make the trigger noise. Not flagging a how-to or a troubleshooting article is correct rather than a gap: the axes never shaped those, so the axes moving cannot have made them stale.
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (family-change row), §9.2 (axis-derived sections), §6.4 (axes).

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — How long a product has been unbuyable is read from the shared change record
Decision: the moment a product went out of stock is taken to be the most recent `availability_changed` entry in the shared change record for it. Where no such entry exists, the pass cannot date the stock and does not flag the product.
Why: no column anywhere records "unbuyable since"; the change record is the only history of a store's stock we keep, and it is written by both producers (the webhooks we process and the nightly walk that catches the ones Shopify dropped). Two consequences are accepted and worth stating. Stored deliveries are pruned at thirty days by the retention sweep, so a stretch of being out of stock that began more than thirty days before the pass first looked cannot be dated — the flag then never appears for that product until its stock moves again. And a product that was already unbuyable when we first synced the store, and has never changed since, is likewise never flagged. Both fail towards silence rather than towards a wrong flag, which is the right direction: a repair the merchant did not need is worse than one that arrives late. Rejected: treating an undatable out-of-stock product as qualifying, which would flag a store's entire discontinued back catalogue on the first pass.
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (out-of-stock row: "continuously OOS ≥14 days"); tech §1 (retention).

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — The collection-deleted policy row exists and nothing can raise it
Decision: the drift policy table carries the collection-deleted row, and no producer in the codebase can emit it. It is not wired to anything.
Why: main §14.1's row says a deleted collection re-runs family grouping for its member products. Two things are missing and neither is this card's to add. The shared change record's list of change kinds — a contract frozen in milestone 0 — has `collection_updated` and no `collection_deleted`; and the Shopify webhook topics the product subscribes to include `collections/create` and `collections/update` but not `collections/delete`. Adding either means editing a frozen contract or another lane's directory. The row is kept so the table is complete and so the gap is visible in one place rather than being an absence nobody notices; it is raised in the session report for the integrator.
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (collection-deleted row); build plan §4 (`CatalogEvents` is a frozen contract, produced by Lane B).

## 2026-09-04 — T5.3 — A repair needing new prose becomes an accepted REFRESH opportunity, and reaches the calendar at the next replenishment
Decision: where a repair cannot be made mechanically, the drift pass writes an accepted REFRESH opportunity and stops. It does not schedule a calendar topic itself.
Why: main §14.1 says these "queue as refresh drafts through the normal Gate 3 pipeline", and the machinery that turns an accepted REFRESH opportunity into a calendar topic through the gates already exists — the monthly replenishment reads exactly that set. Writing a topic directly from the drift pass would be a second, quieter path onto the calendar, past the placement rules (pinned days, the gap rule, the refresh share cap) that the one real scheduler enforces. The cost accepted, and worth flagging: replenishment runs monthly and only when the calendar's planned horizon has dropped below the configured number of days, so a prose repair can wait weeks before it is written, while the merchant sees it on the Opportunities screen from the moment it is found. Nothing in the spec sets a deadline for this case — the 24-hour promise in §14.1 is on *flagging* a withdrawn product, which the daily pass keeps.
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (repair semantics), §9.6.1 (replenishment trigger), §8.7 (placement rules).

## 2026-09-03 — R-ARTICLES — A held-back topic's reason in the monthly summary names the stage that stopped it, not the exact fault
Decision: the monthly email says one of four sentences per held-back topic, chosen by which of the three quality gates stopped it — "we didn't think this subject would earn its place" (gate 1), "there wasn't enough solid material to write from" (gate 2), "the draft didn't clear our quality bar" (gate 3), plus a fallback. Four new strings in `packages/ui/strings/en.json`. The stored per-fault reason is **not** used.
Why: main §8.6 requires the summary to name each held-back topic "and why", and the database does store a more specific reason — a copy key such as `gate3.near_duplicate` — on each gate decision. Three things stop that key being usable here. Most of those sentences take fill-in values ("the draft was too close to *{first_detail}*"), and the values are stored in a different shape by each gate (gate 3 puts them under `reason_params`, gate 2 stores none), so a merchant would read a literal `{first_detail}` in their email. Gate 1's and gate 2's keys have no entry in the string catalogue at all, and a missing key throws rather than degrading — the email would fail to send. And the summary is a monthly recap, not the rejection card: the calendar's per-topic card (main §8.6's first bullet, Lane F's screen) is where the specific fault belongs and where the values to render it exist. The four sentences are true of every fault their gate can find, which is what makes the choice safe rather than merely convenient. Rejected: reusing the one canonical Appendix A rejection line for everything (`appendixA.qualityRejectionRichness` says the store lacks product detail, which is false for a near-duplicate draft) and passing the stored key through unrendered (fails or prints placeholders).
Nearest spec: main §8.6 (per-rejection card vs. monthly summary); tech §1.4 (the summary is assembled from `articles` + `gate_decisions`); CLAUDE.md invariant 8 (every user-facing "why" renders from template strings, never from an LLM — respected: these are template strings).

## 2026-09-03 — R-ARTICLES — Override-published articles are counted in "what went live" and nowhere else
Decision: `publishedArticleCountInMonth` counts every article that reached `published` in the month, including ones the merchant published after we advised against it.
Why: invariant 12 excludes override-published articles from calibration data, pattern learning and headline performance claims. A count of what went live on the merchant's own store is none of those — it is a statement of fact about their month, and leaving it out would tell a merchant nothing happened in a month they watched an article appear. The exclusion that invariant 12 actually asks for already lives one query away, in `gateDecisionsForCalibration`, and is untouched.
Nearest spec: main §8.6 (override, and the summary's own bullet); CLAUDE.md invariant 12.

## 2026-09-03 — R-ARTICLES — "Held back" is read from the topic's own state, not from the free-text gate outcome
Decision: `heldBackTopicsInMonth` returns topics whose state is `rejected_by_gate` and which have a gate decision inside the month; the most recent such decision names the gate. The decision row's `outcome` column is not interpreted.
Why: `outcome` is deliberately free text (DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T4.0) and a gate writes a row when it passes a topic as well as when it stops one, so the presence of a decision proves nothing on its own and matching on outcome strings would be guessing at a vocabulary three unbuilt cards still own. The topic's state is the unambiguous record that it was stopped, and the decision's timestamp is what puts it in a month.
Nearest spec: main §8.6, §8.7 (`rejected_by_gate` is the calendar's own terminal state for this).

## 2026-09-03 — R-ARTICLES — The export-URL reminder's database source is the default rather than an argument passed at the composition root
Decision: `registerExportUrlReminderTask` and `sweepExportUrlReminders` both default to the real cross-account read; the card's text asked for it to be passed explicitly in `apps/web/instrumentation-node.ts`, and that file is unchanged.
Why: two reasons, one of them a lane boundary. `instrumentation-node.ts` is not this lane's file, and defaulting achieves the same running behaviour with no edit to it. More importantly, an optional source meant every caller that forgot to pass one silently swept nothing — the exact failure this card exists to remove — so making the real read the default closes that hole everywhere rather than at the one call site. A test may still pass its own source.
Nearest spec: build plan §3 (lane directories); tech §1.4 (the seven-day reminder is an hourly sweep).

## 2026-09-03 — R-ARTICLES — The reminder sweep reads across accounts, with a stated reason, rather than per account
Decision: `unconfirmedExportedArticlesAcrossAccounts` takes a `SystemScope` carrying the sentence "the export-URL reminder sweep looks across every account for articles published with no address". Every row it returns names its own account, and the notification it produces is attributed to that account.
Why: the sweep runs hourly and has to find the handful of stores with anything waiting. Asking per account means enumerating every store on the platform and running one query each, hourly, to discover that almost none of them qualify. This is the pattern `accountsWithTimezone` already set for the monthly summary. The per-account read (`unconfirmedExportedArticles`, used by the dashboard's attention list) is separate and takes an `AccountScope` like every other read.
Nearest spec: `packages/db/src/scope.ts` (the system-scope carve-out and its "record the reason so review can judge it" rule); CLAUDE.md code-structure rules (no unscoped table access).

## 2026-09-03 — R-ARTICLES — `repairsCompleted` in the monthly summary is still a hardcoded zero, and this is the record of it
Decision: left at `0`, with a comment saying so, and flagged here rather than filled or stubbed.
Why: there is no repairs table anywhere in the schema — the word appears only as `accounts.auto_repair` and two enum values — so there is nothing to count until `T5.3`. It is named here because the rest of this file's stub registrations are what make gaps visible, and this one is not covered by any of them: the surviving `AttentionSources.articles` registration is about the dashboard's repair item, not this line. The consequence is small and one-directional: the summary's "other work" section omits the repairs line entirely at zero rather than claiming none happened, so the email is quiet about repairs rather than wrong about them.
Nearest spec: main §8.6 (the summary reports repairs among the action types); main §14.1 (drift and repair).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — The walk's end marker moves to `awaiting_confirmation`, which does the moving and the telling, nothing else
Decision: a new `confirmationGateStep`, registered as the `awaiting_confirmation` handler, transitions the domain `ingesting → needs_confirmation` and emits `ingestion_review_ready`, then succeeds immediately. It reads and writes nothing about the profile itself.
Why: `T2.6` named this card as the one that moves the dispatcher's stopping point on again, and the dependency graph (`STEP_DEPENDENCIES`, written in `T2.0`) already declares `awaiting_confirmation` as depending on `keywords_competitors` alone — not on `gsc_connect`, which is a sibling of it rather than something it waits for. A store that skips Search Console must still reach the review screen. `ingestion.test.ts`'s resumed-run assertion now executes through `awaiting_confirmation` and stops at `gsc_connect` (still no handler, `T3.1`'s dispatcher-driven half) with the domain in `needs_confirmation` — where before it stopped at `keywords_competitors` with the domain still `ingesting`.
Nearest spec: main §6.8; `packages/jobs/src/runtime/steps.ts` (`STEP_DEPENDENCIES`'s own comment on this exact edge).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — `familyGroupingSourceForScreen` resolves T2.4's flagged four-into-three mismatch
Decision: `collection` (the database's name for the merchant's-own-taxonomy signal) reports as `taxonomy`; `split_variant` and `fact_cluster` both report as `fact_clustering`; `embedding` reports as itself. Implemented once, in `packages/core/src/families/screenView.ts`, and used by both `GET /api/profile`'s families block and (when it is built) the Products screen's.
Why: `T2.4` found that `familySchema.groupingSource` in the frozen contract names three values and the database enum names four, and left the mapping for whichever route exposes a family first — this one. `collection→taxonomy` is not really a compromise: `T2.4`'s own decision log says the `collection` enum value was chosen to hold the merchant's-taxonomy signal specifically because no table stores real Shopify collection membership, so `taxonomy` is the more honest word for what that value already means. `split_variant` has no equivalent in the three-value schema; grouping it under `fact_clustering` was chosen over the alternatives (a fourth value, silently dropping the case) because both signals compute a family from the product data itself rather than from the merchant's filing or the embedding fallback — closer in kind to each other than to either of the other two. This is an approximation on the merchant-visible badge, not a hidden one: the next contract re-freeze should give `split_variant` its own value.
Nearest spec: main §13 `product_families.grouping_source`; DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T2.4 ("CONTRADICTION" entry).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — `keywords.confirmed` is set by "Confirm profile", account-wide, all at once
Decision: `confirmAllKeywords` marks every keyword row the account currently holds as `confirmed = true`, called once inside the same transaction as the persona edits and the top-product reorder, only after the guarded `needs_confirmation → ready_for_planning` transition has already won its race.
Why: the `keywords` table comment and `T3.3`'s decision log (2026-09-02, "The merchant's confirmed search terms are read from the search repository") both name this column as "the merchant's decision at the confirmation screen" but nothing before this card ever wrote `true` to it — every keyword sat at the column's own default, `false`, forever. There is no per-chip confirm control on the screen (ui §3.7 item 3 offers only remove and add), so there is no finer signal to key this off than "the whole list, at the moment the merchant confirmed the profile it sits beside." One consequence worth naming: `competitorSuggestions` (built in `T2.6`) already reads `confirmedKeywords`, so on a merchant's very first visit to the confirmation screen — before they have ever clicked "Confirm profile" — the suggestions list is empty, because nothing is confirmed yet. That first-visit gap was not introduced by this card and is not this card's to fix; it is recorded here because this is where the column's meaning was finally pinned down, and now that it is, the gap is visible rather than merely possible.
Nearest spec: main §9.6.3 ("the confirmed keyword each topic descended from"); `packages/db/src/schema/keywords.ts`'s own column comment; DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T3.3.

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — Confirm writes only after it wins the guarded transition, never before
Decision: `confirmProfile` (packages/core/src/persona/confirm.ts) attempts the guarded `needs_confirmation → ready_for_planning` transition first. Only if it succeeds does it write the persona edits, the top-product order, and the keyword confirmation; a losing request (a double-click, a retried one) touches no table.
Why: undictated by any spec text, and consequential enough to write down rather than assume. The alternative — write the edits, then attempt the transition — would let a losing request's stale form data overwrite whatever the winning request (or a later Settings edit, arriving between the two) had just written, for no benefit: a loser has nothing useful left to do once it knows it lost.
Nearest spec: main §6.8; CLAUDE.md invariant 15 (guarded transitions, 409 on a zero-row guard).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — Three `topProductSchema` fields are honestly empty, not invented: `imageUrl`, `pinned`, `revenueBand`
Decision: `GET /api/profile` returns `imageUrl: null` and `pinned: false` for every row, always, and `revenueBand: null` for every row, always.
Why: none of the three has anywhere to come from. `top_products` has no image column — the catalogue sync never persisted one — and no `pinned` column, so "pin to top" is, in this schema, indistinguishable from a merchant dragging a row to position one; a real pin needs a column, and a feature card may not add one (CLAUDE.md migration rule). `revenueBand` needs boundary values nowhere defined — not in the specs, not in `packages/rules` — and, being a band rather than an amount, needs a currency to be meaningful at all, which nothing about a top-product row carries either. Inventing cutoffs here would be inventing a product decision a data gap does not license. All three are nullable/boolean-with-a-safe-default in the frozen schema, so returning the honest empty value is a legal response, not a broken one — but the screen's pin button and revenue badges will render as if those features do not exist, because today they do not.
Nearest spec: main §6.8 — "sortable list — remove, reorder, pin"; ui §3.7 item 2 — "90d revenue/qty badges"; the profile response schema — `topProductSchema`.

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — A competitor swap has nothing to invalidate, because no per-competitor cache exists yet
Decision: nothing was built for "competitor swap invalidates only that competitor's cache" beyond what `T2.6` already does — `addCompetitor`/`removeCompetitor` touch exactly the one `competitors` row.
Why: main §6.8 asks for this, but the only two caches near a competitor today are `serp_snapshots`, keyed by *our own search term* and shared across every account asking about that market (T2.6's decision log), and the vendor wrapper's 24-hour request cache underneath it — neither is keyed by, or scoped to, a competitor's identity, so there is nothing addressable to invalidate when one is removed. The requirement reads as anticipating a competitor-level cache (a SERP position snapshot, an intent-gap analysis) that `T3.5`/`T10`'s OPTIMIZE and existing-target-check machinery will introduce later. The sentence is satisfied today only in the vacuous sense that removing a competitor changes nothing else — flagged rather than silently claimed as done.
Nearest spec: main §6.8 — "Swapping a competitor later invalidates that competitor's cached analysis, nothing else."; DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T2.6 (the two caches that do exist).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — The SSE stream is a 2-second poll wearing an event stream's clothes
Decision: `GET /api/ingestion/stream` re-reads the run's `job_steps` every 2000ms and forwards what it finds as one `data:` line per read, closing the connection once the run leaves `running`.
Why: tech §1.6 asks for SSE "streaming `job_steps` transitions" and names no mechanism for the server to learn of a transition the moment it happens — a worker writing a row and this route both talk to Postgres, not to each other, and there is no in-process event bus or `LISTEN`/`NOTIFY` wiring anywhere in this codebase to carry that signal. Polling on a short interval and pushing the result as SSE gets the client the live-feeling behaviour tech §1.6 asks for (steps visibly advance without a page reload) without building push infrastructure the same section explicitly says not to build yet ("When notification volume or realtime needs outgrow §1.6's polling, that's a later spec"). 2000ms is UNSIGNED — picked as comfortably faster than a person notices as stale, with no measurement behind it.
Nearest spec: tech §1.6.

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — `/api/ingestion/status` and `/api/ingestion/stream` were built under `apps/web/app/api/ingestion`, a path this session's directory list did not name
Decision: built anyway, plus their store (`packages/db/src/stores/ingestion.ts`) and two small additions to `packages/db/src/repositories/runs.ts`.
Why: the card's own scope and done-when are explicit — "SSE endpoint for `job_steps`", "SSE streams step transitions" — and the frozen route table (`packages/core/src/api/routes.ts`) already carries both routes, summarised as reading "the active ingestion run", which only this lane's `packages/jobs/ingestion/*` produces. The build plan's lane table (§3) does not list `apps/web/app/api/ingestion` under any lane's row at all — not this one, not any other — which reads as an omission in the table rather than a deliberate exclusion, since no other lane's row plausibly owns the ingestion pipeline's own progress screen. Flagged here, as the session instructions require, rather than assumed silently.
Nearest spec: build plan §3 (lane ownership table); build plan §6 (T2.7's own scope line).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — The distillation chaos scenario joins the quarantine test's allow-list, on the same grounds as `distill.test.ts`
Decision: `packages/jobs/src/chaos/distill.scenario.ts` was added to `quarantine.test.ts`'s `ALLOWED` set.
Why: the test fails the build on any file outside a short allow-list that so much as names `rawBodyHtml`, and this scenario has to seed real product descriptions for distillation to have anything to distil — the identical reason `distill.test.ts` is already on the list, doing the same seeding with the same field name.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md invariant 3; `packages/core/src/distill/quarantine.test.ts`'s own docstring.

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — The harness's own kill-point guess can hide a scenario that never gets killed, so a scenario may now say how many checkpoints it has
Decision: `ChaosScenario` gains an optional `initialCeiling`. The harness's first kill-point draw is `1 + random × ceiling`, and `ceiling` starts at a hardcoded 8; `family_group_killed_after_commit` sets it to `1` (its one real checkpoint) and `distill_killed_mid_batch` to `PRODUCT_COUNT` (its six).
Why: found by running both new scenarios directly rather than through the suite and inspecting what they returned — `family_group_killed_after_commit` reported zero kills, seven runs out of eight, silently. The mechanism: when a scenario's clean pass has fewer than 8 checkpoints and the harness's random draw overshoots that count, the pass runs to completion uninterrupted *and finishes the real work* — which is exactly the outcome the "kills < budget, retry" branch is written to handle by re-running the driver. But a second run of a step that already fully succeeded finds its own ledger entry and never calls the handler at all, so it reaches zero checkpoints — which the harness reads as "nothing left to kill" and accepts as convergence. Every existing scenario before this card happened to have close to eight checkpoints already (the catalogue sync's per-page walk) or used a different mechanism entirely (`process_death_mid_step`'s real SIGKILL, which does not go through this draw at all), so nothing had exposed it. `distill`'s six checkpoints hit the same failure with lower odds — still real, and now closed by the same fix.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.9; `packages/jobs/src/chaos/harness.ts`'s own comment on the overshoot-tightening loop.

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — Two more chaos scenarios, each interrupted where its own step's docstring says a crash actually lands
Decision: `distill_killed_mid_batch` kills between two products' model calls, injected through a wrapping `LlmClient` the same way `T2.2`'s scenario injects through a wrapping `ShopifyListReader`. `family_group_killed_after_commit` kills *after* `reconcileFamilies`'s transaction has committed but before the step function returns, injected by wrapping `deps.db.transaction` — because `family_group` makes no port call at all to hook a mid-step checkpoint into (`families.ts`'s own docstring: no network call, no vendor call, one transaction, no checkpoint).
Why: the card asks for these two by name. Each kill point was chosen to match the specific claim its step's own comments make about what a crash does to it, rather than reusing catalogue-sync's per-page shape unexamined. For distillation, the claim under test is the per-product ledger key (`productKey`): a kill lands *before* `recordCompletedWork` for the product mid-flight, so the assertion that matters is that the total count of model calls across every attempt equals the product count exactly — not more. For family grouping, the claim under test is different in kind: this step keeps no checkpoint at all, so a kill after the commit but before the step returns means the step-level ledger row for that attempt was never written, and the *redelivery* genuinely re-executes `reconcileFamilies` a second time over the same input — the assertion that matters is that this second execution reconciles onto the same one family rather than creating a duplicate.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.9 (chaos coverage); `packages/jobs/src/ingestion/families.ts`'s own "why there is no checkpoint here" note; `packages/jobs/src/chaos/harness.ts`.

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — OPEN QUESTION, NOT DECIDED — nothing after "Confirm profile" starts the onboarding opportunity run
Decision: **none taken.** `confirmProfile` performs the guarded transition to `ready_for_planning`, writes the edits, and fires `profile_confirmed`. It enqueues nothing.
Why: main §6.9 says confirmation is not the end of onboarding — the account moving to `ready_for_planning` is supposed to start the onboarding run of the Opportunity Engine, which seeds the first calendar and is the actual activation moment. That machinery (`signal_runs`, the onboarding scan, `TopicScheduler`) is `T3.7`'s, Lane C's, and does not exist on this branch yet. This is the same shape of gap `T2.1`'s OPEN QUESTION entry named for the first ingestion step: the handler that reacts to the state this card produces has not been written by anyone yet, so nothing can call it. Recorded rather than guessed, so the integrator — or whoever lands `T3.7` — knows the trigger point still needs wiring: either `confirmProfile` gains an optional "start the next thing" port once one exists, or a sweep picks up every `ready_for_planning` account the way earlier cards' sweeps do.
Nearest spec: main §6.9; DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T2.1 (the matching open question for the first step of the pipeline).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.7 — A confirm that arrives too early answers `profile_not_ready`, a code the route's own contract entry does not list
Decision: `POST /api/profile/confirm` answers 409 `profile_not_ready` when the guarded transition fails and the domain is not already `ready_for_planning`. The frozen route table lists only `profile_already_confirmed` under this route's `conflicts`.
Why: `T2.6`'s keyword and competitor routes already return this exact code with no `conflicts` entry naming it either — precedent this card follows rather than invents. The alternative, inventing a generic 400, would be a worse lie: the request is well-formed, and the reason it fails is a state the account has not reached yet, which is what 409 with a code means everywhere else in this contract.
Nearest spec: `packages/core/src/api/routes.ts` (`/api/profile/confirm`'s `conflicts` list); DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T2.6 (`profile_not_ready` on the keyword/competitor routes).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — Which search-data provider runs is decided by an explicit switch, never inferred from missing credentials
Decision: `seoProvider()` returns the test double when `SEO_PROVIDER_MODE=mock` and the live provider otherwise. Missing credentials do not select the double; the live provider fails loudly instead.
Why: the Shopify OAuth client in the same file *does* fall back on missing credentials, and copying that was the first version of this. The two failures are not alike. A Shopify client with no credentials cannot get past a consent screen, so the stand-in announces itself. A search vendor with no credentials would quietly report that every store has no search demand and no competitors — a plausible-looking answer that is wrong, and the merchant would confirm it. That is the failure invariant 22 exists to prevent. `.env.example` already carries `SEO_PROVIDER_MODE=mock` and the env sync check requires `.env` to declare it, so local and staging are covered by the switch rather than by an accident of configuration.
Nearest spec: tech §5 (real vendor spend in production only); main §14.4 (degrade to pause, never to a worse answer).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — The search-data step is the first thing in the product to read the enrichment pause switch, and it stops rather than degrades
Decision: `keywords_competitors` and the hand-typed-term enrichment job both check the global `pause_enrichment` switch before any vendor call, and treat it as a *retryable* failure — the store waits and continues rather than being abandoned.
Why: invariant 17 requires spend caps enforced from our own counters at dequeue, and the sweep that raises this switch has existed since `T8.0` with **nothing reading it** — this is the first card that spends with that vendor, so it is the first that can. Retryable rather than terminal because the switch is an operator's or the sweep's judgement that today has cost enough, not a fact about this store; the merchant sees a delayed step. Degrading — a shallower results page, an older snapshot — was rejected outright: main §14.4 says pause, never lower quality. **Note the caps are dormant** because the sweep's schedule is off pending founder question 4; the enforcement point is built and tested, and switching the schedule on is all that is needed.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 (the caps), §14.4 (degrade to pause); CLAUDE.md invariants 17 and 22.

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — Three caching layers, deliberately separate, and only the innermost is about crashes
Decision: a term's search volume is trusted for 30 days (`keywords.enriched_at`); a results page is reused for 7 days (`serp_snapshots.expires_at`, checked inside the query so a stale one cannot be read by accident); underneath both, the vendor wrapper's 24-hour request cache stores every raw response *before* anything parses it.
Why: main §12.1 gives the 30-day and 7-day lifetimes and main §14.3.6 gives the 24-hour one and says it "sits under" them, which DECISIONS 2026-08-31 T0.5 read as three layers with different jobs — and this card is where that reading is cashed. The two outer ones are the product's memory of what a term is worth and who ranks; the inner one exists so a crash between the vendor answering and our writing the answer down does not pay twice. Proven separately: a re-execution of the finished step buys nothing because of the outer two, and a step killed part-way through the results pages resumes without re-buying what it had.
Nearest spec: main §12.1, §14.3.6.

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — A stored results page is keyed by our own canonical string, not by the vendor's request hash
Decision: `serp_snapshots.cache_key` is `serp:<language>-<COUNTRY>:<depth>:<normalised query>`, computed in `packages/core`.
Why: main §13 gives the column and not its contents. Reusing the vendor's request hash would tie the product's seven-day memory to one vendor's endpoint naming, and would mean swapping the vendor invalidated every stored page. Our own key also reads: a person looking at the table can see what a row is about. It carries no account, which is the point — two stores in the same market asking about one search share one purchase.
Nearest spec: main §12.1 (the vendor is swappable behind an interface), §13 `serp_snapshots`.

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — The range of terms the model is asked for lives in the prompt, and the ceiling we pay for lives in the config
Decision: `seeds.v1.md` asks for 15–25 candidates in its own text. `discovery.seed_keywords.candidates_max` in `packages/rules` is the ceiling applied to the *answer* — the most terms we will price.
Why: main §6.6 says "~15–25 candidate terms". Rendering those two numbers into the prompt from config was the first design and was wrong for the reason `packages/llm/prompts/README.md` already gives: a prompt whose words change with configuration is a prompt whose stamped `prompt_version` no longer says what the model was asked. So the ask is versioned with the prompt, and the only number that decides spending is in the config layer where invariant 9 requires it. Changing what is asked for is a new prompt version; changing what we pay for is a config edit.
Nearest spec: main §6.6; main §14.2 / `packages/llm/prompts/README.md` (versions are never edited in place).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — The route handlers reach the database through a store port in `packages/db`, not by importing the handle
Decision: `makeKeywordStore()` joins `makeFamilyStore()` and the other store factories; the profile handlers take it as a dependency.
Why: the lint rule forbidding a raw database handle outside `packages/db` carries an explicit instruction not to extend its exemption list — "a new file that needs it belongs in packages/db in the first place". The port keeps the handle inside the package that owns it, keeps every method demanding an `AccountScope`, and lets the route tests run against an isolated database with no mocking. Its one account-less method, which reads stored results pages, takes a system scope with a written reason for the same purpose the existing ones do.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md code-structure rules; DECISIONS 2026-08-31 R1/R3 (the exemption list and its end).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — The hand-typed-term lane is a job priority, not a synchronous call
Decision: adding a keyword writes the row unpriced, queues `keyword_enrich` at Graphile Worker priority −10 (everything else in the product queues at the default 0, and lower runs first), and answers immediately. The job re-checks, under the account's lock, whether the term still needs pricing.
Why: main §12.1 calls the confirmation screen's hand-added enrichment "the one latency-sensitive case" and says it "still goes through the job queue but with a high-priority lane, and the UI shows a per-chip loading state rather than blocking". Priority is what makes it feel immediate without putting a paid vendor call in a request path, where a double-click would pay twice. The re-check exists because the queue is at-least-once and the gap between the click and the job is exactly where the term gets priced by onboarding, or removed again.
Nearest spec: main §12.1, §14.3 (at-least-once, effectively-once).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — The walk's end marker moves to `gsc_connect`
Decision: `ingestion.test.ts` now asserts a resumed run executes through `keywords_competitors` and stops at `gsc_connect`, which has no handler until `T3.1`'s onboarding half.
Why: each card in this lane has moved this one line to the next unbuilt step, and this is that move. `T2.7` moves it again — to `awaiting_confirmation` — noting that `awaiting_confirmation` deliberately does not depend on `gsc_connect`, so a store that skips Search Console is not stalled.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.1 (the step list and its dependencies); DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T2.0 (the dependency graph).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — Onboarding proposes a draft competitor list, and the domains inside a stored results page still never become competitors on their own
Decision: keyword discovery writes up to five `competitors` rows marked as ours (`source = 'auto'`) during onboarding, before the merchant has looked. The per-search cast of whoever occupies a results page stays in `serp_snapshots` and is never written anywhere else; what a merchant sees of it is a *count* ("ranks alongside you in 8 top searches") with an Add button that is their click.
Why: main §6.6 asks for exactly this — "Take the top candidates as the draft competitor list", "Auto-detection proposes at most 5" — and main §6.8 tells the merchant to "remove any auto-detected entry", with auto and manual rows "visually distinguished (`source` badge)". The confirmation screen already built (`T9.3`) renders both: a list with auto/manual badges and, underneath it, suggestions with an Add button. CLAUDE.md invariant 5 reads more broadly than that ("SERP ranking domains … never enter `competitors`"), but main §7.2.1, which the invariant compresses, defines its terms: a **business competitor** is "auto-proposed at ingestion, hard cap 5", while a **SERP ranking domain** is the per-query, uncapped, non-editable set that lives in snapshots. Both readings were checked against the screens and only this one leaves `source = 'auto'` meaning anything. **Flagged for the integrator** because it is the one place this card could be read as bending an invariant: the structural guarantee actually implemented is that nothing reads a snapshot row into `competitors` — the only writer is `addCompetitor(db, scope, {domain, source})`, every caller of it passes a domain that has been through the blocklist, own-domain and cap filters, and the suggestion reader returns values and performs no writes at all.
Nearest spec: main §6.6, §6.8, §7.2.1; CLAUDE.md invariant 5.

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — A marketplace refusal answers 422, not 409, because the screen that consumes it was built that way
Decision: adding a competitor that is on the marketplace blocklist without an override answers HTTP 422 with code `competitor_on_blocklist`. `CONFLICT_CODES` and the route contract's `conflicts` list are untouched.
Why: `T9.3`'s add control treats a 409 as one of two specific messages (own domain, cap reached) and **anything else** as the blocklist, offering "Add anyway" back. A third 409 code would therefore have rendered as "you already have five", which is wrong and unhelpful; a non-409 renders correctly with no frontend change. It also avoids widening `CONFLICT_CODES`, which is a frozen contract enum the UI maps case by case and which is documented as the set of codes *a lost race* returns — a blocklist refusal is not a lost race. The code is still in the body, so a future client can be specific about it.
Nearest spec: main §6.8 — "cannot be on the marketplace blocklist without an 'add anyway' override"; DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T9.3 (how the screen reads the refusal).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — The marketplace blocklist is a hand-maintained list in core, not a threshold in `packages/rules`
Decision: `MARKETPLACE_BLOCKLIST` lives in `packages/core/src/keywords/blocklist.ts`. Adding an entry is a code change.
Why: `packages/rules` holds the numbers that decide what the product writes about, and invariant 9 is about numeric thresholds. This is a list of names, and the same argument the vendor price map made (DECISIONS 2026-08-31 T0.5) applies: it is an external fact we record rather than a decision we tune. Matching is on a domain boundary — exactly, or as a subdomain — so `smile.amazon.com` is caught and `notamazon.com` is not. Amazon and eBay are listed under each national domain because those are separate registrable domains and a German store's results page is full of `amazon.de`.
Nearest spec: main §6.6 — "filter out marketplaces/aggregators (Amazon, Etsy, eBay, Wikipedia, Pinterest, YouTube — maintain a blocklist)".

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — The numbers behind keyword and competitor discovery go into `packages/rules`, which is another lane's package
Decision: a new `discovery:` block in `signals.config.yaml` (plus its schema, its type and the committed snapshot) holds the seed candidate range, how many keywords survive, how deep a domain counts as ranking, how many searches it must appear for, how many competitors onboarding may propose, how many results pages onboarding buys, and the 30-day / 7-day cache lifetimes.
Why: invariant 9 says no threshold literal lives outside `packages/rules`, and two of these are named as config by the spec itself — main §7.2.1 sends "top 10 for ≥ 3 keywords" to §7.10 explicitly. Comparing a position against a literal is also a lint error outside that package, so there was no other home for them. **For the integrator:** `packages/rules` is Lane C's after M0, and `rules_version` is a hash of the file's bytes — so this card changes `rules_version` for every lane, which is the intended behaviour of that mechanism (any config change does) but is worth knowing at merge time. One block was added and nothing existing was touched.
Nearest spec: main §7.10 (the config layer), §7.2.1 (sends its own threshold there), §6.6, §12.1; build plan §3 (lane ownership).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — `seed_serps_max` is UNSIGNED and is the single largest cost of onboarding a store
Decision: onboarding buys a results page for at most ten of the store's draft keywords.
Why: main §6.6 says to "query DataForSEO SERPs for the top seed keywords" and names no number, and this is the one number on this card that is straight spend: ten paid reads per store onboarded, plus one priced keyword-metrics read. Ten is enough for a domain appearing for three of them to be a pattern rather than a coincidence, which is the threshold §7.2.1 sets. Marked UNSIGNED in the config with what raising and lowering it costs.
Nearest spec: main §6.6, §12.1 (billing per request).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — Onboarding's draft competitors and the standing suggestions are computed by one function, not two
Decision: `rankCompetitorCandidates` takes the ranked domains, the store's own domain, the domains already on the list, and the two thresholds, and returns candidates. Onboarding calls it to fill the draft list; the confirmation screen's suggestions call it against the merchant's *confirmed* terms. Neither can see a domain the other would hide.
Why: the same discipline main §7.7 imposes on the existing-target check, for the same reason — two similar functions drift, and here the drift would be visible as a marketplace appearing in one place and not the other, or the store being offered itself. Concretely it means the blocklist, the own-domain exclusion (including subdomains) and the position ceiling are applied once.
Nearest spec: main §6.6 (the draft list), §7.2.1 (the standing suggestions).

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — Whether a hand-typed competitor domain resolves is checked where it can be, and never blocks the merchant when it cannot
Decision: `validateCompetitorDomain` takes `resolves?: boolean`. `false` refuses the domain; `undefined` — nobody looked, or the lookup failed — proceeds. The DNS lookup itself is an optional dependency of the route, with a short timeout.
Why: main §6.8 asks for a competitor to be "validated as a real resolving domain". The failure modes are not symmetric: refusing a typo saves a merchant a wasted slot, while refusing a real competitor because our resolver had a bad second is us breaking their form for a reason they cannot see or fix. A domain that resolves today can stop tomorrow anyway, so this is a courtesy against typos rather than a guarantee, and it is written to behave like one.
Nearest spec: main §6.8.

## 2026-09-03 — T2.6 — An unpriced search term is kept and sorted last, not discarded
Decision: a candidate the vendor returns no search volume for stays in the draft keyword set, ordered below every priced term, and is cut only if better-evidenced terms fill the set first.
Why: main §6.6 says "the strongest ~10–15 are kept" without saying what happens to a term with no reading. No volume usually means a phrase too specific for the vendor's panel rather than a phrase nobody searches — which is exactly the kind of term a niche store's customers actually type — and the merchant edits this list before it means anything. The draft set is also deliberately not filtered against the demand floor in `packages/rules`: that floor decides whether a topic is worth an article, a later and far more expensive question.
Nearest spec: main §6.6; main §7.3 (the demand floor, which is a different decision).

(entries below, newest first)

## 2026-09-02 — T8.4 — The two rate-based brakes are built, tested, and cannot see, and they say so rather than reading empty as healthy
Decision: the judge fail-rate and publish error-rate trips are complete — the arithmetic, the thresholds, the switch each raises, the fixtures that drive them — but the counts they need come from tables that do not exist. There is no `articles` table and no record of a draft's gate decision; there is no `publish_intents`. Each reads through a declared port (`JudgeOutcomeCounter`, `PublishOutcomeCounter`) whose stand-in returns `measurable: false`, and both stand-ins register in the repo's stub registry, so `pnpm stubs:report` lists them and the gate that turns on `--fail-if-any` fails while they are wired.
Why: the alternative that looks like working is a query returning zero. Zero failures out of zero attempts is a healthy-looking number, and a brake that cannot see would then be indistinguishable from a brake with nothing to do — for months. `measurable: false` makes "nothing went wrong" and "nobody is writing it down" different answers, and the sweep logs the second every run. **What is actually missing is only the counting**: when the content and publishing lanes land their tables, each stand-in becomes a query, not a redesign.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 — names both trips exactly; main §13 — has no table either could read.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.4 — Both rate trips need a minimum sample before they may fire
Decision: `judgeFailRateVerdict` will not trip until the full trailing sample the config names has been graded; `publishErrorRateVerdict` will not trip below five attempts in the window (`PUBLISH_ERROR_MINIMUM_SAMPLE` in `packages/core`, not `signals.config.yaml`).
Why: two rejections out of two is 100% and means nothing. Without a floor, the judge trip would pause the whole product on the third draft of any quiet morning, and the publish trip on one failed post at 6am. The spec's own wording carries the floor for the judge — "over the trailing 50 drafts" is fifty drafts, not a ratio over whatever exists — and is silent for publishing, where five is chosen. It is **not** in `signals.config.yaml` because it decides nothing about a merchant's store: it is arithmetic hygiene on a ratio, the same family as "a day cannot be unusual against a median that includes it", which the same package already keeps as a constant.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 — "> 60% over the trailing 50 drafts", "> 20% over 1h"; silent on small samples.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.4 — The new trips ride the existing five-minute sweep rather than getting a crontab entry of their own
Decision: `evaluateAutoTrips` runs inside the `spend_cap_sweep` task, after `evaluateSpendCaps`. No new scheduled job, no new crontab entry; the existing entry's description now says what the job actually does.
Why: it is the same act on the same clock — read our own counters, compare with the ceilings in `packages/rules`, raise a switch — and a second entry would have added a ninth handlerless-then-handled name to a file the integrator hand-resolves, for no behaviour anybody asked for. Five minutes is well inside the response time either condition needs: the judge trip is measured over fifty drafts and the publish trip over an hour. **The consequence, and it is the important one:** these trips inherit the schedule's fate. Cron is off, so they do not run — see the separate entry on what this card leaves dormant.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 — silent on how often auto-trips are evaluated.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.4 — A chart is identified by a marker written into its description, so renaming it in the analytics UI fails the build instead of duplicating it
Decision: the provisioner writes `[managed by sortiva — edit ops/posthog/definitions, not this. key=<key>]` into every object's description, and finds its own work again by that marker rather than by name. Check mode fails three ways: something missing, something edited in the UI, or something carrying our marker that no file claims any more. Anything without the marker is left alone.
Why: the requirement is create-or-update by a stable key, and names are the one field a person will edit. Matching on name means a renamed dashboard reads as missing and the next apply makes a second one — the exact duplication the stable key exists to prevent. Putting the marker in the description rather than in tags also means whoever opens the chart in the vendor's UI is told, in the place they are about to edit, that the repo is the truth. Leaving unmarked objects alone is deliberate: exploring in the UI is fine, and a provisioner that deletes somebody's scratch chart is a provisioner nobody runs.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 — "created and updated via the API from definition files versioned in the repo, applied idempotently (create-or-update by a stable key)"; silent on where the key lives.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.4 — Without analytics credentials the drift check validates and says it could not compare, rather than failing
Decision: `pnpm posthog:check` with no `POSTHOG_PERSONAL_API_KEY` / `POSTHOG_PROJECT_ID` validates the definition files, prints that it did **not** compare them with a live project, and exits 0. Setting `POSTHOG_REQUIRE_LIVE_CHECK=true` makes the missing key a failure; CI and deploy, which have the secrets, set it.
Why: the spec requires drift to fail the build, and the build that can see the project must keep doing that. But the same command is what every developer runs, and no workspace here has analytics secrets — so failing unconditionally would make the check red for everybody from the moment the first definition landed, which is how a check stops being read. This is a change from the previous behaviour, which did fail: that was written when the directory was empty and the branch was unreachable. **The risk being accepted, stated plainly:** a CI job that forgets the flag passes without checking anything, and says so in its output. The flag is the thing to verify when the analytics project is first set up.
Nearest spec: tech §5 — "the PostHog provisioning script in check mode (drift between repo definitions and the live project fails the build)"; silent on an environment with no project.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.4 — The `domain` group type is declared, cannot be created by the script, and is reported as drift until an event makes it
Decision: `00-group-type.json` declares it; `--apply` reports it skipped with the reason; `--check` reports it as drift with its own message rather than "run --apply".
Why: the analytics vendor creates a group type the first time an event arrives carrying it. There is no endpoint that makes one, so reporting it as created would be a lie that goes unnoticed until somebody asks why a cost breakdown is empty. Failing the check is the right direction anyway: until the group exists, every cost-broken-down-by-domain chart shows nothing, which reads as "this store costs nothing" — the most expensive possible misreading of a cost dashboard.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 — "the `domain` group type … created and updated via the API"; the vendor does not offer that.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.4 — `article_cost_finalized` carries the split as well as the total, and has no caller
Decision: `articleCostFinalized` in `packages/core/src/ops/article-cost.ts` builds the event with `usd_total`, `usd_llm` and `usd_seo`. Nothing calls it: there is no article to finalise the cost of.
Why: the spec's rule is that true cost per domain is model spend plus search-data spend, "never LLM-only". A single total is easy to compute and impossible to audit — a chart showing one number cannot tell you whether the search-data half was included. Carrying the split means a dashboard that shows only one of them is visibly showing only one. `published_via_override` rides along because override-published articles have to be excluded from calibration and segmented in reporting, and the cost event is where the reporting side learns of them. **What is needed to make it fire:** an article that ships, and a way to attribute spend rows to it — `spend_events` has no article column today, so the caller will have to sum the calls it made itself.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 — "`article_cost_finalized` (`usd_total`) stamps the all-in cost when an article ships"; silent on the split and on attribution.
