## 2026-09-02 — T8.4 — An operator command exists, because a manual switch nobody can flip is not a manual switch
Decision: `pnpm switch list | raise | lower` (`scripts/kill-switch.mjs`), beside the existing dead-letter and diagnosis commands. It refuses a raise with no `--reason`, refuses to lower a global switch without a second name, refuses the same name twice, and refuses `auto` as an operator.
Why: the card's scope says "`ops_flags` manual flags", and the repository functions alone leave an operator writing SQL by hand — which is both how a flip gets recorded with no reason and how the four-eyes rule gets bypassed. Same shape and same directory as the two operator commands already in the repo. It is **the mechanics of the approved feature, not a new one**: without it the manual half of this card is unreachable.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 — "every flip is logged with actor + reason", which presumes something an operator uses.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.4 — Every job is put behind the kill switches by being registered, not by remembering to ask
Decision: `registerTask` in `packages/jobs/src/runtime/tasks.ts` now wraps every handler in a check that reads `ops_flags` at the moment the job starts. A paused job returns having done nothing and **does not throw**, so the queue neither retries it nor dead-letters it. Six named jobs are exempt and listed in `UNGATED_TASKS` (`gate.ts`), each with its reason in writing.
Why: the spec puts the check "at job dequeue" and the existing gate function had no production caller at all — it was consulted by tests only. Wiring it into each lane's dispatcher would have meant editing three other lanes' directories, and would have left the guarantee resting on every future card remembering. Registration is the one seam every job passes through, so the check cannot be skipped by forgetting; a job that does not want it has to be named in a file that says why. The exemptions matter as much as the rule: `global.pause_all` means "stop working for merchants", not "stop being a company", so the spend-cap sweep (the brake itself), the retention sweep and account close (deletion deadlines are legal, with a clock), billing reconciliation and the mail drain keep running. Gating those would mean the one action an operator takes in an incident also switches off the brake that catches the next runaway and the sweep that satisfies a GDPR deadline.
**Consequence for other lanes, stated because it is a behaviour change they did not ask for:** any job registered from now on stops when the product is paused unless it is added to that list. `bootstrapWorker` refuses to start if nothing installed a way to read the switches, because a worker whose jobs consult no switches looks exactly like a healthy one; two existing runtime tests were updated to install one.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 — "Manual flags, checked at job dequeue (effective within 60s)"; silent on where in the code that check lives and on which jobs are exempt.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T8.4 — Four eyes is two names on one command, not a proposal waiting for approval
Decision: `reviewReset` in `packages/core/src/ops/kill-switches.ts` refuses to lower a global switch unless two different, named operators are supplied, and records both in `reset_by` as `"alice + bob"`. An account switch needs one name. Nothing automatic may lower any switch.
Why: the spec says a global flag "requires a second operator to reset". The stronger reading — operator A proposes, operator B approves later — needs somewhere to hold "proposed but not yet approved", and `ops_flags` has only `reset_at` / `reset_by`; adding a column is a migration and schema wave 4 is closed. What the built version does guarantee is the property the rule exists for: no one person can lower a global switch on their own authority, and the second person is on the record rather than optional. What it does **not** guarantee is that the two people were in different rooms — one operator who knows a colleague's name can type it. **If that matters, it needs one nullable column (`proposed_by`) and a mini-wave**, and it is the integrator's call, not this card's.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 — "Global flags require a second operator to reset (four-eyes), and every flip is logged with actor + reason"; silent on the mechanism.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T8.4 — The incident record is the flag row, because there is nowhere else to put one
Decision: no incidents table. `listActiveFlags` + `incidentFrom` read `ops_flags` as the open-incident list: an automatic trip never lowers itself, so an open incident *is* an active row whose `tripped_by` is `auto`. Manual pauses appear in the same list, because an operator wants one answer to "what is stopped right now".
Why: the spec asks for an incident record per auto-trip and this card may not add a migration. The flag row already carries everything an incident needs — what raised it, why, when, whether it is still open, who closed it — and the partial unique index means a sweep that keeps re-tripping while the condition holds produces one incident rather than a queue of them. The cost, stated: there is nowhere to write what an operator *found* when they investigated, so the follow-up notes live outside the product. A real incidents table would fix that and is a wave-5 candidate.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 — "Every auto-trip creates an incident record and alerts"; silent on where it lives.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.4 — Two new per-account switches for the two paid things a merchant sets off by clicking
Decision: `account.pause_intent_gap` and `account.pause_optimize` are new flag names, raised when a store crosses its own daily count for intent-gap analyses or OPTIMIZE generations. `mayCallTypeRun` is the gate; crossing one stops that call type for that store and nothing else.
Why: the spec requires "exceeding one pauses that call type only — the content pipeline is unaffected" but names no flag, and the four flags it does name are all coarser than that. Reusing `account.pause_generation` would stop the daily article as well, which is the exact behaviour the sentence rules out. The count comes from the same `spend_events` ledger the dollar caps read — one meter, not two that can disagree — and cache replays are excluded, because a recommendation served back out of the request cache bought nothing new and should not spend the merchant's allowance.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 (per-type caps), §7.10 / `budgets` in `signals.config.yaml` (the numbers); silent on the flag names.
## 2026-09-03 — T3.5 — A shared search listing is one piece of work, not one per page sharing it
Decision: the missing/duplicate listing signal produces **at most one detection per page**, whatever is wrong with it. A group of pages carrying the same listing title or description produces a single detection, on the first of them by address, with the whole group named in the evidence. Comparison is case-insensitive. Only collections and product pages are judged.
Why: two forces point the same way, and the card's own acceptance test only works if they are honoured. The database allows one open row per page per signal — the index is unique on account, signal type and entity — so a second detection for the same address could not survive being written; whichever landed last would silently replace the other. And a merchant fixing this does one job per page, not one per field. The card's fixture is two collections sharing a title plus one with none, and it must yield exactly two: the pair is one job (make them differ, which needs the group listed) and the blank one is another. Restricting to collections and products is main §11's own row — a returns policy is not competing for a shopper's click, and listing it would bury the pages that are. **"Weak" metadata is deliberately not detected**: §7.3 defines it as a mismatch against what the search results expect, which needs a paid search read, and this card's scope says no external calls.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (missing/weak metadata row), §11 (collections & products), §7.9 (dedupe key).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T3.5 — A search worth an article is one where somebody is deciding what to buy
Decision: "commercial intent" means the buying-guide and comparison intent classes. How-to and informational searches do not produce an uncovered-search signal.
Why: main §7.3 says "commercial intent class (buying guide / comparison / category-supporting)" and the frozen intent enum has four members — buying_guide, comparison, how_to, informational — with no "category-supporting" among them. Something had to map. Buying-guide and comparison are named literally. Neither of the remaining two is category-supporting in any reading a merchant would recognise, and treating "informational" as commercial would fill a calendar with definitions that sell nothing, which is the failure §1.4's ecommerce-first principle names. The cost of being wrong this way is recall: a genuinely commercial search classified how_to is skipped. If "category-supporting" was meant to be a fifth intent class, that is a change to a frozen contract and belongs to the integrator.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (uncovered commercial query row); `packages/core/contracts` `INTENT_CLASSES` — four members, none named category-supporting.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T3.5 — CONTRADICTION: the competitor-gap rule and its own worked example disagree about positions 11 to 20, and this card follows the rule
Decision: the competitor-coverage signal fires when the store holds **no position at or better than `our_absent_position_max`** (20) — main §7.3's rule, read literally. Where the store's best position falls in the middling band (`optimize_position_min`–`optimize_position_max`, 11–30) the page and its position travel in the evidence, so the decision that follows can choose to improve that page instead of writing a new one. Positions 11–20 therefore produce nothing from this signal.
Why: §7.3 says the signal needs "we hold no position ≤ 20"; §7.3's own action column and §7.8's mapping table both say "competitor gap + relevant URL exists at #18 → improve it". A page at #18 is a position ≤ 20, so the two sentences cannot both be obeyed. Taking the union — firing whenever we are not on page one — would make the 20 inert, and a configured number that decides nothing is worse than a gap, because the next person to tune it will believe it works. So the rule wins and the overlap is recorded. **This matters to somebody else's card:** `T3.6`'s done-when is "competitor-gap fixture yields ... OPTIMIZE with a URL at #18", and under the rule as written that fixture produces no competitor-gap signal at all. Either §7.3's `our_absent_position_max` should be 10 (making the two consistent), or `T3.6`'s done-when should name a position in 21–30. Founder or integrator, not this card.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (competitor coverage gap row — rule and action column contradict each other), §7.8 (mapping table), build plan `T3.6` done-when.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T3.5 — A range's coverage is content that has been shown, not a page that exists
Decision: a product family counts as covered when something mapped to it is either an article this product published, or a page search has actually shown at least once. A collection page that exists and has never been shown is not coverage, and its address is listed in the signal as evidence.
Why: main §7.3 asks for a family "with zero published/ranking content mapped to it", and almost every family in every store has a collection page — so reading "content" as "any page" would silence this signal on every store that uses collections, which is all of them. Reading it as "published or ranking" is the phrase's own wording and the only reading under which the signal fires at all. It also matches what the merchant would say: a listing page nobody has ever been shown is not coverage of the range, it is a list of products.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (product family coverage gap row) — "published/ranking content", undefined.

## 2026-09-03 — T3.5 — A fact is a field and its value together, so a shelf of clones does not read as a deep catalogue
Decision: the substance floor counts distinct facts as `field=value` pairs, deduplicated across the products that qualify as sources; each entry of a list field counts separately. Only products clearing the per-product field floor contribute either facts or the source count.
Why: main §8.2 requires the measure to survive two specific attacks — "can't be gamed by long fluffy descriptions or by 40 near-identical clones" — and names no counting rule. Counting facts per product would let forty identical listings look like forty sources. Counting distinct values is what makes them read as one. Each list entry counting separately is the other half: three stated uses are three things an article could be built on, and collapsing them would make a richly described product look like a bare one. Same shape as `countFacts` in the distillation module, which the confirmation screen already shows a merchant, so the two cannot drift into telling a store different things.
Nearest spec: main §8.2 (substance inventory) — states the requirement, defines no count.

## 2026-09-03 — T3.5 — Worked example 3 is asserted through the store's page list, and the shared fixture is left alone
Decision: the example-3 test builds the store's inventory as the scenario declares itself — the hiking-boots collection present, no page serving the road-running search — rather than from the fixture's own `pages` array, which lists a collection for every family. `packages/core/src/fixtures/scenarios.ts` is not touched.
Why: the scenario carries `hasSuitableUrl: false` and a note saying the check must find nothing, and its generated page list contradicts that by giving every family a collection. A shop whose products are not all in collections is an ordinary state, so the declared version is buildable without inventing anything. Three lanes read that fixture file and `T3.4` already declined to edit it for the same kind of defect in scenario 5; a fixture change that alters another lane's inputs is not a feature card's to make. **Whoever next owns the fixture should reconcile the two** — either drop the road-running collection from scenario 3's page list, or change the flag.
Nearest spec: main §7.8 example 3; build plan `T3.5` done-when ("examples 3 and 7 pass"); precedent in DECISIONS 2026-09-02 `T3.4`.

## 2026-09-03 — T3.5 — A page the merchant may have deleted still counts as a page we have, and the check says so rather than assuming it
Decision: the existing-target check reads a three-way answer for every address — still published, taken down, or **nobody knows** — and today every address answers "nobody knows", because nothing in the product records a deletion. "Nobody knows" is treated as still published: the page can take the work over, and improving it is what the merchant is told to do. The reading travels in the evidence as `existing_target_presence`, so a card built from it can say the page's continued existence is unverified instead of asserting it. One line in `packages/jobs/src/scan/existing-target.ts` produces that answer, and it becomes correct the moment a marker exists.
Why: `T3.2` left a deleted page's inventory row in place with nothing marking it gone, and `T8.0` declined to add the column because its shape leaks into what this card reads; both entries say the founder or the integrator must settle it before this card ships. It is not settled, so the card had to choose which way to be wrong. The two errors are not symmetrical. Treating a live page as gone is what makes us publish a second page competing with it — the exact failure invariant 6 exists to prevent, and one that shows no error anywhere: the store simply ends up with two pages splitting one search and neither winning. Treating a gone page as live costs one suggestion the merchant dismisses. So the check fails towards not creating. **What the founder still has to decide is the marker's shape**, and there is a second option worth knowing about before they do: the inventory already stamps every row with when the nightly walk last saw it, so "not seen by the last completed full walk" could stand in for a column — but only if something also records that a walk *completed*, because an interrupted walk would otherwise mark live pages as gone and produce exactly the competing pages this rule exists to stop. That trade — a column and a schema wave, against a heuristic that is wrong when a sweep dies — is a product call, not this card's.
Nearest spec: main §7.7 (the check), §12.3 (the inventory sync, silent on removal); DECISIONS 2026-09-02 `T3.2` and `T8.0`; invariant 6.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T3.5 — A new page needs a permission slip only the check can issue, rather than a convention every future caller must remember
Decision: the check returns a `CreateClearance` — a value that cannot be constructed anywhere else, in the type system or at runtime — and it is non-null exactly when a new page may go ahead. A weak match's internal-linking task is carried inside it, so honouring the match and honouring the link cannot come apart. `assertClearedToCreate` throws when a caller has none, or has one issued for a different topic. A test proves exactly one file can mint one.
Why: the rule is "no new page without this check", and the ordinary way to enforce that is to ask everyone to call the check first. That works until one caller forgets, and the failure is invisible — a second page competing with the merchant's own looks exactly like a first page. Making the permission a value moves the failure from "nobody noticed" to "it does not compile, and if you cast past that it throws". Cost and honest limit: **the guarantee only bites once the callers take the argument**, and neither caller exists yet — `T3.6` writes the opportunity row and `T4.1` is Lane D's topic gate. Both should take a clearance rather than a boolean. Flagged in the session report for the integrator, because it is a shape another lane consumes.
Nearest spec: main §7.7, §8.2 ("literally the same function"); invariant 6 — states the rule, names no mechanism.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T3.5 — Search Console's reading of a page is final for that page; the other two lookups speak only for pages Google has said nothing about
Decision: all three lookups run and the best page wins, but they do not overlap. A page Search Console reported for the intent is judged on its measured position and nothing else; the store's own content mapping, and the no-Search-Console vendor fallback, contribute only addresses Search Console did not report. Ties break on source order, then position, then address.
Why: §7.7 says "look ... in order" and then, separately, that a match past position 30 is weak. Taking the first source that answered anything would let a stray page ranking at 88 for some unrelated reason hide a collection that plainly serves the subject. Taking the strongest answer regardless of source would do the opposite: a collection ranking at 61 would be promoted back to a strong match by the mere fact that we can see what it sells, which deletes the weak branch the spec spells out — a page ranking that badly is not rescued by editing, and the right answer is to write the new page and link the two. Splitting by URL keeps both halves true: a measurement beats an inference about the same page, and an inference is better than nothing about a page never measured.
Nearest spec: main §7.7 steps 2–4 — orders the sources, does not say what to do when two disagree.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T3.5 — A page whose purpose nobody has established is a weak match, not a strong one and not a miss
Decision: where the store's own content mapping finds a page selling the right products but nothing records what the page is *for*, the check returns a weak match: the new page may go ahead, and must link to the existing one. A page recorded with a different purpose is not a match at all. A single product's page is always weak, whatever it ranks for.
Why: §7.7 requires families **and** intent class to match, and `store_pages.intent_class` exists in the schema with nothing writing it — the same unowned gap `T3.4` reported for cannibalization. Reading the empty column as "no match" would let a store with a perfectly good collection get a competing article, which is the failure invariant 6 names. Reading it as "match" would block nearly every new page on any store, since almost every collection sells products from some family we would write about — the product would stop producing content. Weak is the honest middle: it is enough to stop us writing blind, because the two pages get tied together, and not enough to hand improvement work to a page that may not be able to do it. The product-page rule is separate and firm: every kind of article we write covers a subject broader than one item, so a product page is a supporting asset rather than a target.
Nearest spec: main §7.7 step 2(b) and step 4; main §13 `store_pages.intent_class`; DECISIONS 2026-09-02 `T3.4` (same empty column).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T3.5 — Our own article is rewritten rather than recommended for changes, and the refresh cooldown is not this function's to enforce
Decision: a strong match on a page the inventory marks as one of our own published articles converts the work to REFRESH; anything else converts it to OPTIMIZE. Whether the article is old enough to be refreshed again is not checked here.
Why: §7.7 says "OPTIMIZE ... or REFRESH if it's our article older than the cooldown", and an article's age is unknowable today — the `articles` table arrives in schema wave 3, and the inventory row carries only when we last read the page, not when it was published. The cooldown's numbers live under `learning.refresh` in `packages/rules`, which is the replenishment card's block, and refusing to schedule a too-recent refresh is a scheduling decision the calendar already owns. The part that matters for invariant 6 is unaffected either way: the work goes to the page we already have, and no competing page is created.
Nearest spec: main §7.7 step 3, §9.6.5 (cooldown); main §13 `articles` — not yet built.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T3.5 — How many of the domain's own rankings to buy in the no-Search-Console fallback is a cost lever, not a threshold
Decision: the vendor is asked for up to 500 of the domain's rankings, and that number lives beside the call rather than in `packages/rules`.
Why: `packages/rules` holds the numbers that decide what the product believes — a band, a floor, a ratio — each with a note saying what changing it would do to a merchant's results. This one changes nothing about any decision: the rows come back or they do not, and the check looks for one intent among them. Putting it in the rules file would mean every change to it moves `rules_version` and invalidates the traceability of every opportunity in the system, for a number that is about the size of a page of vendor results. Same shape as the batch-size decision `T3.2` took.
Nearest spec: main §7.11 (names the fallback, no size); invariant 9; DECISIONS 2026-09-02 `T3.2`.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T8.3 — BLOCKED: the deletion-confirmation email is still unsendable, and it is now two problems rather than one
Decision: no deletion-confirmation email is sent. The deletion is recorded, the vendors are told, and the merchant gets an HTTP answer and nothing in their inbox.
Why: `T8.2` recorded the first half — `email_sends.type` is the notification-type enum and there is no value for a deletion confirmation, so the row cannot be written; schema wave 4 is closed and a feature card does not add its own migration. Building this card surfaced a second half nobody had noticed: **`email_sends` and `notifications` both cascade from `accounts`, so the record of the confirmation is deleted along with the account it confirms.** In practice the send happens within a minute and the erase seven days later, so the mail would go — but the audit trail for "we told them" would then be erased by our own sweep, which is exactly the wrong record to lose. **What is needed:** one value on the `notification_type` enum, *and* a ruling on whether an account-security send is recorded somewhere a cascade cannot reach. Recommended: the same treatment the spend ledger and the completed-work ledger already have — no foreign key, pruned by age only.
Nearest spec: tech §1.5 ("suppressed addresses skip the queue except for account-security email (deletion confirmation)"), §1.7; main §14.6.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.3 — The vendor half of a deletion is a job, because no Stripe call may sit in a request path
Decision: `POST /api/account/delete` does only local work — the deletion stamp, the domain's release deadline, both connections marked dead, the preview row dropped — and queues `account_close`, which cancels the subscription and hands both access grants back.
Why: two documents pull opposite ways. Invariant 16 and tech §3 are absolute — "no Stripe API call ever sits in a request path or at scheduler dequeue" — and main §14.6 asks for "immediate Stripe subscription cancellation" on a screen where the merchant is told there will be no further charges. Splitting satisfies both: the cancel happens seconds later rather than synchronously, and it is *more* reliable there, because a queued step retries on backoff and dead-letters where an alert watches, while an in-request call gets one attempt and leaves a merchant guessing. The tokens deliberately survive the first half — a job cannot be handed a credential through a queue payload, and a queue payload is a row in a table — so the connections are marked dead (which stops the product using them and stops a webhook resolving to that account) and the job deletes them once the vendors have been told. **Consequence to accept:** with the worker not running, a deleted account's subscription is not cancelled until it is. **Contradiction surfaced, not resolved silently:** "immediate" in main §14.6 now means "queued immediately", and if the founder wants it literally synchronous, invariant 16 has to gain a stated exception.
Nearest spec: main §14.6, §4.2; tech §3; invariant 16.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.3 — `shop/redact` is honoured by the nightly sweep, reading the delivery's envelope and never its body
Decision: the retention sweep scans stored `shop/redact` deliveries, resolves the store handle to an account — deliberately including connections that are already dead, because a redaction arrives about two days after the uninstall — and erases everything that came out of that Shopify store. It runs before the prune, in the same sweep. Idempotent with no marker: erasing also deletes the store's connection row, so the next pass finds no account behind the handle; and a connection made *after* the request was received is left alone, so a merchant who reinstalls is not wiped nightly until the request ages out.
Why: `T2.2` recorded that `shop/redact` had a receiver and no consequence and asked for an owner; the `T2.2` audit escalated it, adding that the only durable record of such a request is a row the retention policy would delete at exactly thirty days — the day the obligation matures. This card owns both halves, so the ordering is the fix: honour on the night the request arrives, and the prune can never race it. Reading only the envelope — the shop handle the receiver copies out of a request header — is deliberate and is recorded here because it is a constraint, not a preference: **the stored body of a privacy delivery is the subject of an open, unfixed audit finding, and no new behaviour should depend on its shape.** What "store-derived" was taken to mean: products and their facts, families, top products, store pages, landing-page takings, the persona, keywords and competitors, plus the connection itself. Everything of those is read off the store's own words and products, so leaving any would leave the store described in our database after being asked to erase it. Not touched: the merchant's own account, their billing, and Search Console data, which arrives under a separate grant from Google.
Nearest spec: main §14.6 — "`shop/redact` … purge all store-derived data within 30 days".

## 2026-09-02 — T8.3 — Vacation mode, billing and deletion are answered by one function, and it has no caller yet
Decision: `lifecycleGate` in `packages/core/src/lifecycle` decides what an account may do; `accountLifecycleGate` in `packages/jobs/src/runtime/gate.ts` reads the three facts out of the database in one query and calls it. **Nothing consults it today**, because the daily generation cycle and the publish path that would are another lane's and are not built.
Why: main §14.6 gives vacation mode one sentence — halts topic generation and publishing, keeps catalogue sync and Search Console reporting alive — and that sentence is a product answer that needs exactly one home; two call sites deciding separately is how "does vacation mode stop the nightly walk" gets two answers. It sits beside `mayAccountWorkRun` because that file is already "the one question every dispatcher asks", but stays a separate function: a kill switch is an operator decision about the whole product and an unreadable one means stop, while this is the merchant's own state. **What the content lane must do:** call it at dequeue, alongside the kill-switch gate. **Reported rather than assumed:** the vacation-mode column has existed since wave 1 and the settings screen can set it; until that call exists, switching it on changes nothing.
Nearest spec: main §14.6 (vacation mode), §4.2 (billing gates generation and publishing only); invariant 16.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.3 — Three provider interfaces gain a revocation method, which is a change to contracts frozen in M0
Decision: `StripeBillingProvider.cancelSubscription`, `ShopifyOAuthProvider.revokeAccess` and `GscProvider.revoke`, each with its real implementation and its test double. All three treat "the vendor has already forgotten it" as success.
Why: main §14.6 requires a deleted account's subscription cancelled and its Shopify and Google tokens revoked, and none of the three wrappers had any way to say so. The alternative — a revoker owned by this lane that makes the HTTP calls itself — would put vendor calls outside the wrapper that exists so every call to that vendor is visible in one place, which is the rule invariant 25 states for the LLM and search-data clients and the codebase applies to all of them. The additions are purely additive and no existing caller changes. **Treating "already gone" as success is what makes the deletion job retryable**: without it, a second attempt after a half-finished first one fails on the success of the first. **Cost, stated:** `packages/core/src/contracts/gsc.ts` and `packages/core/src/catalog/ports.ts` are M0-frozen contracts owned by other lanes, and six inline test doubles across three lanes gained a method. All are named in the session report.
Nearest spec: main §14.6; invariant 25; build plan §4 (contracts frozen in M0).

## 2026-09-02 — T8.3 — A deleted account's rows are erased at the moment the domain is released, not before
Decision: requesting deletion writes the deletion stamp, sets the domain's release deadline seven days out, cancels the subscription and hands back both access grants — but erases nothing. The rows are erased by the daily sweep on the day the domain becomes claimable again, seven days later.
Why: main §14.6 asks for two different clocks — the domain claim released after a 7-day grace, the data erased within 30 days — and the database ties them together whether we like it or not. `domains` is a child of `accounts` with `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so deleting the account row deletes the domain row with it, which frees the domain the same hour and destroys the grace window. There is no version of "erase now, release later" that does not first rebuild the account row as a shell that still holds the merchant's email address, which is itself the thing being erased. Erasing at day seven satisfies both sentences of the spec and is well inside the thirty days allowed. Cost stated plainly: a merchant who asks to be deleted stays in the database for a week, during which the lifecycle gate stops every kind of work including read access. Alternative rejected: a table-by-table delete that spares `accounts` and `domains` — it duplicates the cascade in application code, and every future table would have to remember to join the list.
Nearest spec: main §14.6 — "hard-delete of PII and order-derived aggregates within 30 days; domain claim released after a 7-day grace window".

## 2026-09-02 — T8.3 — Retention windows live beside the lifecycle code, not in `packages/rules`
Decision: the number of days each table is kept for lives in `packages/core/src/lifecycle/retention.ts`, as a table of rules each carrying a plain-language reason.
Why: invariant 9 puts every threshold literal in `packages/rules` and stamps that package's hash on each decision it influenced — and it is specifically about the numbers that decide what the product *notices*: search volumes, positions, impressions, click-through rates (main §7.3, §7.6, §8.2, §9.6). A retention window decides nothing about a merchant's store. Filing it there would mean that changing how long we keep email records changes `rules_version`, which is stamped on every opportunity and gate decision — so an operating-cost decision would look, in the audit trail, like a change to how the product judges a store. Nearest spec for the windows themselves is tech §1.7 and §2.1, neither of which is a scoring threshold.
Nearest spec: tech §1.7, §2.1; main §7.10 (the rule this is deliberately outside).

## 2026-09-02 — T8.3 — Search Console history is deleted at sixteen months, not rolled up, because there is nowhere to roll it up to
Decision: the retention sweep deletes `gsc_daily` and `gsc_query_daily` rows older than sixteen months. The spec's monthly aggregates are not built and no table exists for them, so this prune loses the long-run trend rather than compacting it. The warning is written on the rule itself and held by a test.
Why: tech §2.1 says "`gsc_daily` keeps 16 months then rolls up to monthly aggregates" and makes keeping Postgres small a hard cost rule; the roll-up half needs a table, and a table needs a migration, which a feature card may not add. Of the two halves, the prune is the one the cost rule depends on and the one the card's scope names. Doing neither would leave the largest-growing pair of tables in the product unbounded. **What is lost, and it is real:** Google itself only serves sixteen months, so a deleted row can never be re-fetched — after the first store passes sixteen months, its earlier history is gone for good and any "how did this page do two years ago" question becomes unanswerable. **What is needed:** a monthly roll-up table in the next schema wave, and a job that fills it before this prune runs.
Nearest spec: tech §2.1.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.3 — A requested deletion takes read access away, which billing never does
Decision: the lifecycle gate answers `readAllowed: false` once `accounts.deleted_at` is set, and `readAllowed: true` for every billing state including cancelled.
Why: invariant 16 and main §4.2 are emphatic that billing state gates generation and publishing only and that read access is never revoked — a lapsed subscriber keeps everything we already made for them. main §14.6's deletion is a different act: the merchant asked to be erased, the subscription is cancelled outright rather than at period end, and the rows are on a countdown. Continuing to serve their dashboard for the seven days before the erase would be showing someone data they have asked us to destroy. The two are kept in one function precisely so nobody reads the deletion rule as a softening of the billing one.
Nearest spec: main §14.6, §4.2; invariant 16.
## 2026-09-02 — T2.5 — The eval discovery walk now recognises `.smoke`, because otherwise the persona set would exist and never run
Decision: `discoverEvalSets` accepts a directory ending in `.eval` **or** `.smoke`. `packages/llm/eval/persona.smoke/` is the set; a test asserts the walk finds it.
Why: the spec and CLAUDE.md fix the three sets' names as `distillation.eval`, `judge.eval` and `persona.smoke`, and the walk written at M0 matched `.eval` only — its own doc comment lists all three as directory names, so this is a defect rather than a rule. The consequence was not a failure but a silence: the set could be written, reviewed and merged while being discovered by nothing, and `pnpm eval` would report a pass over nine cases it never looked at. The runner already refuses to skip a set whose runner is missing, on exactly that reasoning ("a suite that is not running cannot look like a suite that is passing"); this closes the same hole one level up. The alternative — renaming the directory to `persona.smoke.eval` — was rejected because the fixed names exist so that CI and audits can find them by name.
Nearest spec: main §14.2 (the three frozen sets and their names); CLAUDE.md ("spec-defined suites … have fixed names so CI and audits can find them").

## 2026-09-02 — T2.5 — A description is judged for length and vocabulary against its own script, not against English
Decision: the non-degeneracy check holds a description to 80 characters and three-character words in an alphabetic language, and to 30 characters and two-character words where at least 30% of the text is Han, kana or Hangul.
Why: found by the smoke set's own Japanese case, which failed against the first version of the check. 急須 is a complete noun in two characters and a genuine two-sentence Japanese description runs to about forty; a single English-shaped bar would have marked every Japanese store's description as saying nothing about the store, and the failure would have looked like the model's rather than ours. The share test rather than "contains any such character" so a German description quoting one Japanese product name is still held to a German length.
Nearest spec: main §14.2 — requires "description non-degeneracy", defines no measure.


## 2026-09-02 — T2.5 — A store's default publish timezone is written once and never moved by a later run
Decision: `ensureAccountTimezone` inserts `account_settings` with the zone the persona's country implies and does nothing if the row already exists. The step then reads back whatever the account actually has and reports it, so its recorded output names the zone in force rather than the one it proposed.
Why: this account is the first writer of `account_settings` in the whole product — the row did not exist before this card, and the settings screen and the email schedules read it with a UTC fallback. The zone is a *default*, and main §9.4 explicitly lets the merchant "pick a specific one in settings". Onboarding re-runs for ordinary reasons — a re-sync, a redelivered job, a corrected persona — and an upsert would silently move a merchant's publishing back to our guess every time one happened, with no error and nothing in the interface to explain it. Insert-if-absent makes the write safe to repeat, which is what every step here has to be.
Nearest spec: main §9.4 (publish hour, configurable); main §14.3 (every worker effectively-once).

## 2026-09-02 — T2.5 — Reading the store's own Shopify settings is best-effort; the persona is built without them rather than stalling
Decision: the persona step asks Shopify for the shop's `primary_locale`, `country_code` and `currency`, and on any failure — dead token, outage, timeout — logs it and continues with the evidence it already has (the homepage's markup, the web address). It does not route the store to the reconnect screen and does not fail the step.
Why: this is the strongest link in the locale chain but it is still only a link, and the chain is built to answer without it. Everything the persona is actually made of — families, fact sheets, best sellers — is already in our own tables by the time this step runs, so a metadata read failing is not a reason to take a merchant's onboarding off the road. The connection's own health is not this step's business: `catalog_sync` and `oauth_wait` both route a dead token to the reconnect screen, and doing it here as well would raise the same prompt twice. Consequence to accept: a store whose token dies between the catalogue sync and the persona gets its language from its own markup instead of its Shopify setting, which is a slightly weaker answer the merchant then confirms.
Nearest spec: main §6.5 (the input chain); main §14.4 (degrade to pause) — this is a degraded *input*, not a degraded output, and the model call itself is unchanged.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.5 — The persona's idempotency key covers the catalogue, not the store's own pages
Decision: `persona`'s `input_version` is a hash of the family fingerprints (name, member count, sorted axes), the best-seller list and the catalogue size. The homepage and about-page text are an input to the call and deliberately not to the key.
Why: main §14.3.2 defines this step's key as "hash of their upstream artifacts", and the upstream artefacts are the families and the fact sheets — the pages are a supplement fetched at execution time. Keying on page text would also make the key depend on a third party's markup: a rotating homepage banner or a changed script tag would buy a fresh call on the stronger tier every night, for a store that sells exactly what it sold yesterday. Consequence to accept: a merchant who rewrites their about page without touching their catalogue keeps the existing description until something upstream moves, and can correct it at confirmation (main §6.8).
Nearest spec: main §14.3.2 — names the key's shape, does not enumerate the artefacts.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.5 — `personas.richness_score` is recomputed from the fact sheets at persona time rather than carried from distillation
Decision: the persona step calls the same roll-up distillation calls, over every fact sheet the store has, and stamps the result on the profile row.
Why: the column has existed since schema wave 2 with nothing writing it, and `T2.3` had nowhere to put the number it computed. Recomputing rather than passing it along means the two can never disagree: distillation reports the store's richness as it was at the end of its own run, and a run that only re-distilled twelve changed products must still describe the whole catalogue. It reads the same substance floor from `packages/rules` that the Opportunity Engine's own substance check reads, so the band a merchant is shown at confirmation and the floor that later decides whether we will write about a family are one judgement.
Nearest spec: main §6.3 (per-store richness score), §8.2 (the substance check that shares its floor), §13 `personas.richness_score`.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.5 — The persona step emits no PostHog event of its own
Decision: no `persona_completed` event. The step logs its outcome, and the model call is captured by the instrumented wrapper as `$ai_generation` with `call_type: persona`, `prompt_version` and `cache_hit`, which is what §14.7 requires of it.
Why: §14.7's pipeline taxonomy names `ingestion_step_completed`, `distillation_completed` and `family_grouping_completed` and no persona event, and the card does not ask for one. `T2.4` emitted `family_grouping_completed` because its own card named it. Inventing one here would be adding a funnel step nobody has asked to measure. **Surfaced rather than resolved:** if the activation funnel should be able to see how many stores reach a described profile, the event is three lines and the emitter is already bound — it is a founder call about what is measured, not an implementation gap.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 — the event taxonomy, which does not list one.


## 2026-09-02 — T2.5 — The store's language and country are decided by evidence, and the model's answer is the last resort rather than the first
Decision: the persona call still returns `main_language` and `country` as main §6.5's output schema requires, but they are used **only when the deterministic chain produced nothing**. The chain is, strongest first: the store's own Shopify settings (`primary_locale`, `country_code`) → the homepage's `<html lang>` → its `hreflang` tags, but only where they agree → a currency used by exactly one country → the web address's country suffix, minus the two-letter suffixes people buy for their spelling (`.io`, `.ai`, `.co`, `.me`, …). Whichever link answered is recorded on the profile.
Why: main §6.5 lists these sources with an explicit ordering — "from `read_locales` / shop settings, **else** `<html lang>`, hreflang tags, currency symbols, TLD heuristics" — which reads as a chain, and main §14.2's smoke set asserts "language/country **detection** exact-match", which a model call cannot be held to. The consequence of getting it wrong is the reason for the ordering: main §12.1 passes both values to the paid search vendor as its language and location parameters, so a wrong country does not fail — it silently buys another market's search volumes and everything built on them is confident and wrong. A merchant's own Shopify setting is a statement they made; a model reading a catalogue is inferring. The model is kept in the chain rather than dropped because a store whose settings, markup, currency and address all say nothing has no other answer, and the merchant confirms or corrects the whole profile before anything is written (main §6.8).
Nearest spec: main §6.5 (the input chain and the output schema), §14.2 (the smoke set), §12.1 (where the values are spent).

## 2026-09-02 — T2.5 — Ambiguous evidence answers nothing rather than answering with the likeliest
Decision: `hreflang` tags naming two different languages produce no language; a currency used by more than one country (EUR, USD, GBP) produces no country; a country with more than one commercial language (CH, BE, CA, IN, MY, SG, ZA, AE) produces no language from its web address alone — though it still produces a country. Each case falls through to the next link.
Why: the alternative is picking the most common member of the ambiguous set, which is a coin toss written as a fact and is indistinguishable, downstream, from a confident correct answer. A null is recoverable: the next link answers, or the model does, or the merchant corrects it at confirmation. A wrong country code is not, because nothing later in the pipeline has any reason to doubt it.
Nearest spec: main §6.5 — names the signals, is silent on what to do when they disagree.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.5 — The publish clock comes from a country table, never from the timezone Shopify holds for the shop
Decision: `account_settings.timezone` is defaulted from the persona's country through a table in `packages/core/src/persona/timezones.ts` (roughly sixty countries), with the five multi-zone countries main §9.4 names — US, CA, AU, BR, RU — mapped to their most populous business zone and marked as a default rather than a fact. An unlisted country falls back to UTC. The `iana_timezone` Shopify already gives us for the shop is deliberately not used.
Why: main §9.4 is explicit that the clock is the audience's and not the merchant's — "a German store run from Bali publishes at 09:00 Berlin time" — and Shopify's `iana_timezone` is the shop owner's own working clock, which is the wrong answer for exactly the store the rule was written about. UTC rather than a neighbour's zone for an unlisted country because publishing an hour out is a mild inconvenience the merchant can fix in settings, while a confidently wrong zone is not something anyone would think to look at. The table is not in `packages/rules`: that package holds the numbers deciding what we write about, and a zone name is neither a threshold nor another lane's to own.
Nearest spec: main §9.4 (publish hour, multi-zone countries); main §6.5 (the confirmed country).


## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — Email copy lives in `packages/ui/strings`, exactly like screen copy — including the sign-in email lane C parked
Decision: every word a merchant reads in an email is a key in `packages/ui/strings/en.json`. Templates take copy **keys** and resolve them at send time; no email template, adapter or route contains a sentence — the wordmark included. `T-EMAIL` parked its sign-in email's copy beside the auth code pending this ruling: those strings belong in `en.json` under an `email.signIn.*` prefix, alongside the fourteen `email.*` blocks this card added. Lane G has not moved them — they are lane C's files and the integrator reconciles.
Why: the constitution externalises copy from day one and gives one place for it, and the lint rule that enforces it does not care whether the JSX ends up in a browser or a mailbox. Three concrete things follow from keeping email copy there rather than near its sender. A copy fix reaches mail that was queued before the fix was made, because the lookup happens when the email is sent and not when it was decided. A snapshot test can hold a canonical sentence character for character in one place instead of two. And the denominator rule — which is a rule about **our** sentences, not about a merchant's article titles — can be checked against the catalogue entries with their placeholders still unfilled, which is the only form of that check that does not fail on a topic called "A History of Wool". Copy parked next to a sender has none of those three.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md code-structure rules ("Copy: `packages/ui/strings/*.json` only"); tech §1.4; main Appendix A.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — The email fan-out runs in a savepoint, because a swallowed error otherwise rolls back the thing it was reporting
Decision: `DbNotificationEmitter.fanOutToEmail` wraps its reads and its insert in a nested transaction, which Postgres executes as a savepoint when the emitter has been handed an open transaction.
Why: the fan-out already caught and logged its own failures, on the stated reasoning that losing an email is bad and losing the state change it reports is worse. In Postgres that reasoning does not hold without a savepoint: any failed statement aborts the whole transaction, so catching the error leaves the caller's transaction poisoned — every statement after it fails with `25P02` and the gate decision or publish the notification was reporting rolls back. The catch was producing the exact outcome it existed to prevent. The savepoint confines a failure to the email. Proved rather than asserted: a trigger that refuses every `email_sends` insert makes the test fail with `25P02` without the savepoint and pass with it.
Nearest spec: tech §1.3 (the fan-out); main §14.3.1 (guarded transitions and transactional writes).

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — A webhook receiver stores its delivery through a port, not through a database handle of its own
Decision: new `makeWebhookEventStore` in `packages/db/src/stores/`, mirroring `makeEmailStore` and `makeNotificationStore`. It exposes one method — insert-or-ignore on the vendor's own delivery id — and defaults to the process pool inside `packages/db`. The Resend receiver uses it and holds no handle.
Why: the receiver was importing the raw database handle, which is banned outside `packages/db` because that is how a query ends up running without naming the account whose data it touches. The two existing ways past that rule were both wrong here: the lint exemption list for lane A's misplaced repositories says in writing not to add to it, and the composition-root exemption is for files that hand infrastructure downwards without querying, which a receiver does not do. A port keeps the handle inside the package that owns it and needs no exemption. It is written generally because every webhook receiver has the same shape — store the delivery, answer the vendor, decide afterwards — and the Stripe and Shopify ones can adopt it without a new abstraction.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md code-structure rules (no unscoped table access outside `packages/db`); remediation D5.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — The email fan-out happens inside the notification emitter, so no lane has to know its event is emailed
Decision: `DbNotificationEmitter.emit` writes the bell row and then, for the kinds that are emailed, queues the `email_sends` row — consulting the channel matrix, the merchant's preferences and the suppression list. No emission point changed; no `NotificationEmitter` interface changed. A failure to queue the mail is logged and swallowed.
Why: the tech spec draws the fan-out as one step ("event occurs → write `notifications` row → enqueue `email_sends` row if …"), and every lane already calls this one function at its own points. The alternative — each emission point deciding for itself — would put a product rule ("is this kind emailed?") in fourteen places owned by six lanes, and a lane adding an emission point would silently omit the email. Both rows key on the same derived triple, which is what makes an hourly sweep mail once. The swallow is deliberate: `emit` can be handed an open transaction so the notification commits with the decision it reports, and a mail-queueing failure must not roll that decision back.
Nearest spec: tech §1.3.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — The monthly summary is an hourly sweep, not a monthly job
Decision: the `monthly_summary` crontab entry changes from `0 8 1 * *` to `0 * * * *`. Each run asks, per account, whether it is locally the first of the month at 08:00.
Why: the spec puts the summary at "08:00 persona-country time" and a crontab is server time — one monthly firing can only be 08:00 for one zone, and would reach a store in Auckland at eight in the evening. The same problem and the same answer as the weekly signal scan, whose task already filters to accounts for whom it is locally Monday. Running 24 times a day is free of consequence because the dedupe key is the month covered: the run that matches inserts, every other run inserts nothing. An unknown or malformed timezone falls back to UTC rather than throwing, so a store whose zone we failed to record still gets its summary, an hour or two out.
Nearest spec: tech §1.4 ("a job on the 1st at 08:00 persona-country time"); main §9.4 (the persona clock).

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — A send's attempt number travels in the queue message, because `email_sends` has no column for it
Decision: a queued row becomes one Graphile job carrying `{ emailSendId, attempt }`; a retry is a **new** job with `attempt + 1` and an explicit run time from the runtime's 1m / 5m / 25m backoff. The job is filed under a key derived from the row, with `preserve_run_at`.
Why: §14.3.5 requires three retries then the dead-letter queue, which needs an attempt count, and `email_sends` has no such column — schema wave 4 is closed. Two alternatives were worse. Relying on Graphile's own attempt counter would tie our retry schedule to its backoff, which is not ours. Inferring attempts from the row's age would make the count a guess. Carrying it in the payload keeps the schedule in `packages/jobs/runtime/retry.ts` where every other job's is, needs no column, and is directly testable. The job key stops the minute-by-minute drain stacking a second job on a row that already has one, and `preserve_run_at` stops it dragging a pending retry forward to now — which would defeat the backoff entirely.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.5; tech §1.4 ("retries per §14.3.5, then `failed` + DLQ").

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — Without Resend credentials the process gets the test double, not a crash
Decision: `emailProvider()` returns `ResendEmailProvider` when `RESEND_API_KEY` and `EMAIL_FROM` are both set, and `MockEmailProvider` otherwise, with a warning line. There is no flag; the presence of the credential is the switch.
Why: `ResendEmailProvider` throws on construction without a key, and it is built at worker startup — so on a developer's machine the worker would die at boot over email it was never going to send. The double records what would have gone out and enforces the same idempotency contract, so the pipeline is exercised locally end to end with an empty outbox. Same posture as the search-data provider, which already switches on its credentials. **Consequence worth knowing:** a production deploy that loses `RESEND_API_KEY` mails nobody and says so only in a log line. That belongs on the startup-configuration check, which is `T8.4`'s territory, not this card's.
Nearest spec: tech §1.4 (Resend is the committed vendor); tech §4 (secrets).

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — Delivery reports are stored and not acted on, because there is no state for "delivered"
Decision: the Resend receiver stores every event and acts only on `email.bounced` and `email.complained`. `email.delivered` updates nothing.
Why: tech §1.4 asks for "`delivered` into `email_sends` state", but the `email_send_state` enum is `queued | sent | failed | suppressed` and has no `delivered` value; adding one is a migration, and schema wave 4 is closed. Nothing in the product reads a delivered state today. The event is kept in `webhook_events` either way, so the information is not lost — only unindexed.
Nearest spec: tech §1.4.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — The unsubscribe endpoint lives under the notifications API and answers with a page
Decision: `GET|POST /api/notifications/unsubscribe`. POST (what a mail client sends for one-click) answers 204; GET (what a link in the email body is) does the same work and returns a small HTML confirmation whose words come from the string catalogue. A token we did not sign gets the identical answer and changes nothing.
Why: the build plan gives this lane `apps/web/app/api/notifications` and `apps/web/app/api/webhooks/resend`, and an unsubscribe is a notification preference rather than a webhook, so it belongs in the first. Returning a page rather than redirecting into the app keeps the endpoint working for someone with no session — which is the whole point of a link that has to work in one request. Answering a forged token identically is what stops the endpoint being used to find out whether an account id is real.
Nearest spec: tech §1.5; build plan §3 (lane G directories).

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — CONTRADICTION: one preference column has to carry two different switches, so unsubscribing from the monthly summary also switches off the per-article digest
Decision: `notification_prefs.email_digest_frequency` is read as the monthly summary's on/off switch — `off` means the merchant unsubscribed, any cadence means send, and **no saved row at all** means send, which is what makes the UI spec's "default ON" true for a merchant who has never opened Settings. The same column keeps its plain meaning for the per-article digest. One-click unsubscribe from the monthly summary therefore writes `off` and takes the article digest with it.
Why: the UI spec's channel matrix says the monthly summary is emailed by default **on** and is one of the kinds a merchant may switch off; Gmail and Yahoo bulk-sender rules require a working one-click unsubscribe on it. The tech spec gives `notification_prefs` exactly two columns — `email_article_published` (default off) and `email_digest_frequency` (`off | daily | weekly`, default off) — so there is no third column for the summary, and the second column's own database default (`off`) contradicts "default on" for any account that has a row. Three readings were possible and all three are wrong in some way: give the summary the digest column literally (default off — contradicts the UI spec), give it no switch at all (breaks the bulk-sender rule the tech spec cites), or add a column (schema wave 4 is closed, and this is not a schema-wave card). The reading shipped is the only one that keeps both the default and the unsubscribe working with the columns that exist. **The cost is stated plainly because a founder should overrule it if it is wrong:** a merchant who unsubscribes from the monthly summary silently loses the per-article digest too, and there is no screen that would tell them so. The fix is one boolean column in the next schema wave.
Nearest spec: tech §1.3 (`notification_prefs` shape), ui §10 (email defaults and which rows are toggleable), tech §1.5 (one-click unsubscribe).

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — A partial write to the preference row is impossible, because the writer must state both columns
Decision: `saveNotificationPrefs` takes both preference values and always writes both; there is no method that sets one. The value for the column the merchant did not touch comes from `defaultNotificationPreferences()`, which reads the channel matrix rather than the database defaults.
Why: the row does not exist until a merchant saves something, and the database default for `email_digest_frequency` is `off`. Without this rule, a merchant switching **per-article email on** would create a row whose other column defaulted to `off` and would thereby switch the monthly summary off without being asked — a silent, user-visible change caused by an unrelated action. Making the writer state both columns turns that from unlikely into impossible.
Nearest spec: tech §1.3 — gives the columns and their defaults, silent on partial writes.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — The denominator rule is checked against the copy, not against the finished email
Decision: `assertNoDenominator` runs over the string-catalogue entries an email uses, with their `{placeholders}` still unfilled — not over the rendered message. The rendered plain text is checked too, but only in the test, with fixture data chosen to be clean. Links are removed before either check.
Why: invariant 23 forbids denominators in user-facing counts, and the card's done-when makes the check literal: no `/`, no "of", no target wording. Run against the finished email that rule fails on a merchant's own topic title — "A History of Wool" contains "of" and is nobody's denominator — and a summary that refuses to send because of what someone called their article is worse than the rule it enforces. Checking the copy catches exactly the thing the rule exists for: a sentence **we** wrote that frames output against a total. Links are stripped because `https://sortiva.app/settings` has two slashes and says nothing about how much we published.
Nearest spec: main §8.6 ("reports what happened, with no denominator"); invariant 23.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — Fifteen kinds of email, two templates
Decision: the monthly summary is its own React Email template; every other kind renders through one shared "notice" template — a heading, a line or two, one button. `template_version` is stamped per template (`monthly-summary.v1`, `notice.v1`), not per kind.
Why: the tech spec requires templates versioned in the repo and a version stamped on every send, so a complaint traces to the exact template. It does not require one template per notification type, and fifteen near-identical files would make a copy change fifteen edits and a versioning decision fifteen times. What differs between kinds is words, and words live in the string catalogue where they can be reviewed and translated. What differs structurally is only the summary, which has sections and a list.
Nearest spec: tech §1.4 — "Templates are React Email components, versioned in the repo, `template_version` stamped on every send".

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — Email templates live in `packages/providers`, which is the only place they can
Decision: the React Email components are `packages/providers/src/email/templates/`, and that package gains React, `@react-email/components` and `@react-email/render` plus a JSX compiler setting.
Why: the three candidates each fail for a different reason. `packages/core` is forbidden React by the constitution and by a test that proves it. `packages/ui` holds React and the strings, but it belongs to the frontend lane and is being written in right now, so putting email templates there is both a lane violation and a merge hazard. `packages/providers/email` is this lane's, already holds the Resend adapter, and is where a vendor's native pairing belongs. The consequence to know: `packages/providers` now pulls React into anything importing its barrel. Templates take the words as copy **keys**, not sentences, and resolve them through `@sortiva/ui`'s catalogue — so the constitution's "copy lives in `packages/ui/strings` only" still holds and this package contains no user-facing sentence.
Nearest spec: tech §1.4; CLAUDE.md code-structure rules (core imports no React; copy is externalised).

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — The unsubscribe link is a signature over two facts and carries no session
Decision: a one-click unsubscribe link is `<account id>.<preference column>.<HMAC-SHA256>`, signed with `AUTH_SECRET`, with **no expiry**. The endpoint accepts POST (what a mail client sends for one-click) and GET (what a link in the body is), and does nothing except set that one preference.
Why: the rule requires acting in a single request with no sign-in, so the link itself has to be the proof of who is asking. Signing account and column together means the link cannot be edited into somebody else's account or pointed at a different setting; carrying nothing else means a forwarded email grants no access beyond switching off that one kind of mail. No expiry because a mailbox is read late, and a dead unsubscribe link becomes a spam complaint — which costs far more than the link staying live. `AUTH_SECRET` is reused rather than adding a variable: it already exists in `.env.example`, is already required, and rotating it invalidates unsubscribe links exactly as it invalidates sessions, which is the correct blast radius.
Nearest spec: tech §1.5 — "one-click unsubscribe (List-Unsubscribe header + link) that flips the corresponding preference"; RFC 8058 for the header pair.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — A suppressed address gets a row saying so, rather than no row
Decision: when the address is suppressed the `email_sends` row is still written, in state `suppressed`. It is never handed to the provider.
Why: tech §1.5 says both "enqueue if not suppressed" and "suppressed addresses skip the queue (`state = suppressed`)", which only reconcile if the row exists and is never drained. Writing it is also the more useful of the two: without a row, "why did I never get the payment-failed email" has no answer anywhere, and a merchant whose mail server bounced us looks identical to a merchant we never tried to reach.
Nearest spec: tech §1.5.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.2 — BLOCKED: the deletion-confirmation email has no type it can be recorded under
Decision: the mechanism that lets account-security mail reach a suppressed address is built and tested (`EmailAudience.securityEmail`), but **no email actually uses it**, because `email_sends.type` is the notification-type enum and that enum has no value for a deletion confirmation. `T8.3` cannot send one without a new enum value.
Why: tech §1.5 exempts "account-security email (deletion confirmation)" from suppression, and main §14.6 gives account deletion to `T8.3`. Schema wave 4 is closed and this is not a schema-wave card, so the enum value is not added here. The bypass is built now rather than later because it is the card's done-when and because leaving it out would mean `T8.3` discovering the exemption exists only in prose. **What `T8.3` needs:** one value on the `notification_type` enum — or a decision that account-security mail is recorded somewhere other than `email_sends`.
Nearest spec: tech §1.5, main §14.6.
## 2026-09-03 — T9.6 — Clicks and impressions are two panels sharing one row of dates, not two lines on one plot
Decision: the search-performance chart draws clicks in one panel and impressions in a panel below it, over a single shared row of dates and a single set of event markers. Each panel is scaled against its own maximum.
Why: the spec and the design canvas both draw one plot with two lines and no vertical axis at all. That works in a mockup and not with real numbers: a store with forty clicks and two thousand impressions has no single scale that shows both, and the two ways out are worse than splitting. Pinning both to the same scale flattens the clicks line onto the floor; giving impressions its own axis on the right makes the distance between the two lines look like a finding, when it is decided entirely by where somebody chose to pin the second scale — the two lines cross wherever the axis says they cross. Two panels lose nothing (the shared date row and shared markers keep every comparison the merchant can actually make) and remove a way to mislead. **Departure from the design canvas, stated as one.**
Nearest spec: ui §8.1 ("headline chart — clicks + impressions since `gsc_connected_at`"); main §12.2.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.6 — The chart's lines are ink and its markers carry the colour, which is the opposite of the design
Decision: the clicks and impressions lines are drawn in the body-text ink colour. The three event markers carry the colour: green for the day Search Console was connected, purple for an article publishing, cyan for a page improvement being marked applied. Each marker also has its own dash pattern and its own label.
Why: the question the chart exists to answer is whether what we did moved anything, so the things we did should be what the eye finds first — the trend line is the background against which they are read. It is also the only arrangement that survives colour-blind vision. The design canvas draws the two data lines in the brand indigo and a warm orange; the brand indigo and the purple used for a publish marker are, under deuteranopia, the same colour (a separation of 0.4 against a floor of 8 on the standard measure), so a merchant with the commonest form of colour blindness could not tell a publish marker from the clicks line. The three marker colours as shipped were run through a colour-separation check rather than chosen by eye and pass on all pairs, and each carries a dash pattern and a written label as well, so none of them depends on colour at all. **Departure from the design canvas, stated as one.**
Nearest spec: ui §8.1 (the markers); the design canvas S8/S13; no accessibility requirement is written down anywhere in the three specs, which is itself worth knowing.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.6 — The design's "51 of 58 attributed articles" is not built, because invariant 23 forbids it
Decision: the Performance screen's headline figures are total clicks and total impressions, plus a count of days Search Console reported nothing. There is no ratio of attributed articles to published ones.
Why: the design canvas draws "Attributed articles — 51 of 58" as one of four headline figures on this screen. Invariant 23 admits no exception: no denominators or targets in any user-facing count. The figure is also the most misleading one on the page — the seven-article difference is exported articles whose URL nobody has confirmed, which the spec is explicit must never be counted as failures, and a merchant reading "51 of 58" would read seven missing articles rather than seven unmeasured ones. The same fact reaches them through the attention list, which names the exports waiting for a URL and links to them. **Departure from the design canvas, stated as one.**
Nearest spec: invariant 23; main §8.6; main §9.6.7 ("absence of data is not failure"); ui §8.1.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.6 — The dashboard headline uses the canonical "We found…" rather than the "Sortiva found…" the other two documents write
Decision: the growth headline renders `appendixA.opportunityHeadline` — "We found {N} ways to grow your store organically" — the same pinned sentence the Opportunities screen uses.
Why: three documents write this sentence and one of them differs. Main Appendix A, which is the canonical-copy table and the one invariant 24 makes binding, writes "We found…"; main §7.12 and ui §4 both write "Sortiva found…" for the dashboard specifically. Adding a second near-identical sentence to the catalogue is exactly how two copies of a pinned string drift apart — and the snapshot that holds this one exact would only hold one of them. So one sentence, in one place, on both screens. **Surfaced rather than resolved:** if the dashboard is meant to name the product where the Opportunities screen says "we", that is a founder's call and Appendix A needs a second row, not a second copy of the first.
Nearest spec: main Appendix A ("Opportunity headline"); main §7.12; ui §4; invariant 24.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.6 — Three of the month strip's six lines are not built, because nothing answers them
Decision: the month strip carries articles published this month, topics held back by the quality bar this month, and the next replenishment date. It does not carry Optimize recommendations generated, recommendations marked applied, or repairs.
Why: ui §4 lists six figures. Three are derivable from responses that exist — the articles list carries a publish date, the calendar carries held topics and the replenishment date. The other three are not: the opportunities response carries counts of what is *open* rather than what happened this month, and an article says whether it was ever repaired but never when. A count labelled "this month" that is actually "ever" is worse than an absent line, and this lane may not add a field to a response. The gap is written into the screen contract for whichever card builds the endpoint; the cheapest fix is dated activity on the three, or one summary route.
Nearest spec: ui §4 (the month strip's six figures); the frozen route table.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.6 — The denominator rule is checked against the rendered page, not only against the copy file
Decision: a test renders the whole dashboard to markup, strips the tags, and refuses "22 of 31", "22/31", "22 out of 31", any percentage, and any figure sitting within a clause of the words target, quota or goal. It also refuses a progress bar or progress role anywhere in the month strip.
Why: the catalogue already has a check that no *sentence* contains a denominator, and `T9.4` found two sentences with it. That check cannot see a denominator assembled at render time out of two values that are innocent apart — a count in one element and a cap in the next — which is exactly how this would come back. The words themselves are deliberately allowed: the strip says "a ceiling, not a target" in as many words, and a rule that banned the product from explaining its own position would be the wrong rule. What is banned is a figure beside one of them.
Nearest spec: invariant 23; main §8.6; ui §4; the card's own done-when ("denominator lint on rendered output").

## 2026-09-03 — T9.6 — A Shopify admin link we were handed is refused rather than repaired
Decision: a merchant task renders a product as a link only when its `shopifyAdminUrl` is an HTTPS address on `admin.shopify.com` or a `*.myshopify.com` host. Anything else renders the product as plain text with no link at all.
Why: this is the one place in the product where a screen puts an address from a response straight into an anchor the merchant is invited to click, and it opens in a new tab. A wrong host — through a bug, or through a store handle that reached us from outside and was never checked — would make our own screen an invitation to somewhere we did not mean to send anybody. Refusing rather than rewriting is the choice worth naming: a link that has to be repaired before it is safe is a link nobody should follow, and a silently corrected address hides the defect that produced it. The cost is that a genuine link on a host we have not thought of would be dropped; the test names both shapes Shopify actually uses.
Nearest spec: ui §7 ("deep links to Shopify admin for each product"); no spec says anything about validating them, which is why this is a decision.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.6 — Products renders the confirmation screen's family component rather than a second copy of it
Decision: the read-only family list on Products is `FamiliesSection` from the onboarding module — the same component, the same report-wrong-grouping modal, the same analytics event — and the Products page loads the onboarding stylesheet for it.
Why: ui §7 asks for the family list with "the same modal as §3.7", which is the confirmation screen's. Two components rendering one read-only list is two places for the grouping-source badges, the low-confidence flag and the report flow to drift, and the report modal in particular carries a rule worth keeping in one place — what the merchant types goes to support and only three facts reach analytics. The cost is that a screen in the Products module wears `sortiva-confirm__` class names, which is visible in the markup and is why it is written down here.
Nearest spec: ui §7 ("'Report wrong grouping' per family (same modal as §3.7)"); ui §3.7.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.6 — The cannibalization view is not built, because the response carries none of its evidence
Decision: the Search Console tab has the queries table and the pages table. It does not have the cannibalization panel ui §8.2 asks for.
Why: that panel is a query cluster's competing URLs with each one's share of impressions plotted over time. The Search Console response is a flat list of rows — one key, its clicks, impressions, click-through and position — with no cluster, no per-URL breakdown and no monthly history. Nothing in the contract carries it and this lane may not add a field. What is not lost: a query with a cannibalization opportunity still carries its badge, and the badge links to the FIX opportunity, which is where the evidence lives. A panel drawn from a made-up shape would have been worse than a link to the real one. Registered in the screen contract.
Nearest spec: ui §8.2 (the cannibalization view); main §7.3; the frozen `searchConsoleResponseSchema`.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.6 — Search Console is a second route rather than a tab that swaps in place
Decision: `/performance` and `/performance/search-console` are two pages with a tab strip across both, following what `T9.5` did for the calendar and the article library.
Why: ui §8 calls Search Console a tab, and a tab can be either. Two addresses means each half is bookmarkable and linkable — a signal badge in an email or a support reply can point at the queries table — and each half reads only what it needs, so the overview does not pay for two Search Console reads nobody looked at. It also matches the one precedent in the product. The cost is a page load rather than a swap when the merchant moves between them.
Nearest spec: ui §8.2 ("the strategy document's 'Google' screen, folded here"); ui §1 (six destinations).

## 2026-09-03 — T9.6 — The month strip's counts are worded so they read correctly at one, because the catalogue has no plurals
Decision: the strip's lines read "Articles published: 22" rather than "22 articles published".
Why: the string catalogue fills `{placeholders}` and has no plural machinery at all, so "{count} articles published" reads "1 articles published" on a store's quieter month. `T9.5` hit the same wall and worded around it. The label-then-figure form is correct at every count, and it is what the design canvas actually lays out — a label beside a number rather than a sentence. **This is the second card to work around the same gap**, which is the point at which it is worth saying plainly: the catalogue needs either a plural form per key or an agreed house style of label-then-figure, and one of those should be decided rather than discovered a third time.
Nearest spec: ui §4; tech §1.6 (i18n); no spec covers plural forms.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.6 — The dashboard's five reads are independent, and one failing costs a section rather than the page
