Decision: the steady-state dashboard issues its five reads together and each falls back to the empty shape its own section knows how to draw. A failed performance read shows the Search Console connect card — the same thing a store without a connection sees.
Why: a dashboard is six independent answers, and a page that died because one of six was slow would be a worse screen than one that drew five. Asking for them together rather than in sequence matters as much: five sequential round trips would make this the slowest screen in the product, and none of the five depends on another. The performance fallback is worth naming because it is not neutral — a merchant whose read failed sees "connect Search Console" when they already have. It is still the best thing to put in that space: there is no search data to draw either way, and the card is the only useful thing in it. A distinct "we could not read this" state would be better and is not built.
Nearest spec: ui §4; the `T9.4` and `T9.5` precedent that a failed read renders the emptier screen rather than an error page.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.5 — Today accepts no new topic and no dropped one, so the calendar cannot be used to catch up
Decision: a topic may be added to, or dragged onto, a day **strictly after today**. Days that have passed are inert, and so is today itself.
Why: at most one topic is written per account per day, and only the topic dated today; a quiet day is never made up for later. Today is the ambiguous case, and the ambiguity resolves against acting. The day's generation pass may already have run and taken its one topic, so dropping a second onto today would either do nothing or produce two articles on a day that has had its one — and both are the surprise the calendar exists to prevent. Treating today as still open would also make the screen's most important sentence untrue: it tells the merchant a quiet day is normal while offering to fill the quiet day in front of them. **The cost, stated:** a merchant who wants something written today cannot ask for it, and the earliest they can schedule is tomorrow. If that turns out to be wrong the fix is one predicate, and the server's `calendar_date_in_past` code already covers the race either way.
Nearest spec: main §8.7 (keep the gap, no surprise early publishes), §9.1 (only today's topic dequeues); ui §6.1 ("every empty **future** day has a ghost +").

## 2026-09-02 — T9.5 — A veto is held for the length of its undo rather than sent and taken back
Decision: pressing veto hides the chip and raises the undo toast, and the request is sent only when the five seconds expire. Undo cancels a request that was never made. Leaving the screen sends anything still waiting rather than dropping it.
Why: the spec gives the veto an undo, and the contract has no route that revives a vetoed topic — vetoed topics go onto a list replenishment consults precisely so they are never proposed again, so "un-veto" is not an operation the API can offer. That leaves two shapes. Send immediately and undo by re-adding, which would create a *different* topic with a new id through the admission gate, losing its opportunity link and its place in the plan; or hold the request for the undo window, which is what shipped. This differs from the Opportunities screen next door, which sends its dismiss at once and undoes through a real `restore` route — worth knowing, because the two undos look identical on screen and are not. **Two consequences to accept:** a merchant who closes the tab within five seconds still has the veto sent (deliberate — only Undo takes it back), and a browser killed in that window loses it entirely.
Nearest spec: ui §6.1 (one click + undo toast, 5s); main §8.7 (vetoed topics go to a "not interested" list); the frozen route table, which has veto and no restore.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.5 — Vetoing a topic dismisses its opportunity through the same port the Opportunities screen uses, but raises one notice rather than two
Decision: the second request goes to `POST /api/opportunities/{id}/dismiss`, reusing the request-and-conflict shape `T9.4` built; it does **not** call that screen's `dismiss` action. A refusal on the second request raises no extra sentence.
Why: `createOpportunityActions.dismiss` raises its own toast with its own Undo. Calling it here would put two toasts and two undos on screen for one click, and the merchant would have no way to know which undo took back which half. One decision gets one notice. The conflict wording and the network port are shared, which is what stops the two screens drifting on how a refusal reads. A refusal on the opportunity half is deliberately silent: by then the topic — the thing the merchant was looking at — has gone, and the opportunity is either already dismissed or already acted on, so a second sentence would report a failure the merchant cannot act on.
Nearest spec: ui §6.1 ("vetoing also dismisses the originating opportunity, with the same undo").

## 2026-09-02 — T9.5 — A swap is two move requests, and they are not atomic
Decision: dropping a topic onto an occupied day sends `move` for the dragged topic and then `move` for the displaced one. If the second is refused, the first stands and the calendar is re-read so the merchant sees where things actually are.
Why: the contract has one move route taking one date. A swap endpoint would be a contract change, which this lane may not make. The window is small — two requests, no user input between them — and the failure is visible rather than silent: the merchant sees one topic where they expected two moved. **Worth a swap route if this ever matters**, because the honest description of today's behaviour is "usually both, occasionally one".
Nearest spec: ui §6.1 (drop on an occupied day swaps the two); the frozen route table.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.5 — The screen never compares a judge score to a floor, so the override names the judge's own objections instead
Decision: the quality report renders each criterion with its score and nothing beside it, and the override dialog restates **the criteria the judge wrote a justification for**. Where a report carries no justifications at all, every scored criterion is named rather than none.
Why: the gate holds information gain and factual grounding to one bar and the rest to a lower one, and those numbers live in `packages/rules` with every other threshold, stamped by a rules version onto each decision. Re-deriving them in a browser would put a second, unversioned copy of the quality gate in the product — the exact thing the threshold rule exists to prevent — and the article-detail response carries neither the floors nor a list of what failed. Naming the judge's written objections is closer to what the merchant is actually being asked to overrule than a number would be. **It is right only while justifications are written for what the judge marked down.** The proper fix is a `failedCriteria` list on the quality report, which is a response change and not this lane's to make; it is written into the screen contract. The design canvas shows "2 of 5, against a floor of 4" and the shipped screen does not, which is a visible departure.
Nearest spec: main §8.4 (gate on the minimum, floors of 4 and 3), §8.6 (the override restates the failing criteria); main §7.10 and invariant 9 (no threshold literal outside `packages/rules`).

## 2026-09-02 — T9.5 — A held article shows the judge's report where the calendar shows a reason card, because the response carries no reason
Decision: the calendar's held day renders the shared rejection card from the `rejection` object its topic carries. The article page, for the same held article, renders the judge's per-criterion report instead — it invents no reason line.
Why: the topic shape carries `{gate, reason}`; the article-detail shape carries a quality report and nothing else. Filling the card with the canonical thin-catalogue sentence for every held article would be the product asserting a cause it does not know, which is worse than showing the judge's own words. The two surfaces therefore explain the same outcome differently, which is a real inconsistency and is recorded rather than papered over: the fix is the same `failedCriteria`-shaped response change above, or a rejection reason on the article.
Nearest spec: ui §6.1 and §6.3 (the §8.6 rejection card is shared by calendar, article detail and the notification list).

## 2026-09-02 — T9.5 — "Today" is decided by the server's clock, and the audience timezone it should use is not read yet
Decision: the calendar page computes today from the server's own clock and hands it to the screen as a plain date.
Why: the screen cannot draw anything without knowing which days have passed — an outcome and a plan are the same chip otherwise — and a browser clock is the merchant's laptop, which may be set to anything and is often a different day from the one the scheduler is working in. The server's clock is at least one clock rather than every visitor's. **It is still not the right one.** Publishing is anchored to the persona country's timezone, and the settings response carries that timezone; a merchant in Copenhagen running a US store should see the calendar in the store audience's day, not ours. The correction is one extra read in the page and passing the timezone through — deliberately left because it changes what "today" means on a screen this card is the first to draw, and because the settings read is a second round trip on a page whose first frame is the point.
Nearest spec: main §9.1, §9.4 (the daily cycle and publish hour are anchored to the persona country's timezone); ui §6.1.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.5 — The fixture site's proxy could not forward the Host header, so no authenticated page's server-side reads ever reached the fixtures
Decision: `apps/web/e2e/mock-api-server.ts` now proxies to the app with Node's own HTTP client instead of `fetch`, and stops asking for a compressed answer it forwards without decoding.
Why: **this was a real defect in the browser-test harness, not a change of approach.** A page being rendered on the server has no browser, so it works out where to call itself from the `Host` header it was asked on; the fixture site rewrites that header so the app's own reads land on the fixtures rather than on the real API. `Host` is a header the fetch standard forbids setting, so the assignment was dropped in silence — the app called its real endpoints, every authenticated page rendered as though the merchant had no data, and the navigation locked itself. Nothing failed loudly. It went unnoticed because the only flows running through this server were public pages, whose reads were answered acceptably either way. **Every future Lane F browser flow on an authenticated screen depended on this**, which is why it is repaired here rather than reported. The second half — dropping `accept-encoding` — is the same class of bug one layer down: `fetch` decompressed the answer for us and a raw pipe does not, so the browser was handed gzip bytes as text.
Nearest spec: build plan §6, T9.5 done-when ("Playwright: veto + undo, drag/swap, …"); tech §6.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.5 — The Content flows run against a fixture site that holds state, and reset it between flows
Decision: the calendar and article routes are answered by an in-memory `ContentState` for the length of one run, anchored to the day the run happens on, and a scaffolding route resets it before each flow.
Why: the frozen fixtures cannot serve these flows for two independent reasons. They are dated February 2026, and half of what the calendar renders depends on which side of *today* a day falls on. And they are answers rather than a system: vetoing a topic and then finding it gone is the assertion, and a fixed body answers the second read exactly as it answered the first. The reset is what stops the suite passing or failing by the order it happened to run in — it was added after a second run against the same server failed on three flows that had passed on the first. The two scaffolding routes (`GET /api/_e2e/calls`, `POST /api/_e2e/reset`) exist only inside that server and are reachable from nothing the product ships.
Nearest spec: build plan §6, T9.5 done-when; the `T9.2` precedent of a real local server standing in for a vendor.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.4 — The FIX and HOLD views are drawn from the same generic recommendation shape as OPTIMIZE, because that is all the response has
Decision: the FIX view renders `recommendation.fields` as a numbered list of instructions, followed by the sentence that we do not change themes or redirects. The HOLD view renders the precondition sentence, the task list as a checklist, the promise to re-check after the next catalogue sync, and a link to Products.
Why: the design draws FIX as a comparison of the competing URLs with their impression shares and a "suggested primary" marker, and HOLD as a product-by-product checklist where each row deep-links into Shopify admin. Neither is reachable: the opportunity-detail response carries one generic recommendation object (a list of `{field, current, suggested, evidence}` plus two internal-link lists and an intent note) and tasks that are `{id, label, state}` — no product ids, no per-URL shares. This lane may not add a field. So each view renders what is there, honestly, and both gaps are written into the screen contract for whichever card builds the endpoint. **What is lost:** the FIX view is a paragraph list rather than a picture of the problem, and a merchant on HOLD has to find the products themselves on the Products page.
Nearest spec: ui §5.3 items 5 and 6; main §10.4, §11; the frozen `opportunityDetailResponseSchema`.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.4 — "Mark all as applied" is a loop over the open tasks, because there is no route that takes them together
Decision: the button sends one `POST /api/opportunities/{id}/tasks/{taskId}` per open task.
Why: main §10.4 offers "mark as applied (per task or whole)" and the contract has only the per-task route. Requests are idempotent in the sense that matters — marking an already-applied task applied changes nothing — so a partial failure leaves a consistent state rather than a half-applied opportunity, and the list is re-read afterwards either way. The cost is n requests where one would do, bounded by the handful of tasks an opportunity carries. A bulk route would be a contract change, which this lane may not make.
Nearest spec: main §10.4; the frozen route table.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.4 — The screen's network is a small port, so dismiss, undo and the 409 path are provable without a browser
Decision: every request the screen makes goes through an `OpportunitiesApi` object with three methods, and every visible consequence goes through an `ActionSurface` with four. The component passes the real HTTP implementation; the tests pass doubles.
Why: the two behaviours this card must demonstrate — an undo that really restores, and a refused transition that ends in a re-read plus a sentence rather than a dead button — live entirely in the gap between a click and a re-render. Asserting them through rendered markup would need a browser in the unit run, which this repository does not have; asserting them by reading the component would prove nothing. The seam costs one indirection and makes both testable as behaviour. The React component is left as a thin binding with no logic of its own worth testing.
Nearest spec: ui §5.4 (dismiss with undo, 409 toast); tech §3 (guarded transitions return 409 with a machine-readable code).

## 2026-09-02 — T9.4 — The list header names the date of the next scan; only the empty state still says "Monday"
Decision: the line under the headline reads "Last scan 2 Feb 2026 · next scan 9 Feb 2026", built from the two timestamps the opportunities response already carries. The empty state keeps the sentence exactly as the UI spec writes it — "No open opportunities right now — the next scan runs Monday".
Why: both the spec and the design canvas write the header line as "next Monday", and the scan does run weekly on a Monday today. But the response carries an actual `nextScanAt`, and a store whose scan lands on another day — a different timezone, a re-queued run, a paused account — would be told something untrue every week by a hardcoded weekday. The empty state is quoted in the card's own done-when condition, so it stays word for word; that leaves one seam where the header could name a Tuesday while the empty state promises Monday. **Surfaced rather than resolved:** if scans will never move off Monday, the empty state is fine and this is belt and braces; if they can, the empty state's wording needs the founder's call, because it is quoted copy.
Nearest spec: ui §5.1 — "last scan: {date} · next: Monday" and the empty-state sentence; main §7.5 (weekly scan).

## 2026-09-02 — T9.4 — The refused "generate recommendations" button says the limit is reached without naming a number
Decision: the note beside a disabled OPTIMIZE button reads "Daily limit reached — available tomorrow". A caller that knows the cap can pass it and get "2 per day — available tomorrow" instead, but nothing supplies one today.
Why: the UI spec writes the tooltip as "2 per day — available tomorrow", and the cap really is two — but it is configuration on the server (main §10.2, "config"), and no route in the frozen contract sends it to the browser. Typing the 2 into the string catalogue would put a threshold in copy where nobody would think to look when the config changes, and the merchant would be told a number that had quietly stopped being true. The parameterised string is kept ready so that when a response carries the cap, one call site changes.
Nearest spec: ui §5.4 — the disabled-button copy; main §10.2, §7.10 (thresholds are config).

## 2026-09-02 — T9.4 — The checks that Limited Intelligence switches off are listed by the screen, not by the API
Decision: under the header, a store with no Search Console connection gets a collapsible list of the seven signal types that cannot be evaluated — striking distance, low click-through at a good position, decay, cannibalization, indexing, canonical and freshness. The list is a constant in the screen (`GSC_DEPENDENT_SIGNALS`).
Why: main §7.11 requires the screen to list which signals are unavailable, and names them. Nothing in the opportunities response carries that list, and this lane may not add a field. It is also genuinely a property of how detection works rather than of the account: without Google's query data these cannot run for anybody. The cost is a second place the signal catalogue is written down — if detection ever runs one of these from another source, this list has to be edited to match.
Nearest spec: main §7.11; ui §5.5.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.4 — The canonical existing-page sentence is reached through one alias rather than copied into the catalogue
Decision: the engine's reason key `existing_target.prefer_optimize` resolves to the catalogue key `appendixA.existingPageWhyLine`; every other reason key `k` resolves to `template.k`.
Why: "You already rank for this. Improving the existing collection is safer than creating another page." is one of the sentences the product may not reword, and a snapshot already holds it under its `appendixA.` key. Giving the engine's key its own catalogue entry would put a second copy of a pinned sentence in the file, which is exactly how the two drift. **This creates one requirement on whichever lane builds the existing-target check:** the reason key it stamps on a converted CREATE must be that string, or the merchant gets the "reasoning not available yet" fallback in place of the product's most important sentence. Registered in the screen contract.
Nearest spec: main §7.7 (the why-line), Appendix A; main §7.6 (`reason_template_key` rendered at display time).

## 2026-09-02 — T9.4 — Two inherited sentences were reworded because they read as counts against a target
Decision: "{value} of your URLs on one query" became "{value} competing URLs on one query", and "{products} of your products do not yet say enough…" became "{products} products in your store do not yet say enough…".
Why: the catalogue has a test that rejects any sentence matching "{x} of y" or "{x}/y", because the daily cap is a ceiling and a count against a denominator invites a merchant to read the second number as owed. Neither sentence was a denominator in meaning — both are bare counts — but both matched, and a rule with hand-waved exceptions is not a rule. The rewrites carry the same fact.
Nearest spec: main §8.6; ui §4 — never render "x of y".
## 2026-09-02 — T2.3 — The model is never asked for a price, a variant axis, or a count of its own facts
Decision: the fact sheet the extraction model returns holds only the ten fields that come from a product's *words* — material, dimensions, weight, capacity, compatibility, stated uses, care, certifications, origin, verifiable claims — plus a yes/no on whether marketing language was dropped. `price_range` is merged in afterwards from the numbers already in our own database. `fact_count` is computed in our code from the finished sheet. Both are refused by the JSON Schema, so a model that volunteers one fails validation rather than being trusted.
Why: main §6.3's output schema lists `price_range`, `variant_axes` and `fact_count` inside the model's own object, while the same section says supplementary structured data "is merged in without distillation since it's already factual". Asking a model for a number we already hold exactly is the one fabrication that arrives with a currency symbol on it and looks authoritative; and a model counting its own output produces a number that can disagree with the object beside it, which is worse than no number. Splitting the object costs one merge step and removes both risks. **This is a departure from the literal shape in §6.3 and is flagged as one** — the stored sheet has exactly the fields §6.3 lists, but not all of them come from the model.
Nearest spec: main §6.3 (the output schema, and the merge rule in the same bullet list).

## 2026-09-02 — T2.3 — `variant_axes` is always empty, because Shopify's axis names are not among the fields we ask for
Decision: the `variant_axes` field on every fact sheet is `[]`. Nothing derives it.
Why: main §6.3 wants the axes a product's variants differ along — "Size", "Colour". Shopify puts those names in a product's `options` field, and `T2.2`'s catalogue read does not request it: what we store per variant is an id, a title ("Red / Large"), a SKU, a price and a stock flag. Axis *values* can be split out of the title positionally; the axis *names* cannot be recovered from anything we hold, and inventing "option 1" would be worse than an empty field. Guessing is also exactly what this card exists to prevent. **The gap is one line of Shopify field list plus somewhere to put the answer**, and the card that most needs it is `T2.4`, which records differentiation axes across a family. Left empty and reported rather than filled badly.
Nearest spec: main §6.3 (`variant_axes`), main §6.4 (family axes, `T2.4`).

## 2026-09-02 — T2.3 — Product metafields are still not fetched, so fact sheets are thinner than §6.3 intends
Decision: distillation reads a product's title and description and nothing else. Metafields are not fetched, not merged, and not distilled.
Why: main §6.3 names metafields explicitly in its merged supplementary data, and gives the reason — spec-minded merchants keep real product attributes there rather than in prose. `T2.2` did not fetch them (they need a separate Admin API call per product, or a bulk query), and no card owns adding it. Recording it here so the omission stops being invisible: **this caps how rich a fact sheet can be for exactly the stores whose data is best organised**, which are the stores our richness score will call "sparse" while their attributes sit one API call away. This card's own success criteria do not depend on metafields — the eval grades extraction from descriptions — so nothing here is scored against a lowered bar. Cost of fixing it later: one Admin call per product inside the catalogue sync, a place to store the result, and a merge step here.
Nearest spec: main §6.3 ("Supplementary structured data (variants, price, product_type, tags, collections, metafields, review text) is merged in without distillation").

## 2026-09-02 — T2.3 — `fluff_discarded` is a yes/no stored in a column declared as a count
Decision: `product_facts.fluff_discarded` holds 1 or 0. The model answers "was marketing language present and dropped", which is a boolean.
Why: main §6.3's output schema shows `"fluff_discarded": true` — a flag — while schema wave 2 declared the column `integer not null default 0`, which reads as a count of discarded phrases. A count would arguably be more informative for detecting stores whose descriptions *are* the substance, but nothing in the spec asks for one, and asking a model to count the adjectives it threw away is asking for a number nobody can check. Stored as a flag; the column keeps room for a count if a later card wants one, and no migration is needed either way.
Nearest spec: main §6.3 (the flag), main §13 `product_facts`.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.3 — The richness band is derived from the substance floor already in `packages/rules`, not from new numbers
Decision: the store richness roll-up reads `gates.substance_floor.populated_fields_per_product_min` (4) and `margin_multiple` (1.5) from `packages/rules`. A store whose **median** product populates fewer fields than the floor is "sparse"; at or above the floor times the multiple it is "rich"; between them, "okay". `productsMissingDetails` counts products below the floor. Nothing in this card's code holds a threshold of its own.
Why: main §6.3 asks for a per-store richness score and §6.8 shows it to the merchant, but neither gives a number, and inventing a second set would eventually disagree with the gate that later refuses to write about thin families — a store told "rich" whose articles are then refused for thin data has been lied to. Reusing the existing floor keeps one judgement. The **median** rather than the mean is the other choice here: twenty well-described products among two hundred bare ones pull an average over the floor while nine pages in ten still state nothing, and the band is asked what a typical page is like. `packages/rules` is another lane's directory and is only read, never edited.
Nearest spec: main §6.3 (richness score), §8.2 (substance checks), §7.10 (thresholds live in `packages/rules`); ui §3.7 (the confirmation screen's band).

## 2026-09-02 — T2.3 — A product with almost no description is not sent to the model at all
Decision: below 40 characters of usable description text, no model call is made. The product gets an empty fact sheet stamped with `model_id = "none"`.
Why: extraction from two words cannot yield a material or a dimension, and a large catalogue of thin products would otherwise pay per product to be told so — the most avoidable cost in the step. The empty sheet is not a failure state: main §6.3 says "empty fields are signal, not failure", and this is the signal the richness score is built to read. `"none"` rather than the model that *would* have been used, so the emptiest sheets in a store are distinguishable from a model that answered with nothing.
Nearest spec: main §6.3 (empty fields as signal), §15 (cost control).

## 2026-09-02 — T2.3 — `distillation.eval` fails without a real key rather than grading a stand-in
Decision: the eval set's runner builds the real Anthropic client. With no `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` it throws an error naming the missing key, so `pnpm eval` is red. Everything around the model call — the set loading, the prompt, the schema contract, the F1 threshold biting, the fabrication hard-fail biting — is proved in the ordinary `pnpm test` run against the wrapper's test double.
Why: main §14.2 makes the eval a gate on prompt and model changes; the whole question it answers is what *this model* does with *this prompt*. A stand-in that returns the expected sheet scores 1.0 and means nothing, and reporting that as a pass is the failure mode the overnight rules exist to prevent. **Consequence the integrator must know: this pull request changes a prompt file and an eval path, which is exactly what the CI `eval` job triggers on, and the repository has no Anthropic key — so that job will fail until one exists.** The alternative — skipping the set when no key is present — was rejected because the eval runner already refuses to let a set with cases silently not run, and a green gate that ran nothing is worse than a red one that says why.
Nearest spec: main §14.2 (frozen eval sets, deploy blocks on failure); tech §5.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.3 — The eval grades ten fields; the fluff flag and the fact count are excluded
Decision: `distillation.eval` compares only the ten extracted fields. `fluff_discarded`, `fact_count`, `price_range` and `variant_axes` are not graded.
Why: the scorer treats any predicted `field=value` pair absent from the gold sheet as a **fabricated fact**, which is a hard fail no aggregate can absorb. That is right for a material or a dimension. Applied to a boolean it is wrong: a model saying "no marketing language here" where a human said there was would register as a fabricated product attribute and fail the case over something that is not a claim about the product at all. The count is computed by us rather than by the model, and the price and axes are merged from our own data, so grading any of them grades our own arithmetic.
Nearest spec: main §14.2 ("field-level F1 ≥ 0.85 and zero inferred-fact violations").

## 2026-09-02 — T2.3 — The fifty eval cases are written, not harvested from real stores
Decision: `distillation.eval` ships fifty hand-written product descriptions in seven languages, shaped like real ones — spec tables, marketing copy with a single fact buried in it, copy with no facts at all, pasted markup with scripts and inline styles, and traps that reward inference ("Italian-inspired design" is not an origin).
Why: main §14.2 asks for "~50 **real** product descriptions". Harvesting real ones needs merchant stores we do not have and raises a question about republishing other people's copy that nobody has answered. Written cases can be aimed at the failures that matter, and the set is append-only by design — the first real production failure gets minimised and added, and real descriptions can be added beside these without replacing them. **Flagged as a departure from the word "real"**, and the cheapest thing to correct once there is a dev store.
Nearest spec: main §14.2.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.3 — Distillation's per-product completion is recorded in the shared idempotency ledger
Decision: each product's distillation is recorded in `idempotency_ledger` under a key derived from the account, the step name, and that product's `updated_at` plus content checksum. The step's own key is over the whole catalogue's fingerprints.
Why: main §14.3.2 makes the `distill` key "the product's `updated_at` + body checksum (so re-running distillation for an unchanged product is a no-op by construction)" and §14.3.4 says the step "tracks completion per item via the key mechanism above". There is no column on `product_facts` recording which checksum a sheet was made from, and a feature card may not add one, so the ledger is where "have I done this product" lives — which is also what the ledger is for: the table has no foreign key to jobs or accounts, so the record outlives the run that made it. Consequence: re-running onboarding from scratch does **not** re-distil an unchanged catalogue, which is the intended behaviour and worth knowing.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.2, §14.3.4.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.3 — The fact sheet is written before the ledger row, and the checkpoint is per batch
Decision: for each product, the sheet is written first and the completion recorded second. The step's checkpoint (how far through the catalogue it got) is committed once per batch of 25.
Why: a crash between the two leaves a sheet with no completion record, so the product is distilled again — and that re-distillation is free, because the model wrapper replays the stored completion from the request cache. The other order would leave a product recorded as done with no sheet, which nothing would ever repair. Within a batch the ledger is the finer-grained record, so a write per product would buy nothing; between batches the checkpoint saves re-reading rows already dealt with.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.4 (checkpointing), §14.3.6 (the LLM request cache).

## 2026-09-02 — T2.3 — Three files outside Lane B's directories, each anticipated by the file it is in
Decision: this card adds `packages/llm/prompts/distill.v1.md`, registers the `distill` runner in `packages/llm/src/eval/runner.ts` with its implementation beside it, ships the eval set under `packages/llm/eval/distillation.eval/`, adds two repository functions in `packages/db`, and adds `@sortiva/llm` as a **dev** dependency of `packages/jobs` (which updates `pnpm-lock.yaml`).
Why: `packages/llm/prompts/README.md` allocates the `distill` prompt to this card by name, and the runner registry's own comment says "cards that add a prompt register its runner here: `distill` (T2.3)" — both files expect this edit. The eval set lives beside the machinery that runs it and the prompt it grades. The dev dependency is so the step's test can drive the wrapper's own `MockLlmClient`, which accounts cost the way the live client does — a hand-rolled double would make "did this run pay twice" a weaker claim than it is. **The lockfile is a shared file and a merge surface; the integrator should expect the three added lines.**
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md code-structure rules; build plan §3 (files no lane owns).

## 2026-09-02 — T8.1 — A notification's payload is checked on the way in, and words are refused
Decision: `assertReferenceOnly` runs before every insert into `notifications`. A payload may hold identifiers and short tokens — ids, handles, `2026-W35`, `2026-08` — and a caller that passes an article headline or a paragraph of body text gets an error instead of a stored row. The sentence a merchant reads is produced when the bell is opened, from a copy key plus values looked up at that moment.
Why: tech §1.2 says payloads hold references only and gives two reasons — reworded copy applies to notifications already written, and a deleted thing degrades gracefully. It states the rule and nothing enforces it, which is precisely the shape of the standing audit finding recorded in `docs/overnight-state.md`: "nothing structurally stops product content reaching an analytics event — secrets are redacted, article text is not". Notification rows outlive the articles they describe and are read by the front end, so the same exposure applies. A check on the way in is the only place the rule can be held without asking every future emission point to remember it. Rejected: trusting the convention, and validating on read (by then the text is already stored, which is the thing that must not happen). Cost: an emission point that genuinely wants a display value must add a lookup instead — which is the intended design.
Nearest spec: tech §1.2 (references only), main §14.7 (telemetry carries ids and aggregates, never product content).

## 2026-09-02 — T8.1 — A deleted entity falls back to a per-type generic line, not one shared sentence
Decision: each notification type whose line names something has two copy keys — the normal one and a `.generic` sibling. When the render-time lookup finds nothing, the generic line for *that type* is used: "An article was published", "A draft is ready for your review". Types that name nothing (payment failed, connection lost) have no generic variant, because nothing about them can go missing.
Why: tech §1.2 requires "a graceful generic line" and does not say how generic. One shared sentence — "something happened" — would be cheaper and would strip the bell of its meaning exactly when a merchant is looking back through history for what became of something. Keeping the verb and losing only the name preserves what the entry is for. Cost: fourteen extra copy keys, held against the catalogue by a test that reads the strings file, so a type whose generic line was never written fails a test rather than throwing in a merchant's bell.
Nearest spec: tech §1.2.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.1 — Three of the attention list's five conditions cannot be read yet, and say so rather than returning empty quietly
Decision: the attention list is a port with five readers. Two are wired to real tables today — opportunities holding for merchant-supplied product information, and OPTIMIZE recommendations generated more than a fortnight ago that nobody has marked applied. The other three (a draft awaiting review, an article needing repair, an exported article whose published address we were never told) all read the `articles` table, which does not exist: schema wave 3 (`T4.0`, lane D) creates it. Those three return nothing and are registered in the stub registry as `AttentionSources.articles`, so `pnpm stubs:report` names them and every call that reaches one emits `stub_used`.
Why: the card lists all five and three of them have no table. Building them against a table that does not exist is impossible; returning `[]` silently is worse than not building them, because an attention list that cannot see drafts is indistinguishable from an account with no drafts — the exact failure the stub registry was built for ("a stub that quietly ships is a feature that silently does nothing"). Rejected: waiting for wave 3, which would park the whole card behind another lane's milestone; and adding a migration, which is closed to this card. Consequence for the audit after `T8.2`: two of the card's five conditions are proved against Postgres and three are proved only against a fake.
Nearest spec: tech §1.1 (the attention list's five examples), ui §4 (its five rows); build plan §4 (the stub rule).

## 2026-09-02 — T8.1 — The bell's poll compares timestamps truncated to milliseconds
Decision: `GET /api/notifications?since=` filters on `date_trunc('milliseconds', created_at) > since` rather than on `created_at > since`.
Why: Postgres stores microseconds; a JavaScript `Date` cannot represent them. The value the browser sends back is therefore a rounded-down copy of a stored timestamp, so a plain `>` matches the very row the browser used as its mark and re-delivers it on every poll, for as long as the tab is open. Found by a test that failed for what looked like a clock-skew reason. Rejected: having the client dedupe by id (works, but every poll then carries a redundant row and every consumer has to know why); and adding a millisecond-precision column, which needs a migration this card may not write. Cost: the predicate is not index-sargable, so the account's rows are scanned rather than sought — bounded by the account-scoped index, a 50-row limit, and a table pruned at 90 days.
Nearest spec: tech §1.6 (30-second polling, `?since=`); none of the specs discusses timestamp precision.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.1 — The two attention windows are named constants in `packages/core`, not entries in `signals.config.yaml`
Decision: "a week before we ask where an exported article was published" and "a fortnight before we mention an unapplied recommendation" live as `EXPORT_URL_UNCONFIRMED_DAYS` and `OPTIMIZE_UNAPPLIED_DAYS` in `packages/core/src/notifications/attention.ts`, each with a note saying what raising or lowering it would do.
Why: invariant 9 puts thresholds in `packages/rules`, and its scope is the numbers from main §7.3/§7.6/§8.2/§9.6 — detection, scoring and gates — with a lint rule that fences comparisons against search-volume, position, impression and click fields. Neither of these decides anything about a signal or a gate: they decide how long a reminder waits, and both come from ui §4 and §9.4. Putting them in the rules file would stamp `rules_version` on something no opportunity depends on and would make the hash change for a copy-timing tweak. Flagged for the audit: this is a judgement about invariant 9's boundary, and if the boundary is meant to be "every number anywhere", these two move.
Nearest spec: ui §4 (the attention rows and the "> 14 days" nudge), main §9.4 (export URL confirmation); main §7.10 and invariant 9 for the boundary being judged.
## 2026-09-02 — T-EMAIL — BLOCKED, and not what the card expected: email sign-in never needed database sessions; revoking a session early does, and that needs a table nobody has created
Decision: email sign-in ships with sessions **unchanged** — still a self-contained token in a cookie, still lasting a day. The card's fourth done-when ("a session can be revoked before it expires, proved by revoking one and showing the next request is unauthenticated") is **not met**, and no attempt was made to fake it. Everything else on the card is built and proved.

Why, in two parts, because the card's premise turned out to be wrong in a way that matters.

**First: the two things are not the same thing, and the library's own code says so.** The card, and `T1.1` before it, read "the auth library refuses to run an email provider without a database session adapter" as one requirement. It is two. Auth.js checks the configuration on every request, and what it demands of a magic-link provider is storage for a single-use link and a way to look an account up by address — three functions. It demands the session-storage functions (`createSession`, `getSessionAndUser`, and the rest) only when the session strategy is set to `database`, or when a strategy is left unstated. With the strategy stated as tokens — which it already was — the session half is never asked for. So email sign-in was buildable with the tables we have, and is now built.

**Second: what actually blocks revocation is a missing table, not a missing provider.** Revoking a session before it expires means a session has to *be* somewhere it can be deleted from, and there is no `sessions` table in this schema — schema waves 1 through 4 create forty tables and none of them holds a session. Adding one is a migration, and wave 4 is closed; a feature card does not add its own (build plan §3). **The integrator has to decide whether to open a mini-wave for it.** The shape needed is Auth.js's: `sessions(session_token text primary key, user_id uuid references accounts(id) on delete cascade, expires timestamptz)`.

**What is worth knowing before that decision is taken**, because it is not a free improvement. Moving sessions into the database buys "sign out everywhere" and instant lockout — real, and currently absent — and costs a database read on **every authenticated request**, on a platform chosen for being cheap (tech §2.1). It also raises the session-lifetime question the card asked about, which cannot be answered until then: today a day is a *bound on the damage* of not being able to revoke, and if revocation existed, the same day would just be an inconvenience with no security work left to do.

Consequence, stated plainly: a session issued today cannot be ended early. Signing out clears the cookie in that browser and nothing more; a copied cookie stays good until it lapses, at most a day after it was issued.
Nearest spec: main §4.1 ("standard email + OAuth (Google) signup. Nothing exotic.") and tech §3 — neither names a session strategy, mentions revocation, or lists a sessions table. main §13 lists no such table.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T-EMAIL — The session lifetime stays at one day, and the reason it stays is different from the reason it was set
Decision: `SESSION_MAX_AGE_SECONDS` remains 24 hours. The card asked for it to be changed with a reason or kept with one; this is the reason it is kept.
Why: `T1.1` set a day because a session that cannot be revoked has to expire soon enough to bound the damage, and Auth.js's own default of thirty days does not. That is still exactly the situation — see the blocker entry above — so the number that was right for it is still right. Revisiting the figure is the *second* half of moving sessions into the database, not something to do now: raising it while expiry is still the only way a session ends would trade a real security property for convenience, and lowering it would sign merchants out more often to buy nothing.
Nearest spec: main §4.1; tech §3 — silent on session lifetime.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T-EMAIL — Google sign-in is told to match by email address, because that is what it has always done
Decision: the Google provider is configured with `allowDangerousEmailAccountLinking`, and the sign-in guard is tightened to refuse any identity provider that states it has *not* verified the address it is handing us.
Why: with a database adapter present, Auth.js runs a linking step it previously skipped. It asks storage "which account owns this Google identity", and we have nowhere to answer from — there is no table tying a provider's id to an account, and adding one would need a schema wave for a column nothing else would read. So the answer is always "unknown", and Auth.js's default response to an unknown identity whose address already has an account is to **refuse the sign-in**. Without this flag, every existing Google user would be locked out the moment email sign-in shipped.

The flag's name warns about a real risk and it is worth naming: if an identity provider hands over an address it never checked, matching on that address hands over the account. The guard is what removes it — Google states whether it verified the address, and a stated "no" is now refused. A *missing* statement is deliberately not treated as a "no", because a provider that simply omits the claim would otherwise lock every user out, which is a worse failure than the one being prevented and would arrive silently.

What this is not: a change of policy. Sign-in has resolved accounts by lowercased email since `T1.1` — `provisionAccount` looks the address up and creates it if absent, whatever provider carried it — and the unique index on `accounts.email` is the same statement in the schema. The flag makes the library agree with what the app already did.
Nearest spec: main §4.1 — "standard email + OAuth (Google) signup", silent on identity linking.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T-EMAIL — A sign-in link lasts fifteen minutes
Decision: `SIGN_IN_LINK_MAX_AGE_SECONDS = 15 * 60`. Auth.js's default is 24 hours.
Why: the link is a bearer credential — whoever holds the message holds the account until it lapses — and it sits in a mailbox, which is forwarded, archived, synced to phones and scanned by corporate filters. Fifteen minutes is comfortably more than mail delivery takes and short enough that a message found later is inert. The day-long default is a convenience setting on a credential, which is the wrong trade for a tool that manages somebody's storefront.
**This is a number the founder may want to move**, and moving it is one constant plus the "expires in N minutes" line in the email, which is generated from it. Ten minutes is defensible; an hour is defensible if support finds people are being timed out. Nothing in the specs states a figure.
Nearest spec: main §4.1 — silent on link lifetime.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T-EMAIL — The sign-in email goes straight through the email wrapper, not through the notification queue
Decision: the magic-link email is sent by calling the `EmailProvider` wrapper directly. It writes no `email_sends` row and takes no `dedupe_key`. The send is made idempotent on the link itself: the vendor's idempotency header carries a hash of that one link's secret.
Why: the queue's row is `(account_id, type, dedupe_key)` and `account_id` is required — but the person asking for a sign-in link may have no account, which is the entire point of the link. Dedupe would also be actively wrong here. Every other email in this product is a notification that must not go twice; a sign-in link is the opposite — asking for a second one because the first is stale *must* send a second email, so a dedupe key on a notification type would suppress exactly the send the user just asked for. Keying on the link instead gives the property that is actually wanted: a retry of one send cannot deliver twice, and a new link is a new email.
Invariant 25 is satisfied — the send goes through the one instrumented wrapper, and Auth.js's own bundled email provider (which speaks SMTP through Nodemailer) is deliberately not used. Invariant 26's uniqueness constraint is about `email_sends`, and this send is not one.
**Flagged for the integrator:** `T8.2` (lane G) is building the email pipeline in parallel and owns `packages/core/email`. If that card decides sign-in mail should be queued after all, this is the one send that would have to change, and it would need the account-less case solving first.
Nearest spec: tech §1.4 (email pipeline, `email_sends`), §1.5 (suppression — "account-security email" is exempt, and a sign-in link is that). Neither contemplates mail to a non-account.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T-EMAIL — The sign-in email's words live with the sign-in code, not in the UI string file
Decision: the four sentences of the magic-link email are written in `apps/web/app/api/auth/_lib/signInEmail.ts`, not added to `packages/ui/strings/en.json`.
Why: CLAUDE.md says copy lives in `packages/ui/strings/*.json` only, and this departs from it knowingly rather than quietly. That file is a screen dictionary owned by the frontend lane; nothing in the repo sends an email with words in it yet; and the card that builds the email pipeline (`T8.2`) is choosing where template copy lives *tonight*. Putting email copy into a screen's dictionary now would pre-empt that choice inside a file another lane is editing, and would have to be undone.
**This wants a ruling.** Once `T8.2` lands, either email copy joins the UI dictionary — and these four strings move — or emails get their own home and this file follows it. Neither is decided here.
Nearest spec: main Appendix A lists no sign-in email copy; tech §1.4 says templates are versioned in the repo but does not say where the words go.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T-EMAIL — A new OAuth signup is recorded as `oauth` rather than `google` in the funnel
Decision: the `provider` property on `signup_completed` reads `email` or `oauth`. It previously read `google` for Google signups.
Why: with the adapter in place, the account row for a brand-new user is created inside the adapter's `createUser`, and Auth.js does not tell it which provider is signing in — only whether the address has just been verified, which distinguishes a link signup from an OAuth one and nothing finer. The distinction the founder decision actually turns on is preserved ("does email sign-in bring signups Google was losing us"), and there is exactly one OAuth provider, so `oauth` and `google` currently name the same set. Recovering the exact provider name would mean firing the event from a later callback and threading "was this a create" through to it — more moving parts than the property is worth today.
Consequence: a PostHog breakdown of `signup_completed` by provider will show `google` before this change and `oauth` after, for the same route in. Worth knowing before reading that chart across the boundary.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 (funnel events) — names the events, not their properties.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T-EMAIL — FLAGGED, not fixed: the sign-in screen's Google button cannot work, and the same defect would have hit the email form
Decision: not fixed here. Recorded, because it is one line away from my card and in another lane's directory.
Why: `packages/ui/src/public/SignIn.tsx` posts a form to `/api/auth/signin/google` carrying a `callbackUrl` and nothing else. Auth.js rejects any sign-in POST that does not carry the anti-forgery token it hands out at `GET /api/auth/csrf` — verified in the library's own request dispatcher, and I hit it while writing this card's tests, which have to fetch that token before they can ask for a link. So the button on our sign-in screen returns an error rather than starting a sign-in. The screen belongs to the frontend lane and its copy file (`packages/ui/strings/en.json`) is being edited tonight, so this is surfaced rather than resolved.
Consequence for the founder decision that email sign-in ships in v1: **the capability is built and reachable at Auth.js's own page (`/api/auth/signin`), but our branded `/signin` screen still offers only Google, and that button is broken.** Adding the email field is a small frontend change — an input named `email`, a hidden `csrfToken` fetched first, posting to `/api/auth/signin/email` — plus two or three new strings, and it fixes the Google button in the same edit. It is not done, and until it is, no merchant reaches email sign-in from the product.
Nearest spec: ui §1 (sign-in screen); main §4.1.
Class (filled by audit):
## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — Nine steps on the server become seven rows on the screen, and three of them collapse into one
Decision: the setup progress list shows the seven rows the UI spec names. The server reports nine steps, and `catalog_sync`, `distill` and `family_group` are drawn as the single row "Reading your store". A row's state is worked out from all its steps: any failure wins, then anything running, then all-skipped, then all-finished; a row two-thirds through reads as active rather than done.
Why: the nine exist because fetching a catalogue, reducing each product to a fact sheet and grouping those products into families fail, retry and resume independently — that is a property of the work, not something to show a merchant, who would read three indistinguishable technical names as the product stalling. The spec names seven labels and the response schema names nine steps; neither says how they line up, so the mapping was a choice. The precedence order is the load-bearing part: had "anything succeeded" been tested before "anything running", a row would flicker to done between two of its own jobs.
Nearest spec: ui §3.2 — the seven labels; main §6.3–6.4 and the ingestion status schema — the nine steps.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — Two opposite screens share one domain state, and the connection tells them apart
Decision: `domains.state = awaiting_shopify_auth` renders the blocking connect card when `connections.shopify` is `none`, and the "your connection was lost" parked card when it is `broken`.
Why: the spec describes these as separate screens (§3.3 and §3.5) but the state machine gives them one state — a store that has never connected and a working store whose token was revoked both sit in `awaiting_shopify_auth`. They want opposite words: one is a step in setting up, the other is an account that stopped, with history that stays readable. The connection field is the only thing in the account response that separates them. **This puts a requirement on the backend:** revocation must set the connection to `broken` rather than back to `none`, or a working merchant is shown the first-run screen. Registered in the screen contract.
Nearest spec: ui §3.3, §3.5; main §6.2 — "the account drops back to `awaiting_shopify_auth` with a reconnect banner".

## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — The first scan's progress lines are named, not ticked, because nothing reports the scan's progress
Decision: the "Finding your growth opportunities…" card lists the five stages the design canvas names, all rendered as work in flight. No stage is shown as finished. The card polls `GET /api/opportunities` every five seconds and moves the merchant on when `lastScanAt` stops being null. A `stageStates` prop is where a real feed plugs in.
Why: the spec says the sub-lines mirror `signal_runs` over the same kind of stream as the ingestion stepper. The frozen API contract has no route for that — 55 routes, none of them a scan-progress read or stream — and this lane may not add one. Two ways to fill the gap were available: tick the stages on a timer, or name them without ticking. A timer would be a fabricated progress bar on the one screen whose whole job is to earn trust before the product has produced anything, so it was rejected. What is lost is the sense of movement; what is kept is that nothing on screen is untrue. **This needs a route** — a `signal_runs` status/stream pair shaped like the ingestion one — before §3.8 is actually satisfied.
Nearest spec: ui §3.8 — "sub-lines that reflect `signal_runs` progress … SSE-driven like the stepper"; main §6.9.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — Language and country are validated fields rather than dropdowns, because nothing defines the options
Decision: the confirmation screen's language and country are text inputs holding short codes, validated to the shape the confirm request requires (language at least two characters, country exactly two, upper-cased as typed). "Confirm profile" stays disabled until the description, language and country all hold something.
Why: the spec calls for dropdowns, and a dropdown needs a list. No document names one, no endpoint serves one, and the set that actually matters is whichever locales the paid search vendor accepts as parameters — which lives in another lane's package. Inventing a list here would be inventing product data, and a wrong entry in it quietly sends every later search lookup to the wrong market. Validating the shape gets the same guarantee the confirm request itself asks for; swapping in a select is one component when a list exists.
Nearest spec: ui §3.7 item 1 — "language + country dropdowns"; main §6.5 — the persona produces the codes.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — Three places where the design canvas and the specs disagree, each resolved towards the spec
Decision: **The parked card's sentence.** The canvas renders "…contact us for a custom solution." and the canonical copy table ends "…or join the waitlist." The canonical string is used whole. **The waitlist capture itself is not built:** §3.4 asks for one and no route accepts an email address, so the card offers the contact link alone. **"Try a different domain"**, which the canvas offers on the same card, is not built either: §3.4 says the state persists until support intervenes, with no self-serve exit in v1. **"Save and finish later"** on the confirmation screen is not built: nothing persists a partial edit — the only write is confirming — so the button would silently discard what it appeared to save.
Why: `docs/design/README.md` makes the spec the source of truth for behaviour and copy and asks for disagreements to be stated rather than picked. Each of these is the canvas offering a control the product has nowhere to send.
Nearest spec: ui §3.4, §3.7; main Appendix A — the parked-state sentence.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — The competitor suggestion line was reworded away from the spec's example, to keep a count off a denominator
Decision: a suggested competitor reads "ranks alongside you in 8 top searches" rather than the spec's illustrative "appears in 8 of your top queries".
Why: the constitution forbids rendering a count against a denominator anywhere the merchant can see, and the existing copy test enforces it by refusing any sentence of the shape "{n} of …". The spec's phrasing is an example in parentheses rather than a pinned string, and the meaning is unchanged — the number is how many of the store's own top searches this domain turned up in. Rewording was preferred to loosening the test, because the test is the only thing standing between the product and "3 of 30".
Nearest spec: ui §3.7 item 4 — the example wording; main §8.6 and CLAUDE.md invariant 23 — no denominators.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — The competitor cap appears a third time, in the screen, and enforces nothing
Decision: `MAX_COMPETITORS = 5` lives in `packages/ui`. It disables the two add controls and fills their tooltip; it is not a check.
Why: the cap is already enforced twice, in the API and by a database trigger, which is what the invariant requires and what makes it true regardless of what any screen does. A screen that let the merchant type a sixth and then showed them a refusal would be worse to use for no extra safety. The number is not a scoring threshold, so it does not belong in `packages/rules`; it is a product limit stated in the spec's prose.
Nearest spec: main §6.6 — "Hard cap: 5 competitors per account … enforced in the API and DB"; CLAUDE.md invariant 5.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — A blocklisted competitor is recognised from the refusal, not from a code, and the override is offered back
Decision: adding a competitor treats a 409 carrying `competitor_is_own_domain` or `competitor_limit_reached` as those two specific messages, and any other refusal of a well-formed domain as the marketplace blocklist — which is shown as a warning with an "Add anyway" button that retries with `overrideBlocklist: true`.
Why: the request schema has the override flag, so the blocklist refusal is meant to be overridable, but the conflict-code enum has no entry for it — the two named codes are the only ones the contract gives this route. Guessing a code that does not exist would break the moment one is added; treating "refused, and not one of the two we know" as the overridable case behaves correctly today and degrades to a slightly generic warning if the route ever refuses for a third reason.
Nearest spec: main §6.8 — "cannot be on the marketplace blocklist without an 'add anyway' override"; the route contract — `addCompetitorRequestSchema.overrideBlocklist`, conflicts `competitor_limit_reached` / `competitor_is_own_domain`.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — Best sellers reorder by dragging or by arrow keys, and the revenue figures are a band rather than an amount
Decision: each row is draggable and its handle is a button that answers ArrowUp and ArrowDown. The row shows the source badge and, where the response carries one, the revenue band — not a currency amount or a unit count.
Why: the spec asks for drag to reorder; an ordering control that only answers a mouse is unusable from a keyboard or a phone, and the ranking it sets is what the persona and the seed keywords are drawn from, so it is not optional detail. The keys are the same control, not a second one. On the figures: §3.7 asks for "90d revenue/qty badges", and the profile response carries `revenueBand` and no numbers at all — showing a number would mean inventing one.
Nearest spec: ui §3.7 item 2; the profile response schema — `topProductSchema`.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — Reporting a wrong grouping sends the merchant's words to support and three facts to analytics
Decision: a new browser event, `family_grouping_reported`, carrying the family's id, its member count and which signal grouped it. What the merchant typed goes only to `POST /api/products/families/report`.
Why: the spec names this event. The three properties are what makes a report useful in aggregate — whether we merge too eagerly, and which grouping signal does it — and each fits a kind the property table allows. The merchant's text does not: it is free prose about their own catalogue, and there is deliberately no kind that could hold it.
Nearest spec: ui §3.7 item 5 — "support/feedback record + PostHog event `family_grouping_reported`"; main §14.7 — events carry ids and aggregates only.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — The one-time explainer is dismissed per browser, because nothing stores a dismissal
Decision: the strip explaining the four action types remembers being dismissed in the browser's own storage, under `sortiva.activationExplainerDismissed`.
Why: the spec says it appears once and is dismissible for good. "For good" needs somewhere per account to keep it, and neither the settings response nor any route has a field for one — and this card may not add a column. The cost of the browser-local version is that a merchant who signs in on a second device sees the strip once more; the cost of the alternative is a schema change nobody asked for.
Nearest spec: ui §3.8 — "a one-time explainer strip … dismissible".

## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — Onboarding fills the dashboard, and stops at the edge of the screen another card owns
Decision: `/dashboard` renders every stage of setting a store up and, once that is over, an empty marked slot. `/opportunities` renders the activation header — the count and the one-time explainer — above an empty marked slot for the list.
Why: the spec is explicit that the dashboard is the container and there is no wizard route, so the states had to live here. Both steady-state screens belong to later cards, and building either would be building somebody else's card badly. The slots are empty elements with a name rather than placeholder copy, so the card that fills one deletes a div rather than untangling a stand-in.
Nearest spec: ui §3 opening — "The dashboard is the container for all onboarding states"; ui §4 and §5 — the two screens that follow.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.3 — Contradiction, not resolved here: Google sends a merchant to Settings in the middle of onboarding
Decision: recorded rather than changed. `apps/web/app/api/gsc/_lib/config.ts` returns the merchant to `/settings/connections?gsc=granted` after Google's consent screen. During onboarding the Search Console step and its property picker are part of the progress list on the dashboard, so a merchant who connects mid-setup is dropped out of setup onto a settings screen that does not exist yet.
Why not fixed: that file belongs to Lane C. The dashboard already reads `?gsc=granted` and shows the property picker when it sees it, so the screen works the moment the return address points back at it during onboarding. Two shapes of fix, both someone else's call: carry the origin through the OAuth state, or return to the dashboard whenever the domain is not yet confirmed.
Nearest spec: ui §3.6 — the picker is a step in the stepper; main §12.2 — the property picker mechanics.
## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — The change stream lives in the webhook table, and the account travels in the row

Decision: `CatalogEvents` — the frozen seam that tells the content inventory and the drift rules what a merchant changed — is backed by `webhook_events`, the existing table for "something arrived, deal with it later". Two kinds of row sit there now, told apart by topic: a **receipt** keyed on Shopify's own delivery id, and a **change** keyed on a fingerprint of the change itself. `webhook_events` has no account column, so the account id travels inside the row's JSON payload and the per-store read filters on it.

Why: the stream has to be durable, per-store, ordered and de-duplicable, and no table for it exists. Adding one is a migration, which a feature card may not do. Of the tables that do exist, this is the only one whose purpose matches. The cost, stated plainly: reading one store's changes cannot use an index on the account, so it is a scan over rows arriving in the last thirty days (the retention sweep prunes at thirty days). At v1 volumes — a webhook is a merchant editing something — that is small. **If it stops being small, the fix is a column in the next schema wave, not a second table.**

Alternative rejected: deriving the stream from the `products` table's own timestamps. It cannot represent a deletion, because a deleted product's row is what would have to be missing, and it cannot represent a blog post or a static page at all.

Nearest spec: main §13 (`webhook_events`), §14.3.8 (process from the table, never from the body); build plan §4 (`CatalogEvents` frozen).


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — A change is identified by what changed and when, not by which producer found it

Decision: a change row's key is `catalog_change/<account>:<kind>:<entity>:<the merchant's own edit time>`. Recording is insert-or-ignore.

Why: two things produce changes — a webhook we processed, and the nightly re-read that catches the webhooks Shopify dropped. Both see the same edit. Keying on the edit rather than on the delivery makes the second one a no-op by construction, which is what makes "run the sweep twice and it converges" true rather than merely usually true. It is also what stops a store that never changes accumulating change rows for ever, with every consumer downstream re-reading pages that did not move.

Consequence: a change with no edit time of its own — a deletion — is stamped with the sweep's own clock, so a store left with a deleted product records one removal per sweep. The consumers collapse that (a page removed twice is removed once), and the alternative, a key with no time in it at all, would make a deleted-then-recreated product invisible the second time.

Nearest spec: main §14.3.8 (the sweep is idempotent by construction), §14.1 (webhooks plus reconciliation, never webhooks alone).


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — A product's fingerprint covers its words, not its price or its stock

Decision: `products.checksum` is computed over the title, description, handle, type, vendor, tags, status, variant names and codes, and image addresses. It deliberately excludes prices and stock levels. Price and availability changes are detected separately, by comparing the stored `variants` against the arriving ones.

Why: the same column has two readers with opposite needs. The drift sweep wants to know "did anything change"; distillation (`T2.3`) re-runs when this moves, and a model call per product is the most expensive thing onboarding does. A merchant running a weekend sale changes every price in the shop, and a checksum covering prices would buy a fresh distillation of the entire catalogue for an event that changed no words. main §14.3.2 says the distillation key is "the product's `updated_at` + **body** checksum", which this follows.

The three kinds of change are reported separately into the stream (`product_updated`, `price_changed`, `availability_changed`), which is what lets the content inventory ignore the two that cannot move a page's words — the decision `T3.2` already took.

Nearest spec: main §14.3.2 (the distillation key), §14.1 (drift triggers); DECISIONS 2026-09-02 `T3.2`.


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — Test orders and cancelled orders are not sales

Decision: an order flagged as one of Shopify's own test orders, or one carrying a cancellation, is dropped when the orders are read. It contributes to neither the best-seller ranking nor the landing-page takings.

Why: main §6.2 says to aggregate order line items over the trailing ninety days and does not say which orders count. Both defaults are wrong in a visible way. Shopify's test orders are placed with a test gateway while a merchant is setting their shop up, and counting them means a brand-new store's "best sellers" are whatever the owner bought from themselves — the first thing they would see, and obviously wrong. A cancelled order is not a purchase, and counting it inflates the revenue figure the ranking is sorted by.

Risk accepted: a refunded order still counts, because a refund is a separate record we do not read. Correcting that means reading refunds, which is more order data for a marginal improvement to a ranking; it is worth revisiting if a merchant disputes their best-seller list.

Nearest spec: main §6.2 — "aggregate order line items over the trailing 90 days"; silent on which orders qualify.


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — A landing page is kept as a path with its query string removed

Decision: `landing_revenue_daily.landing_url` holds the path a buyer arrived on — `/collections/trail-shoes` — and never the query string Shopify recorded with it.

Why: two reasons, and either alone would be enough. The query carries campaign parameters and advertising click ids, which are per-visitor and belong to the shopper rather than to the shop; keeping them would put visitor-scoped identifiers in a table whose whole purpose is that it holds none. And a page reached through five campaigns would otherwise be five rows meaning the same thing, which makes the aggregate useless for the one question it exists to answer.

Nearest spec: main §17.3 (`landing_site` aggregated per day per landing URL, aggregates only), §14.6.


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — An order's day is the store's own day, taken from Shopify's stamp verbatim

Decision: the day an order is counted under is the first ten characters of Shopify's `created_at`, not a date computed by converting that timestamp.

Why: Shopify stamps orders with the store's own UTC offset. A sale at 23:30 in a New Zealand shop is the previous day in UTC, so converting would move every evening's takings into the day before and a merchant comparing our numbers with their own Shopify report would find them disagreeing. Reading the characters Shopify already wrote gives the merchant's day with no timezone table and no persona dependency — which matters because the persona, where the store's timezone eventually lives, is built two steps *after* this one.

Nearest spec: main §17.3 (per day per landing URL), §9.4 (per-account clocks); neither says whose day.


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — The catalogue sync's idempotency key is the store plus the day

Decision: `catalog_sync`'s `input_version` is the shop handle and the current date. A redelivery on the same day returns the stored answer without reading the store; the next night is genuinely different work.

Why: main §14.3.2 specifies "the Shopify shop ID + a monotonically increasing sync generation number", and no column holds a generation number — adding one is a schema wave. The date is a generation number that increments once a day and needs no storage. What it costs: two syncs on the same day are one, so a merchant who reconnects after a failed first attempt on the same day gets the stored answer rather than a fresh read. That is the right answer when the first attempt succeeded and the wrong one if a re-read is genuinely wanted the same day; the nightly reconciliation covers the gap within a day, and a deliberate re-sync path would need its own input version rather than a weaker key.

Nearest spec: main §14.3.2 — names a generation number this schema does not have.


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — A product the store stopped listing keeps its row; the removal is reported, not acted on

Decision: when the nightly re-read finds a product Shopify no longer lists, a `product_deleted` change is recorded and the `products` row is left exactly where it is. Nothing is deleted and nothing is marked.

Why: the row is the only record that the product ever existed, and the drift rules (`T5.3`) need precisely that — an article recommending a discontinued product has to be repairable, which means knowing what it pointed at. Deleting the row would cascade the product's fact sheet away and leave every reference to it dangling with no way to explain itself. Marking it needs a column, which is a schema wave. This mirrors the decision `T3.2` took for deleted store pages, and inherits its open question: **what should eventually happen to the row is for the founder or the integrator to settle before the drift card ships.**

How "no longer listed" is decided: the sweep stamps every product it sees with the moment its walk began, so anything still carrying an older stamp was not shown to us. Asked that way rather than by accumulating ids, so a walk spread over several runs still gets the right answer.

Nearest spec: main §14.1 (product deleted → flag referencing articles); silent on the catalogue row itself.


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — There is no request cache on the Shopify reads, deliberately

Decision: invariant 20 requires billable reads and model calls to be cached at request level and written before processing. The catalogue sync and the sweep have no such cache.

Why: the invariant exists to stop us paying twice for one answer — a crash after the vendor responded but before we finished with the response. Shopify Admin reads are not billed; main §14.3.6 classifies them as "pure read: freely retryable, no protection needed beyond backoff". Caching them would add a table write per page for no saving and would make a re-read return stale data during a sweep whose entire purpose is freshness. What *is* protected is the request budget, by the page cursor: a resumed walk does not re-spend the pages it already read, which is the equivalent scarce resource here.

Recorded because a reader checking invariant 20 against this card will find nothing and should find the reason rather than a gap.

Nearest spec: main §14.3.6 (external side-effects by class); invariant 20.


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — The receiver keeps the store's name beside the body, because the header has nowhere else to go

Decision: a stored Shopify delivery's `payload` is `{ shop_handle, body }` rather than the body alone.

Why: Shopify names the store in the `X-Shopify-Shop-Domain` header, not in the body, and `webhook_events` has no column for it. The drain has to answer "whose store is this" without the request still being open, so the name has to survive the response. This card is the first writer of Shopify rows in that table, so nothing else reads the old shape. A column is a schema wave.

Nearest spec: main §14.3.8 (process from the table, never from the request body); main §13 (`webhook_events`).


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — `shop/redact` is recorded and answered; the purge itself is not built here

Decision: of the three mandatory privacy webhooks, `customers/redact` and `customers/data_request` are fully answered — logged, and answered "no customer data held", which is true by construction because order ingestion keeps no customer field. `shop/redact` is verified, recorded and logged, and **no store data is erased by this card**.

Why: erasing a store's data within thirty days is account-lifecycle work (main §14.6): it spans billing, tokens, the domain claim's grace window and every table an account owns, and belongs to whoever builds account deletion. Building half of it here would leave two places that delete a store, one of them incomplete. **Flagged for the integrator: `shop/redact` currently has a receiver and no consequence. Some card must own the purge before launch, and none does today.**

Nearest spec: main §14.6 — the three mandatory topics and what each must do.


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — The rate limit lives inside the one Admin client, so every caller inherits it

Decision: the one-request-a-second budget is enforced inside `ShopifyAdminClient`, per store, and the process holds a single instance of that client. A 429 pauses the whole store for exactly the `Retry-After` Shopify sent.

Why: the budget Shopify enforces is per store, not per caller. Two things read a store on the same night — this card's catalogue sync and the content inventory (`T3.2`) — and a limiter per call site would let each believe it was alone and together earn the rate limiting the pacing exists to avoid. Putting it in the client also means the inventory lane inherits pacing it did not have, with no change to its code. **Visible consequence for Lane C: their nightly walk is now paced. It was not before.**

Nearest spec: main §14.4 — "background sync budgeted at 1 req/s ... honor `Retry-After` exactly".


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — Files touched outside Lane B's directories
