Decision: this card writes into five files no lane owns, listed so the integrator expects them. `packages/db/src/repositories/catalog.ts` and `catalog-events.ts` (new) plus one line in `repositories/index.ts` — repositories, not migrations. `packages/jobs/src/chaos/harness.ts` gains one entry in its scenario list and one import, which is what that file's own comment says T2.2 will do. `apps/web/instrumentation.ts` registers the two new task handlers, beside the ones already there. `apps/web/app/api/webhooks/shopify/[topic]/` is new; the path is the one the frozen route table already names, and `apps/web/app/api/webhooks/` is not in any lane's ownership list (Lane A owns `webhooks/stripe` specifically).

Why: build plan §3 marks `instrumentation.ts` and the chaos harness as integrator-resolved rather than union-merged, and a merge that silently drops half of either is worse than a conflict.

Nearest spec: build plan §3 ("Files no lane owns"), §4 (the frozen route table).


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — The stub report no longer lists the change stream, and a T2.1 test changed meaning

Decision: two small edits outside this card's own files. `scripts/stub-report.mjs` no longer constructs `StubCatalogEvents`, because that seam is filled — the product runs on the real change stream now, and a report that still listed it would fail the M2 exit gate for a gap that no longer exists. The double itself is untouched and tests still use it. And one assertion in `T2.1`'s onboarding test changed: it said "the next step belongs to a later card, so the run correctly stops", and that later card is this one, so a store with permission granted now carries straight on into reading its catalogue and stops at distillation instead.

Why recorded: the second is a behaviour change to something another card wrote, and a reviewer seeing an edited assertion should find the reason here rather than guess at one.

Nearest spec: build plan §4 (a consumer's tests re-run against the real implementation once the producer lands).


## 2026-09-02 — T2.2 — No dev-store evidence: the store reads are proved against an in-memory Shopify

Decision: this card's Shopify behaviour is proved against stand-ins — an in-memory store that pages the way Shopify pages, and, for the rate limiter and the `Link`-header cursor, a real HTTP server standing in for Shopify. There are still no Partner credentials.

Why: the same posture `T2.1` recorded and four earlier cards took with Stripe, Turnstile, Anthropic and PostHog. What a stand-in cannot prove: that Shopify's real cursor format, `Retry-After` behaviour, order and product field shapes, and webhook headers behave as assumed; and that the field lists we send are accepted. **This card must be re-run against a Partner dev store before launch, and it is now the largest single piece of outstanding real-vendor evidence in the build.**

Nearest spec: build plan §6 `T2.2` done-when; `docs/founder-decisions.md` A1.


## 2026-09-02 — T8.0 — A Shopify store is held by its live connection only, so losing one releases the store
Decision: `shopify_conns.shop_handle` is unique among rows where `invalidated_at IS NULL`, instead of unique across every row that ever existed. The abandoned row is kept, not deleted. Because two rows may now carry one handle, `findAccountByShopHandle` — the "whose store is this" lookup an incoming Shopify webhook depends on — now asks for the live row; it previously relied on the unconditional index to make its answer single, and would otherwise have been free to answer with the abandoned account and route an uninstall to a merchant who no longer has that store.
Why: the old index made a lost connection a permanent lock. A merchant who uninstalled us, or deleted their account and came back later under a new one, could never reconnect the same store: the leftover row still owned the handle and no path in the product cleared it, so the attempt died on a database constraint the merchant could do nothing about. Recommended in these words by audit `T0.3` ("makes the release real at the database level ... the version that survives a sweep job failing to run") and by the `T1.4` entry of 2026-09-01, and collected into this wave by the integrator. Risk considered and accepted: another account can now connect a store whose connection is dead, including while the original merchant is still being asked to reconnect. Connecting requires completing Shopify's own install on that store, so only someone with real admin rights there can do it — the exposure is narrower than the failure it replaces. Note for the record that "defence in depth", the phrase the `T1.4` entry uses, describes it wrongly: a partial unique index permits strictly more rows than an unconditional one, so this is a deliberate relaxation, not an extra guard.
Nearest spec: main §14.4 (a rejected token moves the account to `awaiting_shopify_auth`) and §6.2 — both describe losing a connection, neither says whether the store stays reserved afterwards.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.0 — `domains.release_after` does not become a partial unique index; the wave stops and asks instead
Decision: the domain claim's uniqueness is left exactly as it is — unconditional on `domain_normalized` — and the matching change recommended alongside the Shopify one is **not** made in this wave. Deferred to the founder or the integrator, not declined.
Why: the two look symmetrical and are not. Making the domain index partial on `release_after IS NULL` frees the domain the instant the column is set, which is the moment the account is deleted — and main §14.6 sets that column precisely to hold the domain for seven days first, "so a squatter cannot take it the same hour". Postgres index predicates must be immutable, so the deadline itself cannot go in the predicate; there is no version of this index that both survives the release sweep never running *and* keeps the grace window. Three further consequences, none of which this card can take alone: the claim path would have to start reading the deadline it deliberately ignores today (`T1.4`, 2026-09-01), which is a file in another lane's directory pinned by a test asserting the opposite; two rows could then exist for one domain, one lingering and one live, so every lookup by domain would have to disambiguate; and unlike a Shopify connection, claiming a domain has no external gate — anyone can type it — so the grace window is the only thing standing between a deleted account and a squatter. The open question is put in the session report in plain terms.
Nearest spec: main §14.6 (7-day grace), §2/§5 (one account per domain); invariant 1.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.0 — Marking a store page as deleted is not built; the wave stops on the shape of it
Decision: no column is added for "this page is gone" in this wave. The inventory — one row per web address the store publishes — still keeps a row for a page the merchant has deleted, with nothing recording that it is gone, exactly as `T3.2` left it.
Why: `T3.2`'s entry of 2026-09-02 asks for this and ends "This needs the founder or the integrator to settle before the existing-target check ships in `T3.5`", and the integrator's collection for this wave repeats that the shape — a nullable timestamp saying when the page went, or a state column naming which of several conditions the row is in — leaks into what `T3.5` reads. `CLAUDE.md` prime directive 3 makes an interface another lane consumes a stop-and-ask, not a lane's choice. What stays broken until it is settled, unchanged from `T3.2`: `T3.5` would recommend improving a page that no longer exists. Nothing new breaks by waiting; `T8.1` and `T8.2`, this lane's next two cards, do not read the inventory at all.
Nearest spec: main §12.3 — specifies the inventory sync and its checksum, silent on removal.

## 2026-09-02 — T8.0 — The rest of the wave's collected list is closed without a migration, and "retention bookkeeping" has no outstanding item
Decision: schema wave 4 ships one index change and nothing else. Everything else the integrator collected is recorded here as closed rather than left ambiguous. **Already built, re-checked against the migrations rather than the journal:** `verification_tokens` and the completed-work ledger `idempotency_ledger` (both `0004_wave2b.sql`, the ledger's write-once trigger in `0005_wave2b_guards.sql`); and every table `T8.1` and `T8.2` need — `notifications`, `email_sends`, `email_suppressions`, `notification_prefs`. **Declined earlier on substantive grounds and not revived:** a "price unknown" marker on spend events, and an account column on the completed-work ledger. **Conditional on cards nobody has written, so not requested:** the detected store name as a queryable column, a measured-or-defaulted marker on the click-through curve, stored cluster-share tables, and a per-episode marker for the Shopify connect reminder. **Retention:** tech §1.7's rules — notifications pruned at 90 days, `email_sends` kept 12 months — are sweep behaviour for `T8.3`, and both tables already carry the timestamp a sweep reads, so the card's "retention bookkeeping" scope line has no column behind it.
Why: the card's done-when is "every schema-tagged DECISIONS entry is resolved or explicitly deferred", and an entry nobody closed reads the same as an entry nobody found. Note for the spec keepers: the card also says to read "`DECISIONS.md` (class-b entries tagged `schema`)" and **no such tagging exists** — the `Class (filled by audit):` line is empty almost everywhere. The card text should be corrected to point at the collected list instead.
Nearest spec: tech §1.3, §1.7 — the card's own citations; both are satisfied by existing tables.
## 2026-09-02 — T-START — Re-claiming a domain asks for the run again, and the job key is what stops a second one
Decision: the claim queues the "move this store's onboarding along" job on both successful paths — the real claim, and the merchant who submits their own domain a second time (which the code answers as `already_yours`). It is not queued on any refusal. What prevents two jobs is the queue's own de-duplication: the job is filed under a key derived from the account (`ingestion_dispatch:<account id>`), so a second ask lands on the first job rather than stacking behind it.
Why: the card's done-when is "re-running the claim does not queue a second job for the same run", and a merchant re-pasting their address *is* the re-run — the first one committed, so the second is answered as "already yours". Reading that done-when as "only queue on the first claim" would make it trivially true and prove nothing. Queueing on both is also the more useful behaviour: a merchant who sees nothing happen and pastes their address again gets the pipeline nudged, without anyone touching the database. It cannot duplicate work — the steps are claimed by guarded updates and the completed-work ledger returns the stored result — so the worst case of an extra nudge is a worker finding nothing to do.
Alternative rejected: keying the job on the *run* rather than the account. The run id is derived from the domain, so it would behave identically today; the account is what every other queue in the codebase keys on, and it is also the thing a second, unrelated request to move onboarding (the OAuth callback, a sweep) would key on — so keying on the account is what makes those collapse together too.
Nearest spec: main §5 step 3 — "claiming enqueues the deep ingestion job", silent on repeat claims; main §14.3.1–14.3.2.

## 2026-09-02 — T-START — The queue call is a separate file, and the claim calls it directly rather than through an injected port
Decision: `packages/jobs/src/ingestion/queue.ts` is a new file holding the task name and `enqueueIngestionDispatch`, importing only drizzle — matching the Search Console and inventory queues, which were split the same way. The claim calls it directly, rather than taking it as a function it can be handed (which is how the Search Console connect flow does it).
Why: two reasons for the split file, one for the direct call. The dispatcher itself imports the Shopify steps, the LLM wrapper and the threshold config; a request that imported it to queue one job would pull a file-reading config loader into a bundle with no filesystem, and the build would fail while every test passed — this has already happened once (`T-BOOT`). And the property this card has to demonstrate is that the claim and the queued job commit together, which only the real queue in a real transaction can show; an injected stand-in would prove the claim calls *something*, which is not the guarantee. So the test installs the queue's own tables and inspects them.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md code-structure rules — file placement is convention, not spec.

## 2026-09-02 — T-START — The queue's tables are a start-up prerequisite, and two test suites now create them themselves
Decision: the claim's transaction writes into the queue's tables, which are created by the worker at start-up and **not** by our own migrations. `apps/web/app/api/domain/_lib/domain.test.ts` and `funnel.test.ts` now install them in their setup, via a new shared helper (`packages/jobs/src/runtime/testing.ts`). Their test cases are unchanged.
Why: the worker runs inside the web server's own process and installs those tables before the server can answer a request, so in production the prerequisite is met by the time any claim arrives — and a route already queues work this way (finishing a Search Console connection). **The consequence to know about:** a database that no worker has ever started against has no queue in it, and a claim against such a database now fails rather than silently claiming without starting anything. That is the safe direction — a claim that quietly starts nothing is the bug this card exists to fix — but it means `WORKER_ENABLED=false` against a fresh database (local screen work) breaks the claim, where before it merely stalled it. Adding the queue's tables to our own migrations would remove the hole; that is a migration, which this card may not write.
Alternative rejected: catching the "no such schema" error and letting the claim commit anyway. That reintroduces exactly the failure this card removes, and hides it.
Nearest spec: tech §2 — silent on who installs the queue's schema.
## 2026-09-02 — T-ANALYTICS — What stops store content reaching analytics is the shape a property is allowed to have, not a list of banned words
Decision: each browser event's property table now says, for every property, *what kind of thing it may hold* — an identifier, an enumerated name we chose, a count, or a yes/no — and that table exists at run time rather than only in the type system. Everything a screen reports is filtered through it: a property the event does not declare is dropped, and a declared property holding the wrong kind of thing is dropped too. There is deliberately no kind for text.
Why: an open audit finding (`docs/audits/false-confidence.md`, finding 9) records that the server-side wrapper redacts secrets and lets an article title, an article body and a prompt straight through, and that the rule's only enforcement was a doc comment. The founder's decision that browsers talk to the vendor directly removes the last place our code could inspect an outgoing event, so the check had to move into the definitions. Two candidate designs: a list of property *names* that are allowed, or a rule about the *values*. The name list alone is defeated by anyone adding a name; the value rule alone lets an undeclared field through. Both are in, and the value rule is the one that carries the guarantee: identifiers and enumerated names must be under sixty-four characters with no whitespace, so a headline, a paragraph or a prompt is not expressible under any property name at all.
One case the shape rule does not catch on its own: a vendor access token is short and has no spaces, so it looks exactly like an identifier. The same secret matcher the server-side wrapper already uses rejects it here. It is **rejected rather than redacted** — an id that is really a token is a bug, and an event carrying `[redacted]` where an opportunity id should be is worse than an event missing the property.
What this does *not* claim: a future card could still add a property called `article_title` and give it the enum kind, and a one-word title would pass. Two things bite if it tries — a test refuses any property whose name contains `title`, `body`, `text`, `prompt`, `token` and a dozen similar words, and the value rule rejects anything with a space in it — but neither is a proof. The real protection against that is that adding a property is a visible edit to one table rather than a field appearing at a call site.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 ("events carry ids and aggregates only — never product content, article text, prompts") — it states the rule and says nothing about how it is held.

## 2026-09-02 — T-ANALYTICS — Session replay is off everywhere, and every view has to say whether it renders a merchant's store
Decision: nothing is recorded. The vendor library starts with recording disabled, no code anywhere turns it on, and a list in `packages/ui/src/analytics/replay.ts` records for every view whether it renders a merchant's store data. A view that does can never be recorded whatever else is configured, and a view nobody has classified counts as store data.
Why: session replay saves a film of the page and plays it back to us later, so a replay of a merchant's screen is a copy of their catalogue sitting on a vendor's server. The card requires this to be provable rather than configured, and the honest proof needs three parts, because each alone is weak: the start-up options (which a later edit could change), the per-view answer (which is only as good as the list), and the list being complete (which nothing but a test can keep true). A test walks the app's routes and fails naming any view that is not classified — proved by adding an unclassified screen and watching it fail — and a second test tries to switch replay on for a store-data view and shows it stays off.
Judgement call worth knowing about: `/`, the public landing page, is classified as **store data**. It is the one public page that renders a shop's products — the preview card shows what was read from whichever address the visitor typed. That shop is a stranger's rather than a customer's, which is exactly why recording it would be worse rather than better.
The list is empty of enabled views and no card has asked for replay, so today this fences off something nobody has switched on. That is the point: the day someone asks for it on a marketing page, the store screens are already out of reach rather than needing to be remembered.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 privacy note — "Turn off PostHog session replay on any view that renders store data, or mask it." It does not say how "off" is held true over time.

## 2026-09-02 — T-ANALYTICS — Everything the vendor's browser library does by itself is switched off
Decision: the library starts with autocapture, page-view capture, page-leave capture, performance capture, rage-click detection and surveys all disabled, and creates a person record only for someone who has signed in. The only events that exist are the ten a screen can report.
Why: **autocapture is the setting that would defeat the property table.** Left on, the library records every click along with the text of whatever was clicked — and on a product list or a draft article, that text is the merchant's store. Every guard in the event definitions would be beside the point. Page views are off for a different reason: the funnel is already emitted server-side as the work happens (`preview_requested`, `signup_completed`, and the rest), so a browser-side copy would leave the funnel's numbers depending on which of the two you counted, which is the specific thing the card asks not to happen. Surveys and rage clicks are vendor features nobody asked for that put things on a merchant's screen we did not write.
Consequence for later cards: a screen needing a new event adds it to the property table. There is no autocapture safety net catching it in the meantime, deliberately.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 — the taxonomy is a closed list of named events; nothing in it is captured automatically.

## 2026-09-02 — T-ANALYTICS — Only the authenticated screens report from the browser; the public funnel is untouched
Decision: the reporting provider is mounted in the authenticated layout (`apps/web/app/(app)/layout.tsx`) and nowhere else. The landing page, the plan page and the sign-in page load no analytics library at all.
Why: `T9.2` already established that every step of the public funnel is recorded where the work happens, so there is nothing for a browser to add there. Mounting nothing is a stronger guarantee than mounting something configured not to duplicate — no library, no events, no possibility of a second copy of the funnel. It also keeps a vendor script off the page a stranger lands on.
Consequence: `useUiAnalytics()` on a public screen returns the do-nothing default, which is correct rather than broken. A card that later wants a genuinely browser-only public event mounts the provider there and classifies the view; `/` is already classified as store data, so it would arrive with replay already fenced off.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 (server-side capture); ui §11 (browser-only events). The two disagree, and `T9.1` recorded that; this decision is where the disagreement is resolved for the public half.

## 2026-09-02 — T-ANALYTICS — The browser's key is its own environment variable, even though it holds the same value as the server's
Decision: a new variable, `NEXT_PUBLIC_POSTHOG_KEY`, holds the analytics project's key for the page. `POSTHOG_API_KEY` — the same value, in practice — stays server-only.
Why: the project key is public by design; that is what makes reporting from a browser possible at all. But one variable read in both places means the day somebody puts a different kind of key in `POSTHOG_API_KEY`, it is written into every page with no code change and no test failing. Two variables cost one line in `.env.example` and make "this one goes to the browser" a property of the name.
Nearest spec: none. tech §2 covers deployment configuration and names no variables.

## 2026-09-02 — T-ANALYTICS — The mock-fixture fields these screens now depend on
Decision: registered in `packages/ui/src/fixtures/screen-contract.ts` — `GET /api/account` must carry `accountId` and `domain.normalized`.
Why: the account id is who the analytics vendor is told is using the product and the normalised domain is the group every cost and usage question is asked by; they are the same two the server-side events already carry, so a click on a screen and a job on a queue land on one story rather than two. Both are already in the response schema and in the mock fixture; nothing new is asked of the backend. The shell's own view of the account (`ShellAccount`) gained them as **optional** fields, because nothing rendered needs either: an account response without them draws exactly the same screen and simply reports nothing.
The one requirement on whoever fills this in for real: `domain.normalized` must be the *claimed* domain and never a host a visitor typed into the preview, or a stranger's browsing would be attributed to a real store — the same rule the server-side attribution already enforces by having no field for it.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 ("everything is groupable by domain"; preview traffic is a property, not a group).
## 2026-09-02 — T3.4 — A store's own middle page is the yardstick, measured across pages rather than across searches
Decision: "the store median" that three of the four Search-Console signals compare against is the middle value of **what each page was shown for in the window, totalled across every search**, counted only over pages the store's own inventory holds and that Google showed at least once. Decay uses the same shape over clicks in its earlier window. A page's own total is what gets compared against that middle, not the slice of it belonging to the one search the signal is about.
Why: the specs use "store median" four times and never say the median of what. Two readings were available and they behave differently. Across page-and-search pairs it would be a much smaller number, so nearly every page would clear it and the floor would stop doing its job. Across pages is what §9.6.2 means where it does define the term — "median clicks/impressions across its rated **articles**" — and what §9.6.5 means by "impressions ≥ store median" for a refresh candidate, and it is a sentence a merchant could be told: half your pages are shown more often than this one. Comparing a page's total against a median of page totals also keeps both sides of the comparison the same kind of number, which the mixed reading would not. Pages the inventory does not hold are excluded from the middle rather than counted, because a page we cannot act on should not move the bar for the pages we can — and they are counted and returned instead of dropped, so a mismatch between the URL Google reports and the one the store gave us surfaces as a number rather than as signals quietly going missing.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (striking distance, low CTR, decay rows), §9.6.2, §9.6.5 — none defines the population.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.4 — One piece of work per page, not one per phrasing
Decision: striking distance and low CTR produce at most one detection per page. Where several search intents on the same page clear the same test, the page keeps the intent it is shown for most often, and the number of others travels with it as evidence (`other_qualifying_clusters`).
Why: two forces point the same way. A collection can sit just off page one for a dozen wordings of one thing, and a merchant's list showing that as twelve items would bury everything else — it is one job, improve the collection. And the database only allows one: the open-opportunity index is unique on account, signal type and entity, so two detections on the same page could not both survive; whichever was written last would silently replace the other, in whatever order the rows happened to come out. Ties break on the head search's text so the same data always produces the same card. The cost is a fact rather than a guess: if a page is off page one for two genuinely different intents wanting different work, only the busier one is surfaced, and the count on the card says the other exists.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 ("Page × query-cluster"), §7.9 (dedupe on `(account_id, signal_type, entity_ref)`), main §13 `opportunities`.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.4 — Nothing stores whether a page was decaying last week, so the detector is handed that and reports what has not been confirmed yet
Decision: decay's "the decline must hold across two consecutive weekly runs" is implemented as an input — how many earlier consecutive weekly runs already found this page decaying — and the detector returns two lists: confirmed decays, and **provisional** ones seen for the first time. Provisional decays are not opportunities and the merchant never sees them.
Why: the rule is about history across runs and this card builds pure functions, so the count has to come from somewhere the card does not own. Nothing holds it today. Two homes are possible and both belong to `T3.7`, the card that runs the weekly scan: the count can live in the evidence of the open decay row, which needs no new column but means writing a row before the decline is confirmed; or it needs a small table of weekly observations, which is a migration and therefore a schema-wave card. **Neither is decided here.** What is decided is that the detector never guesses: with no history handed in, a decaying page comes back provisional and firing is impossible, which is the safe direction — a single bad month around a holiday, an outage or one of Google's experiments cannot reach the merchant. The consequence to accept: until `T3.7` supplies the history, content decay produces nothing at all.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (decay row — "trend must hold across ≥ 2 consecutive weekly evaluations"), §7.5 (weekly scan), main §13 — no table holds this.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.4 — Cannibalization cannot validate today, because nothing writes what job a page is doing
Decision: the same-intent half of cannibalization's validation reads `store_pages.intent_class`. Where any competing page's intent class is unknown — which is every page in every store right now — the candidate is returned **unvalidated with the reason**, never as a finding and never dropped. Same for two pages whose intent classes differ.
Why: main §7.3 makes this validation mandatory ("Not treated as an error until validated: same intent class for both URLs *and* …"), and guessing it is the one thing this signal must not do: the work it leads to is consolidating pages, and consolidating a buying guide into a how-to because we assumed they were the same thing destroys something that was working. So unknown fails closed. **The consequence is that this P0 signal produces nothing on any real store until something populates that column.** `store_pages.intent_class` exists in the schema from wave 2 and no code writes it — `T3.2` built the inventory and left the column null by design, and no card in the plan claims it. `query_clusters.intent_class` is equally empty. Whoever picks this up should note that the intent class wanted here is the *page's*, not the search's, and the natural producers are the inventory sync (Lane C) or the persona/families work (Lane B). Flagged rather than fixed: classifying pages is a feature nobody asked this card for.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (cannibalization row), §13 `store_pages.intent_class`; build plan T3.4 — requires the validation, names no producer for the input.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.4 — Worked example 5's fixture states the alternation and does not contain it; the test supplies it and the shared fixture is left alone
Decision: the acceptance fixture for example 5 spreads each URL's 28-day totals evenly across every day, so the leading URL never changes and the "alternate" the example describes is not in the data. The cannibalization test re-arranges the *same totals* into weeks so the leader does change, and asserts the re-arrangement against the fixture's own numbers before detecting anything. `packages/core/src/fixtures/scenarios.ts` is not changed.
Why: the fixture is a shared file no lane owns, written in M0 and read by other lanes' tests; changing the rows under three lanes mid-build to fix a fixture is a worse trade than carrying the re-arrangement in one test with the reason written down. The evidence the detector reports is still the example's — 3,000 / 2,400 / 1,800 impressions at positions 8.2 / 9.6 / 12.4 — because only the split across weeks moved. **For the integrator:** the fixture would be better with weekly variation in it, and adding it is a small change to `window28`; it belongs to whoever next owns that file, not to a lane building on top of it.
Nearest spec: main §7.8 example 5; build plan T3.4 done-when ("fixtures for examples 1/2/5/6 produce exactly the expected signals").
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.4 — A page Search Console reports that the inventory does not hold produces nothing, and is counted
Decision: every detector filters to pages present in `store_pages`, and returns the addresses it had to skip. Trailing slashes and `#fragments` are normalised away before matching; query strings are not.
Why: an opportunity is a piece of work on a page, and a page we hold nothing about is one nobody can do the work on — the recommendation stages need its title, its body and its headings. But silently dropping is how the two spellings of a URL (what Google shows in a results page, what the storefront calls it) turn into a signal that stops being found with nothing to notice. Counting and returning them makes the mismatch a number the scan can log. Query strings are deliberately kept distinct because on a storefront they usually *are* a different page — a filtered collection, page two of a listing — and merging them would pool pages that rank separately.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 ("applies to any store URL"), §12.3 (the inventory), §7.7 (content mapping).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.4 — Five more numbers into `packages/rules`, because §7.3 states them in words and the config had no key for them
Decision: `signals.config.yaml` gains five values, all marked UNSIGNED. Striking distance gets `impressions_store_median_multiple_min: 1` and content decay gets `baseline_clicks_store_median_multiple_min: 1` — the "impressions ≥ store median" and "baseline clicks ≥ store median" halves of their rules, which existed as English and as nothing else. Cannibalization gets the three numbers its validation half needs: `leader_changes_min: 1` (how many times the page Google leads with has to change across the window's weeks before that counts as the pages alternating), `baseline_offset_weeks: 12` (how far back the "before" comparison reaches) and `aggregate_loss_clicks_ratio_max: 1.0` (this window's clicks as a fraction of that earlier window, below which the search counts as having lost ground).
Why: invariant 9 says no threshold literal lives outside `packages/rules`, and a number the spec expresses as "≥ store median" is still a number — written in code as a bare comparison it would be a `1` nobody could find or change. Two of the five are the spec's own values restated (the median multiples, and the twelve weeks decay already uses). The other two had to be invented because the spec asks for a test and gives no size: "the leading URL flips week-over-week" does not say how many flips, and "aggregate performance loss vs. the 12-week baseline" does not say how much loss. **`aggregate_loss_clicks_ratio_max: 1.0` is deliberately a no-op bound** — at 1.0 any decline at all counts, which is the literal reading. It is set that way rather than at some invented figure so that the looseness is visible in the file and can be tightened in one edit instead of found in code. It matters less than it looks: it is one branch of an OR, behind a gate that already requires two of the store's own pages each holding a fifth of the same search within the top thirty.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (striking distance, low CTR, decay and cannibalization rows), §7.10 (every number in §7.3 lives in the config layer).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T-EMAIL (founder decision) — Email sign-in ships in v1, and it is an auth change rather than a provider toggle
Decision: magic-link email sign-in is built for the first release. It is carded as `T-EMAIL`.
Why: signing in with Google only excludes every merchant who has no Google account or will not use one to log in to a business tool, and that is a signup-funnel loss rather than a missing convenience.
**What it actually costs, which "the table exists now" hides.** Two things blocked it, not one. The table for a single-use link token was missing and now exists (`verification_tokens`, migration `0004_wave2b.sql`). The second is still true: the auth library refuses to run an email provider without a database adapter, and this app deliberately has none — sessions are self-contained tokens (`strategy: 'jwt'`), which is why a session cannot be revoked before it expires and why its lifetime is a day rather than the library's default thirty. Adding the adapter moves every session into the database. That is an **auth-wide behaviour change**: sessions become revocable (better), every authenticated request gains a database read (slower), and the session lifetime should be revisited now that expiry is no longer the only revocation. The card owns all of that, not just adding a provider.
Nearest spec: main §4.1; tech §3. Neither names a session strategy.

## 2026-09-02 — T-ANALYTICS (founder decision) — The browser reports events straight to the analytics vendor
Decision: screens send their own events to PostHog directly from the browser, using its browser library and a public key in the page. Carded as `T-ANALYTICS`, and it binds the reporting seam `T9.1` left with a do-nothing default.
Why: it is the standard arrangement and the least code, and the alternative — posting to a route of ours that forwards server-side — needs a route added to a frozen API contract.
**Two obligations this choice creates, and they are requirements of the card rather than advice.** First, **session replay must be off on every view that renders a merchant's store data**, deliberately and provably, because replay captures the page and the page contains their catalogue. Second, invariant 26 forbids product content in analytics events, and an open audit finding already records that nothing structurally stops article text reaching the vendor — routing through our own code would have closed the browser half of that; going direct does not. So the card must carry the structural guard instead: the event definitions `T9.1` wrote already declare exactly which properties each event may have, and the card must make that the only way to send one, with a test that a free-text field cannot be attached.
Alternative rejected: browser posts to our own endpoint. It keeps every decision about what leaves the browser in our code, but costs a route outside the frozen contract. The founder chose the direct route knowing the two obligations above.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 (events carry ids and aggregates only, never product content); tech §1.6.

## 2026-09-02 — T-START (founder decision) — The domain claim queues the first onboarding step itself
Decision: claiming a domain pushes the first step onto the job queue in the same transaction that writes the run. Carded as `T-START`.
Why: nothing in the running product started a merchant's onboarding. The claim wrote the nine steps and queued nothing — correct while no handler existed, and `T2.1` has now written one. A merchant who connects their store moves forward, because the connect flow resumes onboarding itself; it is only the very first step, detection immediately after the claim, that had nothing to trigger it.
Alternative rejected: relaxing the worker's rule that no recurring job runs until every one of them has a handler. That is also one line, but it would have started two further finished-and-dormant machines at the same time — the nightly billing repair and the spend caps — immediately before `T2.2`, the first card to spend real money at scale. The founder chose to fix the thing that is broken and to leave switching the scheduler on as its own change with its own evidence.
Consequence still open: the scheduler remains off, so no recurring job runs at all. That is now a separate, still-unanswered question rather than a side effect of this one.
Nearest spec: main §6.1, §14.3.1.


## 2026-09-02 — T4.4 (founder decision, taken ahead of the card) — The banned-vocabulary list is dropped; the banned-structure rules move into the language-quality judge's instructions instead of a maintained list
Decision: `content-pointers.md` §6's literal word blacklist ("delve", "unlock", "leverage", "seamless", …) is dropped as a required artefact — no per-locale version will be written, and none is needed before a locale launches. The other half of §6 — banned *shapes*, not words: a cliché opening, filler used as a transition, a summary that repeats the section above it, everything arriving in threes, uniform section lengths, a "Conclusion" heading, a hedge standing in for an answer — is kept, but as instructions written into Judge 6's ("language quality", content-spec.md §9.6) prompt rather than as a standalone list.
Why: the word blacklist was tested against Hungarian samples, so it is half in the wrong language already, and every further launch locale would need its own list hand-written by someone who reads that language — an open-ended content-ops cost, not a one-time translation. It also buys less than it looks like: the build plan never actually wired it into a check. `T4.4`'s free, pre-judge-call checks (citations, claim-evidence matching, contradiction detection, structural validity) don't include it, and the judge itself is **one blind model call scoring several criteria** (`T4.4`: "blind judge... separate call... per-criterion 1–5"), not six separate calls — so there was never a judge call being saved by catching a banned word early. An LLM asked to judge "does this read as AI-slop" already recognises these words as tells in whatever language it's judging, so a maintained list mostly duplicates capability the judge already has. The structural rules survive because they describe *patterns*, not vocabulary — "don't restate the heading as the first sentence" translates into an instruction with no per-locale authoring, unlike a word list.
Consequence: `T4.4`'s language-quality criterion prompt must contain the structural rules from `content-pointers.md` §6 (and `content-spec.md` §7.7's "banned structure" / "banned empty claims" rows); it does not need a word list, in any language, launch or otherwise. `content-pointers.md` §6 should be edited to reflect this the next time that file is touched.
Nearest spec: `content-pointers.md` §6, `content-spec.md` §7.7, §9.6 — none of these are canonical (main/ui/tech) specs; `content-spec.md` was already flagged (docs/overnight-state.md) as written against a different codebase and not to be planned from directly.

## 2026-09-02 — integrator (founder-directed) — The change contract can now say a blog post or a static page was edited or deleted
Decision: `CatalogEvent.kind` gains four values — `article_updated`, `article_deleted`, `page_updated`, `page_deleted` — and the inventory's fan-out maps them: edits queue a re-read of that one page, deletions take it off the re-read list the same way a deleted product already does. The delete branch stopped being a special case for products and became a lookup like the re-read branch, so a fifth kind is one line in one table rather than a second `if`.
Why: `T3.2` found that all six existing kinds were about products and collections, so a merchant editing a blog post was noticed by the nightly walk within a day rather than within minutes. The cost of leaving it was only staler blog data, never wrong data — but `T2.2` is the card that fills this contract and it starts next, and adding a kind after that means changing a seam two lanes already consume. Founder directed it be done now rather than later, for that reason.
Evidence: two tests, and both were proved by breaking the mapping — disabling the blog-post case fails the first with the article missing from the re-read list. The second pins the ordering case that matters: an edit followed by a deletion must not queue a re-read, because reading a page that no longer exists spends a request to learn nothing.
Consequence for `T2.2`: it now has four more kinds to emit, from Shopify's `articles/*` and `pages/*` webhooks. Emitting none of them leaves today's behaviour exactly as it is, so this is additive and cannot break that card.
Note on scope: this is a frozen M0 contract, and build-plan §9 rule 4 says contracts change only through the integrator. Taken there rather than in a lane for that reason.
Nearest spec: main §12.3 (content inventory), §14.1 (change detection). Neither enumerates the kinds.


## 2026-09-02 — T-BOOT (founder decision) — The threshold config is read on first use, not when the module loads, and a lint rule keeps it that way
Decision: the loader for `signals.config.yaml` stops working out its own file location at module load. It resolves and reads on the first call that actually needs a number, and caches from then on. A lint rule forbids the module-load form returning, and the gate gains a step that starts the built application and asks it for a page.
Why: **every request the product served answered 500**, including the landing page and the health check, and had done for some time. The thresholds that decide the product's behaviour live in a YAML file on purpose, so they can change without a deploy. The code finding that file computed its path from its own module address at the top level of the module; once the web app is bundled for production a module address is not a place on disk, so the calculation threw. It threw inside the start-up hook, and a failed start-up hook makes Next serve nothing at all. The build compiled and 1,357 tests passed throughout, because tests run in Node where module addresses really are file paths.
Alternatives and why not: **bundling the file in** would have made it reachable, but tuning would then need a deploy, which is the thing keeping it a separate file was for. **Marking the package external to the server bundle** keeps deploy-free tuning, but it is a build setting that has to stay correct and gives no sign when it rots. First-use loading keeps both properties and is the smallest change; its one weakness is that it is a discipline, so the lint rule is part of the decision rather than an extra.
Consequence to accept knowingly: a genuinely missing or malformed config file is now discovered on the first call that needs a threshold rather than at start-up. The card is required to make that failure loud and to say plainly which file is wrong.
History: this is the **third** time this defect has been worked around instead of repaired. `T1.3` wrote up the identical problem for the prompt loader; `T3.1` dodged it by splitting a queue call into its own module. Found independently by `T9.2` and `T-OPS`, confirmed a third time by the integrator starting the built app at `894c1dc`.
Nearest spec: main §7.10 (thresholds live in `packages/rules`, stamped by `rules_version`); tech §2, §6 (architecture and CI). Neither says when the file is read.


## 2026-09-02 — T-BOOT — The new gate step asks for two pages, and requires the health check to be green as well as answering
Decision: `pnpm smoke:boot` (`scripts/smoke-boot.mjs`) starts the application `pnpm build` just produced, the way the platform starts it, and requires **200 from both `/` and `/api/health`**. It runs last in CI, after `Build`. It does not build anything itself.
Why these two: `/` is the public funnel — the page a stranger lands on — and `/api/health` is what the platform polls to decide whether to keep the container. A server whose start-up hook throws answers 500 to both, which is the exact failure that shipped, so either one alone would have caught it; asking for both costs nothing and covers the case where only one is broken.
Two judgement calls worth knowing about:
- **Requiring the health check to be *green*, not merely to answer.** It answers 200 only when the database responds and the background worker is running, so this step now also fails if a build cannot reach its database. That is a wider net than "does the server boot" and it can fail for a reason that is not the code's fault — a CI database that did not come up. The failure message names which probe failed, so that is one line to read rather than an investigation. Judged worth it: a container that cannot reach its database is not serving the product either.
- **A throwaway encryption key per run.** The start-up hook refuses to run without a key for encrypting merchant tokens, and the repository ships none — correctly. The check stores no token, so it makes one per run and throws it away, exactly as the browser-flow harness already does.
Cost: **under a second** on top of the build (0.8 s wall clock locally, of which the server needs 0.6 s to start and answer). It waits up to 90 s for a server that is slow to answer and 30 s more for the health check to go green, so a *failing* run costs at most two minutes.
Proved by breaking it: the pre-card loader was put back, the application rebuilt, and this step failed with `GET / -> 500`, `GET /api/health -> 500`, printing the server's own "Failed to prepare server … Invalid URL" underneath. Restored and re-run: both 200.
Nearest spec: tech §6 (the testing strategy table has no row for "the application starts"), tech §2.1 (the health check and the restart policy this exercises).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T-BOOT — What the new lint rule covers, and the three judgement calls inside it
Decision: `sortiva/no-module-load-path-resolution` reports a module that asks where it sits on disk while it is loading. Three calls the card did not make for me:
1. **It covers `import.meta.dirname` and `import.meta.filename`, not only `import.meta.url`.** Node 20.11 added the two shortcuts; they are the same question asked in fewer words and fail identically. Banning one spelling and not the others would be a rule that reads as protection and is none.
2. **A function called where it is written is still reported.** `const X = (() => …)()` runs while the module loads; the function is scenery. Without this the rule is one pair of brackets away from useless.
3. **It is on for `packages/rules` and `packages/llm` only, and off in test files.** Those two are the packages that read a file off disk to do their job and are reached from the server's start-up hook, which is the whole failure path. A test is never bundled and never imported by the running server, and locating a fixture relative to itself is a normal thing for one to do — the repository has such a test today.
Why not repository-wide: it would fire on the lint-proof harness and the eval suite, both of which are correct as they are, and the exemptions needed to quieten it would be longer than the rule. **On the day a third package starts reading a file off disk it belongs on this list**, and the list is two lines in `eslint.config.mjs`.
Proved by breaking it: the pre-card version of `packages/rules/src/load.ts` was put back and `pnpm lint` rejected it; the repaired form in the same file passes; the planted case in `pnpm lint:prove` fails the gate if the rule ever stops firing.
Nearest spec: main §7.10 (the config layer already has a lint rule guarding it, for the numbers rather than the file); CLAUDE.md code-structure rules.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T-BOOT — Deferring the read was not enough on its own: the path also had to stop being written in the one form the bundler rewrites
Decision: `packages/rules/src/load.ts` now does both halves. It works out where the configuration file sits **inside a function called by the first caller that needs a threshold** (the founder decision), and it *composes* that location — `join(dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url)), '..')` — instead of writing `new URL('signals.config.yaml', new URL('../', import.meta.url))`.
Why: the second half is not optional, and the founder decision does not name it because it is a mechanical detail. The bundler rewrites `new URL('<something written out literally>', import.meta.url)` **while it is building**, not while it is running: it treats the target as a file it must publish and replaces the expression with a web address that has no disk behind it. Moving that expression inside a function changes only *when* the broken value is used, not that it is broken — the server would have started and then every background job would have failed the first time it asked for a number. Composing the same path out of pieces is invisible to the bundler and resolves identically at runtime. This is the same repair `R4` already made to the prompt loader for the same reason (2026-09-01 R4), and it is why that one has worked since.
Proved rather than assumed: a throwaway route that reads a threshold was added to the built application, served, and answered with the live `rules_version` and a real threshold value — so the file is genuinely found from inside the production bundle, not merely found by a test running from the repository tree. The route was deleted afterwards and is not in the branch.
Nearest spec: main §7.10 — the configuration file and `rules_version`; silent on bundling.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T-BOOT — `CONFIG_PATH` is now `configPath()`, a function
Decision: `@sortiva/rules` exports `configPath()` in place of the `CONFIG_PATH` constant. Nothing outside the package's own tests used it; those were repointed.
Why: a constant is a value computed when the module loads, which is exactly the thing this card exists to remove — leaving it in place would leave the defect in the package's public surface for the next caller to reintroduce, and the new lint rule would have to be switched off for the line that defines it. A function also tells the reader the truth: the path is worked out when asked for.
For other lanes: if you need the file's location, call `configPath()`. If you need a number, call `rules()` and never touch the path at all.
Nearest spec: none — package surface, CLAUDE.md code-structure rules.

## 2026-09-02 — T-BOOT — The prompt loader is read on first use too, and the lint rule covers both packages
Decision: `packages/llm/src/prompts.ts` works out where the prompt files live inside `loadPrompt` rather than at the top of the module, matching `packages/rules`.
Why: part 2 of the card puts both packages under a rule that forbids the module-load form, so the existing constant would have failed the new rule — and it has the identical defect, which `T1.3` wrote up in January and `R4` half-repaired (it fixed the *shape* of the path, not *when* it is computed). Both halves now match.
Not done here, and deliberately: `apps/web/app/api/preview/_lib/config.ts` still reads its prompt file by hand, which `T1.3` said should be deleted "the moment `loadPrompt` is bundler-safe". That moment has arrived, but the preview endpoint belongs to another lane and deleting its reader is a behaviour change in a card that has none. **Proposal for the integrator or the preview lane:** that local reader can now go, and the endpoint can call `loadPrompt` like everything else.
Nearest spec: main §14.2 — prompts are versioned files loaded by version; silent on when the file is located.

## 2026-09-02 — T-BOOT — Contradiction with tech §2, surfaced rather than resolved: the config is no longer "schema-validated at worker start"
Decision: proceeding as the founder decided; recording that it departs from a written line in the tech spec.
What the spec says: tech §2 and main §7.10 both say `signals.config.yaml` is "loaded at worker start" and "schema-validated at worker start". It now is not — it is loaded and validated by the first piece of work that needs a number.
Why it is accepted: loading it at start-up is precisely what stopped the server starting, and the founder decision names the trade knowingly ("a genuinely missing or malformed config file is now discovered on the first call that needs a threshold"). The card's answer to the cost is the loud, file-naming error, which is built and proved.
What it means in practice, plainly: a deployment carrying a broken configuration file will now start, serve pages and answer its health check, and fail only when a background job or a screen actually needs a threshold. Before, it would have refused to start at all — which sounds safer and was not, because "refuses to start" is also what a healthy deployment looked like all week. **For the integrator:** if the spec should be amended rather than departed from, this is the entry to promote; if start-up validation is wanted back, it is a deliberate call to `rules()` in the start-up hook, which is a founder decision and not this card's to take.
Nearest spec: tech §2 (rules/config layer), main §7.10.

## 2026-09-02 — integrator (merging T3.2 and T9.2) — The mock API server is no longer part of `@sortiva/ui`'s public surface
Decision: `packages/ui/src/index.ts` no longer re-exports `./msw`. The fixtures and request handlers are reached at `@sortiva/ui/msw` (a new entry in the package's exports map) by the five files that want them — all of them tests or the end-to-end fixture server. One import in `apps/web/app/(app)/_lib/shell-state.test.ts` was repointed; the other four already used direct paths.
Why: neither card broke the build alone, and together they did. `T3.2` added a page-checksum helper that uses `node:crypto`, exported through the `@sortiva/core` barrel; `T9.2` added a browser component that imports `@sortiva/ui`, whose barrel re-exported the mock fixtures, which import that core barrel. A Node-only module therefore ended up in a browser bundle and `next build` refused it. Dropping the re-export is the smallest fix that is also the right one: a mock server exists to serve tests and the fixture site, and nothing in a production browser bundle should be able to reach it by accident. Taken by the integrator at merge because `main` did not compile; both packages' barrels are shared files no lane owns.
Consequence to know: any future code wanting `RESPONSE_FIXTURES` or `apiHandlers` must import `@sortiva/ui/msw` explicitly. That is the intent — the import now says what it is.
Nearest spec: none; this is build-plan §3's "files no lane owns" and the monorepo layout in CLAUDE.md's code-structure rules.

## 2026-09-02 — T3.3 — Thirteen numbers nobody has signed off, all in `packages/rules`, all marked UNSIGNED
Decision: two new sections in `signals.config.yaml`. `clusters` — how much history a cluster is built from (28 days), how many times a search must have been shown before it is worth pooling (10), how many words a head search needs before others may join it (2), how often a narrower search must be shown before it becomes an intent of its own rather than folding into the broader one (250), and ceilings on members per cluster (200) and clusters per store (500). `ctr_curve` — how much history the fit uses (90 days), how far down the results it describes (position 20), how many impressions and how many distinct positions are needed before the store's own data is fitted at all (1,000 and 5), a floor and ceiling the fitted curve is clamped into (0.05% and 60%), and a twenty-row fallback table of click rate by position.
Why: invariant 9 says every number that decides something lives here, and all twelve do. Two examples of what each decides: raise the impressions floor and small markets stop producing clusters at all, so their opportunities go undetected; drop the fit's minimum sample and a store with almost no history gets a curve fitted from noise, which is worse than a sensible default because it *looks* specific to them. Only one of the thirteen is stated by the specs — the 28-day window, which every Search Console signal already uses. The rest are starting heuristics and are marked UNSIGNED in the file, alongside the ones `T0.2` already flagged. **The fallback click curve in particular is a founder-facing number set**: it is what every store with too little history of its own is judged against, and its shape decides how often the low-click signal fires for a new store.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 ("fallback: standard curve", never given), §9.6.3 (head query plus expansion, no grouping rule), §13 `query_clusters`/`ctr_curve` (columns, no bounds), §7.10.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.3 — A search joins an intent by containing every one of its meaningful words, and the specs never say how
Decision: two searches are the same intent when one contains all of the other's meaningful words — word order ignored, filler words ("best", "for", "the") removed. A search carrying exactly the head's words plus filler always folds in. A search adding a real word ("electric garden shears" under "garden shears") folds in only when the store is shown for it rarely; shown for it often, it becomes an intent of its own.
Why: the spec asks for "a head query plus its related-keyword expansion" and never says what makes two searches related. Three ways were available. Meaning-similarity through an embedding model costs a paid call per search and puts a model in the middle of arithmetic that has to be explainable to a merchant. Comparing whose pages Google actually returns for each search is the most accurate and costs a paid lookup per search, which for a store with thousands of searches is a bill nobody has approved. Word containment is free, deterministic, and — the reason it was chosen — fails in the safe direction: it can leave a variant in a cluster of its own, which costs us a signal, but it can never merge two unrelated searches, which would have us telling a merchant to consolidate two pages that should stay apart. **Two known limits.** The filler-word list is English, so a Danish or Hungarian store's searches pool slightly less tightly — costing recall, never correctness. And "shoes" contains "running shoes" without being the same intent, which is what the third rule above exists to separate; the number behind it is UNSIGNED.
Nearest spec: main §9.6.3 — "the confirmed keyword each topic descended from, plus its DataForSEO related-keyword expansion"; main §13 `query_clusters`.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.3 — The related expansion comes from the store's own search history, because the frozen vendor interface cannot supply one
Decision: a cluster's members are the near-variants Search Console reports the store being shown for, not a vendor's related-keyword list. Where the merchant confirmed a search term during onboarding, that term is preferred as the head, so the name on screen is the one they chose.
Why: main §9.6.3 says the expansion is DataForSEO's related keywords. `SeoDataProvider`, frozen in M0, has three methods — keyword metrics, SERP results, and a competitor's ranked keywords — and none of them is related keywords. Widening a frozen seam is the integrator's call, not a feature card's, and the alternative reading is both free and better suited to what reads these clusters: two of the four Search-Console signals are about *impression share within a cluster*, and only searches the store was actually shown for have impressions at all. A vendor's related list would contribute members with no impressions, which would dilute every share without changing any of them. **For the integrator:** if `SeoDataProvider` ever gains a related-keywords call, adding those terms as extra members is additive — the cluster row already holds a member list and nothing about the shape changes.
Nearest spec: main §9.6.3; build plan §4 — `SeoDataProvider` frozen in T0.5 without the method §9.6.3 assumes.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.3 — The click curve is a power law fitted by weighted least squares, and a curve that rises is refused
Decision: the store's own click rate by position is fitted as `rate = a × position^(−b)`, solved by least squares on the logs, with each position weighted by how often the store was actually shown there. Three conditions send it to the fallback table instead: too few impressions in the window, too few distinct positions carrying clicks, or a fit whose curve rises down the page.
Why: main §7.3 requires "the CTR predicted by the store's own fitted position-CTR curve" and never says what is fitted. A straight line is the obvious alternative and is wrong here: click rate collapses across the first few positions and then flattens, and a line through that predicts a negative click rate somewhere on the first page. Weighting by impressions is what removes the need for a separate "this position is too thin to trust" threshold — a position seen twice contributes twice, not equally. The third fallback condition is not in the spec and was added because the arithmetic permits it: a store with noisy data can fit a curve saying clicks get *more* likely further down, and publishing that would make the low-click signal fire on nearly every page. Two clamps hold the fitted curve inside a sane range for the same reason.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (Low CTR row), §13 `ctr_curve`.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.3 — A store with no Search Console connection gets no curve row at all, rather than one holding the fallback
Decision: the weekly refit writes nothing for an account with no connection or no property chosen. A connected store with too little history *does* get a row, holding the fallback table, with `sample_n` recording how little there was.
Why: `ctr_curve` has no column saying whether the numbers in it were measured or defaulted — `sample_n` is the closest, and reading "the sample was small" as "this is not a measurement" is an inference the reader has to make correctly every time. For a connected store that inference is available and correct. For an unconnected one there is no sample to reason about at all, and a stored fallback would be indistinguishable from a measurement of a store we have never measured. An absent row is unambiguous, and every reader already has to handle a store that has never been fitted.
Nearest spec: main §7.11 (signals that need Search Console are not evaluated without it), §13 `ctr_curve` — silent on the unconnected case.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.3 — A cluster keeps its identity across every rebuild, and a cluster the store stopped being shown for keeps its row
Decision: clusters are written by matching on the head search. An existing head has its members updated in place, keeping its `cluster_id`; a new head is inserted; a head that no longer appears is left alone. Nothing is ever deleted.
Why: `topics.keyword_cluster` (schema wave 3) points at a cluster id, so an article's lineage — which search it descended from — hangs off that id surviving. Rebuilding weekly by delete-then-insert would hand every published article a dangling reference, and the failure would surface a year later as an unanswerable "where did this come from". Keeping vanished heads follows the same posture main §7.9 takes for opportunities, where expiry never deletes. The cost accepted: the table grows with the store's search history and nothing prunes it. Bounded in practice by the ceiling on clusters per store and by real stores having a finite search surface; if it ever needs bounding properly, that is an age-based sweep and a schema-wave conversation, not this card.
Nearest spec: main §13 `query_clusters` (no unique index on the head, so no insert-with-conflict is available), §7.9.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.3 — The page-by-cluster share table is computed on demand, not stored
Decision: how much of each intent each of the store's pages holds is produced by a function over the search rows and the clusters, and written nowhere.
Why: the card calls these "share tables" and main §13 has no table for them — it lists `query_clusters` and `ctr_curve` and nothing else in this area. Adding one would be a migration, which only a schema-wave card may add. It is also the right answer independently: the shares are a pure restatement of `gsc_query_daily` plus the clusters, both of which are stored, so a stored copy is a second source of truth that goes stale the moment either moves. The cost is that every detector that wants shares recomputes them; they run weekly, over one window, per store.
Nearest spec: build plan T3.3 ("page-cluster share tables used by detection"); main §13 — no such table.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.3 — The store's own brand words are taken from its address and its Shopify name, because nothing holds a brand name
Decision: the words that mean "somebody searched for this store by name" are derived from the claimed domain (split on hyphens, suffix dropped, and the joined form kept) and the Shopify shop handle. Words that are nobody's brand ("shop", "store", "online") and words shorter than three letters are refused. When nothing usable comes out, no searches are excluded and `ctr_curve.branded_excluded` is written false.
Why: main §7.3 says branded searches are excluded "where the brand token is detectable from the persona". The `personas` table holds a description, categories, language, country, audience and tone — no name. The two fields that *are* a merchant's own choice of name are the address they claimed and the name Shopify knows them by, and in practice both are the brand far more often than not. Guessing wrongly is the failure that matters: a brand word that matches ordinary searches would silently delete real evidence from the fit, which is why short and generic words are refused and why matching is on whole words rather than substrings. Recording that nothing was excluded is better than pretending; the column exists for exactly that.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (Low CTR row), §6.5 `personas`, §13 `ctr_curve.branded_excluded`.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.3 — Rebuilding a store's intents is deliberately left off the schedule, for the signal scan to call
Decision: the weekly click-curve refit is registered as the scheduled job the crontab has named since M0 (`ctr_curve_refit_weekly`). Rebuilding clusters is exported and scheduled by nothing.
Why: clusters have to be current at the moment detection reads them, and detection is the weekly signal scan — `T3.7`, another card in this lane. The crontab runs that scan an hour before the curve refit, so a cluster rebuild bolted onto the refit would have every scan working from clusters a week old. The scan calling `rebuildQueryClustersForAccount` as its first step is both correct and one line. Same shape as the decision `T3.2` took when it left the nightly inventory walk for the sweep that owns it.
Nearest spec: main §7.5 (weekly scan cadence), §9.6.1; `packages/jobs/src/runtime/crontab.ts`.

## 2026-09-02 — T3.3 — The merchant's confirmed search terms are read from the search repository, because the keywords one does not exist
Decision: `confirmedKeywordTerms` — one column, no writes — sits in `packages/db/src/repositories/search.ts` rather than in a `keywords.ts` of its own.
Why: no keywords repository exists; the `keywords` table is written by the store-intelligence lane's `T2.6`, which is not built. Creating `packages/db/src/repositories/keywords.ts` here would be creating exactly the file that lane will create, and two lanes creating the same new file is a merge conflict rather than a merge. Parking it in this lane's own file costs nothing and moving it later is mechanical. **For whoever writes `T2.6`:** move it, or leave it; it reads `keywords.term where confirmed`.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md code-structure rules (repositories in `packages/db`); build plan §3 — divides directories, not repository files.
