## 2026-09-02 — T3.2 — The frozen change stream cannot report a blog post being edited, so blog posts refresh nightly only
Decision: the inventory reacts to catalogue changes through `CatalogEvents` exactly as frozen. That contract reports six kinds of change, all about products and collections, and none about pages, blogs or blog posts. So a merchant editing a blog post has that edit picked up by the nightly walk — within a day — rather than within minutes.
Why: T3.2's card says the sync runs "on `collections/update` / `articles/*` events via `CatalogEvents`", and `articles/*` has nowhere to arrive. Widening the contract is not this card's to do — the build plan says a frozen seam changes only when the integrator re-freezes it — and the producer (`T2.2`) is not built, so there is nothing to widen it against yet. The consequence is bounded and one-directional: staler blog data, never wrong blog data. **The integrator or Lane B should decide whether `CatalogEvent.kind` gains `article_updated` / `page_updated` before `T2.2` is written**, since adding them afterwards means changing a contract two lanes already consume.
Nearest spec: main §12.3 — names the `articles/*` webhooks as a sync trigger; build plan §4 — freezes `CatalogEvents` without them.

## 2026-09-02 — T3.2 — Our place in the change stream lives in the job's own payload, not in a table
Decision: the cursor into `CatalogEvents` is carried in the payload of the draining job, which re-queues itself with the new cursor whenever a pass found anything.
Why: the stream's own interface hands the cursor to the consumer and expects it back, so somewhere has to hold it. A column would be a migration outside a schema wave, which the build plan forbids a feature card. The queue already stores a payload per job durably, and one job per store at a time is enforced by its job key, so a burst of forty changes is one pass rather than forty. The cost accepted: if that job row is ever lost, our place is lost with it, and the next pass re-reads the stream from the beginning — which costs one extra pass and re-queues re-reads that the checksum diff will then find unchanged and discard. No wrong data, only wasted work.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.4 — requires resumable steps; silent on where a consumer's stream position lives.

## 2026-09-02 — T3.2 — Blog posts and static pages get no product families, and the inventory says so rather than guessing
Decision: a collection's families are the families of the products in it, and a product's family is its own. A store page and a blog post get an empty family list.
Why: main §12.3 asks for blog posts to be mapped "via the same topic-mapping used for articles". That mapping is part of the topic model, which is Lane D's `T4.1` and does not exist. The alternatives were to invent a second mapping here — a text match against family names — which would be a different answer from the real one and would have to be unpicked later, or to leave the column empty and let `T4.1` backfill it. Empty is honest: nothing downstream can mistake "no family recorded" for "belongs to no family" more badly than it could mistake a wrong family for a right one.
Nearest spec: main §12.3 — names the mapping, points at a mechanism that is not built.

## 2026-09-02 — T3.2 — A page the store has deleted keeps its inventory row; the deletion is reported, not acted on
Decision: the fan-out from the change stream returns deletions separately from re-reads, and the sync does nothing with them. No row is deleted, and nothing marks a row as gone.
Why: three things read this inventory and none of them has been built, so what a deleted page should do is genuinely open. Deleting the row loses the only record that the address ever existed, which is what a 404 signal and a redirect recommendation would both need. Marking it gone needs a column, and adding one is a schema wave. Doing neither leaves one stale row per deleted page, which the existing-target check could act on — it would propose improving a page that is gone. That is the cost, and it is bounded and visible. **This needs the founder or the integrator to settle before the existing-target check ships in `T3.5`.**
Nearest spec: main §12.3 — specifies the sync and the checksum, silent on removal.

## 2026-09-02 — T3.2 — The two search fields are read one request per page, through Shopify's older interface
Decision: a page's search title and description are fetched as `global.title_tag` / `global.description_tag` metafields, one request per page, using the same REST Admin client every other Shopify read in the product already goes through.
Why: Shopify's REST interface does not return those two fields inline; its GraphQL interface returns them with 250 pages in one request, which is roughly fifty times cheaper on request budget. But the product has exactly one Shopify client, and it speaks REST. A second client would be a second set of retry, rate-limit and dead-token behaviours, and only one of the two would get fixed the next time Shopify changes something. The cost is paid in walk length, not in money — Shopify's request allowance is per second, not metered — and the walk is already batched and resumable for other reasons. **Worth revisiting as one piece of work when the Shopify client gains a GraphQL path**, not as a second client bolted on here.
Nearest spec: main §12.3 — names the fields as available through the Admin API, silent on which interface.

## 2026-09-02 — T3.2 — A store's inventory addresses are built from the address the store says it serves on, not the domain the merchant claimed
Decision: the storefront origin is read from the store's own record (`shop.domain`, falling back to its `myshopify.com` address), not from the claimed domain in `domains`.
Why: these disagree more often than they look like they should — a store on `example.com` whose canonical storefront is `shop.example.com`, or one still on its `myshopify.com` address. Every inventory row's address exists to be joined against the addresses Search Console reports, and Search Console reports the canonical one. An inventory spelled the other way would silently match nothing, and the failure would look like "this store has no search data" rather than like a bug.
Nearest spec: main §12.3 — "one row per URL", silent on which spelling; main §2 — claims at eTLD+1, which is a different question.

## 2026-09-02 — T3.2 — The walk through a store is staged and resumed by "everything after this id"
Decision: the store is read in a fixed order — hand-picked collections, rule-based collections, products, pages, then blog posts — a hundred pages per job run, resuming from the last id seen in the current stage. The resume marker is an opaque bag of strings that only the Shopify reader understands, passed through the job payload.
Why: collections come first because they carry product membership, which is what gives every later row its families; Shopify keeps hand-picked and rule-based collections in two lists with no combined endpoint, so both are walked. Paging by last-id-seen rather than by Shopify's newer cursor because the cursor arrives in a response header the shared client does not surface, and because last-id-seen restarts from any point — which is what makes a killed run resumable at all. Keeping the marker opaque means a second storefront (WooCommerce, say) can number its pages however it likes without changing the domain code.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.4 — requires a checkpoint on any step over 60 seconds; silent on its shape.

## 2026-09-02 — T3.2 — A price change or a stock change never re-reads a page
Decision: of the six kinds of change the catalogue stream reports, only product-created, product-updated and collection-updated cause a re-read. Price changes and availability changes are dropped.
Why: neither can move a page's title, its search fields, its headings or its links, so the checksum would come back identical and the re-read would have bought nothing. They are also the two most frequent events a busy store emits, so dropping them is most of the saving. The risk accepted: if the emitter ever reports a body edit *as* a price change, we would miss it — the nightly walk catches it within a day.
Nearest spec: main §12.3 — names the webhooks, silent on which changes matter to the inventory.

## 2026-09-02 — T3.2 — The batch size and the membership sample are job-tuning numbers, deliberately not in `packages/rules`
Decision: "a hundred pages per run" and "read at most 250 of a collection's products" live as named constants in the job that uses them, not in `signals.config.yaml`.
Why: invariant 9 puts thresholds in `packages/rules` — the numbers from main §7.3/§7.6/§8.2/§9.6 that decide what counts as an opportunity. These two decide nothing a merchant ever sees: raising the batch size makes a large store finish in fewer nights and each run longer, and a collection with more than 250 products already tells us everything about which families it covers. Putting operational tuning in the calibrated-thresholds file would dilute the thing that file is for, which is a reviewable record of the judgements the product makes.
Nearest spec: main §7.10 — scopes the config layer to the scoring and detection numbers.
## 2026-09-02 — T9.2 — The landing page's words were checked against the design canvas; nineteen of them are not in it
Decision: an earlier session added 113 lines of copy claiming the marketing text was "transcribed from the design canvas". It was checked sentence by sentence against `docs/design/sortiva-ui-mockups.html`, and the claim holds for the marketing copy: 89 of the 108 sentences appear in the canvas character for character, including every landing-page section, all eight question-and-answer pairs, the three quality gates and the pricing aside. Those were kept. The other nineteen were authored, and are listed here so a review has a list rather than a diff.
Six are labels a mockup cannot show, because they are read aloud rather than displayed: the two navigation region names, the address field's label, the footer's region name, the billing-period group and the step row. One is a template where the canvas showed one example (`Here's what we understood about {domain}`). One is the rate-limit line, which comes from the UI spec verbatim rather than from the canvas. **Eleven are genuinely new**: the whole sign-in screen, which the canvas does not contain at all (its own screen list has no sign-in screen) — "Sign in to Sortiva", "One account per store. We'll ask for your store's address next.", "Continue with Google", and the line offering the previewed address back; the empty-address hint; the two price period labels; the two "we could not reach Stripe" lines; and the line shown when the payment webhook is slow.
Why: the screens cannot render without words, and leaving them out drops behaviour the specs require. None is a canonical string, so no invariant governs them, but `docs/founder-decisions.md` §E3 makes copy the founder's call, and T9.1 set the precedent of listing authored sentences rather than burying them.
Nearest spec: ui §2.1–2.3 — describe every state, give wording for four sentences; main Appendix A — the two that are pinned.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.2 — Where the design canvas and the UI spec disagree, the spec was followed
Decision: three disagreements, each resolved towards `docs/sortiva-ui-spec.md`.
**The plan's button.** The canvas labels it "Get started" in both places it appears; ui §2.3 says "One button: 'Subscribe'". Built as "Subscribe" — the canvas's word is the landing page's sign-up call to action, and reusing it on the screen that takes money makes the moment of purchase read like another navigation step.
**The price.** The canvas shows $79 a month; main §4.2 says $89. Neither number is written anywhere in this repository — the card reads the amount from Stripe on every request — so the disagreement costs nothing today and is settled by whatever the Stripe price is set to. Worth knowing before anyone treats the canvas as the price of record.
**The line under the plan.** The canvas says "Billed monthly. Cancel anytime from the billing portal."; ui §2.3 says "Cancel anytime." The shorter one is already a shared string with a test holding it, and the canvas's version states a billing period that is wrong on the annual option.
Also noted, and not a disagreement with the spec: the canvas's own note describes a three-step progress row and its markup draws four. Four are built, because that is what it draws and what onboarding actually has.
Nearest spec: ui §2.3; main §4.2; `docs/design/README.md` — "Where the two disagree, say so rather than picking one".

## 2026-09-02 — T9.2 — Following the teaser opens a sign-in page, not a modal over the landing page
Decision: the teaser and both "Get started" buttons are ordinary links to `/signin`, which is a page of its own.
Why: ui §2.1 says the teaser opens a "signup modal", and §2.2 then describes it as a screen with its own behaviour — carrying the previewed address, and being reachable directly. The design canvas draws no sign-in surface at all, so it settles nothing. A page is what makes the address survivable in a link, what gives the identity provider somewhere to return to, and what a merchant can bookmark; a modal would need all three anyway and would have to become a page the moment the provider redirects back. The cost is that the landing page is left rather than layered over, which is a slower-feeling step on a page whose whole job is conversion. Reversible: the same component renders inside a dialog unchanged.
Nearest spec: ui §2.1 — "signup modal"; ui §2.2 — describes it as a screen.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.2 — The address a visitor typed is remembered by the tab, not carried in the link
Decision: the teaser puts the previewed address in the sign-in link, and the sign-in screen copies it into the tab's own storage under `sortiva.previewedDomain`. The connect step reads it from there. It is offered back as a suggestion and never claimed.
Why: ui §2.2 requires the address to reach the connect step as a pre-fill, and the journey between the two leaves our site twice — the identity provider, then Stripe Checkout — so a link parameter survives neither hop. Per-tab storage does, and it empties itself when the tab closes. The alternative, storing it against the account server-side, means a column and a migration for something that is only ever a convenience, and this card may not add either. **The connect screen (T9.3) reads it through `readPreviewedDomain()` rather than guessing the key**, and should clear it once the domain is claimed. The limit worth knowing: a visitor who previews in one tab and signs up in another gets an empty field, which is what everyone who never used the preview gets.
Nearest spec: ui §2.2 — "carry the previewed domain in state and pre-fill it later at Connect Domain — pre-fill only, never auto-claim".

## 2026-09-02 — T9.2 — The plan block keeps the cap line when Stripe cannot be read
Decision: when the plan endpoint fails, both the landing page's pricing block and the plan screen render the plan with the amount replaced by "We can't show the plan price right now", the cap line still stated, and no purchase button. No fallback amount is invented.
Why: ui §2.3 requires the cap line verbatim on the plan screen, and it is a promise about quality rather than a price — it is true whatever Stripe says, so a Stripe outage is not a reason to stop saying it. The inherited version dropped the whole block on the plan screen, which made the one screen where the promise matters most the one screen that could silently omit it. No amount is invented because no amount exists anywhere in this repository; that is what makes repricing a change in Stripe.
Nearest spec: ui §2.3 — the cap line verbatim; main §4.2 — amounts live in Stripe only.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.2 — The public screens send no analytics of their own, so the open transport question does not block them
Decision: nothing on the landing page, the sign-in screen or the plan screen reports anything through the browser analytics seam. No transport was bound and no route was added.
Why: every step of this funnel is already recorded where the work happens — `preview_requested` and `preview_served` by the preview endpoint, `signup_completed` when the account row is created, `checkout_started` by the checkout endpoint, `subscription_activated` by the payment webhook. Main §14.7's rule that everything observable is captured server-side is therefore satisfiable here without any browser event at all, which is not true of the later screens (a card being read, a veto clicked). The founder question T9.1 raised — whether a browser talks to the analytics vendor directly or posts to an endpoint of ours — stays open and stays unblocking for these three screens.
Nearest spec: main §14.7, §3.1, §4.2; ui §11 — names no UI-only event on a public surface.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.2 — The browser flow runs against a fixture-serving server placed in front of the app
Decision: `apps/web/e2e/mock-api-server.ts` answers the API from the frozen contract fixtures and passes every page through to the running app. The public-funnel flows run against it as their own Playwright project; the sign-in handshake is answered the way an identity provider does, with a redirect to where the screen asked to land.
Why: two steps of this funnel reach vendors this repository has no credentials for, and a third costs a model call per run. Intercepting inside the browser is not enough, because the plan screen asks for its price while it is being rendered on the server, where the browser cannot see the request; putting the fixtures in front of the app catches both. It is the same shape the Shopify and Search Console cards used — a real local server standing in for the vendor — so the pages, components and copy under test are the ones that ship. The harness also hands the app the repository's configuration, which Next reads only when started from the directory holding it, and makes a throwaway key for encrypting merchant tokens when none is configured, because these flows store no token and a committed key is not a key.
**These flows do not pass yet, for a reason outside this card:** the application cannot start at all — see the entry below.
Nearest spec: build plan §6, T9.2 done-when — "Playwright: URL → card → teaser → signup → plan → Checkout redirect"; the card also says it runs on the existing mock server.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.2 — The application does not start, in development or in production, and the cause is not in this lane
Decision: recorded rather than fixed. The start-up hook (`apps/web/instrumentation.ts`) imports `@sortiva/jobs`, which reaches the threshold-config loader (`packages/rules/src/load.ts`), which finds `signals.config.yaml` on disk by resolving a path from its own module URL. Bundled into the web build that URL is not a file path, the resolution throws, and Next reports "Failed to prepare server". Every route then answers 500 — including `/api/health` and the component gallery, both of which predate this card, so the smoke test written in milestone 0 fails identically. Two further gaps sit behind it: the repository's `.env` is not read when Next is started from `apps/web`, and it ships no value for the key that encrypts merchant tokens, without which the hook refuses to start.
Why not fixed here: `packages/rules` and `packages/jobs` belong to other lanes and `instrumentation.ts` is integrator-resolved, so a change to any of them from this lane is a review failure. The diagnosis was confirmed by patching the loader temporarily, watching the boot get past that error, and reverting — the branch carries no change to those files. The shape of the fix is the one T1.3 already wrote up for the prompt loader: compose the path so bundling cannot rewrite it, or resolve it from the working directory. Until it lands, `pnpm e2e` cannot pass for anybody.
Nearest spec: tech §2 — one `app` service running web and worker in one process; CLAUDE.md §3 — lane ownership.
## 2026-09-02 — T-OPS — The app does not start at all in a production build, and this was found by trying to prove the health check
Decision: **not fixed here** — reported. The defect is in `packages/rules/src/load.ts`, which Lane C owns, and this card owns none of it.
What happens: `pnpm build` succeeds, `pnpm start` starts, and then every single request — the landing page included — answers **500**. The start-up hook that boots the worker throws before it finishes, and Next fails the whole server when it does. The cause is the same one the wave-1 notes already warned about in a different place: the threshold config reads its YAML file off disk using a path worked out from its own module address, and the bundler rewrites that address to a URL that has no disk behind it (`ERR_INVALID_URL, input: 'signals.config.yaml', base: '/_next/static/media/index.ab74d0d8.ts'`). The earlier occurrence was fixed for one route by splitting the queue call out; the start-up hook reaches the same loader by a different path — the `@sortiva/jobs` barrel, whose Search Console job imports `@sortiva/rules` — and nothing catches it, because `pnpm build` passes and every test passes.
Why it is reported rather than fixed: `packages/rules` belongs to Lane C, which is building right now; and the fix is a real choice (read the config through a bundler-visible import, mark the package external to the server bundle, or move the loader off the start-up path) rather than a mechanical repair.
Verified by hand on this branch, 2026-09-02: `pnpm build`, then `next start` — `Failed to prepare server ... An error occurred while loading instrumentation hook: Invalid URL`, and `/`, `/api/account` and `/api/health` all answering 500. With the path temporarily hard-coded as a throwaway local experiment (reverted, never committed), the same build starts cleanly and the worker reports `started with 7 task(s)`.
**What this means for the health check:** the endpoint is proved to work over HTTP once the app can start — `{"ok":true}` / 200 with the worker up, and 503 naming `database` when the database is taken away under a running server. It is *not* what catches this defect, because Next never reaches our route: the platform sees a 500 rather than our 503. It would still restart the container, which is the behaviour that matters.
Nearest spec: tech §2.1 — the deployment topology and the health check; main §7.10 — thresholds live in `packages/rules`.

## 2026-09-02 — T-OPS — Both operator commands are command-line scripts, not API routes
Decision: `pnpm diagnose <email or domain>` and `pnpm dlq list` / `pnpm dlq replay <id>` are `scripts/*.mjs` run under `tsx`, following `pnpm db:seed`. No route, no screen.
Why: an HTTP surface for either would need a route in the frozen API table, an authorisation story we do not have — there are no operator accounts, only merchant ones, and every route resolves `account_id` from the merchant's session — and, for the replay, a mutation that acts across accounts. All three are decisions well outside this card. A script run against `DATABASE_URL` by whoever already has database access adds no new authorisation surface at all. If an ops screen is wanted later, the read (`diagnoseStore`) and the write (`replayDlqEntry`) are both ordinary functions it can call.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.5 — "Ops can replay a DLQ item with one action", silent on what the action is; tech §3 — every route resolves the account from the session, which an operator tool cannot do.

## 2026-09-02 — T-OPS — The diagnosis read lives in `packages/db` and takes a `SystemScope`
Decision: `diagnoseStore` (`packages/db/src/diagnose.ts`) is a repository-shaped read taking `SystemScope('an operator is looking a store up by email or domain — finding the account is the point')`. It is **not** added to the `@sortiva/db` barrel; the script imports it by path, exactly as `pnpm db:seed` imports `seed.ts`.
Why: the query genuinely cannot take an account scope — finding the account is what it does — and `SystemScope` is the existing way to say that in the type system rather than in a comment. Putting it in `packages/db` also keeps the script free of raw table access: the lint rule that bans raw table imports has no exemption for `scripts/`, and CLAUDE.md's "outside migrations and admin scripts" carve-out is a licence, not a reason to reach past the rule. Left out of the barrel because it is an operator tool, not domain code — the same reasoning that keeps the service bundle out of it. The `pnpm dlq` script does import `packages/db/src/client.ts` by path for a database handle, which is the admin-script exception used deliberately and named here so a reviewer sees it rather than finds it.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md code-structure rules — "no unscoped table access outside migrations and admin scripts"; main §13 — the tables.

## 2026-09-02 — T-OPS — The diagnosis prints connection state and open dead letters, not only the steps
Decision: alongside the nine pipeline steps, the report carries the domain's state, the Shopify and Search Console connections with their invalidation timestamps, the subscription status, and this store's unreplayed dead-letter entries.
Why: the card asks for "state and every pipeline step", and for a stuck store those five extras *are* the state: the single most common reason a pipeline stops is that Shopify rejected the token and the account is waiting for the merchant, which no step row says. The dead-letter entries are there because a step that failed for good carries its reason on the dead-letter row, so without them the report names the failure and not the reason — and the entry id it prints is what the replay command takes. Nothing else was added: no catalogue counts, no opportunity state, no article history.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.1 — the step model; main §6.2 — `awaiting_shopify_auth`.

## 2026-09-02 — T-OPS — Route crashes are reported from Next's own error hook, not from a wrapper on each route
Decision: `apps/web/instrumentation.ts` gains an `onRequestError` export, which Next calls for every server-side error it catches; it forwards to `reportCrash`. No route file changes, and `withAccount` does not report.
Why: the alternative — reporting inside `withAccount` — covers only the authenticated routes, and the ones most likely to break unattended are the unauthenticated ones: the Shopify and Google OAuth callbacks, the Stripe and Shopify webhook receivers, the public preview. Doing both would report every authenticated crash twice, which is worse than either. The hook also needs no route to remember anything, so a route added by a later card is covered the day it lands. Cost: the hook cannot see the session, so a route crash is attributed to the system actor rather than to the merchant it happened to — recoverable later by resolving the session inside the hook, which was not built because nothing needs it yet.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 — "Error tracking uses PostHog's exception capture with the same properties", silent on where the call sites are.

## 2026-09-02 — T-OPS — A job step is reported when it runs out of retries, not on every failed attempt
Decision: `runStep` calls the crash reporter only on the path that dead-letters — a terminal failure class, or a retryable one that has used its attempt budget. A failure that is going to be retried is logged and not reported. A dead Shopify token (`awaiting_reauth`) is not reported at all.
Why: the queue is at-least-once and most retryable failures — a vendor timeout, a 429 — succeed on the next attempt. Reporting those would put hundreds of self-healing events in front of the handful nobody is going to handle, which is how an alert stops being read. Dead-lettering is precisely the moment the system has given up and only a person can act. A dead token is excluded because it is not a fault: it needs the merchant to reconnect, and it already has its own path and its own email.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.5 — retry and dead-letter policy; main §14.7 — exception capture. Neither says which failures are exceptions.

## 2026-09-02 — T-OPS — A crash with no account behind it is attributed to a `system` sentinel rather than a new kind of attribution
Decision: `reportCrash` defaults to `accountAttribution('system')` — the literal actor id `system`, carrying no domain group — when the caller cannot name an account.
Why: `EventAttribution` is one of the M0 contracts, and it has exactly two shapes: an account (optionally with its claimed domain) or a preview of a stranger's domain. A route crash before the session is read, and a sweep that belongs to nobody, are neither. Adding a third shape is the clean answer and is a frozen-seam change a single card may not make; the sentinel gets the same grouping behaviour with no contract edit, and carries no domain group so it cannot pollute any store's cost numbers. **Flagged for the integrator**: if a third `kind: 'system'` is wanted, this is the one call site to change.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 — "All events carry `account_id` and the `domain` group ... where applicable"; silent on events with no account.

## 2026-09-02 — T-OPS — The crash reporter reads the process service bundle rather than being handed a client
Decision: `reportCrash` (`packages/core/src/observability/crash.ts`) resolves the analytics client from `appServices()`, logs as well as captures, and never throws. It is kept out of the `@sortiva/core` barrel, so importing it is visible in a grep.
Why: its two callers are a Next error hook and the job step runner, both invoked by their framework with no caller of ours able to pass them anything — the same boundary exception `runtime/services.ts` already documents for the analytics client itself. Threading a client through every job dispatcher instead would mean editing several lanes' directories for a reporter that reads nothing back. It logs as well as capturing because the log line is what someone tailing a deploy sees while the capture is what alerts; losing either is how a fault becomes invisible in the place people are actually looking. It cannot throw because a reporter that fails while reporting turns one fault into two, and the second one has nowhere to go.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 — server-side capture; tech §4 — "scrubber on the exception path" (the wrapper's, unchanged).

## 2026-09-02 — T-OPS — An unhealthy instance answers 503 with the shared error envelope, so the frozen API contract is untouched
Decision: `/api/health` keeps answering exactly `{ "ok": true }` when it is well, which is the response the API contract table already describes for it. When a probe fails it answers **503** with the error envelope every other route uses (`{ error: { code: "unhealthy", message, details } }`), one `details` entry per failing probe (`path` is `database` or `worker`). No schema, route-table or OpenAPI change.
Why: the health response schema is `z.literal(true)` and sits in the M0 route table, which a lane may not re-freeze on its own. Widening the success body to carry per-probe detail would have done exactly that. Errors are outside the per-route schemas already — every route answers the same envelope — so putting the detail there costs nothing and stays inside the contract. An operator who curls the endpoint still learns which half is down without opening a log, which is the whole point of the detail.
Nearest spec: tech §2.1 — names the health check as the platform's restart trigger; tech §3 — one error envelope for every route; main is silent on the endpoint's shape.

## 2026-09-02 — T-OPS — The worker's liveness is a record on `globalThis`, not a database heartbeat
Decision: the worker records `running` / `stopped` / `not_expected` in a single process-wide slot keyed by `Symbol.for('sortiva.observability.worker-liveness')` (`packages/core/src/observability/health.ts`). The health route reads that slot. `WORKER_ENABLED=false` records `not_expected`, which is **healthy**.
Why: three alternatives were considered. A heartbeat column would be a migration, which only a schema-wave card may add. Graphile Worker 0.16 keeps no worker-presence table of its own to read — the crontab table it does keep is only written when cron runs, and cron is currently disabled because most scheduled jobs have no handler. A plain module variable would be read by the wrong copy: the startup hook and the route handler are compiled into separate bundles, so each can hold its own instance of the module, and the reader would see "never started" forever while the worker ran happily. A process has exactly one `globalThis`, which is what makes writer and reader agree. The cost is that this only answers for **this** process, which is correct today — the worker runs in the web process — and would need revisiting on the day the worker becomes its own service, at which point the health check for that service is a different endpoint anyway. `not_expected` is healthy because otherwise a deliberately worker-free process would be restarted forever by the platform.
Nearest spec: tech §2.1 — "Next.js + Graphile Worker in-process", and the health check; silent on how liveness is observed.

## 2026-09-02 — T-OPS — The database probe is `select 1`, with its own five-second budget
Decision: `pingDatabase` runs `select 1` and rejects if no answer arrives within 5 s (`packages/db/src/health.ts`).
Why: a probe that reads a real table starts failing for reasons a restart cannot fix — a migration mid-flight, a lock someone else holds — and a health check that fails for unfixable reasons is worse than none. The timeout exists because a refused connection fails fast but a host that accepts the socket and never answers does not: without it the probe hangs, the platform sees its own timeout instead of our answer, and the reason is lost. Five seconds sits well inside the platform's 30-second health-check timeout. This is a liveness budget, not a product threshold, so it is a named constant beside the probe rather than a `packages/rules` entry.
Nearest spec: tech §2.1 — `healthcheckTimeout = 30`; silent on what the check does.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.1 — Copy lives in `packages/ui/strings/en.json`, and the billing copy that predates it is held to it by a test
Decision: `packages/ui/strings` now exists and is the single home for every user-facing sentence. The thirteen sentences the main spec's canonical-copy table pins are keyed by their row (`appendixA.*`) and asserted character for character. Billing's five strings, which an earlier card had to park in `packages/core/src/billing/copy.ts` because this package did not exist, were **not moved** — moving them means editing Lane A's directory and its snapshot tests. Instead a test in `packages/ui` asserts the two homes still say the same thing.
Why: the constitution names this location and four cards have now parked copy elsewhere waiting for it. Duplicating a sentence in two places is how the two drift apart; a test that fails the moment they disagree is the cheapest guard available to a lane that does not own one of the two files. The follow-up — delete `copy.ts`, point billing at the catalogue — is mechanical and belongs to the integrator or to whichever card next touches billing.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md code-structure rules — names the location; main Appendix A — the strings themselves.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.1 — Sentences the specs name but do not write were authored, and are flagged for the founder
Decision: three banners exist in the UI spec by name with no wording given, so wording was written for them: the Search Console reconnect banner ("Your Search Console connection was lost — reconnect to keep query and page data current."), the vacation-mode banner ("Vacation mode is on — nothing will publish until you turn it off."), and the counted-overflow line ("{count} more notices in your notifications"). Also authored: the six navigation labels, the locked tooltip ("Available after your store is connected"), and the Opportunities progress note ("Finding opportunities…").
Why: the banners cannot render without words, and the alternative — leaving them out — drops behaviour the spec requires. These are ordinary interface copy rather than canonical strings, so no invariant governs them, but `docs/founder-decisions.md` §E3 makes copy the founder's call. They are named here so a review has a list rather than a diff to read.
Nearest spec: ui §1 — names each banner and its dismissibility, gives wording for only the payment-failed and service-pause ones.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.1 — A locked destination renders with no address at all, not as a greyed-out link
Decision: the four destinations that lock before the store is connected render as a `role="link"` element with `aria-disabled="true"` and **no `href`**, rather than as an anchor styled to look inactive.
Why: the spec asks for "visible but non-interactive". An anchor that is merely grey is still reachable by keyboard, still appears in a screen reader's list of links, and still navigates — which is how "non-interactive" quietly turns out to be interactive. Removing the address makes the treatment and the behaviour the same fact. The cost is that the element is not focusable, so the tooltip explaining the lock is reachable by pointer and by the accessible name but not by tab; a rail note carrying the same sentence sits at the foot of the rail for that reason.
Nearest spec: ui §1 — "visible but non-interactive, lock icon + tooltip", silent on the mechanism.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.1 — "The first scan has finished" is read from the scan timestamp, because no field says it
Decision: Opportunities shows its progress note while `domain.state` is `ready_for_planning` and `connections.lastScanAt` is null.
Why: the spec wants the note shown "until the onboarding run completes", and `GET /api/account` carries no field for that. The scan timestamp is the same fact by another name — the onboarding run is what writes the first one. This puts a requirement on the backend card that fills the endpoint: the timestamp must be written when a scan **produces** something, not when one starts, or the note disappears while the screen is still empty. Recorded in `packages/ui/src/fixtures/screen-contract.ts` so that card inherits it.
Nearest spec: ui §1, §3.8; main §6.9 — describe the state, name no field.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.1 — When the shell cannot read the account, it locks everything and raises nothing
Decision: if `GET /api/account` fails or is missing, the frame renders as though no domain were claimed: every product destination locked, no banners. A missing `GET /api/settings` (still a mock) leaves vacation mode off.
Why: the frame wraps every screen, so throwing takes the whole product down. Of the two directions to fail in, locking shows a merchant fewer destinations than they have, which corrects itself on the next load; unlocking shows destinations that then fail to load, and raising a banner from a failed fetch would tell a merchant their payment failed when it did not.
Nearest spec: ui §1 — silent on what the shell does when it cannot read the account.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.1 — The gallery of component states is a route that does not exist in production
Decision: the card asks for "Storybook/preview for the banner stack". Rather than adding Storybook, the states are a component in `packages/ui` mounted at `/ui-gallery/banner-stack`, which returns not-found when `NODE_ENV` is `production`.
Why: Storybook is a large dependency tree and a second build for one gallery; a route reuses the build that already exists and renders the real components with the real stylesheet, so what is on the page is what ships. The trade is that there is no isolated component runner, no interaction testing and no visual-regression baseline — if any of those are wanted later, Storybook is still the answer and this page is not in its way. Files named `*.preview.tsx` are exempt from the copy rule, because the labels naming each state are scaffolding rather than product copy.
Nearest spec: build plan §6, T9.1 done-when — "Storybook/preview", either.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.1 — Browser analytics ships as a typed seam with no transport bound — the transport is a founder question
Decision: screens report what a merchant did through a `UiAnalytics` interface with a do-nothing default and a recording test double. Nothing is sent anywhere. Each event declares exactly which properties it may carry, so a call site cannot attach a free-text field.
Why: the two specs disagree about whether browser-observed events exist at all. Main §14.7 says everything observable is emitted "from the backend (server-side capture)"; ui §11 requires seven events that no server call can see — a card being read, a veto clicked, a calendar drag. Sending them needs one of two things, and both are decisions rather than details: the browser talks to the analytics vendor directly, which means a new browser SDK, a public key in the page, and turning session replay off on every view that renders store data; or the browser posts to an endpoint of ours which captures server-side, which means a route that is not in the frozen contract. Building either would foreclose the other, so neither was built. The seam costs nothing and unblocks every screen card. The property table also closes the UI half of the open audit finding that nothing structurally stops product content reaching an analytics event.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 — server-side capture; ui §11 — UI-only events with the same taxonomy. They contradict each other.

## 2026-09-02 — T9.1 — Six navigation destinations, taken as the assumed default rather than a signed-off decision
Decision: built with six destinations — Dashboard, Opportunities, Content, Products, Performance, Settings — with Search Console as a tab inside Performance.
Why: main Appendix B assumes six and `docs/founder-decisions.md` §C8 records it as awaiting sign-off while noting Lane F is building navigation now, and recommends confirming as it stands. Building to the assumed default is what the plan's kickoff checklist expects; noted here so the sign-off has something to point at. Changing it later is a change to one table.
Nearest spec: main Appendix B; ui §1.
## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — The connect-screen copy this card wrote was deleted again, because T9.1 landed its proper home mid-card
Decision: `packages/core/src/catalog/copy.ts` and its snapshot test were written, then removed before the card closed. The four sentences — the not-a-Shopify-store parked card, the read-only reassurance, the connect headline, the reconnect banner — live only in `packages/ui/strings/en.json`.
Why: when this card started, `packages/ui/strings` did not exist and four earlier cards had parked their copy in a core module with a snapshot test; this followed that precedent. `T9.1` merged to `main` while the card was in flight and created the package the constitution actually requires, with three of these four sentences already in it, byte-identical, and pinned by its own test. Two copies of a canonical sentence is precisely what the single-source rule exists to prevent, and the duplicate would have merged cleanly — different files — leaving the drift invisible. Nothing in the backend renders these strings: the API answers with a state and the screen chooses the words, so deleting them costs no behaviour. **Anyone rendering the connect or parked screens takes them from `packages/ui/strings`.**
Nearest spec: main Appendix A; CLAUDE.md — "Copy: `packages/ui/strings/*.json` only".


## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — OPEN QUESTION, NOT DECIDED — nothing in the running product starts a merchant's onboarding
Decision: **none taken.** This card built the handler that runs a merchant's first two onboarding steps and can be called by anything; it did not choose what calls it first. The founder is being asked.
Why: claiming a domain writes the record of the work and pushes nothing onto the queue, deliberately, because there was no handler. There is now. Two things could pick it up and they are not equivalent. (a) Turn on the scheduled jobs that have handlers — today the worker refuses to run *any* schedule until every one of the fourteen scheduled jobs has one, and eleven still do not; relaxing that to "run the ones that have handlers" is a one-line change that also switches on the nightly billing repair and the spend caps, both written and tested and both currently dormant. (b) Keep the all-or-nothing rule and have the domain claim push a job the moment it commits — which is a one-line edit inside Lane A's claim code, a directory this card does not own. Either works; (a) has wider blast radius and (b) crosses a lane boundary. Recorded here rather than guessed, per `docs/handoff-wave2.md`, which names this as the question belonging to whoever writes the first handler.
Meanwhile: the OAuth callback resumes onboarding directly, so a merchant who connects their store does move forward. It is the very first step — detection, straight after the claim — that has no trigger.
Nearest spec: main §5 step 3 ("claiming enqueues the deep ingestion job"); DECISIONS 2026-09-01 T1.4.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — A Shopify store that hides its `*.myshopify.com` name fails the step rather than guessing
Decision: detection looks for the store's canonical name in three places — the claimed address itself, anywhere in the homepage source, and where `/admin` redirects to. If none of them reveal it, the step fails as retryable and, after its retries, dead-letters. No fallback address is invented and the merchant is not asked to type one.
Why: main §6.1 says to resolve the name "if discoverable" and never says what to do when it is not, though it also says the name is needed for the handshake. The handshake is addressed to that name, so an invented one sends the merchant to a consent screen for somebody else's store — the worst available failure. Asking them to type it means a screen that ui §3.3 does not describe and a request body the frozen route table does not allow, both of which are product decisions this card may not take. Failing puts it on the existing path for "something went wrong reading your store": the progress screen shows a retry card with a support link (ui §3.2). Every real storefront we know of reveals the name through at least one of the three sources; a store that reveals none is rare enough to be worth a support conversation.
Nearest spec: main §6.1 — "resolve the canonical `*.myshopify.com` handle if discoverable"; ui §3.2 — step failure UI.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — A Shopify store moves to `awaiting_shopify_auth` as soon as it is detected, not only when the merchant abandons
Decision: the detect step moves the store to `awaiting_shopify_auth` the moment it decides the site is Shopify. Granting permission moves it back to `ingesting`.
Why: main §6.2 describes the state as where an account "parks" if the merchant abandons the consent screen, which implies a state before abandonment — but the dashboard renders from `domains.state` (ui §3), and ui §3.3 says the connect card replaces the step *as soon as* we know it is Shopify. Distinguishing "showing the connect card" from "gave up on the connect card" would need a timer and buys nothing: both render the same card. The two screens that genuinely differ — first-time connect (ui §3.3) and reconnect after a lost connection (ui §3.5) — are told apart by whether a connection row exists at all, which `shopifyConnectionState` answers. **Lane F needs this**: `awaiting_shopify_auth` alone does not tell you which of the two cards to render.
Nearest spec: main §6.2; ui §3.3, §3.5.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — Re-running detection against the same site is meant to return the same answer
Decision: the detect step's idempotency key is derived from the claimed domain alone, so once a site has been judged, the completed-work ledger answers for it and the site is never fetched again for that account.
Why: the ledger exists so redelivered work is not redone, and the key has to be derived from the step's inputs. The only input detection has is the site. The consequence is that a site parked as unsupported stays parked even if the merchant later moves it to Shopify — which matches the product: ui §3.4 says the parked state "persists until support intervenes; no self-serve exit in V1". If a self-serve retry is ever wanted, it needs a deliberate re-detection path with a new input version, not a weaker key.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.2 (derived keys); ui §3.4 (no self-serve exit).

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — A store parked as unsupported has its remaining steps marked skipped and its run closed as succeeded
Decision: when detection finds a non-Shopify site, the seven steps after `detect` become `skipped` and the run's status becomes `succeeded` with a finish time. The domain claim is untouched.
Why: ui §3.2's progress stepper renders these rows directly, and leaving seven `pending` steps shows a merchant work that will never start. `skipped` is an existing state the stepper already knows how to draw. On the run's own status, the four available values are running, succeeded, failed and abandoned: nothing failed and nothing was abandoned — detection did its job and the pipeline correctly terminated — so `succeeded` is the honest one, and leaving it `running` forever would make every "is this store still working" query wrong.
Nearest spec: main §6.1 ("stop ingestion"); ui §3.2, §3.4 — silent on the run's recorded status.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — The store name detection found is kept in the step's recorded output, not a column
Decision: the `*.myshopify.com` name is read back out of the `detect` step's `output_ref` when the OAuth start endpoint needs it.
Why: this card adds no migration, and no wave-2 column holds it (`shopify_conns.shop_handle` exists but the row only comes into being *after* the handshake, which is exactly when the name is needed *before*). The step output is durable, is already the record of what that step decided, and is one indexed row away. If a later card wants the name queryable across accounts before a connection exists, it is a column in the next schema wave, not a second store now.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md migration rule; main §13.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — One reminder per account, ever, for an unfinished Shopify connection
Decision: the 24-hour reminder is deduplicated on the account alone, so a merchant is nudged once and never again — including if they connect, later lose the connection, and abandon a reconnection.
Why: main §6.2 says "a reminder email after 24h", singular, and the connect card already persists on every dashboard visit, so the reminder is for someone not coming back at all rather than a recurring prompt. A key that could send twice needs a per-episode marker, and no column records when a store entered the waiting state. The later case is covered anyway: losing a connection sends its own `connection_lost_shopify` message when it happens.
Nearest spec: main §6.2; tech §1.2 (append-only notifications with a unique dedupe triple).

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — The OAuth callback is not in the frozen API route table
Decision: `GET /api/shopify/oauth/callback` exists as a route but is not added to `packages/core/src/api/routes.ts`, so the generated API document does not describe it.
Why: that table describes the JSON contract the frontend and its mock server are built from, and it already excludes routes that are not that — `/api/auth/*` is absent on purpose because Auth.js owns those shapes. A vendor redirect target takes a browser, not a client, answers with a redirect rather than a body, and has no schema a mock could usefully return. The Search Console flow has the same shape and the same absence: the table lists `/api/gsc/oauth/start` and no callback. Changing the frozen table needs the integrator to re-freeze it, and there is nothing here for a consumer to consume.
Nearest spec: build plan §4 (frozen contracts); `packages/core/src/api/routes.ts` — "`/api/auth/*` is absent on purpose".

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — Our own OAuth state is signed rather than stored
Decision: the `state` value sent to Shopify carries the account and the store, signed with the app secret and stamped with a moment, and is checked on return against the signature, a 15-minute age limit, and the session's account. Nothing is written between the two requests.
Why: the state's job is to prove the returning browser is finishing the handshake we started, for the person we started it for. Storing a nonce would need a table this card cannot add. A signed value is unforgeable without the secret, and the account inside it is checked against the session — so a state lifted from another browser completes nothing there. The 15 minutes is long enough to read the consent screen and short enough that a link left in a chat window stops working. The app secret does double duty rather than adding a second secret to rotate; it is already the secret Shopify signs their own callbacks with.
Nearest spec: main §6.2, tech §4 — silent on CSRF for the install flow.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — A grant that comes back carrying write permission is dropped, not stored
Decision: if Shopify's answer to the code exchange lists any `write_*` scope, the token is discarded and the merchant is returned to the dashboard with a refusal code. Nothing is saved.
Why: the connect screen states in fixed words that this permission cannot change anything in their store. The app's configured scopes live in Shopify's Partner dashboard, outside this repository, so a merchant re-installing an app that once had write scopes could be handed them back without anyone here changing a line — and storing that token would make the sentence on the screen untrue. Failing loudly is recoverable (fix the app configuration, reinstall); a silently over-permissioned token is not.
Nearest spec: main §6.2 ("explicitly not requesting any `write_*` scope here"); invariant 21.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — `app/uninstalled` handling is built; its HTTP receiver belongs to T2.2
Decision: the behaviour an uninstall triggers — find the account holding that store, mark the connection lost, move the store to the reconnect screen, notify once — is built and tested here as a function taking a shop name. The webhook endpoint that verifies Shopify's signature, deduplicates by webhook id and answers 200 within five seconds is **not** built here.
Why: T2.1's scope says "`app/uninstalled` handling"; T2.2's scope says "Shopify webhook receiver (HMAC, `X-Shopify-Webhook-Id` dedupe, 200 < 5 s, async, out-of-order guard)". Building the receiver twice is how two lanes end up with two verification paths. The signature check itself is written here, in the Shopify adapter, and tested against the exact-bytes requirement, so T2.2 inherits it rather than writing a second one.
Nearest spec: main §14.6; tech §3; build plan §6 T2.1/T2.2.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — The one guarded HTTP client now returns response headers and can be asked to read JSON
Decision: `PageFetchResult` gains `headers` (the final hop's, lower-cased) and `PageFetchRequest` gains `contentTypes`, a per-caller widening of the MIME types it can read. Both are additive; no existing caller changes behaviour.
Why: main §6.1 names response headers and a 200 on `/products.json` among the four Shopify tells, and neither was reachable — the client discarded headers and refused `application/json`. tech §2 requires one fetcher for everything that reaches out to a stranger's server, so the alternative was a second, weaker copy of the anti-SSRF guard. Widening is per request rather than global so the preview and persona keep their existing diet. A test asserts the guard is unaffected: a private address is still refused when the wider content types are requested.
Nearest spec: main §6.1; tech §2 (single fetcher).

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — Files touched outside Lane B's directories
Decision: this card writes into four files no lane owns, listed here so the integrator expects them. `packages/db/src/repositories/connections.ts` (new) and a widened `from` argument on `transitionDomainState` in `repositories/domains.ts` — repositories, not migrations; the eslint rule's own note says a new file needing table access "belongs in packages/db in the first place". `packages/jobs/src/runtime/steps.ts` gains two run-level queries (find an account's live run; close a run whose remaining steps are skipped), beside the run and step queries already there. `eslint.config.mjs` gains one entry in its composition-root block, for the file that decides which Shopify, which database and which cipher the store-connection routes run against — the same shape as the two entries already in that block; the file that actually runs queries takes its handles as arguments and is not exempt. `apps/web/instrumentation.ts` registers the two new task handlers, the way it already registers the billing and spend-cap ones.
Why: recorded because build plan §3 marks the last two as integrator-resolved rather than union-merged, and a merge that silently drops half of either is worse than a conflict.
Nearest spec: build plan §3 ("Files no lane owns").

## 2026-09-02 — T2.1 — No dev-store evidence: the Shopify handshake is proved against a local server, not a real store
Decision: the card's "dev-store OAuth smoke passes" is **not** satisfied. There are no Shopify Partner credentials. Instead the handshake — authorize URL, callback signature, code exchange, a first authenticated read, a 401, a 429 with `Retry-After` — runs against a real HTTP server standing in for Shopify, and the in-memory Shopify used elsewhere signs its callbacks the way Shopify does so no route check is skipped in any test.
Why: the same posture three earlier cards took with Stripe, Turnstile, Anthropic and PostHog — build and test against fakes, record real-vendor evidence as outstanding rather than claimed. What a local server cannot prove: that Shopify's actual scope string, signature construction, error shapes and API version behave as assumed. **This card must be re-run against a Partner dev store before launch.**
Nearest spec: build plan §6 T2.1 done-when; `docs/founder-decisions.md` A1 (Shopify Partner dev store, "Lane B, ~1 week").

## 2026-09-02 — T3.1 — Limited Intelligence stays derived; the build plan's done-when is wrong as written
Decision: the card's last done-when — "skip path sets `limited_intelligence = true`" — is not met literally, and no column is added. Whether an account is running without Search Console is computed from its `gsc_conns` row: no row, or a row whose `property` is still empty, means yes. `GET /api/account` now returns that computed value instead of the constant `true` it has returned since T1.1.
Why: T2.0b was asked to add the column, read main §7.11 and §6.7, and declined, journalling it on 2026-08-31 ("NOT BUILT: `account_settings.limited_intelligence`, because §7.11 defines it as derived"). Its reasoning holds and is the reason nothing was added here either: §7.11 is titled "Limited Intelligence mode (**no GSC**)" and §6.7 says the account runs in it "until connected" — one entry condition and one exit condition, both about a connection existing. A stored flag is a second source of truth that can disagree in two visible ways: the badge outlives the connection the merchant just made, or it reads false with nothing to read and the GSC-dependent signals §7.11 forbids get evaluated against no data. The merchant's explicit *skip* is still durable, on the `gsc_connect` step, exactly as main §14.3.1 prescribes. Migrations are schema-wave-only in any case, so the literal reading was not available to this card without a mini-wave.
Underlying requirement met instead: after skipping, the account reports itself as running on limited data; after connecting, it does not; and there is no stored value anyone has to remember to clear. Tested in `packages/core/src/account/account.test.ts` and `packages/core/src/search/connect.test.ts`.
Nearest spec: main §7.11, §6.7, §14.3.1; build plan T3.1 — contradicts.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.1 — A connection that has gone dead is not Limited Intelligence
Decision: three connection states, not two. No connection, or a grant with no property chosen → limited intelligence, badge shown. A chosen property → connected. A chosen property whose Google permission has since died → `broken`: the merchant is asked to reconnect, and the badge is **not** shown.
Why: main §7.11 defines the mode as the absence of search data, and a store whose grant died last week still has sixteen months of it — the GSC-dependent signals can still be evaluated, on data that is stale rather than missing. main §14.4's Search Console row says a refresh failure stops the reporting sync while the content pipeline continues, and prescribes a reconnect card, which is different copy for a different situation. The frozen account contract already carries `connections.searchConsole: 'none' | 'connected' | 'broken'` alongside the boolean, which only makes sense if they are not the same question. The cost accepted: an account whose grant died long ago goes on evaluating search signals against ageing data without the badge saying so. Bounding that — a staleness horizon after which a dead connection does count as limited — needs a number nobody has supplied, and belongs with the detection cards that would read it.
Nearest spec: main §7.11, §14.4 (Search Console row), ui §5.5 — none of them says what a dead grant does to the badge.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.1 — The grant is stored before the property is chosen, in the same row, with an empty property meaning "not yet"
Decision: `gsc_conns` gets its row when Google returns the tokens, with `property = ''`. The property is written when the merchant picks one, and `connected_at` is stamped at that moment rather than at the grant. Everything that asks whether Search Console is connected treats an empty property as not connected.
Why: there is a real gap between Google granting access and the merchant saying which of their properties this store is — the picker cannot even be drawn without the tokens. The alternatives were worse. A nullable property needs a migration, which only a schema-wave card may add. Holding the tokens in a browser cookie until the choice keeps the row clean but puts a merchant's Google credentials in their browser, which is the one place they have no reason to be. `connected_at` is stamped at the choice because it anchors the "your search performance since connecting" chart (main §12.2), and to a merchant connecting is the moment they finished choosing, not the moment Google redirected them back.
Nearest spec: main §12.2, §13 `gsc_conns` — lists the columns, silent on the half-finished state.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.1 — Google is reached over plain HTTPS, not through the `googleapis` SDK
Decision: `packages/providers/src/gsc` calls four Google endpoints with `fetch`. The lint rule that reserves the `googleapis` package for this directory is left in place and unused.
Why: four endpoints, no streaming, no resumable uploads, no need for a token cache of its own — the SDK's whole value here is the transport we already have. It would also bring its own retry and auth-refresh behaviour, and the point of this wrapper is that a dead grant and a bad network get *opposite* treatment, decided in one place we own. The same argument the DataForSEO wrapper already makes. If Google's API surface ever grows past this, the rule is already there to allow the SDK in exactly this directory.
Nearest spec: main §14.2, tech §1.4 — require one instrumented wrapper per vendor, silent on how it talks.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.1 — The history import checkpoints in the queue, not in a table
Decision: the sixteen-month import is a chain of queued jobs rather than one long step. Each job fetches one date range, writes it, and enqueues the next with the ranges already done listed in its payload. Ranges are taken newest first.
Why: the card says "backfill as a job step", but `job_steps.step` is a fixed set of nine values and adding a tenth is a migration, which only a schema-wave card may add. main §6.7 and §14.3.1 say the backfill is *enqueued* by the `gsc_connect` step, which is what this does. The queue's own table is a durable record, so progress survives a crash without a schema change, and a crash between writing a range and queueing the next costs that one range — search rows are written over themselves, so redoing it is free. Newest-first because a merchant who confirms their profile while the import is still running already has the recent months, which nearly every signal weighs most heavily.
Nearest spec: main §6.7, §12.2, §14.3.1, §14.3.3 (checkpoint any step over 60s); build plan T3.1 — "as a job step" reads as the nine-step machine, which cannot hold it.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.1 — Search Console sync numbers live in `packages/rules`
Decision: a new `search_console` section in `signals.config.yaml`: how far back the import reaches (16 months), how large its chunks are (30 days), how many recent days each nightly pull re-fetches (7), and how far behind today Google's data is (2 days).
Why: invariant 9 puts every threshold in `packages/rules`, and the precedent for non-§7 numbers is already there — `budgets` and `auto_trips` come from main §14.5. These four decide what data detection has to work with, which makes them numbers that shape a result. Accepted cost: they are part of the hash stamped on every opportunity, so changing the sync window changes `rules_version` even though no detection rule moved. Nothing is stamped yet — no opportunities exist — and the alternative, a second config file with a second provenance story, is worse. Two of the four are stated by main §12.2 (16 months, ~2 days lag); the chunk size and the seven-day re-pull are unsigned and are marked as such in the file.
Nearest spec: main §12.2 ("the last N days" — N unstated), §6.7 (16 months), §7.10.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.1 — The OAuth callback is not in the frozen route contract
Decision: `GET /api/gsc/oauth/callback` exists as a route but is not added to `packages/core/src/api/routes.ts`. The four routes the contract already declares — start, properties, property, skip — are implemented as declared.
Why: the contract is the set of routes the front end is built against, and it already excludes `/api/auth/*` on the grounds that a third party defines those shapes. This is the same case: Google's browser redirect, answering with a 302 to a screen, never called by our own code. Adding it would mean inventing a response schema for a redirect and re-freezing a contract eight lanes build against, for an entry no client could use. Flagged rather than done silently: if the integrator wants every route in the table, this is a five-line addition.
Nearest spec: build plan §4 (contracts frozen in M0); `routes.ts` — "every `/api/*` route the product exposes", with `/api/auth/*` already excepted.

## 2026-09-02 — T3.1 — The consent flow's CSRF token is signed, not stored
Decision: the `state` parameter is `v1.<accountId>.<expiry>` with an HMAC over it using `AUTH_SECRET`, valid ten minutes. At the callback the signature is checked, the expiry is checked, and the account inside it is compared with the session's account.
Why: without the last check, a link an attacker prepared finishes *their* Google consent inside *your* account, and the merchant then looks at a stranger's search data believing it is their own. A table would work equally well and would be a table of single-use rows nobody ever reads twice; a cookie adds a second thing that can be missing. The account comparison is the substantive control — the signature only stops the account id being edited.
Nearest spec: main §12.2, tech §4 — neither says how the flow is protected.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-02 — T3.1 — Copy parked in `packages/core/src/search/copy.ts`, and one lane-A file changed
Decision: the connection's five user-facing sentences live in a core module with a snapshot test, following the pattern T1.2 (billing) and T1.4 (domain claim) already set, rather than in `packages/ui/strings`. Separately, `packages/core/src/account/view.ts` and `apps/web/app/api/account/_lib/load.ts` — lane A's directory and an unowned route — were changed to read the derived value.
Why (copy): `packages/ui/strings` did not exist on this branch when the card was written; it lands with T9.1, which was in flight in another worktree. Creating it here would have collided with that lane. Mechanical move for whoever next touches it. The two canonical strings this area has — the connect prompt and the badge — are deliberately **not** restated here, because they are rendered by screens and a second copy is a copy that drifts.
Why (lane A): `buildAccountView` returned `limitedIntelligence: true` unconditionally, correct while nothing could connect Search Console and a badge that never turns off the moment something can. The new input field is optional, so no other lane's call sites change. **For the integrator**: this is a cross-lane edit, and `apps/web/instrumentation.ts` — an integrator-resolved file — gained a task registration.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md code-structure rules (copy in `packages/ui/strings`; lane ownership in build plan §3).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-01 — T4.4 (founder decision, taken ahead of the card) — A draft with nothing new to say is rejected outright, with no repair attempt
Decision: of the quality criteria, **information gain** — does a reader get anything here they would not get from the pages already ranking — is the one whose failure ends the run immediately. The draft is rejected, the repair attempt is not spent, and the topic's calendar slot reports the reason like any other gate failure. Every other criterion keeps the existing single repair attempt.
Why: the other criteria fail for reasons a rewrite can fix — a claim stated too strongly, a contradiction, a section that misses the question, weak language. Information gain fails because the evidence had nothing distinctive in it, and no amount of rewriting adds material that was never gathered. Spending a frontier-model repair call on it buys a second draft that says the same nothing, slightly differently, and then fails the same check. The saving is one expensive call on every article that was never going to be publishable; the cost is that a draft which failed only for want of a better angle on the same evidence no longer gets a second try, which we accept because that case is indistinguishable from the common one at the point the check fires.
Compatibility: this does **not** contradict invariant 11's "one repair loop max" — that is a ceiling, and this criterion now sits at zero. The ≥ 4 floor for information gain is unchanged; only what happens on failure changes.
Source: `docs/content-spec.md` §9.4/§9.7 (cofounder, 2026-09-01).
Nearest spec: main §8.4 — "Fail → revision instructions → regrade once", silent on treating any criterion differently.


## 2026-09-01 — T4.0 (founder decision, taken ahead of the card) — Prices live in articles as a reference to the current value, never as a number
Decision: an article body never stores a price, sale status, stock state or product URL as literal text. Where one of those belongs, the body stores a reference to the product and the field, and that reference is filled in from Shopify at the moment the article is published or republished — from the live store, never from the research the article was written against. The reader sees a real figure; it is simply always the current one. Two consequences follow and are part of this decision: **prose may not build an argument on a price** ("the cheapest in the range", "the best option under €100"), because a rendered figure being current does not make a sentence reasoning about it true; and **a price change stops being a refresh trigger**, because there is no longer anything to correct.
Why: the plan sells at most one article per day, and a refresh spends that day exactly like a new article does (main §8.1, §8.7). Today a price moving 20% from what it was at writing time queues the article for refresh (main §14.1), so a merchant running a seasonal sale across a product range loses a day of new content per affected article — to fix, in most cases, nothing but a number, while the article's reasoning was still entirely correct. Under the reference model the article was never wrong, so no day is spent. What is *genuinely* stale still costs a day and should: an argument that rests on price, and a product out of stock long enough that the recommendation itself is wrong (that trigger is unchanged). The cost accepted is a real constraint on what articles may say about price, and one external write per re-render, which goes through the existing two-phase publish path and falls under the existing mechanical auto-repair setting — export-mode accounts get an action card rather than a silent edit, as they do for every other repair.
Timing: recorded now rather than at the card because migrations are added only by schema-wave cards. The reference tokens and the claim/provenance tables have to exist in **T4.0** or every article published before them carries literal prices that cannot be retrofitted.
Source: the mechanism comes from `docs/content-spec.md` §11.2 (cofounder, 2026-09-01); the pointers taken from that document are summarised in `docs/content-pointers.md`.
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (price-drift row → refresh queue), §8.1 and §8.7 (a refresh occupies a daily slot), §9.2 (article construction) — **this contradicts the §14.1 price-drift row as written** and is taken on the founder's authority.


## 2026-09-01 — T-SWEEP (recorded by the integrator) — Shipped migrations keep their spec references
Decision: the citation sweep removed 1,007 references across the codebase and left 15, all but one inside `packages/db/migrations/*.sql`. The exception is one test's name. This was not journalled by the card, so it is recorded here rather than left as folklore.
Why: a migration that has run is a historical record, not live code. Rewriting the comments in one changes the account of why a table looks the way it does, at a moment when nobody can re-derive it — and the file is never read to understand current behaviour, only to understand what happened. The reasoning that makes a citation useless in live code (it points at a document that may have moved on) does not apply to a file that is itself frozen in time. The line is therefore: sweep everything that describes how the product works now; leave what records how it came to be.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md code-structure rules ("no spec citations in code"); the founder's ruling of 2026-09-01 recorded in `docs/audits/remediation.md` D13.

## 2026-09-01 — T8.4a — Calls that failed at the vendor **do** count towards the spend caps
Decision: `COUNT_FAILED_VENDOR_CALLS` in `packages/core/src/ops/spend-caps.ts` is `true`. It is a single exported boolean and the only place the policy is written; every query takes it as its `includeFailed` argument.
Why: when a paid call fails, neither vendor tells us what the attempt cost, so the ledger stores our estimate and marks the row `failed`. DataForSEO very likely bills nothing for a request it rejected as malformed, so counting those rows probably over-counts. Counting them anyway, because the disaster this brake exists to catch — a retry storm, a bad prompt version rejected over and over, a credential that stopped working — is made almost entirely of *failed* calls, and a meter that ignores failures is blind to exactly that shape. The two errors are not symmetric: over-counting pauses one account until an operator looks (minutes, reversible); under-counting runs an unbounded bill (not reversible). **This is the founder's switch to flip**, one line, and every cap and test follows it. Recorded as still-open in `docs/handoff-wave2.md` under "open decisions" — this entry closes it with a default, not with a ruling.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 — names the caps, silent on what a failed call contributes.

## 2026-09-01 — T8.4a — The gate refuses when the kill switches cannot be read
Decision: `mayAccountWorkRun` in `packages/jobs/src/runtime/gate.ts` returns `{allowed: false, reason: 'unreadable'}` when the database throws, rather than treating an unreadable switch as permission to proceed.
Why: "assume fine" means the one condition under which the brakes silently stop existing is a database problem, which is also when a runaway is most plausible. The cost is near zero in practice: the work being gated reads and writes the same database and would fail moments later anyway, so refusing loses nothing but never lets a paid vendor call go out on the strength of a switch we could not read. This is main §14.4's posture — degrade to pause, never to a guess — applied to the brake itself.
Nearest spec: main §14.4, §14.5 — silent on an unreadable flag.

## 2026-09-01 — T8.4a — One gate for all paid account work, not one per work type
Decision: `mayAccountWorkRun(db, accountId)` takes no work-type parameter. It checks `global.pause_all` and `account.pause_generation`, and a caller that gets `allowed: false` runs nothing for that account. Publishing is out of its scope and keeps its own switches.
Why: main §14.5 says the per-account spend trip "pauses that account's generation". Read narrowly, an account paused for runaway model spend would still be free to run catalogue ingestion — which spends model money on distillation, and is therefore a likely source of the runaway the trip just caught. A brake that leaves the throttle open is not a brake. The narrow reading also gives every future caller a per-work-type decision to make, which is how half the code paths end up not consulting the switch at all. The accepted cost: an operator who manually raises `account.pause_generation` also stops that account's ingestion, which is broader than the flag's name suggests. **Flagged as a deviation from the spec's wording**, taken in the safe direction.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 — "pause that account's generation".

## 2026-09-01 — T8.4a — A day is a UTC day, and the trailing comparison excludes today
Decision: caps are measured midnight-to-midnight UTC (`utcDayWindow`), and the trailing window used for "unusual for this account" ends where today begins (`trailingWindow`).
Why: the vendors bill us on one clock, not on each store's, so a per-store local day would make the same dollar fall on different days depending on who spent it — the same runaway would trip at different totals for a Copenhagen store and a Los Angeles one. Excluding today from its own comparison is arithmetic hygiene: a day cannot be unusual against a typical day that includes it.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 — "daily", "trailing-30-day median"; silent on the clock.

## 2026-09-01 — T8.4a — A typical day is the median of days the account actually spent; no history means the flat ceiling only
Decision: `dailyAccountVendorSpend` returns a row only for days with spend, and `accountSpendVerdict` skips the multiple rule when there is no earlier spending day or the median is zero.
Why: counting no-spend days as zero would make the median zero for any account that does not work daily, and ten times zero is zero — so any spending at all would trip. That would pause every new account on its first paid call. With no history the flat dollar ceiling is the only honest rule: the first day of a store's life has nothing to be unusual against.
