Decision: `stripe_events`, `webhook_events`, `request_cache`, `preview_cache` and `email_suppressions` are reached through repositories taking `SystemScope('<reason>')` rather than through unscoped access.
Why: CLAUDE.md says "there is no unscoped table access outside migrations and admin scripts", but these five genuinely have no `account_id` — a webhook is HMAC-verified and stored before it is routed (main §14.3.8), a preview happens pre-signup (main §3), the request cache is keyed by canonical params rather than by tenant (main §14.3.6), and a suppressed address stays suppressed across accounts (tech §1.5). **Flagged as a contradiction rather than resolved silently**: the rule as written has no case for them. `SystemScope` keeps the rule's intent (no method without a scope; system access is explicit and greppable) while admitting the exception in the type system instead of in a comment.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md code-structure rules vs main §13 — the rule does not contemplate account-less tables.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.3 — Three columns added beyond main §13's table sketches
Decision: `job_steps.output_ref` (jsonb), `webhook_events.source` (enum shopify|resend), `request_cache.kind` (text). Plus `request_cache.response_ref` is realised as `response_json` (jsonb).
Why, one at a time. **`job_steps.output_ref`**: main §14.3.2 requires "completed keys are stored with their output reference; a worker seeing a completed key returns the stored output without executing", and T0.4's done-when tests exactly that — but §13's `job_steps` sketch has nowhere to put the output. Without this column T0.4 cannot pass. **`webhook_events.source`**: tech §1.4 routes Resend webhooks through the same receiver pattern as Shopify's; without a discriminator the table cannot tell whose id it holds. The unique constraint stays on `webhook_id` alone exactly as §13 states — `source` is for routing, not identity. **`request_cache.kind`**: §14.3.6 distinguishes billable reads from LLM calls and the retention sweep and cost dashboards need to separate them; deriving that from the key's shape would be parsing. **`response_json`**: tech §2.1 rules out Redis and any blob store ("Postgres is the cache"), so there is nothing for a "ref" to point at.
Nearest spec: main §13 (sketches), §14.3.2, §14.3.6, §14.3.8; tech §1.4, §2.1.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.3 — Two columns added for lifecycle mechanics §13 implies but does not list
Decision: `domains.release_after` and `shopify_conns.invalidated_at`.
Why: main §14.6 requires the domain claim be "released after a 7-day grace window" on account deletion — the row must survive that window still holding the unique index, so the release needs a timestamp to sweep on (T8.3 fills it). main §14.4 requires a 401 from Shopify to move the account to `awaiting_shopify_auth`; recording *when* the token was found invalid is what lets the 24h reminder sweep (T2.1) be idempotent.
Nearest spec: main §14.6, §14.4 — both mandate the behaviour, §13 lists no column.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.3 — Partial unique indexes make an active kill switch singular
Decision: `ops_flags` carries two partial unique indexes — one active `global` flag per name, one active `account` flag per (account, name), both `WHERE reset_at IS NULL` — plus a check constraint that a `global` flag has no `account_id` and an `account` flag must have one.
Why: main §14.5 says auto-trips "never auto-reset" and "every flip is logged with actor + reason", which implies at most one open incident per flag. Without the index a retrying auto-trip writes a new incident row on every dequeue. With it, the second trip is a no-op at the database level and the repository returns `undefined` — the same insert-with-conflict shape as every other dedupe in this schema.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 — states the semantics, not the constraint.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.3 — `notifications.dedupe_key` is NOT NULL
Decision: Every notification row must name its dedupe key.
Why: tech §1.2 makes `(account_id, type, dedupe_key)` the exactly-once guarantee under at-least-once workers, and lists a key for every type (article_id, topic_id, opportunity_id, ISO week, date). A nullable column would silently disable the constraint for the one type someone forgot, since Postgres treats NULLs as distinct in a unique index — the exact failure mode invariant 26 exists to prevent.
Nearest spec: tech §1.2 — names the key per type, does not say the column is required.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.3 — The constraint suite fails loudly when no database is present
Decision: `constraints.test.ts` skips its cases when Postgres is unreachable, but a final test then throws with instructions to run `pnpm db:up`.
Why: A silently-skipped constraint suite reports green while proving nothing — and this is one of the three audit-flagged cards. Skip-plus-fail keeps the developer-experience benefit (a clear message rather than 21 connection errors) without letting CI or a local run pass on an empty result.
Nearest spec: tech §5, §6 — CI gates on integration tests; silent on absent-dependency behaviour.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.3 — Relative imports are extensionless repo-wide
Decision: `import { x } from './y'`, not `'./y.js'`, in every package.
Why: `moduleResolution: "Bundler"` (T0.1) makes extensionless the idiom, and drizzle-kit's config loader transpiles to CJS and cannot resolve `.js` specifiers pointing at `.ts` files. Applied consistently rather than per-package so there is one convention.
Nearest spec: none — internal.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.2 — Per-locale demand floors: values invented, flagged UNSIGNED
Decision: `gates.demand_floor.monthly_search_volume_min` defaults to 100/mo, with per-language overrides descending to 10/mo for the smallest EU markets (en 100 · de/fr/es 60 · it/pt 50 · nl/pl 40 · sv/da/nb/fi/cs/hu/ro/el 20 · sk 15 · sl/et/lv/lt 10). Keyed by language subtag; the resolver falls back full tag → language → global default.
Why: main §8.2 requires per-country floors ("50/mo in Danish ≠ 50/mo in English") and supplies no table, and T0.2's done-when requires the keys to exist. Floors are set roughly proportional to the search market's size so equivalent commercial seriousness clears the bar in a small language as in English. **These numbers need founder calibration sign-off** — they decide which topics Gate 1 admits, which is user-visible. Nothing consumes them until T4.1, so changing them is a one-line YAML edit.
Nearest spec: main §8.2 — mandates per-locale floors, states no values. Appendix B does not cover them.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.2 — Five further UNSIGNED numbers the specs mandate but do not state
Decision: `gates.winnability.limited_intelligence_constant: 0.25` (main §9.6.4 says "a conservative constant"); `gates.substance_floor` = 8 distinct facts / 4 populated fields per product / 3 contributing products, margin ×1.5 (main §8.2 defines the metric, no floor); `auto_trips.account_llm_spend.hard_cap_usd_per_day: 5.00`, `auto_trips.dataforseo_spend.global_cap_usd_per_day: 50.00`, `auto_trips.preview_spend.global_cap_usd_per_day: 10.00`, `budgets.intent_gap.analyses_per_account_per_day: 10` (main §14.5 names each cap as "configured"/"config", none with a number); `learning.outcomes.optimize.impressions_not_collapsed_ratio_min: 0.5` (main §9.6.10 says "impressions not collapsed").
Why: T0.2's scope is "**every** number from ... §14.5 (auto-trip thresholds)", so the keys must exist. Each is marked `UNSIGNED` in the YAML. Sizing rationale: the account LLM cap is a loud-failure ceiling far above the ~$2.90/day of revenue a $89/mo plan produces, not a budget; the preview cap follows main §14.5's own note that this trip firing "means Turnstile, rate limits, or the cache are being defeated — investigate, don't just raise the cap", so it is deliberately low.
Nearest spec: main §14.5, §8.2, §9.6.4, §9.6.10 — each mandates the knob, none states the value.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.2 — main §8.4's gate floors are in signals.config.yaml, beyond the card's enumeration
Decision: `gates.draft_grading` holds §8.4's numbers — information gain ≥ 4, grounding ≥ 4, other criteria ≥ 3, one repair loop max, 1–5 score range.
Why: T0.2's card enumerates §7.3/§7.6/§8.2/§9.6/§10.2/§14.5 and does not name §8.4, but invariant 11 gates on those four numbers and there is no other home for them; leaving them out guarantees Lane D writes them as literals in T4.4. The lint rule cannot catch it (it matches volume/position/impression/CTR field names, not judge scores), so the config is the only defence. Trivially reversible: delete one YAML block and one schema block.
Nearest spec: main §8.4 — states the floors; §7.10 does not list §8.4 among the sections it governs.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.2 — main §14.1 drift thresholds stay out of signals.config.yaml
Decision: The Product Change Impact drift numbers (OOS ≥ 14d, price ≥ 20%, deleted, family-axis change) stay with the drift/repair card; the signal entry here carries only priority and GSC-dependence, with a pointer.
Why: §7.10's config layer is scoped to §7.3/§7.6/§8.2 (+§9.6 via its parenthetical), and §7.3's row for this signal defers wholesale to §14.1 ("already built as the repair loop"). Pulling §14.1's table forward would put thresholds in this file that no §7.10 reader expects and that the drift card owns. Flagged for the integrator: if the invariant sweep prefers one home for every number, this is the block to move.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (row) → §14.1 — silent on which module owns the values.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.2 — rules_version is the full sha256 hex of the file's bytes
Decision: `rules_version = sha256(utf8 bytes of signals.config.yaml)`, 64 hex chars, not truncated and not derived from the parsed document.
Why: main §7.10 and tech §2 both say "content hash". Hashing raw bytes rather than the parsed tree means a comment or ordering change also produces a new version — correct, because main §8.5's calibration posture treats every edit to this file as a reviewable change, and §14.7's weekly review breaks PostHog down by `rules_version`.
Nearest spec: main §7.10 — "its content hash", no format given.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.2 — Locale overrides are validated after merging, not in isolation
Decision: A locale layer restates only the keys it changes. The loader deep-merges it over `defaults` and validates the *merged* layer against the full schema (`additionalProperties: false`).
Why: A partial layer cannot be validated on its own without a second, weaker schema — which is exactly how a typo (`monthly_search_volume_typo`) becomes a value that is silently never read. Validating the merge turns that into a startup failure. Tested.
Nearest spec: main §7.10 — specifies the layering, not its validation.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.2 — Hand-written TypeScript types alongside the JSON Schema
Decision: `packages/rules/src/types.ts` mirrors `schema/signals.config.schema.json` by hand rather than being generated from it.
Why: Two sources of truth, accepted knowingly. The generator route (`json-schema-to-ts`) adds a dependency and a build step to a package that deliberately has neither, for a document that changes only through the §8.5 calibration process. The 100-row threshold-enumeration test walks the config through the typed accessor, so a drift between schema and types surfaces as a test failure rather than as a runtime surprise. Revisit if the document starts changing often.
Nearest spec: none — internal to packages/rules.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.2 — Committed snapshot is the fully resolved config, defaults plus every locale
Decision: `packages/rules/snapshot/loaded-config.json` holds `rules_version`, the resolved defaults, and each locale's fully merged layer, written and checked by vitest's file snapshot.
Why: A snapshot of the raw YAML would only restate the file. Snapshotting the *resolved* layers makes the merge semantics reviewable in a diff — a change to one default that silently alters twenty locale layers shows up as twenty lines in the PR. Also means editing a threshold without updating the snapshot fails CI, so no threshold change lands unreviewed.
Nearest spec: build plan T0.2 done-when — "snapshot of the loaded config committed".

## 2026-08-27 — T0.1 — pnpm workspaces with no build-orchestrator layer
Decision: Monorepo is plain pnpm workspaces (`apps/*`, `packages/*`, `tools/*`) with fan-out via `pnpm -r`. No Turborepo/Nx.
Why: The constitution names the package list and the tech spec names a single deployable; neither implies a task graph. One `pnpm -r run typecheck` and one root `vitest`/`eslint` invocation cover CI today, and adding a cache layer is reversible at any point without touching package boundaries.
Nearest spec: tech §2 — names the stack, silent on build tooling.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.1 — Workspace packages are consumed as TypeScript source
Decision: `@sortiva/*` packages point `main`/`types`/`exports` at `src/*.ts` and have no build step. `apps/web` lists them in `transpilePackages`; vitest and `tsc` read the sources directly.
Why: Removes an entire build-ordering problem between eight packages for a codebase that ships as one Next.js deployable. Consequence a later card must respect: any non-Next consumer (the Graphile worker bootstrap, scripts) must run through a TS-aware loader, which the worker bootstrap in T0.4 does.
Nearest spec: tech §2.1 — "same codebase", one image; silent on module format.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.1 — Constitution lint rules are a local ESLint plugin, not `no-restricted-imports`
Decision: `tools/eslint-plugin-sortiva` provides three rules: `no-direct-provider-sdk` (invariant 25), `no-threshold-literals` (invariant 9), `route-handler-imports` (code-structure rule).
Why: Two of the three cannot be expressed with built-in rules. The threshold ban must inspect *what a number is compared against*, and the route-handler rule must be an allowlist (handlers may import only core + serialisers) where `no-restricted-imports` only expresses a denylist. Making the SDK ban a custom rule too keeps all three in one place with spec citations in the messages, and makes the vendor list extensible as `SeoDataProvider`/`EmailProvider`/Stripe/PostHog wrappers land.
Nearest spec: main §7.10, §14.7; CLAUDE.md code-structure rules.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.1 — Threshold-literal rule: field-term list and test exemption
Decision: `no-threshold-literals` fires on a numeric literal compared against an identifier or property whose name (camelCase normalised to snake_case, whole name or trailing word) is one of `volume, position, impression(s), click(s), ctr`. It is disabled inside `packages/rules`, and inside `*.test.ts` / `fixtures/`.
Why: The constitution's wording is "numeric comparisons against volume/position/impression/CTR fields"; this is that list made mechanical. The test exemption is necessary because the tests that prove `packages/rules` serves the right numbers must state those numbers. The risk it accepts: a threshold smuggled into production code through an intermediate variable with an unrelated name is not caught by lint — the T0.2 config-key enumeration test is the second line of defence.
Nearest spec: main §7.10 — mandates the lint, does not define its matcher.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.1 — Planted lint violations are a CI check, not a one-time demonstration
Decision: `pnpm lint:prove` (`scripts/prove-lint.mjs`) writes the two violations the card names into throwaway files, asserts `eslint` rejects each with the expected rule id, and deletes them. It runs in CI.
Why: The done-when is a property of the lint config that can silently regress (a broadened ignore, a disabled rule). Proving it once at authoring time proves nothing about next month.
Nearest spec: build plan T0.1 done-when — silent on how the proof is retained.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.1 — Vitest as the unit/integration runner; Playwright reserved for UI flows
Decision: Vitest at the repo root for every `*.test.ts`; Playwright is scaffolded separately in T0.6 for the flows tech §6 assigns to it.
Why: tech §6 names Playwright only for UI flows and leaves everything else as "unit/integration tests". One root vitest project keeps the fixed-name suites the constitution requires (`distillation.eval`, `judge.eval`, `persona.smoke`, `signals.fixtures`, `chaos`) discoverable by a single glob.
Nearest spec: tech §6 — names the mapping, not the runner.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.1 — Local Postgres on port 54329
Decision: `docker-compose.yml` maps Postgres 16 to host port 54329, not 5432.
Why: Avoids colliding with a developer's system Postgres (one is installed on this machine). Purely local; CI and Railway are unaffected.
Nearest spec: tech §5 — "local: docker-compose Postgres", silent on ports.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.1 — `.env` keeps non-secret local defaults rather than being literally empty
Decision: `.env` mirrors every key in `.env.example`; secrets are blank, but `DATABASE_URL`, `NODE_ENV`, `APP_URL`, `POSTHOG_HOST`, `SEO_PROVIDER_MODE=mock` and the `WORKER_*` values are carried over. `pnpm env:check` fails if the two files' key sets ever diverge.
Why: The founder's instruction was "an identical `.env` with empty values"; blanking the local database URL and the mock-provider switch would make a fresh clone fail to run tests, which is the opposite of the intent. Flagged rather than resolved silently. `SEO_PROVIDER_MODE=mock` in particular is a cost guard — tech §5 puts real DataForSEO spend in prod only.
Nearest spec: tech §4 — "`.env.example` documents every required variable", silent on `.env`.

## 2026-08-27 — T0.1 — Railway `postgres` is template-provisioned, not declared in `railway.toml`
Decision: `railway.toml` configures the `app` service only (build, start command with `--max-old-space-size=384`, healthcheck on `/api/health`, `sleepApplication = false`, one replica). The `postgres` service is created from Railway's Postgres template and reaches `app` via `DATABASE_URL`.
Why: Railway's repo-level config describes the service built from the repo; database services are provisioned from templates and cannot be declared there. This is a Railway constraint, not a departure from tech §2.1's two-service topology.
Nearest spec: tech §2.1 — specifies the topology, not the file that expresses it.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.4 — A family's axis names come from the merchant's own tags and our fact sheet, and Shopify's `options` field stays unrequested
Decision: an axis name is drawn from one of three places, all of which we already hold: a tag the merchant wrote as a key and a value (`terrain:technical` — the key is *their* word for the axis), a fact-sheet field name (`material`, `care`, `origin` — our nine, extracted from their description), or the variant word class a split-variant title carried (`color`, `size`). The catalogue sync's Shopify field list is **not** changed, and nothing about product metafields changes either.
Why: `T2.3` reported, and the integrator confirmed, that Shopify's `options` field — which names a product's variant axes — is not requested and is stored nowhere, and asked whether `T2.4` needs it. It does not, for two reasons. First, `options` names the wrong axes: it names what one product's *variants* differ along ("Size", "Colour"), where main §6.4 asks what a *family's members* differ along ("terrain, drop, width") — a different question over a different set of products, and §6.4 derives it from "attribute sets [that] match except along one or more axes", i.e. from fact sheets, not from variant metadata. Second, even if it were wanted there is nowhere to put it: `products` has no column for option names, and adding one is a schema wave's decision, not a feature card's. So the field-list change would buy a value we could not store, to answer a question it does not answer.
What this costs, stated plainly: a store that keeps its attributes in Shopify metafields or in option names rather than in tags or in its descriptions yields fewer axes — the family is still correct, it simply reports less about what makes its members different. **For a schema wave and the integrator: a `products.options` / `products.metafields` column plus the two words of field list would add a fourth source and is the single highest-value thing that could be added here.** It works retroactively for nothing — existing rows would carry it only after the next full sync — which is one more reason it belongs in a wave rather than in this card.
Nearest spec: main §6.4 ("the differing axes are recorded"), main §6.3 (`variant_axes`); DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T2.3.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.4 — Collections are not a grouping signal, because no table holds which products are in one
Decision: the taxonomy signal reads `product_type` and nothing else. A family it produces is recorded with `grouping_source = 'collection'` — the schema's value for the merchant-taxonomy signal — and the exact taxonomy name that produced it is written into the family's merged sheet, so provenance names the real thing rather than the enum's approximation.
Why: main §6.4 calls collections "the primary grouping candidate", and this build stores no product-to-collection membership anywhere. The catalogue sync (`T2.2`) does not fetch collections; the store-page inventory (`T3.2`, another lane) does read a collection's members, but keeps the *families* of those members rather than the members themselves — so using it here would be circular, and it runs nightly rather than during onboarding. Fetching collections would need a table to hold membership, which is a migration. `product_type` is the same kind of statement by the same merchant about the same product, is named in the same §6.4 sentence, and is already in the row. The promo blocklist §6.4 asks for is built and applies to it: a `product_type` of "Summer Sale" groups nothing.
Nearest spec: main §6.4 signal 1; main §13 `product_families.grouping_source`.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.4 — The messy-store fallback clusters on title words, and is not a learned embedding
Decision: the fourth signal — the one §6.4 reserves for stores with no taxonomy, junk collections and sparse facts — compares the words in product titles rather than embedding vectors. Its families are recorded with `grouping_source = 'embedding'`, because that is the schema's only value for this lane, and the merged sheet records `method: 'title_tokens'` so a reader is not misled about what actually ran.
Why: there is no embeddings vendor in this build. `packages/llm` wraps one text-completion model; adding an embeddings provider means a new vendor, a new bill, a new credential and a new entry in the wrapper rule of invariant 25 — a provider decision, not something a feature card takes. The alternatives were to leave the fallback unbuilt, which gives a messy store two hundred families of one and no grouping at all, or to build the honest cheap version. Title words are a real signal for exactly the stores this lane exists for, cost nothing, and are deterministic. Every family it produces is flagged low confidence, which is what §6.4 asks of this lane regardless of how it is implemented. **Swapping in a real embedder later replaces one function and changes nothing else.**
Nearest spec: main §6.4 signal 4; CLAUDE.md invariant 25.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.4 — The grouping thresholds are named constants in the module, not entries in `signals.config.yaml`
Decision: how much two products must agree before they are one family, how closely a split-variant candidate's fact sheet must match, how many members a family needs before its axes are believed, and how alike two titles must be in the fallback all live as exported named constants beside the code that reads them, each with a note saying what moving it would do. All four are marked uncalibrated.
Why: invariant 9 puts thresholds in `packages/rules` — specifically the numbers from main §7.3/§7.6/§8.2/§9.6 that decide whether an opportunity is detected, scored or published, each stamped into `rules_version` so a past decision can be explained. §6.4 is not in that list and states no numbers at all. `packages/rules` is also another lane's directory, which this card may read and not edit. The one number that *is* shared is deliberately not duplicated: the floor a product must clear to take part in similarity merging is `gates.substance_floor.populated_fields_per_product_min`, read from `packages/rules` and passed in — the same bar a product must clear to be written about, so the two can never disagree. **For a later wave: these four are the natural candidates to promote into the config file once a real store has been grouped.**
Nearest spec: main §6.4 (no numbers given); CLAUDE.md invariant 9; the precedent in DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T3.2.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.4 — A sparse product sits out similarity merging but not the merchant's own filing
Decision: a product below the substance floor is never merged on the strength of how similar it looks — not by fact-sheet clustering and not by the fallback — and stays a family of one. It *is* still placed by `product_type` and by a split-variant title match.
Why: main §6.4's guardrail says "products below a fact-count floor don't participate in **similarity merging**" and that "sparse must read as *unknown*, never as *same*". Two empty fact sheets agree on nothing and disagree on nothing, which any similarity measure reads as a perfect match — that is the collapse the guardrail exists to prevent, and it is an inference we would be drawing. A merchant putting a bare product in "Trail Running Shoes" is not an inference; it is a statement they made, and overriding it would mean telling a merchant their own filing is wrong because we could not read the description. The done-when "sparse products stay singleton" is tested against the similarity path, which is the one the guardrail governs.
Nearest spec: main §6.4 ("Guardrails — No merging on sparse data").

## 2026-09-02 — T2.4 — A family is recognised across runs by its name, because there is no key column
Decision: recomputing a store's families matches each planned family to an existing row by `product_families.name`, updates it in place, inserts what is new and deletes what no longer exists. Names are made unique within a store by suffixing.
Why: family ids are pointed at from `products.family_id` and from `store_pages.family_ids`, so replacing every family on every run would break both nightly. Recognising a family needs a stable derived key, and `product_families` has no column for one — this card may not add one. The name is already derived deterministically from what produced the family (the taxonomy name, the residual title of a split-variant group, the words a cluster's titles share), so it *is* that key, and it has the side benefit of being the thing the merchant sees. The cost: a merchant-visible rename would read as a delete and an insert. **For a later wave: a `grouping_key` column on `product_families` would separate identity from the label and remove that.**
Nearest spec: main §13 `product_families`; main §6.4 ("recompute … for affected products only").

## 2026-09-02 — T2.4 — CONTRADICTION: the frozen route contract names three grouping sources where the spec and the database have four
Decision: nothing changed. The database keeps the spec's four values (`collection | split_variant | fact_cluster | embedding`), which is what this card writes.
Why: `familySchema.groupingSource` in `packages/core/src/api/schemas.ts` is `'taxonomy' | 'fact_clustering' | 'embedding'` — three values, differently spelled, with no equivalent of `split_variant`. Main §13 and the `family_grouping_source` database enum both carry four. The two routes that would expose the difference are `GET /api/products/families` and the families block of the profile response, neither of which this card builds; the report endpoint it does build carries no grouping source. So nothing is broken today, and a frozen contract is the integrator's to re-freeze. **Whoever builds either of those routes has to map four values onto three and will have to decide what a split-variant family reports** — flagged here rather than resolved.
Nearest spec: main §13 `product_families.grouping_source`; the frozen route contract, `familySchema`.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.4 — A wrong-grouping report is written to our own log, because there is no support-feedback table
Decision: `POST /api/products/families/report` checks the family belongs to the caller, writes the merchant's words to the structured application log as `family_grouping.reported` with the family id beside them, emits `family_grouping_reported` to analytics carrying the family id, the member count and the grouping source — and changes nothing about the family.
Why: ui §3.7 asks for "a support/feedback record + PostHog event". The event half is unambiguous and invariant 26 fixes its contents: ids and aggregates only, so what the merchant typed may not go there — it is free prose naming their own products. The record half has nowhere to live: no support or feedback table exists in this schema and a feature card may not add one. The log is durable, is already where every operational record of what a merchant did lives, and carries the family id, so a report can be traced back to the exact grouping that prompted it. Both halves are asserted on the bytes rather than on intent: one test reads the merchant's sentence out of the log and then proves it is absent from the event. **For a schema wave: a `feedback` table would make these reports queryable rather than greppable, which is what anyone acting on them will want.**
Nearest spec: ui §3.7 item 5; main §6.4 ("a 'report wrong grouping' escape hatch exists"); CLAUDE.md invariant 26.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.4 — Family grouping keeps no checkpoint, unlike the two steps before it
Decision: the `family_group` step runs in one pass and saves no checkpoint.
Why: invariant 18 requires a checkpoint on any step that can run past sixty seconds. This one cannot: it makes no network call and pays nobody — two queries and some arithmetic over data already in our tables — where the catalogue sync is eight minutes of paced Shopify reads and distillation is a model call per product. A checkpoint would also be answering the wrong question: a family is a property of the whole catalogue, not of a page of it, so a half-finished grouping is not a position to resume from. The step is still effectively-once by the ordinary route — a key derived from every fact sheet's timestamp and every product's fingerprint — and the whole reconciliation is one transaction, so a crash leaves the previous families intact rather than a half-regrouped store. Proved by the test that runs the step twice and gets the stored answer back without executing.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.1–14.3.4; CLAUDE.md invariant 18.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.4 — Onboarding's fifth step reports its own analytics, and the ingestion bundle gained a capture port
Decision: `IngestionDeps` takes an optional `capture`, bound in the Shopify composition root to the same `PosthogServerCapture` the model wrapper already uses there. `family_grouping_completed` carries the account, how many products were grouped, how many families resulted, how many have no axes, how many rows were merged as one product, the count by grouping signal, and how many are low confidence.
Why: the card names the event and nothing in the ingestion bundle could emit one — the steps had a notification emitter and no analytics client. Optional rather than required, so a missing client can never fail the work it was only reporting on. Every property is a count or an enum name: a family's *name* is the merchant's word for their own products, which is store content, and a test asserts none of the fixture's names, titles, tags or attribute values appears in the serialised event.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 (server-side capture); CLAUDE.md invariant 26.

## 2026-09-02 — T2.4 — The report route reads families through a store port, not a database handle
Decision: a new `makeFamilyStore` in `packages/db/src/stores/`, exposing `find` and `list`, both taking an `AccountScope`. The route handler holds it and holds no database.
Why: the route first imported the database handle directly and `pnpm lint` refused it — the rule exists because a handle outside `packages/db` is how a query ends up running without naming the account whose data it touches. This is the same shape and the same answer as `makeWebhookEventStore` and `makeNotificationStore` (DECISIONS 2026-09-02, T8.2): the handle stays in the package that owns it, the scope stays mandatory, and an integration test hands in its own isolated database, which is what lets the route test run end to end against real Postgres rather than against a mock.
Nearest spec: tech §3; CLAUDE.md code-structure rules ("every repository method requires an `accountId` scope parameter").

## 2026-09-02 — T2.4 — The onboarding walk's end marker moved from grouping to the persona
Decision: `ingestion.test.ts`'s assertion that a resumed run stops at `family_group` because it "belongs to a later card" now says the run executes it and stops at `persona`.
Why: `T2.3` wrote that assertion naming this card as the one that would change it, exactly as `T2.2` did for `T2.3`. It is one line in another card's test and is the intended way the marker moves down the pipeline; `T2.5` will move it again, to `keywords_competitors`.
Nearest spec: main §6.4–6.5 (the step order); the build plan's card list.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.7 — Settings & the notification bell, built on top of a contract that already existed
Decision: all four Settings screens (`packages/ui/src/settings/`) and the bell (`packages/ui/src/shell/NotificationBell.tsx`) are built against routes, schemas, fixtures and copy that earlier cards (chiefly `T1.2a`, `T8.1`–`T8.3`) had already written in anticipation of this card — `GET/PATCH /api/settings`, the blog-picker routes, `POST /api/account/delete`, the notification routes, and the `settings.deleteAccount.*` / `notification.*` copy all existed untouched before this session. Nothing in that layer was changed except two small, in-lane corrections recorded below.
Why: recorded so the two facts don't read as one thing built to look like it was always there — the contract was genuinely pre-built; only the screens and the bell are new in this card.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.7 — Two settings-screen URLs were already load-bearing elsewhere, so they were adopted rather than invented
Decision: Settings → Account lives at `/settings/account` and the "payment failed" banner's link (`packages/ui/src/shell/banners.ts`) was changed from `/settings/billing` (a path nothing else in the repo used) to `/settings/account` to match it. Settings → Connections lives at `/settings/connections`.
Why: `packages/core/src/billing/checkout.ts`'s `openBillingPortal` already hard-codes the Stripe Customer Portal's return address as `/settings/account?billing=returned`, and `apps/web/app/api/gsc/_lib/config.ts`'s `GSC_RETURN_PATH` already hard-codes `/settings/connections` as where Google sends a merchant back to. Both were written before Settings existed, by cards that could only guess at the URL. Building the real screen at a third address would have left the Portal and the Google return both landing on a page that does not answer them; matching what those two already assume was the only reading that makes both flows land somewhere real. The banner's old href pointed at a page nothing else expected, so it was corrected in the same file — `packages/ui` is Lane F's own directory, and no other lane's code named `/settings/billing`.
Nearest spec: ui §9.4 (billing lives under Account, not a screen of its own); main §9.5 / tech §1.6 (the two return trips).

## 2026-09-03 — T9.7 — The auto-publish write-scope grant has no backend to run against; the flow is built and wired to the one route that exists, not invented
Decision: `PublishingSettings`'s "grant posting access" step calls `POST /api/shopify/oauth/start` — the only Shopify OAuth-start route in the contract — because it is the sole thing there is to build the click-through against on the mock server this lane runs on. Nothing was added to `packages/core/src/api/routes.ts` or to the Shopify provider.
Why: main §9.5 asks for a *second*, separate OAuth pass that adds `write_content`, distinct from the read-only grant made at install. `packages/core/src/catalog/scopes.ts` hard-codes `SHOPIFY_READ_SCOPES` into the one `authorizeUrl` the provider builds, and `apps/web/app/api/shopify/_lib/handlers.ts`'s callback calls `assertReadOnlyGrant` and **discards the token** if Shopify ever hands back a write scope — behaviour a T2.1 test holds in place on purpose (invariant 21). So today, clicking "Grant access" in the real product would send the merchant through a consent screen and then have its own backend throw the result away. This is a genuine gap in the interface another lane owns (`apps/web/app/api/shopify`, `packages/providers/shopify`, and the shared route table), not a decision this card is positioned to make — a second start route, a second callback path that keeps rather than drops a write grant, and where it returns the merchant to, are all choices with consequences for Lane B and the integrator. Flagged rather than guessed. Also unresolved for the same reason: `settingsSchema` carries no field naming which blog is currently the auto-publish target, only `GET /api/settings/blogs` (what could be chosen), so the "Target blog" row can offer to change the target but cannot state it.
Nearest spec: main §9.5, §14.1 (invariant 21); build plan §3 (lane ownership of `apps/web/app/api/shopify`).

## 2026-09-03 — T9.7 — Store profile's business fields and top-seller order are shown, not made editable, in Settings
Decision: `StoreProfileSettings` renders the description, language, country, audience, tone and the ranked top-sellers as plain text. Only keywords, competitors and the family report — each with its own standing route — are live controls, exactly as they are during onboarding.
Why: ui §9.2 calls this screen "the §3.7 confirmation screen, permanently editable", and main §6.8 promises an edit here never re-triggers full ingestion. But the only route that writes the business-profile fields or the top-seller ranking, `POST /api/profile/confirm`, answers `profile_already_confirmed` once the store has been confirmed — there is no route in the contract that accepts a later edit to any of them. Building an editor with nowhere to send its save would either silently do nothing or need a new endpoint in `apps/web/app/api/profile`, which is Lane B's directory. Recorded as a gap rather than guessed past: whoever owns that route next either adds a profile-update endpoint or the spec's "permanently editable" is scoped down to the three fields that already are.
Nearest spec: main §6.8 (edits never re-trigger ingestion); ui §9.2; build plan §3 (Lane B owns `apps/web/app/api/profile`).

## 2026-09-03 — T9.7 — The bell renders every notification's generic line, because resolving a reference into display text has no caller anywhere yet
Decision: `notificationLine` (`packages/ui/src/shell/notifications.ts`) calls `renderNotification({ type, refs })` from `@sortiva/core` with no `resolved` argument. Every notification type whose sentence needs a resolved value (an article's title, a page's name) therefore always shows its `.generic` sibling — "An article was published" rather than the article's name.
Why: `renderNotification` was built already (by `T8.1`) with `resolved` as an explicit, separate argument for exactly this reason, and grepping the whole repository turns up no caller anywhere — not in the email pipeline `T8.2` built, not in a route — that ever supplies one. Resolving a `refs` id into today's display text needs a lookup against the thing it names (an article by id, an opportunity by id), and no route answers that; building one is a job for whichever lane owns the entity in question, not this one. This is the renderer's own designed degrade path rather than a shortcut taken here — the same fallback already fires for a notification naming something since deleted — but it means every notification the bell shows today is generic. Flagged because it changes what the bell visibly says, not because it required a decision this card could make differently.
Nearest spec: main §9.6.8 (the why-line pattern this mirrors); tech §1.2 (payloads are references, resolved at render time).

## 2026-09-03 — T9.7 — The IANA timezone picker pre-selects the country default and offers every zone, rather than a filtered per-country list
Decision: `timezoneOptions()` (`packages/ui/src/settings/settings.ts`) returns the single zone `packages/core`'s country table already computes as `suggested`, plus every zone `Intl.supportedValuesOf('timeZone')` knows as the full override list.
Why: ui §9.1 asks for "an IANA picker pre-filtered to the persona country's zones, override allowed". `packages/core/src/persona/timezones.ts` records exactly one default zone per country — the one onboarding actually wrote — not the set of zones a country spans, so there is no per-country *list* in the codebase to filter down to. Building one (a full IANA zone-to-country table) is a data-modelling addition to `packages/core`, outside what this card was asked to do and outside Lane F's directories. Pre-selecting the country's default and offering the complete zone list as the override satisfies both halves of the sentence — a sensible starting point, and nothing withheld — without inventing geography data. A country genuinely spanning zones (the five main §9.4 names) still starts on the right one; a merchant who wants a different zone in that same country picks it from the full list rather than a pre-filtered subset.
Nearest spec: main §9.4 (the timezone default rule); ui §9.1.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.7 — The five Settings routes were classified for session-replay eligibility
Decision: in `packages/ui/src/analytics/replay.ts`'s `VIEW_CONTENT` — the list `T-ANALYTICS` built that a test walks against every route this app serves — `/settings/publishing`, `/settings/profile` and `/settings/connections` are `store_data` (blog names, the reused confirmation-screen business profile, and the merchant's own claimed domain all render there); `/settings` (a redirect with no content) and `/settings/account` (billing status, vacation mode, email preferences, UI language — no catalogue, calendar or search content) are `no_store_data`.
Why: `T-ANALYTICS`'s own test fails a build the moment a new view under `apps/web/app/(app)/**` has no entry here, precisely so a screen cannot start recording merchant data by omission. Five new routes needed a classification to keep that test green; the reasoning above is the same the file already gives for the other store screens.
Nearest spec: `DECISIONS.md` 2026-09-02 `T-ANALYTICS`; main §14.7.

## 2026-09-03 — T4.0 — `gate_decisions.outcome` is free text, not a closed enum
Decision: the `outcome` column on `gate_decisions` (the audit row written every time a topic clears or fails one of the three quality gates) is a plain `text` column, not a Postgres enum.
Why: Gate 1 alone has at least five distinct outcomes on its own (pass; auto-reject for zero search demand; not winnable; off-catalog; converted to an OPTIMIZE/REFRESH instead of a new article), the manual-add path adds its own "proceed" / "proceed with a warning" pair (main §8.7), and Gate 3 adds `overridden` (main §8.6). None of that vocabulary is fixed by any spec, and deciding it is explicitly `T4.1`–`T4.4`'s job, none of which is built yet. A Postgres enum is a closed set: adding a value later needs a migration, and only a schema-wave card may add one — so a guessed-now enum would either block those cards on a value they discover they need, or force a founder-approved "hot-add a mini-wave" detour (build plan §3) for what is really just implementation vocabulary. Free text costs nothing today (the column is already indexed by `(topic_id, gate)`, not by outcome) and lets each gate card define and test its own outcome strings without touching this table again.
Nearest spec: main §13 `gate_decisions`; main §8.2, §8.4, §8.6, §8.7 (the outcome shapes cited above, none of them exhaustive).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.0 — `article_claims.staleness` is a new three-band enum, not named by any spec
Decision: `article_claims.staleness` is `stable | seasonal | volatile`, defaulting to `stable`.
Why: the card's own text and content-pointers.md §1 both say a claim carries "how quickly it goes stale," which content-pointers.md §9's refresh-diagnosis table reads as one of the causes a refresh re-checks ("claims aged out"). Neither document says how staleness is measured or banded. A coarse three-value band is enough to let that diagnosis prioritise what to re-verify first without inventing a numeric decay model nobody asked for, and — because it is a genuinely new axis, not a value on top of an existing enum — a bad guess here costs only a column default, not a rewrite of something else's vocabulary. `T4.3` (which writes claims) or `T4.5`/refresh work (which reads staleness) may find this too coarse; that is a decision for whichever of them owns the diagnosis logic, not this card.
Nearest spec: `docs/content-pointers.md` §1 ("how quickly it goes stale"), §9 ("claims aged out").

## 2026-09-03 — T4.0 — `article_product_refs` is reshaped around the reference model, superseding main §13's literal `price_at_write` column
Decision: `article_product_refs` does not have a `price_at_write` column. It has `placeholder_key` (the marker the article body carries instead of a literal value), `fields_rendered` (a `product_ref_field[]` naming which of price/stock/sale_status/url/title this reference stands in for), `resolved_values_json` (the last values actually shown to a reader), and `resolved_at`.
Why: the founder decision of 2026-09-01 journalled under `T4.0` ("Prices live in articles as a reference to the current value, never as a number") retires the idea of a value captured "at write" — under that model nothing is captured at write time at all; a reference is resolved fresh at publish and at every republish, from the live store. Main §13's `price_at_write` column is the pre-decision shape and is superseded by it, as that decision entry itself anticipates ("the reference tokens ... have to exist in T4.0"). A reference can also stand for more than price (stock, sale status, URL, title — content-pointers.md §9), so a single scalar column could not have held the general case even under the old model.
Nearest spec: main §13 `article_product_refs` (superseded column); `docs/content-pointers.md` §9; DECISIONS 2026-09-01 T4.0 (the founder decision itself).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.0 — `article_product_refs.product_id` is nullable with `ON DELETE SET NULL`, because what happens to a deleted product's row is unsettled
Decision: `product_id` is a nullable foreign key to `products.id` with `ON DELETE SET NULL`, not `RESTRICT` and not `CASCADE`.
Why: content-pointers.md §9 requires that when the product a reference points at is gone, "the publish fails and a repair is raised" — which needs the reference row to still exist, naming what it used to point at. But whether a deleted Shopify product's local `products` row is itself hard-deleted or only soft-marked is an open question this codebase has already flagged and left open (`packages/core/src/inventory/events.ts`'s own comment, "what should happen to the inventory row of a page that no longer exists is not settled — see DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T3.2"). `SET NULL` is the one choice that is correct under either eventual answer: if products are hard-deleted, the reference row survives with `product_id = NULL`, which is exactly the "gone" state the repair logic needs to detect; if products are only soft-marked, the foreign key never fires at all and this is moot. `RESTRICT` would have made deleting a product impossible while any article mentions it, which nothing asks for; `CASCADE` would delete the very row that has to exist to raise the repair.
Nearest spec: `docs/content-pointers.md` §9; DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T3.2 (the unsettled question this defers to).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.0 — `incident_findings`: a small table for what an operator found, separate from `ops_flags` and not append-only
Decision: a new table, `incident_findings` (`ops_flag_id` FK → `ops_flags.id` cascade, `author`, `finding`, `created_at`), collected from the integrator's T4.0 preparation list. `ops_flags` is unchanged. `incident_findings` carries no append-only guard.
Why: `T8.4` already decided, on the record, not to build a separate incidents table — it reads `ops_flags` itself as the open-incident list (an auto-trip that has not reset *is* an open incident) and named the one thing that decision could not give an operator: "there is nowhere to write what an operator *found* when they investigated." That gap, not the trip record itself, is what this table exists to hold, so it holds only that: who looked, and what they found, keyed to the trip they were looking into. It cascades from `ops_flags` because a finding about a trip has no meaning once the trip it explains is gone — unlike `spend_events` or `idempotency_ledger`, this is not a financial or completed-work ledger where erasing the parent must never erase the child. No append-only guard, for the same reason: correcting a typo in an operator's own note is not the hazard invariant 18's ledgers guard against, and demanding a fresh row for every correction would make the investigation harder to read, not more honest. More than one finding may attach to the same trip, because an investigation is not always a single note.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 (auto-trip incident records); DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T8.4 (the decision this table completes, which called a real incidents table "a wave-5 candidate" — moved up to this wave by the integrator's T4.0 preparation list); `docs/handoff-next.md` "`T4.0` needs integrator preparation" (item 5).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.0 — `gsc_monthly` / `gsc_query_monthly`: the roll-up drops device and country, keeping only page (and, for the query table, query)
Decision: two new tables. `gsc_monthly` is keyed `(account_id, month, page)`. `gsc_query_monthly` is keyed `(account_id, month, page, query)`. Neither carries `device` or `country` — both are summed away in the roll-up.
Why: `T8.3` pruned `gsc_daily`/`gsc_query_daily` at sixteen months with nowhere to roll the history into, and named "a monthly roll-up table in the next schema wave" as what it owed. Keeping all four of `gsc_daily`'s dimensions (`page, query, device, country`) at monthly grain forever would defeat the reason the roll-up exists — tech §2.1's "Postgres stays small" — for a precision nothing downstream asks for: detection (main §7.3) reads the daily table directly, within its live window, and the weekly-refit CTR curve (`ctr_curve`) does too. What the roll-up exists to answer is coarser and longer-range — main §13 calls `gsc_query_daily` "the input to §7.3 detection," a role this table does not take over — "how did this page (or this page for this query) do, months or years ago," which page/query grain answers without device or country. This is a genuine loss (a store could no longer ask "how did mobile Denmark do for this query, two years ago") and is stated here so it can be revisited if it turns out to matter.
Nearest spec: tech §2.1 ("`gsc_daily` keeps 16 months then rolls up to monthly aggregates … `gsc_query_daily` … rolled up to monthly page × query aggregates"); DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T8.3 (the prune this completes).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.0 — The account-deletion confirmation email gets its own ledger table, not a row in `email_sends`
Decision: a new table, `deletion_confirmation_emails` (`account_id` with no foreign key, `email`, `dedupe_key`, `template_version`, `state`, `provider_message_id`, `queued_at`, `sent_at`, `last_error`; unique on `(account_id, dedupe_key)`). `notification_type` gains one new value, `account_deletion_confirmed`, for the bell/matrix registry's own consistency (every `NotificationType` needs a channel-matrix row and a copy key, enforced by existing code — `packages/core/src/notifications/matrix.ts`'s `assertMatrixCoversEveryType`). Nothing in this card calls `EmailProvider` to actually send this email, or writes to this table — the deletion flow (`packages/core/src/lifecycle/deletion.ts`) is unchanged.
Why: `T8.2` found half the gap (`email_sends.type` had no value for a deletion confirmation) and `T8.3` found the other half: `email_sends` and `notifications` both cascade from `accounts` (`ON DELETE CASCADE`), and the account row is erased about a week after this mail would go out (main §14.6) — so a send recorded in `email_sends` would be destroyed by the very sweep that made it true, erasing the one audit trail ("we told them") that most needs to survive account deletion. `T8.3` recommended "the same treatment the spend ledger and the completed-work ledger already have — no foreign key, pruned by age only," and that is what this table does: same triple `email_sends` uses for its own idempotency (`account_id`, implicit type, `dedupe_key`), minus the foreign key, on the same reasoning `spend_events` already established (DECISIONS 2026-08-31 T2.0) — erasing the payer must not erase the record of the payment; erasing the account must not erase the record that its owner was told it was gone. No `type` column: this table exists for exactly one notification type, so a column repeating that fact would say nothing an index could use. Wiring an actual call into this table is left to whichever future card owns the deletion flow's mail — this card is schema only.
Nearest spec: main §14.6 (deletion sweep timing); tech §1.5 (account-security mail exempt from suppression); DECISIONS 2026-08-31 T2.0 (`spend_events`' no-FK precedent); DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T8.2/T8.3 (the two halves of this gap).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.0 — `products.options` / `products.metafields`: plain JSONB, defaulting to an empty array, populated only from the next full sync
Decision: two new `jsonb NOT NULL DEFAULT '[]'` columns on `products`, storing Shopify's variant option definitions and the product's metafields verbatim (as Shopify returns them), with no shape validation at the database level.
Why: `T2.4` called this "the single highest-value thing that could be added here" and deferred it for lack of a wave — a store that keeps its differentiating attributes in Shopify metafields or option names rather than in tags or descriptions yields fewer family differentiation axes without it. Verbatim JSONB, not a typed shape, because Shopify's own metafield and option shapes are the vendor's vocabulary and validating or normalising them is `packages/providers/shopify`'s and the family-grouping code's job, not the migration's — matching how `variants` and `price_range` on the same table are already stored. Defaulting to `'[]'` rather than allowing null keeps every reader's code the same shape (`for (const o of product.options)` never needs a null check) and matches every existing row until its account's next full catalogue sync backfills the real values — this column, like the ones `T2.0` added under the same reasoning (DECISIONS 2026-08-31 T2.0, "products.checksum" et al.), works only from that point forward.
Nearest spec: DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T2.4 ("a `products.options` / `products.metafields` column ... is the single highest-value thing that could be added here"); main §6.4 (differentiation axes).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.0 — `spend_events.article_id`: no foreign key, on the same ledger reasoning as `account_id`
Decision: `spend_events` gains a nullable `article_id uuid` column with no foreign key to `articles.id`.
Why: `article_cost_finalized` (`packages/core/src/ops/article-cost.ts`) exists and is tested, but as `T8.4`'s own entry records, "the caller will have to sum the calls it made itself" without a column to filter this ledger by article. The reasoning for no foreign key is identical to `account_id`'s own, stated on that column already: `spend_events` is append-only and a cost that has been incurred is never erased by anything happening to what it was spent on — an article being discarded, rejected, or never finished must not erase the record of what it cost to attempt it. Nullable because most spend (persona, keyword enrichment, preview) is not attributable to a single article at all.
Nearest spec: main §14.7 (`article_cost_finalized`); DECISIONS 2026-08-31 T2.0 (`spend_events`' append-only, no-FK shape, established for `account_id`); DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T8.4 (the gap this column closes).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.0 — Smaller shape choices main §13 leaves open, recorded together
Decision, in one entry because none is large enough to need its own:
- `article_claims.confidence` reuses the existing `confidence_band` enum (`low | medium | high`) rather than a new one or a 0–100 integer like `opportunities.confidence`. Claims are judged qualitatively by whoever plans them (`T4.3`), not scored by a formula the way opportunity ranking is, so a band fits the same way it already fits `product_families.confidence`.
- `article_claims.evidence_json` and `article_product_refs.resolved_values_json` are JSONB rather than child tables. Evidence entries vary in shape by claim kind (a product ref, a page ref, a quoted passage, or — for a derived fact or a recommendation — a reference to another claim in the same article), and resolved values vary by which fields a reference renders; a child table would need its own polymorphic-reference machinery for a shape `T4.3`–`T5.2` may still be refining. JSONB is what the existing `evidence_json` on `opportunities` already does for the same reason.
- `topics` and `articles` both gain plain `updated_at` bookkeeping columns beyond what main §13's summary lists. Both are state machines with guarded transitions (`topics.state`, `articles.state`) — the same shape `job_steps.updated_at` already serves for guarded transitions elsewhere — and a guard that cannot see when a row last changed cannot reason about staleness or races.
- `articles.delivery` reuses `delivery_mode` (the enum `account_settings.delivery` already uses), defaulting to `'export'`, rather than a new enum or free text — an article's delivery mode is the same concept as the account's, just possibly captured per-article for accounts that change the setting mid-flight.
Why, for all four: none is dictated by a spec section, none changes any interface another lane consumes (nothing outside `packages/db` reads these columns yet — the tables are new), and each is reversible by a later schema wave if the card that actually writes through it (`T4.1`–`T4.6`) finds the shape wrong. Recorded together rather than left silent, per CLAUDE.md's "every choice the specs don't dictate, immediately."
Nearest spec: main §13 (all four tables/columns); main §7.4's `evidence_json` on `opportunities` (the JSONB-evidence precedent).

## 2026-09-03 — FOUNDER — The competitor-gap signal fires unless we already rank in the top 10, not the top 20
Decision: `signals.competitor_coverage_gap.our_absent_position_max` moves from 20 to 10 in `signals.config.yaml`. `rules_version` changes with it, from `9fb26ae9…` to `2bd23161…`. No code changes; the detector already reads the number. `T3.6`'s done-when stands as written and its #18 fixture is now producible.
Why: main §7.3's rule ("we hold no position 20 or better") and main §7.8's worked example plus the same row's own action column ("our page at #18 → improve it") contradict each other — #18 is inside "20 or better", so the literal rule silences the very case the example illustrates. The config already carried `optimize_position_min: 11` / `optimize_position_max: 30`, an improve-the-page-you-have band that under the literal rule could only ever be reached at 21–30. `T3.5` shipped the literal rule and confirmed by running the detector that a live position-18 input produces nothing; `T3.5`'s scheduled audit re-derived the same contradiction independently. Put to the founder as question 8 with both one-line fixes costed; the founder chose to open positions 11–20 rather than reword `T3.6`. This produces more detections than §7.3 as literally written allows, and every one of them lands in the OPTIMIZE band rather than proposing a competing new URL.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (the rule, now departed from), main §7.8 (the example this now matches); `docs/overnight-state.md` "Questions waiting on the founder", question 8.

## 2026-09-03 — FOUNDER — A deleted store page gets a status field, not a deleted-at date; it needs a mini-wave
Decision: when a page disappears from a merchant's store, `store_pages` records it with a status field naming which condition the row is in (live / gone, with room for "moved" or "unreachable" later), not a single deleted-at timestamp and not an inferred "not seen by the last completed walk". Schema wave 3 (`T4.0`) closed without it, deliberately, so this needs a mini-wave card; nothing is built yet.
Why: open since `T3.2`, deferred by `T8.0` and again by `T4.0` because whatever shape is chosen is read by `T3.5`'s existing-target check — the thing that stops the product proposing a new page for something an existing page already covers, and which today can recommend improving a page that no longer exists. The founder chose the status field over the date on the grounds that it costs nothing extra today and avoids a second migration if a third condition appears. The sweep-inference option was rejected because it is only safe once something also records that a walk *completed* — an interrupted walk would otherwise mark live pages as gone.
Nearest spec: main §12.3 (content inventory); `docs/overnight-state.md` "Questions waiting on the founder", question 1.

## 2026-09-03 — FOUNDER — The recurring job schedule stays off until the four missing handlers land
Decision: the crontab is not switched on and the worker's all-handlers-present rule is not relaxed. No change to any file; recorded so the next session does not re-open it.
Why: the worker refuses to start any recurring job until every crontab entry has a handler, and the handlerless count fell from ten to four over the run (`generation_cycle_daily`, `signal_scan_weekly`, `replenishment_monthly`, `publish_intent_recovery_sweep`) — all four belong to Lanes C and D, whose cards are next in line anyway. Waiting is therefore a real option rather than a stall, which it was not that morning. What stays dormant until then, and is not a surprise: nothing reads the spend meter so spend is unbounded, the preview cap (the one paid path a stranger can trigger) has no brake, no deleted account is ever actually erased and nothing is pruned, and no scheduled mail goes out. The manual kill switches do work — `T8.4` put the check at dequeue, so anything queued by a request or webhook is gated; only the automatic trips never fire.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 (kill switches and auto-trips); `docs/overnight-state.md` "Questions waiting on the founder", question 4.

## 2026-09-03 — FOUNDER — `R-PRIVACY` is authorised now, and queued behind `T2.7` because Lane B is live
Decision: the founder authorised fixing `R-PRIVACY` immediately rather than holding it until Shopify credentials exist. It is not started: Lane B is mid-`T2.7` in the same directories, so it is dispatched to Lane B once `T2.7` merges. No files changed by this entry.
Why: the fix's only stated blocker was that Lane G was building in that territory when the finding was carded; Lane G finished M8 and is idle, so that objection has expired. The card is unchanged from build plan §7 — stop storing the whole webhook body for the three privacy topics, keep the non-personal envelope, and prove it with a planted realistic `customers/redact` body asserted absent from the stored row. Starting it inside a live worktree would collide with a running card, which is why it waits on the merge rather than on the decision.
Nearest spec: main §14.6, §6.2, §17.3; invariant 4; build plan §7 (`R-PRIVACY`).

## 2026-09-03 — FOUNDER — An outside SEO tier list was checked against the build; scope is unchanged, and one item on the list is out of date
Decision: an SEO consultant ranked nine ecommerce tactics for the founder (alt text D, internal linking A, meta description E, product FAQs A, faceted navigation A, review schema B, Google Merchant Center super, product reviews A, digital PR super). Each was mapped against the spec and the code. **No scope changes.** Google Merchant Center stays at V1.5/V2 (main §17.2). Faceted navigation is not added and the gap is stated plainly rather than half-covered. Digital PR / off-site stays out, with competitor link-gap work left where the roadmap already has it (main §17, V2). Product reviews stay out — collecting them is a different product, and review *text* is already a distillation input (main §6.3).
Why: three of the nine are already V1 scope and unbuilt rather than missing — internal links, FAQ copy and meta/title all appear in the OPTIMIZE recommendation JSON (main §10.3) and are carded as `T6.1`–`T6.3`, which have not started (`packages/core/src/optimize` and `.../fix` do not exist). Two more are declared and deliberately deferred: product structured data is P1 "flag only, deep schema automation V1.5" and internal-link-gap/orphan detection is P1 blocked on a crawler, because Shopify's Admin API does not expose navigation menus and without them an orphan cannot be told from a header-linked page (main §11). The four absent ones are absent by prior decision, and two of them are structurally unreachable rather than merely unscheduled: faceted navigation lives in theme code and URL handling, which invariant 21 and main §11's own rule forbid Sortiva from touching, and detecting it needs the full site crawler main §18 rules out; digital PR is human outreach, not a tool feature. **Meta description stays P0 despite its E rating**, because the rating is about rankings, where it is correct, while Sortiva uses it for click-through — main §7.3's low-CTR-at-strong-rank signal is exactly the case a rewritten description addresses, and main §11 notes it costs a database query with no model or search-data spend.
Nearest spec: main §10.1–10.4 (OPTIMIZE), §11 (the FIX table and its rules), §17.2 (Merchant Center), §18 (out of scope); invariant 21; build plan M6.

## 2026-09-03 — REFERENCE — FAQ markup no longer earns a search result feature, and the content guidance assumes it does
Decision: `docs/content-pointers.md` §4's FAQ paragraph gains one sentence recording that FAQ structured data no longer produces a rich result. Nothing else changes: the FAQ block stays conditional, the verbatim-heading and self-contained-first-paragraph rules stand, and no card is told to stop emitting the markup.
Why: Google retired FAQ rich results for all sites on 2026-05-07, superseding the 2023 change that had limited them to government and health domains. The guidance M4 and M5 build from ends its FAQ rule with "produces structured data that answers nothing", which reads as though the markup still earns a search feature — a card following it could reasonably invest in the markup for a payoff that no longer exists. **The reason to keep FAQ content is unchanged and still strong**: it covers buyer questions the body does not, and it is the shape AI answer engines cite. The reason to keep the *markup* is now only that it is nearly free and machine-readable, not that it wins a SERP feature. Recorded because this is a dated external fact the build cannot discover for itself, and because the same fact should stop anyone adding `FAQPage` markup to the OPTIMIZE recommendation in `T6.2` expecting a rich result. Sortiva generates no structured data anywhere today — the only JSON-LD in the codebase is read-only, in the preview extractor (`packages/core/src/preview/extract.ts`).
Nearest spec: `docs/content-pointers.md` §4 (FAQ conditionality); main §10.3 (the OPTIMIZE `faq` block); build plan `T6.2`.

## 2026-09-03 — R-PRIVACY — A privacy request is stored by allowlist, keeping the store and the order ids, dropping the shopper entirely
Decision: `storableWebhookBody` (`packages/core/src/catalog/webhooks.ts`) keeps exactly four top-level fields from Shopify's three privacy topics — `shop_id`, `shop_domain`, `orders_to_redact`, `orders_requested` — and drops everything else, including the whole `customer` object and the `data_request` object. An allowed key whose value is an object or an array of objects is also dropped. Every topic is passed through the function, not only the privacy ones; non-privacy bodies are returned by identity. The receiver reduces before writing rather than after.
Why: the card offered two shapes — reduce to the non-personal envelope, or store no body at all — and the envelope was chosen because it is what makes the stored row an audit trail rather than a bare timestamp: it records that this store's shopper asked, about these orders, and that we answered. **Allowlist rather than denylist** because the two fail in opposite directions: a denylist ("drop `customer`") keeps whatever Shopify adds to the message later, and what a vendor adds to a message *about a shopper* is likely to be about that shopper. The value-shape guard exists because `customer` is precisely a harmless-looking name over a value that grew; a key allowlist alone would have let `shop_domain: { owner_email: … }` through. **`customer.id` is dropped, not just the email and phone** — a Shopify customer id names one of the merchant's shoppers, and invariant 4 says we hold none; nothing in the drain reads it. `data_request.id` is dropped for the same reason plus a simpler one: nothing reads it, and Shopify's signed delivery id already identifies the event uniquely. Reducing before the write, rather than cleaning up afterwards, because any gap between the two is a period in which the answer we give Shopify is false.
Nearest spec: main §14.6 (the mandatory GDPR webhooks and the "no data held" answer), §6.2 (customer stripping at read time); invariant 4; build plan §7 (`R-PRIVACY`).

## 2026-09-03 — R-PRIVACY — Invariant 4's guard now walks JSONB contents, not only column names
Decision: `packages/db/src/no-customer-data.test.ts` gains a second scan that enumerates every `jsonb` column in the live schema, walks the keys inside each row's value however deeply nested, and applies the existing customer-word rule to those keys. It plants a real `customers/redact` body first and asserts the scan catches it, then deletes it and asserts the scan is clean. Keys only, not values.
Why: the existing guard asks the database what columns it has, which is the right question for a column and structurally blind to a blob — and the first real breach of this invariant was inside one. The receiver stored whole webhook bodies in a column honestly named `payload`, so no column name was ever wrong and nothing was caught. Planting before scanning is the same non-vacuity discipline the rest of this suite already uses: a scan that has never caught anything is indistinguishable from a scan that cannot. **Keys rather than values** because a value rule would flag a product description containing an `@` and earn itself an allowlist entry, and an allowlist is where this kind of check goes to die. The row limit per column (500) keeps it usable if a suite ever leaves real volume behind; today every test database is close to empty, which is why the planted row is what makes the assertion mean anything.
Nearest spec: invariant 4; `docs/audits/false-confidence.md` finding 2 (the reason this suite exists at all).
## 2026-09-03 — T9.8 — "Playwright against staging" is run against a build + `next start`, because staging does not exist
Decision: the M9 exit gate's browser flows run against the built app (`pnpm build`, then `next start`, with a throwaway `ENCRYPTION_MASTER_KEY` — the same trade `playwright.config.ts` already makes) fronted by the same fixture-backed mock server `T9.2`/`T9.5` built, not against `next dev` and not against a deployed environment.
Why: the project has never been deployed (recorded in `docs/overnight-state.md`'s "BLOCKER — the deployed start command" section), so there is no staging address to point Playwright at, and `next dev` cannot start at all (`R-DEV`, tech §6). This is the same substitution `T9.5` and `T9.7` made, not a new one. `apps/web/playwright.config.ts`'s `webServer` still lists `pnpm --filter @sortiva/web run dev` first — untouched, because that entry only runs when nothing already answers at `baseURL`; starting a `next start` process on that port ahead of the run makes Playwright's own `reuseExistingServer` skip the broken command rather than needing a second, dev-specific config path.
Nearest spec: build plan T9.8 ("Playwright against staging"); tech §6; `docs/overnight-state.md`'s R-DEV entry.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.8 — Onboarding's browser flow is driven by a second run-length mock, the same shape `ContentState` already is
Decision: `apps/web/e2e/onboarding-state.ts` adds an `OnboardingState` class that `GET /api/account`, `POST /api/domain/claim`, `GET /api/ingestion/status`, `POST /api/gsc/skip` and the confirmation routes answer from, mirroring how `e2e/content-state.ts` already answers the calendar. It only intervenes once the onboarding spec calls `begin()` — every other flow's `GET /api/account` keeps answering from the frozen static fixture, because the dashboard's own nav shell reads that route on every screen and must not start seeing a merchant with no domain.
Why: `resolveOnboardingSurface` (`packages/ui/src/onboarding/surface.ts`) decides the entire onboarding screen from these two reads, and walking a merchant from no domain to activation needs each read to answer differently as the run progresses — a fixed body, which is what every other route in this harness uses, cannot do that. The steps between the Shopify grant and Search Console advance on a short wall-clock timer (a few hundred milliseconds each) rather than instantly, so the seven-row stepper genuinely ticks through pending → active → done across real polls, not a single jump.
Nearest spec: ui §3 (all); build plan T9.3, T9.8.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.8 — Shopify's OAuth grant is driven by a real redirect to the mock server's own address, not intercepted; Search Console's is exercised only by skipping
Decision: while the onboarding run is waiting on the Shopify grant, `POST /api/shopify/oauth/start` answers with the mock server's own `/api/_e2e/shopify-callback` address instead of Shopify's. `ShopifyBlockingCard` does `window.location.assign(url)` unchanged — a real navigation happens, the mock server's `GET` handler advances the run and redirects the browser back to `/dashboard`. Search Console's own OAuth round trip and property picker are not driven the same way; the flow only ever clicks "Skip for now".
Why: the public funnel already answers Google's sign-in this way (`signInAnswer` in `e2e/mock-api-server.ts`) precisely because two vendors here have no credentials in this repository, so an actual OAuth round trip cannot run; a real redirect proves the click, the fetch and the navigation the real component does, rather than a browser-side network intercept that would leave `window.location.assign` itself unexercised. Building the same trick a second time for Search Console's OAuth path would add a second redirect simulation without new coverage: `SearchConsoleStep`'s own component tests (`packages/ui/src/onboarding/onboarding.test.ts`) already exercise the connect/picker/mismatch paths against injected fixtures, and the ui spec makes "Skip for now" an equal, always-available path through the same real screen — so skipping is not a shortcut around the step, it is the step, taken the way the product allows a merchant to take it.
Nearest spec: ui §3.6 ("Skip for now… equal weight"); main §6.7.

## 2026-09-03 — T9.8 — Found and fixed: the Opportunities drawer never re-read its own subject after generating a recommendation or marking a task applied
Decision: `OpportunitiesScreen.tsx`'s `onGenerate`, `onTask` and `onMarkAllApplied` handlers now re-fetch `client.detail(id)` and update the open drawer once the action they triggered has settled (`reloadDetail`), in `packages/ui/src/opportunities/OpportunitiesScreen.tsx`.
Why: `actions.generate()` and `actions.markTask()` (`packages/ui/src/opportunities/actions.ts`) only ever call `surface.refresh()`, which re-reads the *list* (`GET /api/opportunities`) — a response whose rows carry no `recommendation` or `tasks` field at all (`OpportunityRow` in `types.ts`). The open drawer's own `detail` state was therefore never touched by anything that happened inside it: clicking "Generate recommendations" or "Mark all as applied" in the drawer posted successfully and then left the drawer showing exactly what it showed before the click, forever, until the merchant closed and reopened it by hand. `T9.4`'s own tests never caught this because none of them drive the screen through a real network round trip — they render the drawer from injected, already-correct props. This is exactly the class of defect `T9.8` exists to catch, and the requested behaviour ("OPTIMIZE generate → download → mark applied") cannot be honestly demonstrated without it — reproducing the bug in the test rather than fixing it would mean asserting a false pass. In-lane (`packages/ui`), correctness-only, no interface another lane consumes changed: the fix adds a read, not a new field or route.
Nearest spec: ui §5.4 ("drawer opens on completion"); CLAUDE.md §4/§7 (fix what correctness/the requested behaviour needs).

## 2026-09-03 — T9.8 — Scheduling an opportunity and generating a recommendation are simulated with fixed, single-example content, not the engine's own arithmetic
Decision: `ContentState.scheduleOpportunity` always lands the new topic three days out (a day nothing else in `content-state.ts`'s seed data occupies), and `ContentState.generateRecommendation` always returns the same two fields (`title`, `metaDescription`) for the one OPTIMIZE fixture (`fixtureOpportunity` from `@sortiva/core`) the flow exercises.
Why: no OPTIMIZE recommendation generator or CREATE scheduler exists anywhere in this codebase yet — both are backend cards not yet built — so there is nothing to call and no real arithmetic to reproduce; the point of this flow is that the *screen* renders whatever comes back correctly, which one realistic example proves as well as ten would. The three-days-out date is a `T9.8` choice with no product meaning of its own to preserve; a caller after generation lands via the real `POST /api/opportunities/{id}/schedule` route once one exists.
Nearest spec: build plan T9.8; main §7.9 (autopilot scheduling, not yet built).

## 2026-09-03 — T9.8 — Found and fixed: every authenticated screen 500s in a production build, because the shell passes a server-built translator into a Client Component
Decision: `apps/web/app/(app)/layout.tsx` no longer passes `t={t}` into `<NotificationBell>`; it passes the resolved language code (`language={language}`) instead, and `NotificationBell` (`packages/ui/src/shell/NotificationBell.tsx`) now takes a `language` prop and builds its own translator with `createTranslate` (already a plain, side-effect-free function safe to call on either side of the boundary), rather than a `t` function prop.
Why: `AppLayout` wraps every route under `apps/web/app/(app)/**` — dashboard, onboarding, opportunities, content, products, performance, settings, all of it — and is a Server Component; `NotificationBell` is a Client Component (`'use client'`). React refuses to serialise a plain function across that boundary, and Next answers every request through this layout with a 500 the instant it tries: `Error: Functions cannot be passed directly to Client Components`.
