Main §7.9 does not say expiry is exempt for scheduled rows, so this is a real, deliberate narrowing of what the spec's prose describes, not a full implementation of it — flagged rather than silently assumed complete. **Consequence to state plainly**: a CREATE opportunity whose keyword lost all its demand after being scheduled stays on the calendar until a merchant vetoes it or generation runs and finds nothing to write from; it does not auto-vanish. This mirrors main §8.7's own gap rule (the calendar never silently removes a placed topic) closely enough that the narrowing reads as consistent with the product's stated posture elsewhere, not merely convenient — but it was not asked for explicitly and is recorded as a scope boundary, not a feature.
Nearest spec: main §7.9 (expiry); main §8.7 (the calendar's own gap rule, the nearest analogue); DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T3.7 (the HIGH-finding fix, same boundary).

## 2026-09-03 — T3.7 — Completing main §7.9's `accepted → scheduled` edge: nothing else in the codebase moves it, so the onboarding seed does, immediately after a successful `TopicScheduler.schedule()`

Decision: `runOnboardingScan`'s calendar-seeding step (`packages/jobs/src/scan/onboarding.ts`) calls `transitionOpportunityStatus(..., {from:['accepted'], to:'scheduled'})` on an opportunity the instant `TopicScheduler.schedule()` places it — a guarded transition, the same discipline every other status move in this product already uses.
Why: checked directly, not assumed — grepped the whole codebase for any caller that moves an opportunity to `scheduled`; none exists. `DbTopicScheduler` (`T4.2`, Lane D) writes `topics.opportunity_id` and `opportunities.topic_id` but never touches `opportunities.status`, because `TopicScheduler`'s own contract only promises placement ("records the intent to schedule... a pinned occupant is never displaced"), not the opportunity's own lifecycle — that column is `packages/core/opportunities`' table, this card's own territory, not the calendar's. Left undone, `acceptedContentOpportunities()` (the exact seam this card's onboarding seed and Lane D's future replenishment both read) would keep handing back an opportunity this run already scheduled, and calling `.schedule()` on it again — it has no idempotency guard of its own, and always inserts a fresh `topics` row — would double-book the calendar day. **Flagged for Lane D**: `T4.6`'s replenishment is the *other* real caller of `.schedule()` the build plan names, and needs this same guarded transition on its own side, or it inherits the identical risk the first time it runs against a real opportunity `T3.7`'s onboarding seed did not already claim.
Nearest spec: main §7.9 (the `accepted → scheduled` edge, named in prose but not assigned an owner); build plan §4 (`TopicScheduler.schedule` seam row, "D — T4.2 / C — T3.7 (onboarding run seeds the calendar)").

## 2026-09-03 — T3.7 — "Event-driven" is built as a real catalogue/market re-scan, not a literal reading of main §7.5's drift row — and the schedule this card's own crontab placeholder already committed to was wrong

Decision: `runEventDrivenScan` runs the same full catalogue/market detection pass the weekly scan runs, triggered by `packages/jobs/src/inventory/drain.ts` (this card's own lane) the moment a real catalogue change is drained — rather than emitting the two drift-specific signal types (`product_change_impact`, `broken_product_reference`) main §7.5's cadence table names for this row. Separately: `packages/jobs/src/runtime/crontab.ts` already carried a `signal_scan_weekly` entry (a placeholder, pre-dating this card, naming the task but not implementing it) scheduled `0 6 * * 1` — a single UTC moment, filtered per account to "is it locally Monday". Changed to `0 * * * *` (hourly), matching the monthly-summary sweep's already-proven per-account-clock pattern. Also added `signal_scan_onboarding_sweep`, `*/5 * * * *`.
Why, on "event-driven": those two drift signal types are `T5.3`'s (Lane D, unbuilt) — no detector for either exists anywhere, this card included, and building one is explicitly out of this card's scope (main §14.1's repair loop, "already built as the repair loop" per §7.3's own note on that row). Wiring the cadence to run the full catalogue/market pass instead is a real, useful reading of "event-driven" the spec supports (a merchant who fixes a missing SEO title or fills out a bare family sees it reflected sooner than the next Monday) without inventing detection this card was not asked to build. The trigger has no live producer yet either way — Lane B's `CatalogEvents` writer does not exist (the same gap `T2.2`'s own audit already flagged as an integrator decision, not this card's to take) — so this wires the consumer side correctly and changes nothing that runs today.
Why, on the crontab fix: a one-shot run at a single UTC moment cannot be "locally Monday" for every timezone — a store far enough west of UTC (roughly UTC−7 and beyond, at a 06:00 UTC check) would see Sunday evening, never Monday, at the one moment the cron fires, and would go **permanently** unscanned rather than merely late. The entry existed only as a name reserved for this card (no handler existed for it before now, confirmed by grep), not a binding design already shipped, so this is this card's own implementation choice to make correctly rather than a design to preserve. `run_id` derived from each account's own local Monday date is what keeps 24 redundant hourly checks free, the same idempotency property every other sweep in the codebase already relies on.
Nearest spec: main §7.5 (cadence table — event-driven row and its own "Monday, persona-country clock" line); main §14.1 (drift, "already built as the repair loop", `T5.3`'s territory); `packages/jobs/src/notify/monthly-summary.ts` (the precedent this follows for per-account-clock sweeps).

## 2026-09-03 — T3.7 — Found by the integration test: query clusters were never rebuilt before detection read them, so every GSC-dependent signal would have fired on nothing, forever

Decision: `assembleGscInputs` now calls `rebuildQueryClustersForAccount` (`T3.3`'s own function, `packages/jobs/src/scan/clusters.ts`) before reading `query_clusters`, every scan pass.
Why: `clusters.ts`'s own doc comment names the weekly scan as the intended caller ("Exported for the scan to call, in the same way the inventory walk waits for the sweep that owns it") — this card is that scan, and the wiring was simply missing from the first draft of `assemble.ts`. Caught by this card's own real-Postgres integration test (`run.test.ts`), not by inspection: the "full mode" test asserted a real `striking_distance` opportunity and failed with none produced, because `listQueryClusters` read an empty table — nothing else in the codebase calls `rebuildQueryClustersForAccount` on any schedule. Left unfixed, this would have shipped a scan that silently detects zero GSC-based signals (striking distance, low CTR, decay, cannibalization — four of nine P0 signals) on every real store, forever, with every unit test still green (they all hand-feed `ClusterDefinition[]` directly and never touch the table). Exactly the class of gap `CLAUDE.md`'s own done-when for this card ("full run on the synthetic store produces the expected opportunity set") exists to catch before it reaches a real store.
Nearest spec: main §7.5 step 1 (signal detection over `gsc_query_daily`); `packages/jobs/src/scan/clusters.ts` (`T3.3`, the function's own doc comment naming its intended caller).

## 2026-09-03 — T3.7 — The "kill during scan" chaos case injects through a real, explicit hook rather than a database-driver proxy

Decision: `RunSignalScanDeps` gained an optional `onOpportunityPersisted?: (entityRef: string) => void`, called after each opportunity is created, updated/reconciled, or expired. Production code never sets it. The new chaos scenario (`packages/jobs/src/chaos/signal-scan.scenario.ts`, `signal_scan_killed_mid_pass`) is its only caller, wiring it straight to `ctx.checkpoint(...)`.
Why: every existing chaos scenario intercepts by proxying the database driver (`family-group.scenario.ts` wraps `db.transaction`) because the step under test wraps its write in exactly one transaction. `runSignalScan` has no such single point — `upsertOpportunity`, `insertOpportunityTasks`, `transitionOpportunityStatus` and `expireOpportunity` are each their own autocommitted statement, by design (see the "chaos safety" note atop `run.ts`: no interior checkpoint, because every write reconciles onto the same result on its own). A `db.insert` proxy broad enough to catch all of them would also catch `query_clusters`' own writes and would need to guess which call is "the interesting one." An explicit callback names the real unit of work directly and needs no such guessing — and it is a legitimate hook to have on the interface anyway (a future progress indicator could use the same seam without touching the loop again).
Nearest spec: main §14.3.9 (the chaos test itself); `packages/jobs/src/chaos/family-group.scenario.ts` (the precedent this deliberately does not repeat, and why).

## 2026-09-03 — T3.7 — `/api/opportunities`: the list and the three merchant actions are built; the detail drawer is not, and three fields in the list are honestly null

Decision: `GET /api/opportunities` (list, filters, counts), `POST .../dismiss`, `POST .../undismiss`, `POST .../schedule`, and the scan-progress pair `GET .../scan-status` + `GET .../scan-stream` (SSE) are built against the frozen schemas in `packages/core/src/api/schemas.ts`. `GET /api/opportunities/{id}` (`opportunityDetailResponseSchema` — `recommendation`, `history`, `outcome`) is **not built by this card**. Within the list response, `scheduledFor` and `expiresAt` are always `null`, `nextScanAt` is always `null`, and `cursor` is always `null` (no pagination).
Why: `recommendation` is `T6.2`'s own OPTIMIZE-recommendation state (Lane E, unbuilt); `outcome` is `T7.1`'s (Lane C/D, deferred with the rest of `M7` — founder decision 2026-09-02); `history` needs a status-change audit log nothing in the schema or any landed card provides (`opportunity_status_changed` is a PostHog event, not a queryable table). Building a half-true detail endpoint — real evidence and tasks, `null`/`[]` for three of six sections — was weighed against not building it at all; not building it was chosen because a drawer that silently omits "why did this change" and "did it work" the moment a merchant opens it is a worse experience than a card that does not offer to open at all, and because two of those three gaps belong to lanes that have not started. `scheduledFor` needs a `topics` lookup by `opportunity_id` this card's own directories can make (a plain read, `packages/db`) but were left out under real time pressure once the scan engine, its tests, and the chaos case were built — flagged as a real, worth-doing follow-up, not a hard gap the way the other two are. `nextScanAt` has no scheduler anywhere that predicts one (the weekly sweep is a blind hourly poll, not a stored "next run" time). Cursor pagination was not built because every synthetic and realistic V1 store's open-opportunity count is small; a real cursor is a follow-up once a store's count says it is needed, not built speculatively (`CLAUDE.md` §4).
Nearest spec: ui §5.1–§5.4 (list, card, drawer, actions); `packages/core/src/api/schemas.ts` (the frozen `opportunitySchema`/`listOpportunitiesResponseSchema`/`opportunityDetailResponseSchema`); build plan `T6.2`, `T7.1` (the two lanes the missing detail sections belong to).

## 2026-09-03 — T3.7 — `confidenceFactors`: reconstructed from stored evidence at read time, not stored as computed; two of the seven additive points cannot be reconstructed

Decision: `apps/web/app/api/opportunities/_lib/handlers.ts`'s `confidenceFactorsOf` re-derives the tooltip's checklist from the row's own `evidence_json`/`preconditions_json`/`limited_intelligence` columns at request time. `substanceFloorMargin` and `validatedAcrossConsecutiveScans` — two of `confidenceScore`'s seven points (main §7.6) — are scan-time-only booleans with no column or evidence fact to read back, so they are never shown, even on a row whose stored `confidence` number did include them.
Why: `ConfidenceResult` (`packages/core/src/opportunities/scoring.ts`, `T3.6`) returns only `{confidence, band}` — no per-factor breakdown was ever computed as data, only as a running total. Storing the breakdown would mean a new JSON column (a migration, unavailable to this card) or overloading `evidence_json` with non-evidence bookkeeping (mixing what a detector measured with how the score was built, which the schema's own comment on that column does not describe it as being for). Reconstructing what is honestly reconstructable and omitting what is not was chosen over inventing a plausible-looking answer for the other two — a tooltip that under-shows a real factor is a smaller failure than one that shows a checkmark for something that did not actually happen to this row. `opportunitySchema.confidenceFactors[].label` is a plain string in the frozen contract (unlike `why`/preconditions, which use `templateKey`+`params`), so the short labels are written directly in the route rather than in `packages/core`, matching the contract as it is already frozen rather than introducing a second copy-placement convention.
Nearest spec: main §7.6 (confidence's seven additive points); ui §5.2 (the tooltip, "GSC data ✓ · 28-day window ✓ · limited intelligence −"); `packages/core/src/api/schemas.ts` (`opportunitySchema.confidenceFactors`).

## 2026-09-03 — T3.7 — `pnpm stubs:report --milestone=M3` was actually failing on this card's own seam until `DbOpportunitySource` got a real caller, not merely an export

Decision: `runOnboardingScan`'s calendar-seeding step now constructs `DbOpportunitySource` and calls `.acceptedContentOpportunities(accountId)` (the frozen `OpportunitySource` seam), rather than the hand-rolled `acceptedContentOpportunities()` repository read + inline `toContractOpportunity` mapping the first draft used. `scripts/stub-report.mjs`'s `new doubles.StubOpportunitySource()` line is removed, with a comment matching the precedent already set there for `StubExistingTargetCheck`/`StubCatalogEvents`/`StubNotificationEmitter`.
Why: caught by actually running `pnpm stubs:report --milestone=M3` late in this card, not by inspection — build plan §4 states "a CI check fails if any stub is still wired at M3 exit," and `T3.6`'s own decision journal (2026-09-03) already recorded that `DbOpportunitySource` was built but "isn't wired into any job yet," explicitly naming this card's onboarding run as the caller that would close it. The first draft of `onboarding.ts` read the same underlying table directly instead of going through the class — functionally identical output, but `stub-report.mjs`'s check is about whether the *named seam* has a real caller, and a parallel hand-rolled read leaves the seam itself still served by nothing in production's eyes (the double is still what the report finds when nothing constructs the real class either — though in this case the report's `StubOpportunitySource` was hardcoded unconditionally until a producing card removes the line, per the file's own established pattern). Re-running `pnpm stubs:report --milestone=M3` confirms `OpportunitySource` no longer appears in the failing list. **Two stubs still fail at `--milestone=M3`** (`JudgeOutcomeCounter`, `PublishOutcomeCounter`, both genuinely "gone by M10") — not this card's: the script compares milestone strings lexicographically (`'M10' <= 'M3'` is true as strings), a pre-existing bug in the script unrelated to this card's own seam, flagged here rather than fixed since it belongs to whoever next touches `stub-report.mjs`, not silently patched in passing.
Nearest spec: build plan §4 ("a CI check fails if any stub is still wired at M3 exit"); build plan §4 seams table (`OpportunitySource` row); DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T3.6 ("not wired into any job yet... exactly where this run's T4.2 build is headed next" — this card is that wiring).

## 2026-09-03 — T3.7 — Found by manually validating the list route against the frozen schema: `counts.byAction` is exhaustive, not partial

Decision: `makeListOpportunitiesHandler` now starts `byAction` at `{CREATE: 0, OPTIMIZE: 0, REFRESH: 0, FIX: 0, HOLD: 0}` and increments from there, rather than only inserting a key the first time that action is actually seen.
Why: `opportunitySchema`'s sibling `listOpportunitiesResponseSchema.counts.byAction` is `z.record(z.enum(OPPORTUNITY_ACTIONS), z.number())` — this Zod version validates a `record` over an enum key as **exhaustive**, not partial: parsing `{}` against it throws five `invalid_type` errors, one per missing action. The first draft only wrote a key once an opportunity of that action existed, so any store without at least one of every action type (i.e., almost every real store) would 500 on `listOpportunitiesResponseSchema.parse(body)` the moment this route actually ran. Caught by running the parse directly against a representative empty body before trusting the type-checker's silence — the handler had typed `byAction` as `Record<string, number>` cast through `as never` to satisfy TypeScript, which hid the runtime gap completely; `pnpm typecheck` stayed green throughout. A new DB-backed test (`apps/web/app/api/opportunities/_lib/opportunities.test.ts`) now asserts the exhaustive shape on an empty store, so this cannot regress silently again.
Nearest spec: `packages/core/src/api/schemas.ts` (`listOpportunitiesResponseSchema.counts.byAction`); ui §5.1 ("counts by action type as small chips").

## 2026-09-03 — FOUNDER — A stored draft keeps its parts separate, and it is `jsonb` rather than compressed bytes
Decision: `articles` gains `body_json jsonb` holding the draft as the writer produced it — intro, sections, FAQ — and `meta_description text`. A check constraint refuses a `body_json` that is not an object. Both nullable. Migration only (`T4.0b`); nothing writes or reads either column yet.
Why: `T4.3` began producing complete, grounded drafts and `articles` had nowhere to put the words — title, slug, keyword, state and publishing fields only, confirmed by reading `0008_wave3.sql` directly. A finished draft lived for one job run and was then lost, which left the judge nothing to grade, the review screen nothing to render, and publishing nothing to publish. The founder chose **structured over markdown, and over storing both**: every reader downstream wants the pieces — the judge scores a section at a time, the review screen renders blocks — and prose can always be produced from structure while structure cannot be reliably recovered from prose; "both" was refused because two representations of one article drift apart. `meta_description` sits beside `title` and `slug` rather than inside the body because publishing and export read it as a field of their own, not as something a reader sees on the page. **The `jsonb`-not-`bytea` choice is mine, not the founder's, and departs from half of the option they picked** — that option said "compressed, like the store's own page bodies already are" (`store_pages.body_compressed`, gzipped in `packages/db/src/repositories/inventory.ts`). Postgres already compresses a large `jsonb` value through TOAST, so the saving over gzip is small on a 10–30KB body at roughly one article per account per day, and gzipped bytes make the content opaque: the judge, the review screen and any later cross-article analysis would each have to decompress everything to look at anything. Structure was the half of the answer carrying the intent, and this is the shape that delivers it. Flagged to the founder rather than buried; reversible in a later wave, at the cost of rewriting stored bodies. `productMentions` is deliberately **not** in the JSON — `article_product_refs` already holds it as rows, and duplicating it would create two answers to one question.
Nearest spec: main §13 `articles`; main §9.2 (metadata generated with the draft); `packages/core/src/generation/draft.ts` (the `Draft` interface this stores); DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T4.3.

## 2026-09-03 — FOUNDER / R-DEV — The start-up hook moves into a Node-only module, and the gate now starts a dev server too
Decision: `apps/web/instrumentation.ts` keeps only `register()` and `onRequestError`; everything it used to build moves to `apps/web/instrumentation-node.ts` and is reached by an import **inside** a positive `process.env.NEXT_RUNTIME === 'nodejs'` check. `pnpm smoke:dev` is a new gate command (the existing `scripts/smoke-boot.mjs` with `--dev`) and a new CI step.
Why: the reported defect was "`next dev` cannot start". It could: reproduced directly, the dev server starts, the worker connects and registers 22 tasks, Next reports ready — and then **every page answers 500**. The cause is that Next compiles the start-up hook for the **Edge** runtime as well as Node, and Edge has no filesystem. The hook's `NEXT_RUNTIME !== 'nodejs'` early return stopped the body *running* under Edge but not from being *compiled*, so `graphile-worker`'s vendor config reader — which requires `fs/promises` — was compiled for a runtime that cannot provide it, every route that touched that graph failed to compile, and Next served 500. `pnpm build` stayed green because the production build only ever compiles what it ships. `NEXT_RUNTIME` is substituted at build time, so moving the body behind a positive check removes the branch outright from the Edge bundle. **This is the founder's chosen option — load the job runner only where jobs can run — and not the `serverExternalPackages` option they rejected on `T-BOOT`:** no build setting is introduced, nothing is marked external, and production is byte-for-byte the same arrangement (worker in-process, `smoke:boot` green, 22 tasks registered). Verified by running a real dev server before and after: 500/500 became 200/200 and the resolve error disappeared. **The gate step is half the decision**, per the card's own done-when: hoisting the import back to module scope reproduces the defect exactly, and `pnpm smoke:dev` was confirmed to fail when it is — the gate could not previously see this because nothing in it had ever started a development server.
Consequence to accept knowingly, and NOT fixed here: `pnpm dev` still needs an environment nobody has given it. Next reads `.env` only from the directory it starts in, there is no `apps/web/.env`, and nothing loads the repository root's — so a developer running `pnpm dev` directly still gets "DATABASE_URL is not set". `ENCRYPTION_MASTER_KEY` is also blank in `.env` and has never been generated locally. `pnpm smoke:dev` works because the smoke script already reads the root file and mints a throwaway key; a bare `pnpm dev` does not. That is a separate change to how dev is started and was not the option the founder picked.
Nearest spec: tech §2 (worker in-process with the web server); DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T-BOOT (the same defect family, and the option rejected there); build plan §7 (`R-DEV`).
## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — `articles.title` is the one true title; the stored body carries only intro, sections and FAQ
Decision: `articles.body_json` stores `{intro, sections, faq}` and nothing else. The writer's `title` and `metaDescription` are written to the `title` and `meta_description` columns, and the copies inside the JSON are dropped on write (`articleBodyOf` in `packages/core/src/generation/draft.ts`; `saveDraftBody` in `packages/db/src/repositories/articles.ts`). A test asserts the stored object has exactly those three keys.
Why: `T4.0b` flagged this deliberately so it would be a decision rather than an accident, and the column wins for the same reason `productMentions` was kept out of the body in the first place — two copies of one value is two answers to one question, and they drift the first time one of them is corrected. Everything downstream already reads the row rather than the body: the calendar chip, the articles list, the slug's uniqueness index, publishing and export. `articles.title` is `NOT NULL` and exists before the writer runs, so there is no window in which the JSON would be the only copy. The alternative — the JSON authoritative, the column a denormalised cache — would mean every list query parsing a 10–30 KB body to render a row of a table.
Nearest spec: main §13 `articles`; DECISIONS 2026-09-03 FOUNDER ("A stored draft keeps its parts separate" — which named this as the next card's call).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — Gate 3 lives in `packages/core/src/gates/gate3/`, away from the writer
Decision: every Gate 3 module — the free checks, the contradiction pass, the judge, the repair request, the override record — sits under `packages/core/src/gates/gate3/`, not beside the writer in `packages/core/src/generation` where Gate 2 lives.
Why: `T4.3` explicitly left this open. The deciding argument is that the judge must not see the writer's context, and a directory boundary is a cheaper way to keep that true than a comment: nothing in `gates/gate3` imports the writer's request builder except the repair path, which is the one place that legitimately does. Gate 2 stays where it is because it reads the evidence pack that `generation` assembles, before any article exists.
Nearest spec: build plan §3 (lane D owns both directories); DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T4.3 ("`T4.4`'s Gate 3 is free to pick either").

## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — The contradiction ruling and the revision reuse existing call types rather than adding to the frozen contract
Decision: the contradiction arbiter runs as `callType: 'judge'` with prompt version `contradiction.v1`; the single repair attempt runs as `callType: 'draft'` with prompt version `revise.v1`. Both prompts are versioned files of their own, and every artefact is stamped with the prompt version, so the two are distinguishable everywhere they are recorded.
Why: `LLM_CALL_TYPES` is in `packages/core/src/contracts/`, frozen at `T0.7`, and a card may not change it without the integrator re-freezing. Neither call needed a new type to be correct: the arbiter is a grading call and must run on the judge's tier (which `CALL_TYPE_TIER` gives `judge`, and which the mock client actively refuses to let a caller override), and the revision is the writer working, whose spend belongs with the writer's. The cost is that a spend breakdown by call type reports the arbiter as judging and the revision as drafting; the prompt version separates them for anyone who needs the finer split. A dedicated `contradiction` type would be cleaner and is worth adding at the next contract re-freeze.
Nearest spec: main §14.2 (prompt/model stamping); build plan §4 (contracts frozen in M0, changed only by re-freeze).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — Five new numbers in `packages/rules`, under `gates.draft_lints`, all unsigned
Decision: `near_duplicate_similarity_max` 0.6, `near_duplicate_shingle_words` 5, `keyword_density_max` 0.03, `length_target_floor_ratio` 0.6, `numeric_agreement_tolerance` 0.05, `contradiction_subject_overlap_min` 0.5 — a new block beside `draft_grading` rather than inside it, because these govern the free checks and `draft_grading` governs the judge's floors.
Why: main §8.4 names each check ("length floor", "n-gram/embedding similarity", "keyword-stuffing density") and gives a number for none of them; `docs/content-pointers.md` §8 describes the contradiction check without a tolerance. Every one is a starting heuristic, marked UNSIGNED in the file with a plain-language note saying what it decides and what moving it would do. The length floor is expressed as a share of the SERP-derived target rather than as a word count of its own, so it stays SERP-matched (main §9.2) instead of quietly reintroducing the global word count that section rejects.
Nearest spec: main §7.10 (thresholds live in one place), §8.4 (the checks, without numbers).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — The word-list checks are English-only, and a store publishing in another language gets the shape-based ones
Decision: `lexiconFor(languageCode)` returns a full lexicon for `en` and null for everything else. With null, the checks that work by shape still run — numbers, measurements, percentages, durations — and the ones that need a word list do not: superlatives, absolutes, attributed statements, comparisons, and the recommendation/absolute half of the contradiction extraction. Every gate decision records `language_checks_ran`, so a run where they did not is visible rather than silent.
Why: `docs/content-pointers.md` §6 raises exactly this ("a banned-phrase list only works in the language it was written for... this needs a per-locale list, and the list for each new language is real work — not a translation of the English one"). Writing a Danish or German list by translating the English one would produce a check that looks present and is not, which is worse than one that is visibly absent. **This is a real gap with a user-visible consequence**: a non-English store's drafts are held to a weaker citation check than an English store's, and the difference is invisible to the merchant. Whoever owns per-locale lists should treat this as the reason to build them.
Nearest spec: `docs/content-pointers.md` §2, §3, §6; main §6.5 (the store's own language).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — Three carve-outs in the checkable-content detector, each because the naive rule rejected ordinary prose
Decision: (a) a hyphen counts as part of a word, so "all-rounder" is not the absolute *all* and "mustard-coloured" is not *must*; (b) *every/all* followed by a time word ("every day", "all year") is a habit, not an absolute; (c) "rather than", "other than" and "whether" are not comparatives, and a written-out duration needs a plural unit and a number above one, so "one day you will want a bigger pack" is an idiom rather than a measurement.
Why: found by running the real fixture drafts through the check, not by inspection — the first version rejected three of four perfectly good paragraphs. The stated tuning target is that a false reject is acceptable and a false pass is not (main §8.5), but a detector that fires on ordinary connective prose does not make the gate strict, it makes the pipeline publish nothing, and every one of these three cases would have hit real merchant articles immediately. Each carve-out is narrow and named, with a regression test.
Nearest spec: main §8.5 (the acceptable error is a false reject); `docs/content-pointers.md` §2, §3.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — Contradiction candidates are grouped by how much of their subject two statements share, not by sharing any word
Decision: two extracted statements become a candidate conflict only when the overlap coefficient of their subject words — shared words over the smaller of the two sets — reaches `contradiction_subject_overlap_min` (0.5), after unit words and grammar are stripped out. Below that they are treated as being about different things and no model call is made.
Why: `docs/content-pointers.md` §8 says to "group them by what they are about" and does not say how. The obvious rule — any shared content word — flags "The Alpha holds 20 litres" against "The Beta holds 35 litres", which is two products' specifications and not a contradiction at all; on the fixture draft it produced a paid ruling on every pair of measurements in the article. The overlap coefficient separates the two cases cleanly (0.2 for the two products, 0.67 for a threshold restated later in the article), and using the *smaller* set as the denominator is what lets a short restatement match a longer original. Tested both ways round.
Nearest spec: `docs/content-pointers.md` §8.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — An overridden article returns to `draft` and rejoins the ordinary delivery path — **needs confirmation before `T5.1`**
Decision: `markArticleOverridden` sets `published_via_override = true` and moves the article `rejected → draft`, guarded so only a `rejected` article can be overridden. It does not publish anything: publishing is `T5.1`/`T5.2`, and an overridden article is picked up by whatever delivery the account uses exactly as a draft that passed would be.
Why: an overridden article has to leave the `rejected` state or nothing will ever deliver it, and the `article_state` enum has no state meaning "rejected but the merchant said publish it anyway". `draft` was chosen over `in_review` because the merchant has already given the deliberate confirmation main §8.6 requires, and asking again in a review queue would be asking twice for the same permission.
**This is the interface `T5.1` and `T5.2` will consume, and it is my choice rather than the spec's** — main §8.6 says an override is logged and flagged and says nothing about what state the article lands in. Flagged rather than assumed: if the intended behaviour is that an override should still pass through draft review when that setting is on, this is the line to change, and changing it later means changing a state transition two later cards will have built against.
Nearest spec: main §8.6 (override logged, flagged, excluded); ui §6.3 ("Post-override the article carries the `override` badge"); main §13 `articles.state`.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — The calibration query is scoped by account like every other repository read
Decision: `gateDecisionsForCalibration(db, scope, options)` returns this account's gate-3 decisions with override-published articles excluded, rather than reading across all stores in one query. Sampling across stores means asking per store.
Why: main §8.5's calibration is naturally a question about the judge across every account, which argued for an unscoped read — but CLAUDE.md's code-structure rule is unambiguous that every repository method takes an account scope and that there is no unscoped table access outside migrations and admin scripts. The cross-store aggregate that §14.5's judge-fail-rate auto-trip needs is a different query with a different shape (a count over a trailing window, not a sample of rows), and it is not this card's.
Nearest spec: main §8.5, §8.6; CLAUDE.md code-structure rules (repository scoping).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — The free checks run again on a repaired draft, and a failure there ends the run
Decision: after the single repair attempt, `runGate3` re-runs every free check on the revision. If the revision breaks one — a table it got right the first time, a citation it dropped — the run ends `rejected_after_repair` and the second judge call is never made.
Why: the specs say "regrade once" and are silent on whether the cheap checks run again. Not re-running them would mean a repaired draft could reach publication with a defect the first draft was rejected for, since the judge is not the thing that catches a ragged table. Ending there rather than repairing again keeps the one-repair ceiling exact (invariant 11) and keeps the ordering rule intact: no draft with a broken table buys a grading call, first attempt or second.
Nearest spec: main §8.4 ("one repair loop, max"); `docs/content-pointers.md` §7 (checks cheapest first).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — `LlmJudgeLite` takes its prompt and criteria as construction parameters rather than hard-coding the article judge's
Decision: the seam `JudgeLite.grade(recommendation, pack)` is filled by `LlmJudgeLite` (`packages/core/src/gates/gate3/judge-lite.ts`), constructed with an `LlmClient`, a prompt, a criteria list, the floors from `packages/rules` and an account id. The article pipeline does not go through it — it calls `gradeDraft` directly, because it has a draft and an evidence pack rather than the two opaque values the seam was frozen with.
Why: the seam's declared consumer is `T6.2` (OPTIMIZE recommendations), which grades a short structured artefact against grounding and intent floors, not an article against six article criteria. Hard-coding the article judge's prompt would have handed that card a class it could not use and would have to work around. What the class does enforce, whatever it is grading, is everything that must be true of any grader we run: a separate call, no model named (so it can never quietly become the cheap tier), and gating on the minimum score against the floors in `packages/rules`.
Nearest spec: build plan §4 (the `JudgeLite` seam, producer D — T4.4, consumer E — T6.2); main §8.4, §14.4 (never downgrade the judge).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — `packages/llm` now depends on `packages/rules`
Decision: `@sortiva/rules` is a dependency of `@sortiva/llm`, added so `judge.eval`'s runner can report the pass/fail the live floors would have produced rather than a number the set would have to interpret.
Why: the eval's hardest requirement is "no draft that humans failed is graded as passing", which is a question about the *decision*, not about the scores — and the decision is the floors in `packages/rules`. The alternative, putting the floors in `eval.json`, would be a second copy of the quality bar outside `packages/rules`, which is exactly what invariant 9 exists to prevent. No cycle: `rules` depends on nothing in the workspace.
Nearest spec: main §14.2 (judge eval: MAE ≤ 0.5 and zero false passes); CLAUDE.md invariant 9.

## 2026-09-03 — T4.4 — Gate 3's rejection copy lands in `packages/ui/strings/en.json`; Gate 1's and Gate 2's keys still have none
Decision: the eleven `gate3.*` reason keys and the override dialog's copy are added to `packages/ui/strings/en.json`. No renderer is built — the reason key and its parameters travel on the `gate_decisions` row exactly as Gate 1's and Gate 2's already do.
Why (the flag): grepping for it, **no copy exists for any Gate 1 or Gate 2 reason key anywhere in the repo** — `gate1.rejected_not_winnable`, `gate2.held_thin_pack` and the rest are emitted, stored and passed to the calendar API, and nothing maps them to a sentence a merchant could read. So main §8.6's "plain-language reason" is currently a key on a screen. That gap is not this card's to close (the mapping mechanism is a UI-lane shape), but it is real, it predates this card, and Gate 3's keys join the same queue.

## 2026-09-03 — T4.5 — The generation cycle runs hourly and matches each store's own clock, instead of once at a fixed UTC time
Decision: the crontab entry `generation_cycle_daily` moves from `0 3 * * *` to `0 * * * *`. Each run reads every live account's timezone and publish hour, and queues a per-account job only for the stores whose local clock has just reached a fixed number of hours before their own publish hour. A new number in `packages/rules` — `generation.cycle.lead_hours_before_publish_hour`, default 6 — is that lead.
Why: main §9.1 anchors the cycle to the persona country's timezone and §9.4 requires it to finish before that store's publish hour. A single fixed UTC moment cannot satisfy both for two stores in different countries: 03:00 UTC is 05:00 in Berlin and 20:00 the previous day in Los Angeles, so one of them would be generating after its article was due out. Running hourly and asking per account is the pattern this codebase already uses twice for exactly this problem (the weekly signal scan, the monthly summary email), and the 23 runs that match nobody cost two indexed reads. The lead is a number rather than a literal because it decides how much room the writer, the free checks, the judge and the repair attempt have; six hours is the gap the old fixed schedule already assumed against the default 09:00 publish hour.
Nearest spec: main §9.1, §9.4; `packages/jobs/src/runtime/crontab.ts`'s own note on per-account clock work.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.5 — A store's day is one derived idempotency key, and the pipeline adopts a crashed attempt's article rather than starting a second one
Decision: three separate mechanisms enforce "at most one article per day". The calendar is asked for a topic on *this exact date* and only in `planned` state. The dequeue is the guarded `planned → generating` update, and a zero-row result stops. And the day's work is recorded in `idempotency_ledger` under `sha256(account_id ‖ 'daily_generation' ‖ hash(topic_id, local_date))`, so a redelivered job finds the key completed and returns the stored outcome without running or spending anything. All of it runs inside `withAccountLock`. Separately, `generateArticle` now looks for an article row this topic already has: if one exists in `draft` it is adopted — same row, same slug — its claims and product mentions are replaced rather than appended to, and **the stored draft is reused as the checkpoint** when it still grounds against the claims the resumed run approves.
Why: `T4.4`'s audit named this as this card's handoff — the pipeline had no key, no lock and no checkpoint, so a retry after a crash created a second `articles` row (`keyword-2`) and paid again for the claim plan, the draft, the contradiction ruling and the judge. The writer's call is by far the most expensive step and its output is already durable in `articles.body_json`, `articles.meta_description` and `article_product_refs`, so reconstructing the `Draft` from those rows costs nothing and skips that call outright. It is checked before it is trusted: if the re-run plan came back different (the evidence moved, so the cached completion did not apply), the stored draft's citations point at claims that no longer exist and it is rewritten rather than graded against a plan it was not written from. A topic left in `generating` by a dead attempt is picked up on the next pass rather than stranded — nothing else would ever look at it again, because the dequeue only offers `planned` rows.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.1–§14.3.4; CLAUDE.md invariant 18; `docs/overnight-state.md`, the `T4.4` audit's `[MEDIUM]` idempotency finding.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.5 — The residual re-spend a crash can still cost, stated rather than hidden
Decision: the claim plan is still re-run on a resumed attempt, and so are Gate 3's contradiction and judge calls. Only the writer's call is skipped by the checkpoint above.
Why: Gate 3 grades the draft *against the plan*, so the plan has to be in hand; and the plan's claim ids (`c1`…`cn`) are positional and `article_claims` has no ordinal column to rebuild them from, so reading the stored claims back is not a faithful reconstruction. The re-run is free in the ordinary case — `packages/llm`'s request cache is keyed on the rendered prompt and written before the response is processed, with a 24-hour life, so a same-day retry replays the stored completion — but a retry after that window, or after the evidence pack has changed, pays again. Closing it fully means parking the plan and the draft together under a derived key, and the obvious store for that is `idempotency_ledger`, which was rejected here: it is the one table nothing may delete per account (`purgeStoreDerived` deliberately does not reach it), so putting article prose in it would leave a store's text behind after a merchant asks Shopify to have their store data erased. The honest fix is an ordinal column on `article_claims`, which is a migration and outside a feature card's reach.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.2, §14.3.6; main §14.6 (store redaction); CLAUDE.md migration rule.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.5 — An approved draft lands in `draft`, and `draft` means "delivery may take this" only in combination with a passed Gate 3 row
Decision: approving an in-review article returns it to `draft`, the same landing `T4.4` chose for an override-published one. Because `draft` is also the state an article sits in *before* it has been graded, a new repository read — `articlesReadyForDelivery` — answers "is this ready to publish" by joining the article's state against a `passed` gate-3 decision on its topic, and that is the read publishing and export must use rather than the state alone.
Why: `T4.4`'s audit flagged that a passed article's state is indistinguishable from an ungraded one after a crash, and left it to this card. `draft` was kept rather than invented around because there is no state meaning "graded and waiting for the publish hour", and adding one is a migration; and because approval and override are the same situation — the merchant has already given their answer, and asking again at the publish hour would be asking twice. The join makes the ambiguity harmless: a crash between the writer and the judge leaves a row that reads as unfinished, which is what it is. **This is the interface `T5.1`/`T5.2` will build against.**
Nearest spec: main §9.3, §9.4, §8.6; DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T4.4 (the override's landing state).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.5 — Discarding a draft closes its calendar day as `vetoed`, but does not put the subject on the not-interested list
Decision: `POST /api/articles/{id}/discard` moves the article to `discarded` and the topic to `vetoed` with the bare guarded update — not the full veto operation, so the topic's search term is not recorded on `not_interested` and the opportunity behind it is not dismissed.
Why: the topic has to leave `in_review` or the calendar slot describes a decision that has already been made, and `vetoed` is the only terminal state that fits ("the merchant said no"). But a discard is a judgement on *this article*, not on the subject: main §8.7's not-interested list exists so a topic a merchant rejected is never re-proposed, and someone who throws away a weak draft about a subject they do want covered would be silently barred from ever getting another. Vetoing the topic remains the action that says "not this subject", with its own different consequences.
Nearest spec: main §9.3 (approve or discard), §8.7 (veto, the not-interested list).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.5 — Gate 1's link task is read back off its own decision row; a topic with no stored cluster falls back to its own keyword
Decision: the daily cycle reads `gate_decisions.scores_json.linkTask.existingUrl` for the topic's Gate 1 row to supply the pipeline's `linkTaskUrl`, and where a topic has no `keyword_cluster` it hands the pipeline a one-term cluster made from the topic's own target keyword.
Why: both facts the pipeline needs are stored somewhere other than the topic. Gate 1 writes its link task — the existing page an article should link to where the existing-target check found a weak match (main §7.7 step 4) — only onto its audit row, so that row is the only place to read it; the alternative was passing `null` always, which would silently drop a spec-required link. The cluster fallback is because `topics.keyword_cluster` is nullable by design (a manually-added topic may name no cluster) and the head term of a cluster is the same term the topic targets, so a one-term cluster is accurate rather than invented. Stopping instead would refuse to write for every manually-added topic.
Nearest spec: main §7.7 step 4; main §8.7 (manual add); `packages/db/src/schema/content-engine.ts` (`topics.keyword_cluster` nullable).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.5 — A store with no confirmed profile stops the day rather than generating in a guessed language
Decision: `runDailyGenerationForAccount` throws `TopicNotGeneratable` (non-retryable, its own error class) when the account has no `personas` row, because that row is where the store's language and country come from.
Why: the search vendor and the writer both take a locale, and a wrong one silently buys the wrong market's numbers and writes in the wrong language. Main §14.4's governing rule is "degrade to pause, never to lower quality", and there is no defensible default — `en-US` would be a guess about a merchant's own market. A non-retryable failure dead-letters where somebody is alerted rather than retrying forever against a condition no retry fixes.
Nearest spec: main §14.4; main §6.5 (persona language and country).

## 2026-09-03 — T4.5 — The cycle emits one `generation_cycle_completed` analytics event, carrying ids and an outcome only
Decision: a day that actually ran captures `generation_cycle_completed` with the account attribution and `{status, outcome}`. A day that was skipped captures nothing and is recorded in the logs only.
Why: not asked for by the card, and small enough to state rather than assume. Main §14.7 wants the pipeline observable, and this is the one job in the product that can spend several dollars in a run; a skipped day emits nothing because the skips are frequent, uninteresting individually, and already in the structured logs. No article text, no prompt and no token count crosses the boundary — invariant 26. Nothing in `ops/posthog` charts it yet; that is the ops lane's to add if it wants to.
Nearest spec: main §14.7; CLAUDE.md invariant 26.

## 2026-09-03 — T4.6 — Replenishment fills the calendar to 90 days, and that number is new
Decision: a new threshold, `learning.replenishment_target_horizon_days: 90`, marked UNSIGNED in `packages/rules/signals.config.yaml`. Replenishment fires when the planned horizon falls under `replenishment_horizon_days` (60, already there) and fills every open day from tomorrow to 90 days out.
Why: the specs give the trigger and never the target. Main §9.6.1 says "runs when the calendar's planned horizon drops below ~60 days"; main §8.7 describes "a ~3-month backlog built at onboarding, replenished monthly". 90 is that three months as a number. It has to be above the trigger or every run would leave the calendar back on the edge it had just crossed. The consequence worth naming: on an empty calendar one run offers 90 days, so a store with a deep candidate pool commits three months of topics in one pass, against evidence that will be up to three months old when the last of them is written.
Nearest spec: main §8.7 ("~3-month backlog"); main §9.6.1 (the trigger only).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.6 — The refresh cap is a share of the days on offer, not of the days actually filled
Decision: at most `floor(open_days × 0.40)` of a replenishment batch may be refreshes. When there are fewer new-coverage candidates than remaining days, the realised proportion of *filled* days can exceed 40% — and is allowed to.
Why: main §9.6.5 says "refreshes fill at most 40% of any replenishment batch, **so new coverage never stalls**". The purpose clause decides the ambiguity. Read the other way — the cap as a share of what actually gets placed — the rule turns destructive at the edges: a store with 30 refresh candidates and no new-coverage candidates at all would be allowed zero topics, and its calendar would go empty while work was waiting. Under the reading taken here, the realised share only goes above 40% when nothing was displaced, i.e. when every new-coverage candidate already has a day and the refreshes are using days that would otherwise be empty. Nothing stalls, which is what the cap is for.
Nearest spec: main §9.6.5 — the word "batch" is not defined.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.6 — The exploration reservation is filled by promotion after scoring, not reserved before it
Decision: the score picks the batch first. Unexplored candidates — ones whose intent class, family, keyword cluster and action type all carry no active pattern — are pulled in afterwards, displacing the weakest *explored* picks, only until the reservation (`max(2, ceil(slots × 0.15))`) is met. Only the candidates promoted this way are stored as `source: 'exploration'` and carry the "trying something new" why-line.
Why: main §9.6.6 reserves the slots and says "these are marked `source: exploration` internally, so their performance can later be compared against exploited picks". The comparison is the point, and a reserve-first rule destroys it under the conditions the product actually ships in: nothing writes `pattern_stats` (that is `T7.1`, and M7 is deferred out of v1), so today **every** candidate is unexplored and a reserve-first algorithm would label an entire batch "trying something new" with no exploited arm to compare against. Promotion-after-scoring degrades correctly: with no patterns at all the score pass already satisfies the floor, nothing is promoted, and nothing is mislabelled.
Nearest spec: main §9.6.6.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.6 — A candidate is ranked on the opportunity's own stored score, not on a re-derived volume × winnability
Decision: the "opportunity" term of main §9.6.4's `score = opportunity × pattern_multipliers × source_bonus` is the `opportunities.impact_score` the engine already computed at detection. The two multipliers are applied fresh at planning time.
Why: §9.6.4 defines the opportunity term as `log(volume) × winnability`, and the engine already computes exactly that (`createOpportunityScore`, `packages/core/src/opportunities/scoring.ts`) when it creates the row. Re-deriving it here would be worse, not merely redundant: **winnability is not stored on the opportunity at all** — only Gate 1 and one signal write it as evidence — so a re-derivation would have to substitute the pessimistic Limited-Intelligence constant for every account, including accounts with Search Console connected. Reusing the stored number keeps whatever real calibration the engine had. The multipliers are deliberately *not* reused, because re-ranking by what the store has learned since detection is the entire purpose of scoring at replenishment rather than at detection.
One consequence: `impact_score` is a percentile within its own action family (§7.6, "never across actions"), so a CREATE score and a REFRESH score are not strictly on one scale. The batch never has to compare them directly — refreshes compete against the cap, not against creates — but a tie between the two families is resolved arbitrarily rather than meaningfully.
Nearest spec: main §9.6.4; main §7.6.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T4.6 — Replenishment claims an opportunity before it writes the topic, not after
Decision: for each pick, the guarded `accepted → scheduled` transition runs first; only a pass that wins it writes a calendar topic. A placement that then fails puts the opportunity back to `accepted`.
Why: this is the opposite order to the onboarding seed (`packages/jobs/src/scan/onboarding.ts`, DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T3.7), deliberately, and the difference is which way a crash can go wrong. Place-then-claim means a run that dies between the two comes back, finds the opportunity still waiting, and gives it a second calendar day — two articles on one subject, on two days, which is what the calendar exists to prevent. Claim-then-place can only lose a candidate: a crash leaves an opportunity marked `scheduled` with no topic, which costs one candidate and nothing a merchant sees. A duplicated article-day is worse than a missed one. Caught by this card's own test, which reproduced the duplicate before the order was changed.
Nearest spec: main §7.9 (`accepted → scheduled`); CLAUDE.md invariant 15 (guarded transitions).

Decision: `schedule(opportunity, date?, placement?)`, where `placement` carries the score, the source (`auto` or `exploration`) and the why-line key. The frozen `TopicScheduler` interface is untouched; extra optional parameters still satisfy it.
Why: replenishment needs to stamp three things on a topic that the opportunity alone cannot supply, and the alternatives were worse. Re-opening the frozen contract is an integrator decision and would touch Lane C. A second insert path in the replenishment job would mean two pieces of code writing `topics` rows, which is how the two drift. The onboarding seed passes nothing and keeps exactly the behaviour it had.

## 2026-09-03 — T4.6 — The scheduler now reads `family_id` evidence facts onto the topic, which it previously dropped
Decision: `DbTopicScheduler` collects every `family_id` evidence fact into `topics.family_ids` (de-duplicated, non-uuid values discarded). An opportunity carrying none still schedules and still writes `[]`.
Why: **this was a live defect, not a refinement, and it is why the M4 end-to-end flow did not run.** `T4.2` wrote `familyIds` as `[]` unconditionally because the frozen `Opportunity` type has no array-valued field, and flagged it for whoever built the first real caller. Meanwhile `T3.7` established exactly the convention needed — a repeated `family_id` fact, one per family, with the encoding explained in the detectors' own comments — and every new-coverage detector already emits it. The scheduler simply never read the other half. The observable consequence: every automatically scheduled topic reached the pipeline with no families, so its evidence pack had no product facts, so Gate 2 held it as a thin pack. **The product could not write a single article on its own.** Found by this card's own end-to-end test, which returned `held_thin_pack` before the fix and a graded article after. This is the application of a documented convention, not a new one, so it is journalled rather than escalated.
Nearest spec: main §8.2 (substance inventory); DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T4.2 and T3.7.

## 2026-09-03 — T4.6 — REQUEST TO THE INTEGRATOR: a unique index on `(account_id, scheduled_date)` for `topics`
Decision: **not taken, and no migration written.** Recording the request for the next schema wave.
Why: "at most one article a day" rests on there never being two non-vetoed topics on one calendar date, and nothing at database level enforces that. The manual-add route checks the day is free and then inserts, with no account lock around the pair — the exact check-then-insert shape invariant 1 rejects for domain claims ("insert-with-conflict, never check-then-insert"). Two simultaneous adds both pass the check and both land, and that day can then dequeue twice. Replenishment is the reason this is worth raising now: it inserts in bulk, and although it holds the per-account advisory lock — so it cannot race itself or the daily cycle — the manual-add route does not take that lock, so a merchant adding a topic while a top-up is running is unprotected. Also flagged as MEDIUM by the `T4.5` audit. What is needed: a partial unique index on `(account_id, scheduled_date)` where `state <> 'vetoed'`, plus turning the manual-add check into an insert-with-conflict. A feature card may not add a migration (CLAUDE.md), so this waits.
Nearest spec: main §8.7 ("at most 1 article per day"); CLAUDE.md invariant 1 (the shape), invariant 14.

## 2026-09-03 — T4.6 — `pnpm stubs:report --milestone=<M>` compared milestone labels as strings
Decision: fixed, in `scripts/stub-report.mjs`: milestone labels are parsed to numbers before comparison. A label the script cannot parse now sorts last, so it can never fail a gate by accident.
Why: `'M10' <= 'M4'` is `true` in JavaScript, because "1" sorts before "4". Every milestone gate from M2 onwards was therefore reporting the two M10-due counters (`JudgeOutcomeCounter`, `PublishOutcomeCounter`) as overdue, and any reader had to know to discount them. The M4 gate said seven; four are real. Not a design change — the gate's own arithmetic was wrong, and this card's deliverable is a truthful report from it. Nothing became green: M4 still fails on four genuine entries, and M3 still fails on one.
Nearest spec: build plan §4 ("a CI check fails if any stub is still wired at M3 exit").

## 2026-09-03 — T4.6 — FLAGGED, NOT DECIDED: a run killed before local midnight is never finished, and `pnpm chaos` is now red because of it
Decision: **none taken.** A new chaos scenario reproduces the `T4.5` audit's HIGH finding instead of describing it, and it fails. Nothing in the product was changed to make it pass.
Why: the daily cycle resumes a dead attempt by looking for a topic left `generating` **on today's local date only**. A retry that lands after the store's local midnight looks at the new day, finds nothing planned, reports "skipped", and the queue marks the job green — while yesterday's half-written article sits in `generating` with nothing in the product that will ever look at it again. Reproduced: `generation_cycle_killed_same_day` converges (one article, one Gate 3 decision); `generation_cycle_killed_across_midnight`, identical except that the retry runs a day later, ends with one article written, none graded, and the topic stuck. Ordinary rather than exotic for a store whose writing starts in the evening.
Why it is not fixed here: every way of converging is a product decision. Finishing yesterday's draft today means an article appears on a day the calendar did not schedule; abandoning it means deciding what the merchant is told about a day that silently produced nothing; a separate sweeper is a third shape with its own cadence. It also interacts with invariant 14 (gaps stay gaps, missed days are never back-filled). And today the resume is only consulted when the current day has *no* planned topic at all, so any real fix is more than one clause. Recommendation: a sweeper that finds topics left `generating` past their own day, finishes the one that is furthest along, and raises a dead letter for the rest — but that is the founder's or integrator's call.
Consequence to accept knowingly: `pnpm chaos` fails on exactly this one scenario until the decision is made. That is the M4 exit gate reporting a hole rather than hiding one.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.1–14.3.4 (effectively-once), main §9.1, main §8.7 (the gap rule); `docs/overnight-state.md`, `T4.5` audit, second HIGH finding.

## 2026-09-03 — T4.6 — FLAGGED, NOT DECIDED: nothing runs Gate 1 on a replenished topic after scoring
Decision: **none taken.** Replenishment schedules its picks without a Gate 1 pass.
Why it matters: main §9.6.4 ends with "Gate 1 runs *after* scoring, on the top-ranked candidates only (cheapest ordering: score with data already on hand, spend DataForSEO/SERP checks only on likely picks)." Nothing does that. What *is* checked, and where: the demand floor, the substance backing and the existing-target check are all applied when the opportunity is detected, weeks earlier — so the checks are made, but never re-made against the store as it is when the topic is actually scheduled. A family that has lost substance since detection, or a page the merchant has since published covering the same intent, would not be noticed. The visible symptom is small today (a stale candidate is held by Gate 2 at generation time, with a reason card) and the audit trail is the honest tell: an automatically scheduled topic has **no `gate_decisions` row for gate 1 at all**, while a manually added one does.
Why it is not built here: it is not in this card's scope line, and the design has real content — does a re-check that now fails reject the topic, convert it to OPTIMIZE, or just skip it and free the day; and does it spend a SERP request per pick, which is the cost §9.6.4's ordering exists to control. Recommendation: a small `admitScheduledTopic` mirroring `admitManualTopic`, run on the batch's picks before placement, with a failing re-check freeing the day rather than writing a rejected topic.
Nearest spec: main §9.6.4 (last line); main §8.2; main §8.7 ("filtered by Gate 1").

## 2026-09-03 — T4.6 — Gate 2 records only its refusals, so a passing pack leaves no audit row
Decision: unchanged; recorded because this card's end-to-end test asserts the absence and a reader would otherwise assume the row is there.
Why: `generateArticle` writes a `gate_decisions` row for gate 2 only on `held_thin_pack`. Gate 3 writes one either way. So the audit trail for a successful article shows Gate 3 and nothing else, and "was the evidence pack checked" can only be answered by the absence of a hold. Defensible — the merchant needs a reason card for a refusal, not for a pass — but it makes gate coverage un-auditable after the fact, which is what `gate_decisions` is for. Not this card's to change.

## 2026-09-03 — FOUNDER — An overridden article is delivered on its flag now, and on a recorded decision later
Decision: two halves, both authorised. **Now:** `articlesReadyForDelivery` (`packages/db/src/repositories/articles.ts`) returns an article whose subject passed the quality gate **or** which carries `published_via_override`. **Later, when the override route is actually built (T5.1/T5.2):** that route also writes a `gate_decisions` row with outcome `overridden`, in the same transaction as the flag, and the read accepts that outcome too.
Why: the read is the single place that answers "which finished articles go out today", and it currently requires a gate decision recorded as `passed`. Overriding a rejection sets the flag and moves the article back to `draft` and writes nothing to the decision trail at all, so an overridden article's only decision stays the rejection and the read can never return it. Publishing would have been built and tested against an ordinary passing article and would have silently done nothing for the one case "publish anyway" exists to serve. Both halves were offered separately. The flag-only fix is correct today with nothing to remember, but leaves `published_via_override` carrying two meanings — "keep this out of what we learn from" and "cleared to go out" — which is the muddle open question 9 already names. The decision-row fix is the cleaner model and fills a real hole (an override presently leaves no trace in the audit trail), but only works if whoever builds the override route remembers two writes in one transaction — which the `T4.4` audit specifically called "the shape most likely to be got wrong later". The founder chose both, so delivery is correct even if the second write is forgotten, and the trail is complete when it is not. No migration: `gate_decisions.outcome` is free text, and `OVERRIDE_GATE_OUTCOME = 'overridden'` is already defined in `packages/core/src/gates/gate3/override.ts` and written by nothing.
Nearest spec: main §8.6 (override), main §13 `gate_decisions`; CLAUDE.md invariant 12.

## 2026-09-03 — FOUNDER — The draft-ready notification is built now, in Lane D, alongside the override fix
Decision: `draft_ready_for_review` is emitted at the moment an article enters `in_review` — `packages/jobs/src/generation/daily-cycle.ts`. Carded as `R-DELIVER` with the override fix, ahead of `T5.1`. This answers the first half of open question 13; the second half of that question (does an overridden article still pass through draft review on accounts that have review switched on) is untouched and still open.
Why: draft review's toggle exists, defaults to off, and switching it on today makes articles disappear — nothing tells the merchant a draft is waiting. The notification type, its wording and its email template all already exist; only the sending was missing. The alternatives offered were to build only the dashboard list (a merchant who never visits is still never told) or to hide the toggle in Settings (takes a shipped feature back off the shelf and needs another lane's file). It is placed in Lane D rather than in Lane G's `R-ARTICLES`, despite Lane G owning notifications, because the emission point is in Lane D's directory and `T5.1` is about to edit that same file for the publish-hour day-skew fix — a cross-lane write there would have collided at merge.
Nearest spec: main §9.3 (draft review); tech §1 (notifications are append-only, unique on `(account_id, type, dedupe_key)`).
