## 2026-09-03 — R-DELIVER — The two override arms of the delivery read are one subquery, not two
Decision: `articlesReadyForDelivery` asks for a `draft` article that either (a) has a gate 3 decision on its topic whose outcome is `passed` **or** `overridden`, or (b) carries `published_via_override`. The founder's decision describes three routes to delivery; the first and third are the same shape — "a gate 3 decision on this topic, with one of two outcomes" — so they are one `IN` list over the decision table rather than two separate subqueries OR-ed together.
Why: the two forms produce identical rows and identical plans (the same index on `(topic_id, gate)` serves both), and one list is the shorter thing to read. The three routes are still three, and each has its own test: an overridden article with only a rejection recorded, an article whose override was recorded as a decision and whose flag is *not* set, and an ungraded draft that must stay out.
Nearest spec: main §8.6; `DECISIONS.md` 2026-09-03 FOUNDER "An overridden article is delivered on its flag now".
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — R-DELIVER — The draft-ready notification is sent at the transition, refuses to announce a draft that is not waiting, and is a compile error to leave unwired
Decision: three choices the founder's decision did not settle. (a) **Where in the run:** the notification is written immediately after the article is moved into review, inside the per-account lock and *before* the day is recorded as done — the same emission point as the state change it reports, matching every other emission point in the codebase, which all let a failure fail the run. (b) **When not to send:** the move into review is a guarded update that matches nothing if the merchant vetoed the topic while the article was being written. When it matches nothing, the article is read back, and only one actually sitting in review is announced. A retry of a run that died between the move and the send therefore still announces (the article is in review, the key is its id, the second write is a no-op); a discarded article never does. (c) **How it is wired:** the emitter is optional on the cycle's own dependencies, so the chaos scenario and the sweep tests run without one, but **required** on `GenerationTaskDeps`, the type the running product is built from — leaving it unwired is a type error rather than a silence.
Why: (a) putting the send after the day's ledger write would mean a crash between the two loses the notification permanently — the retry finds the day already done and returns without running anything. Putting it before means a bell failure costs a re-run of the day, which is two model calls (the writer's stored draft is reused; the claim plan and the judge run again), and that is the cheaper of the two mistakes. (b) the `T4.5` audit recorded that both guarded updates in this branch ignore their zero-row result and the run then reports that the merchant was asked to review a draft that was already discarded — it specifically warned this would matter "once something emits the 'your draft is waiting' notification from that branch". This is that moment, so the notification checks; **the wider finding is untouched — the run still reports `awaitsReview: true` in that case, and the analytics event still says so.** That belongs to whoever actions the audit finding. (c) an optional dependency that silently does nothing is the failure this card exists to fix, restated one level up.
Nearest spec: main §9.3 (draft review), main §8.7 (the veto race); tech §1.2 (notifications are written at the domain event, unique on `(account_id, type, dedupe_key)`).
## 2026-09-03 — T6.1 — The comparison is cached on the page's fingerprint and the identity of one reading of the results, not on the search
Decision: an intent-gap analysis is stored in `request_cache` under `intent-gap.v1:<page checksum>:<serp cache key>:<snapshot fetched-at>`, with `kind: 'llm'` and the results page's own expiry as its lifetime. What is stored is the model's raw answer; the gap set is derived from it on every read.
Why: the card asks for a cache keyed by "(page_checksum, serp_snapshot_id)". There is no `serp_snapshot_id` — `serp_snapshots` is keyed by a cache key derived from the search, the market and how many results were asked for (`packages/db/src/schema/search.ts`), and a refresh **replaces** the row under that same key. So the key alone identifies the search, not the reading; adding the moment the reading was taken is what makes "the same snapshot" mean the same snapshot. The practical difference: without the timestamp, a re-bought results page a week later would silently serve last week's comparison. Storing the raw answer rather than the gap set matters for a second reason — the consensus floor (`subtopic_present_on_top_pages_min`) lives in `packages/rules`, and a stored conclusion would freeze the threshold that produced it into data, so changing the number would require buying every comparison again. `kind` stays one of the two values the port documents (`llm` / `dataforseo`) rather than gaining a third: nothing reads it for behaviour, and a third value with no consumer is a vocabulary change without a reason.
Nearest spec: main §10.3 step 2, §14.3.6, §7.10; invariants 9 and 20.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T6.1 — Evidence from a coverage comparison is labelled as a reading, not a measurement
Decision: facts derived from the subtopic analysis carry `source: 'serp_coverage_analysis'` — a new value beside the existing `gsc`, `dataforseo`, `catalog` and `content_inventory`. Position and impressions on the same opportunity keep `gsc`; the count of results pages analysed carries `dataforseo`.
Why: every other evidence fact in the product is a number somebody measured. "This page settles waterproofing and ours does not" is a model's reading of two documents, and a card that presents both under one source label invites a merchant — or a later card writing a why-line — to treat them as equally hard. Main §7.6 requires a source per fact precisely so the difference survives into the row. The name says which analysis produced it, so a future comparison of a different kind gets its own label rather than diluting this one.
Nearest spec: main §7.6 (evidence with source per fact), §10.3 step 2; invariant 7.

## 2026-09-03 — T6.1 — Each missing subtopic contributes three evidence facts under a shared index
Decision: a gap set of four subtopics writes `missing_subtopic_1` (the name), `missing_subtopic_1_top_page_count` (how many of the ranking pages settle it) and `missing_subtopic_1_top_page_headings` (which pages, under which headings), and the same triple at `_2`, `_3`, `_4`, alongside a `missing_subtopic_count`.
Why: `EvidenceFact` is a flat `{key, value}` pair with no nesting (`packages/core/src/contracts/opportunities.ts`, frozen at T0.7), so a subtopic's name and its support cannot travel as one object. Pairing by index lets a card render "waterproofing — on 5 of the 5 pages above you" without parsing a sentence, and keeps the addresses in the row so the merchant can open the pages the claim rests on. The alternative — one fact per subtopic with the support flattened into its value string — would have made every renderer a parser.
Nearest spec: main §10.3 step 2, §7.6; build plan `T6.1` done-when ("with per-subtopic evidence").
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T6.1 — One weekly pass compares at most as many pages as the store may analyse in a day
Decision: `scanIntentGaps` caps its shortlist at `budgets.intent_gap.analyses_per_account_per_day` (10). No new number was added to `signals.config.yaml`.
Why: the shortlist could be long — a store with a hundred pages sitting between positions 4 and 20 is ordinary — and comparing all of them in one pass would spend the store's whole allowance and trip its own kill switch in the first minute of the scan. A per-pass cap is needed, but it answers the same question the daily allowance already answers ("how many of these may we pay for"), and two numbers for one question drift apart. Consequence, stated plainly: a store with more shortlisted pages than its allowance never reaches the ones ranked lowest by impressions, because each pass restarts from the top of the same ordering. That is a real limitation and the right one to have first — the pages with the most impressions are where editing pays — but it should be revisited if a store's shortlist routinely exceeds its allowance.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 (per-type cap), §7.10; invariants 9 and 17.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — T6.1 — FLAGGED: nothing calls the intent-gap scan, because the call site is in another lane's file
Decision: **none taken.** `scanIntentGaps` is built, tested and exported, and no code path reaches it.
Why: the card asks for the analysis to be wired "as the *Existing Page Intent Gap* signal (weekly scan, shortlisted pages only)". The weekly scan is one function, `runSignalScan` (`packages/jobs/src/scan/run.ts`), which assembles every detector's inputs, runs them, and reconciles the results onto the `opportunities` table under the account lock. That file belongs to Lane C (build plan §3), and this lane may not edit it. There is no registry a detector can add itself to — `runSignalScan` names its detectors in a hard-coded list — so there is no in-lane way to wire this up.
What it costs today: the `existing_page_intent_gap` signal is detectable but never detected in production. Everything downstream of a detection is already proven — this card's test drives a real store page and a real results page through the analysis and then through Lane C's own `buildOpportunityDraft`, which turns the signal into an OPTIMIZE on the page that exists.
What the wiring needs, precisely: inside `runSignalScanLocked`, after the GSC detectors, one call — `signals.push(...(await scanIntentGaps({db, seo, pageFetcher, llm, prompt, logger}, {accountId, clusters, rows, locale, allowSerpSpend})).signals)` — plus `existing_page_intent_gap` added to `GSC_SIGNAL_TYPES`, and three new dependencies on `RunSignalScanDeps` (a page fetcher, an LLM client, and the loaded prompt) threaded from the composition root. It should sit behind the same limited-intelligence check as the other GSC detectors, since the shortlist reads Search Console positions. Integrator or Lane C, not this card.
Nearest spec: main §10.3 step 2, §7.3 (Existing Page Intent Gap, "runs only on candidates that already passed cheap filters"); build plan §3 (lane ownership), `T6.1`.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — FOUNDER — An article belongs to the day it appears, not the day it was written
Decision: the calendar day a topic is taken from is derived from the clock at the **publish** moment, not at the moment writing starts. Tuesday's calendar slot publishes on Tuesday whatever hour the store publishes at; the writing for it starts Monday evening. Folded into `T5.1`, whose subject matter it is, rather than patched separately afterwards.
Why: writing starts six hours before the publish hour and wraps backwards over midnight — deliberate, and already tested. But the day whose topic was taken was the local date when writing *started*, so a store publishing at 02:00 took Tuesday's topic on Tuesday evening and published it on Wednesday, leaving the calendar and the live store **permanently one day apart** with nothing explaining it. The publish hour is merchant-settable with no lower bound, so this is configuration rather than a corner case. Raised as a MEDIUM finding by the `T4.5` audit and as open question 16. The two rejected alternatives: keeping the current behaviour (the skew is permanent and invisible), and forbidding publish hours before 06:00 (cheapest, but removes a setting the spec gives the merchant, and an early-morning publish is a reasonable thing to want).
Nearest spec: main §9.4 (publish hour), §9.1 (generation cycle), §8.7 (one topic per day).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-03 — FOUNDER — The intent-gap comparison runs as its own job, and the weekly scan reads what it produced
Decision: the paid page comparison `T6.1` built does **not** run inside the weekly signal scan. It runs as a scheduled pass of its own; the scan reads what that pass already produced and derives the signal for free, buying nothing. Carded as `R-INTENTGAP-JOB` (Lane E, the job) and `R-INTENTGAP-SCAN` (Lane C, the read).
Why: `T6.1`'s card says the analysis is "wired ... as the *Existing Page Intent Gap* signal (weekly scan, shortlisted pages only)", and the obvious reading is to call it inside the scan. But the weekly scan is currently free arithmetic over data already assembled — every other detector is a pure function — and putting external page fetches and a Sonnet call inside it would make it a paid, minutes-long job whose failure takes every other signal down with it. Spend is already capped and pausable through `budgets.intent_gap` and `account.pause_intent_gap`, so the *cost* was bounded either way; the runtime and the blast radius were not. The rejected third option was leaving it unwired, which keeps the analysis available to `T6.2`'s pipeline but leaves `T6.1`'s scope half-delivered and easy to forget.
The mechanism, and why it needs no migration: `analyseIntentGap` already writes the model's raw answer to `request_cache`, keyed on the page checksum, the results-page cache key and when that results page was fetched — a key the scan can recompute for a shortlisted page without buying anything. So the cache *is* the feed. If that turns out not to hold, the lane stops and reports rather than reaching for a table, because a new table is a schema wave.
Nearest spec: main §10.3 (steps 1–2), §7.3 (the Intent Gap row), §14.5 (per-type cap); build plan §6 `T6.1`.
Class (filled by audit):
## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — A suggestion cites evidence by address, and there are exactly three kinds of address
Decision: every passage a recommendation suggests names the evidence behind it as `product:<id>/<fact-sheet field>`, `family:<id>/axis:<axis>`, or `subtopic:<name>`. The grounding lint fails anything citing an address the evidence pack did not contain, and fails a passage citing nothing at all. `packFacts()` (`packages/core/src/optimize/pack.ts`) is the one place the set is built, and it is what both the prompt and the lint read.
Why: main §10.3's own example writes `"family:123/material"` and says every `facts_used` "must resolve to a fact-sheet field". Taken literally that would leave the commonest suggestion we make — "the pages above you all settle waterproofing and yours does not" — with nothing citable behind it, because it rests on a reading of competitor pages rather than on a fact the store recorded. So the address space is the two the spec names plus one for the comparison, and the third is deliberately a different word: anything rendered from it can say that it is a reading of other people's pages, not a measurement, which is the same distinction `T6.1` drew for evidence sources. **The consequence to be aware of**: a section may be grounded entirely in `subtopic:` addresses, which means the store's own facts are not necessarily behind every suggestion — the grader's grounding criterion is what catches copy that then asserts something the store never recorded.
Nearest spec: main §10.3 steps 3–4; invariant 7.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — The evidence pack is not stored, and the download rebuilds only the labels it needs
Decision: `optimize_recommendations` holds the recommendation, the grader's scores and the stamps, and not the evidence pack it was made from. The Markdown/HTML download takes the plain-language labels as an argument, and the route rebuilds them from the store's families and products at download time. An address whose label has gone is still shown — a subtopic as the phrase it names, anything else as the address itself.
Why: the pack is large and almost all of it is a copy of rows that have their own home in the database — the page, the fact sheets, the results page. Keeping a copy would mean a second, staler answer to every question those tables already answer, and there is no column for it (schema wave 4 is closed to this card). What the merchant actually needs from the pack when they open the download days later is one label per citation, and those come back from the store's own rows for the price of two indexed queries and no vendor call. The cost, stated plainly: if a product is deleted after the recommendation was made, its citation renders as the raw address rather than as "Fell Walker — material". Never dropped, because a citation the merchant cannot see is a claim with nothing behind it.
Nearest spec: main §10.3 step 1, §10.4; invariant 20.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — The one re-ask is spent on the free checks; a grader failure ends the run
Decision: a recommendation failing the lints is generated once more with the failures attached. If the second attempt also fails, or if the grader marks either criterion below its floor, the run stores a `failed_validation` record and stops. The grader is never asked about output the lints already refused.
Why: main §10.3 step 4 allows exactly one regeneration and step 5 adds the grader without saying what a grader failure does. The two failures are not alike: a lint failure is objective and local — "you cited an address nobody gave you" — and a model can act on it, whereas a grader failure is a judgement about the whole artefact, and asking again with "it was not grounded enough" attached is paying to talk a recommendation past its own quality bar. Invariant 22's posture (degrade to pause, never to lower quality) points the same way. What this costs: a merchant whose page produces a weak recommendation twice sees one sentence and has spent a generation from their daily allowance — deliberate, because the alternative is showing them the parts that passed, and they have no way to know what was dropped.
Nearest spec: main §10.3 steps 4–5, §14.4; invariant 11 (one repair loop), invariant 22.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — Intent match is held at 4, which the shared grader's own floors would not do
Decision: `LlmJudgeLite` (Lane D's seam) makes the model call and returns the scores; the pass/fail decision for OPTIMIZE is made in `evaluateOptimizeFloors` (`packages/core/src/optimize/grading.ts`) against two new numbers in `signals.config.yaml`, `gates.optimize_recommendation.grounding_min` and `intent_match_min`, both 4. The verdict's own `passed` is deliberately not read.
Why: main §10.3 step 5 says the two criteria have floors of 4. The shared grader computes `passed` from the *article* floors, where only information gain and grounding sit at 4 and everything else — search-intent match included — passes at 3. Consuming its verdict as-is would silently drop the OPTIMIZE bar by a point on the criterion that decides whether the edits address the search at all. The seam is still used for what it is for (a separate, blind grading call on a model that is never the cheap tier); only the floors are ours.
Nearest spec: main §10.3 step 5, §8.4, §7.10; invariants 9 and 11.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — The daily cap is counted in generations, not in model calls, and the two meters disagree
Decision: the in-request cap counts recommendations written today plus generations currently running for the account (`countOptimizeGenerationsSince`, `packages/db/src/repositories/optimize.ts`), against `budgets.optimize.generations_per_account_per_day`. The third request in a day is refused with 409 `optimize_daily_cap_reached`.
Why: main §10.2 caps *generations*, and one generation may make two model calls when the first output fails a lint — charging a merchant's allowance for our own re-ask would take a day away from them over our mistake. The in-flight half is needed because a generation asked for a minute ago has written no row yet, and without it two quick clicks both pass. **Flagged, not fixed**: the nightly auto-trip sweep (`packages/jobs/src/sweeps/auto-trips.ts`, Lane G's file) counts the same allowance in `optimize_reco` *model calls*, trips at used ≥ cap, and the flag it raises is sticky until an operator clears it. So a store that legitimately uses both its generations today — or one whose single generation needed a re-ask — has its OPTIMIZE calls paused indefinitely from then on. That is a real defect reachable for the first time now that anything makes these calls, and it belongs to the sweep's owner: either the trip should fire above the cap rather than at it, or the account flags need a daily expiry.
Nearest spec: main §10.2, §14.5, §7.10; invariants 16 and 17.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — Generation happens in a job, and the route only records that the merchant asked
Decision: `POST /api/recommendations` checks entitlement, the cap and the switches, moves the opportunity to `executing` with a guarded update, and queues `optimize_recommendation_generate`. The work — a results-page read, up to five competitor fetches and two model calls — runs in the worker under the account's advisory lock. On completion the opportunity goes back to `accepted` with the recommendation attached, and `optimize_recommendation_ready` is emitted.
Why: the frozen API contract answers this route with `ok` rather than with a recommendation, and its detail response has a `generating` state, so an asynchronous generation is what the contract already describes. It is also the only shape that works: a minute-long request would be cut off by any proxy in front of the app long before it finished. No notification is emitted when a generation fails its checks — the only notification type available says the recommendations are ready, and they are not; the card carries the failure instead.
Nearest spec: main §10.2, ui §5.4; tech §3; `packages/core/src/api/routes.ts`.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — FLAGGED: the generation job is registered by a function nothing calls
Decision: **none taken.** `registerOptimizeTasks` (`packages/jobs/src/optimize/tasks.ts`) is built, typed and exported, and no code path calls it.
Why: task handlers are registered in `apps/web/instrumentation-node.ts`, the process's composition root. Build plan §3 lists that file as one of the ordered, integrator-resolved files no lane may edit, and it is outside this lane's directories. So the queue row is written by the route and the handler that would pick it up is not installed.
What it costs today: a merchant clicking "Generate recommendations" moves the opportunity to `executing` and the job waits in the queue forever. Everything either side of that seam is proven — this card's tests drive the route to the queue row, and the pipeline from an opportunity to a stored recommendation, against a real database.
What the wiring needs, precisely: in `startServerRuntime()`, alongside the other `register*Tasks` calls, `registerOptimizeTasks({ getPool: dbPool, deps })` where `deps` is a `GenerateOptimizeDeps` — the database, a `SeoDataProvider`, the guarded page fetcher, the LLM client, `loadPrompt('intent-gap', 1)` and `loadPrompt('optimize-reco', 1)`, a `judge` factory returning `new LlmJudgeLite({ llm, prompt: loadPrompt('optimize-judge', 1), criteria: OPTIMIZE_JUDGE_CRITERIA, config: rules().defaults.gates.draft_grading, accountId })`, and the notification emitter. Integrator, not this card.
Nearest spec: build plan §3 (ordered files, integrator-resolved), `T6.2`; main §10.2.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — FLAGGED: the OPTIMIZE routes live at `/api/recommendations`, which the frozen contract does not have
Decision: the routes are `POST /api/recommendations`, `GET /api/recommendations`, `GET /api/recommendations/{id}/download` and `POST /api/recommendations/{id}/apply`, as the card's scope line and the lane's directory list say.
Why: the frozen route table (`packages/core/src/api/routes.ts`, T0.7) puts the same two actions under Lane C's directory instead — `POST /api/opportunities/{opportunityId}/recommendations` and `POST /api/opportunities/{opportunityId}/tasks/{taskId}` — and has no `/api/recommendations` path at all. Both files are outside this lane, so this card can neither implement those paths nor add its own to the contract. The build plan (§3 and the `T6.2` card) is the document this lane is directed by, so it is the one followed. **What this costs**: the frontend builds its mocks from the contract, so Lane F will mock two endpoints that do not exist and miss four that do; `pnpm contracts:check` does not catch it, because it compares the zod table against the generated OpenAPI document and never against the routes on disk. The fix is one of two things, and it is the integrator's call: add these four routes to the contract table, or move them under `/api/opportunities` when Lane C's directory is free.
Nearest spec: build plan §3 and `T6.2`; `packages/core/src/api/routes.ts`; ui §5.3–5.4.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — The applied-detection prompt is a live read, not a stored flag, and it is exact rather than fuzzy
Decision: "Looks like you applied this — confirm?" is computed on every read of a recommendation, by comparing the stored suggestions against the page as the last inventory sync found it (`detectApplied`, `packages/core/src/optimize/applied.ts`). A suggestion counts as applied only when the page's search-result title or description matches it exactly once whitespace and case are levelled, or when a suggested heading is now present. Nothing is stored, and nothing is ever marked applied without the merchant saying so.
Why: main §10.4 asks for detection "at the next content-inventory sync". The sync itself is Lane C's job and outside this lane, but the question the prompt answers — "is this suggestion on the page now?" — is a question about the world at the moment somebody looks, so a live read gives the same answer at the same time without a cross-lane seam and without a stored flag that would keep asking after the merchant had said no. It is also what invariant 26 asks for: attention items are live queries. **Exact rather than fuzzy** because a wrong guess here tells a merchant they did work they did not do; a merchant who retyped the suggestion in their own words is missed, and the only cost of that is the absent prompt.
Nearest spec: main §10.4, §12.3; invariant 26.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — Marking a recommendation applied books the measurement as a queued job, because there is no outcomes table
Decision: "Mark all as applied" completes the opportunity, stamps `applied_at`, and enqueues `opportunity_outcome_measure` with `run_at` set to `applied_at + learning.outcomes.maturity_days` (28). No handler for that task exists.
Why: the card's done-when asks that mark-applied "schedules an outcome row at +28 d". `opportunities.outcome_json` and `outcome_measured_at` are the columns an outcome eventually lands in, and they stay null until there is something honest to put in them (main §9.6.13's "no verdict before 28 days"), so there is no row to write today — what can be written is the *appointment*, and the queue is where appointments live. Writing it at the moment the merchant marks the work applied, rather than having a later sweep go looking for applied opportunities, makes "we will look at this page again on 1 October" a fact in one place from the moment the promise is made. A queued job whose task no worker knows about is not picked up and not lost. **What it costs**: the learning loop (`T7.1`) is deferred out of v1, so these jobs accumulate unrun until it ships; whoever builds it inherits a queue of real appointments rather than an empty table.
Nearest spec: main §10.4, §9.6.10; build plan M7 (deferred).

## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — Ranking-page content is read once, by the comparison, and not fetched again for the writing call
Decision: the evidence pack carries the ranking pages' addresses, domains and positions, and their *content* only as the subtopic comparison's output — what each page settles, under which heading. The pages are not fetched a second time to put their text in the writing prompt.
Why: main §10.3 step 1 lists "the top-5 ranking pages' extracted content" in the pack and step 2 then spends that content on the coverage comparison. Fetching them again would double the page-fetch bill and the SSRF-guarded fetcher's work for information the comparison has already distilled into exactly the form the writer needs — and on a cache hit the comparison does not fetch them at all, so a second fetch would also be the only thing making a cached comparison cost money. The writer therefore sees "three of the five pages above you settle sizing, under these headings", not five pages of competitor prose.
Nearest spec: main §10.3 steps 1–2, §14.3.6; invariant 20.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — Keyword stuffing is measured across the suggested prose, and never over the title or description
Decision: `gates.optimize_recommendation.keyword_density_max` (0.05) is read against all the recommendation's sections, answers and headings taken together, excluding the suggested search-result title and description.
Why: measured per passage the number is meaningless — one mention of a four-word search in a sixty-word section is already 7%, so every correct suggestion would fail — and measured including the title it is worse, because naming the search in the title is the thing a title is *for*. Main §10.3 says only "no keyword stuffing" and names no figure, so both the scope and the number are ours; the scope is the part that decides whether the check is real.
Nearest spec: main §10.3 step 4, §7.10; invariant 9.

## 2026-09-04 — T6.2 — This lane wrote outside its directories in three places, all of them additive
Decision: besides `packages/core/optimize`, `packages/jobs/optimize` and `apps/web/app/api/recommendations`, this card added `packages/db/src/repositories/optimize.ts` (a new file, plus one line in the union-merged repositories barrel), the thresholds above in `packages/rules` (`signals.config.yaml`, its JSON schema, its TypeScript view and the committed snapshot), one contiguous block of copy in `packages/ui/strings/en.json`, and two new prompt files in `packages/llm/prompts/`.
Why: recorded so a reviewer sees it rather than discovers it. The repository file is where the constitution puts table access — `packages/db` holds the repositories and `packages/core` may not import a database driver — and it touches no existing file except a barrel that is `merge=union` for exactly this reason. The thresholds are in `packages/rules` because no threshold literal may live anywhere else, and the strings are in the one catalogue all user-facing copy lives in. Nothing else in any of those packages was edited.
Nearest spec: CLAUDE.md code-structure rules; build plan §3 (append-only files); invariant 9.

## 2026-09-04 — R-INTENTGAP-JOB — The pass runs on the store's own Sunday, the day before the scan that reads it
Decision: the paid page comparison is queued for each store on its own local Sunday, by an hourly sweep that asks per store whether it is Sunday there — the same shape the weekly signal scan already uses for its Monday. One job per store, keyed on the store and that date, so the other 23 hours of the day queue nothing.
Why: the founder's split makes the comparison a producer and the weekly scan a consumer, and the scan runs on each store's local Monday. Something has to decide which day the producer runs on, and the day before is the only one that puts an answer on the shelf before the scan reaches for it. It is not tight timing: a comparison stays usable for as long as the results page it was made against, which is seven days (`discovery.cache.serp_snapshot_ttl_days`), so Sunday's answers are still there for Monday's scan and for the six days after it. Same-day was the alternative and was rejected because nothing orders two hourly sweeps against each other — on a bad week the scan would run first every time and find nothing. Hourly rather than a single weekly moment for the reason the crontab file already states: a crontab names a fixed UTC time, and no fixed time is Sunday everywhere at once, so a store far enough west would go permanently uncompared rather than merely late.
Nearest spec: main §7.5 (the cadence table), §10.3; DECISIONS 2026-09-03 FOUNDER (the intent-gap comparison runs as its own job).

## 2026-09-04 — R-INTENTGAP-JOB — Only stores with Search Console connected are swept, and the pass never rebuilds their clusters
Decision: the sweep considers a store only if it has a live Search Console connection, and the pass reads the store's query clusters — the groups of near-identical searches it is shown for — as the weekly scan last wrote them, rather than rebuilding them itself.
Why: two halves of the same point, which is that this pass must shortlist the same pages the scan will later come looking for. The shortlist is a filter over search positions, so a store without Search Console has nothing to shortlist and a pass for it would be a walk that buys nothing and finds nothing. And a cluster's head search is half of the key the scan recomputes to find a comparison — if the pass rebuilt the clusters and produced even a slightly different head, the scan would look under a key nothing was ever stored under. Rebuilding is also a write into another lane's table on a second schedule, which is the sort of thing that races. **The consequence, stated plainly:** a store that has never been scanned has no clusters, so this pass does nothing for it until its first scan has run. That is the right order — there is nothing to compare it on yet — but it does mean the pass is silent rather than merely empty for a brand-new store.
Nearest spec: main §6.7, §7.11 (Limited Intelligence), §10.3; DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T6.1 (the cache-key entry).

## 2026-09-04 — R-INTENTGAP-JOB — The pass has no cursor, because the cache it writes is the checkpoint
Decision: a pass that runs for minutes keeps no record of how far through the shortlist it got. A killed pass restarts from the top of the same shortlist.
Why: the constitution requires anything that can run past a minute to checkpoint, and this can — ten pages, each a bought results page, five page reads and a model call. Progress is already durable without a cursor: the results page is written to `serp_snapshots` when it is bought and the model's answer to `request_cache` the moment it arrives, both before anything is done with them. A restarted pass therefore replays each finished page for four indexed reads and pays for none of it again; the test that kills a pass half-way and resumes it measures exactly one model call, five page reads and no results page bought. A separate "this page is done" marker would be a second record of the same fact, and the way the two would disagree is the damaging one: an entry that expired while its marker survived would leave that page silently never compared again, and the scan finding nothing where it was told to look.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.2–14.3.4 (effectively-once, checkpointing), §14.3.6 (billable reads cached, written before processing); invariants 18 and 20.

## 2026-09-04 — R-INTENTGAP-JOB — A pass stopped by the store's daily allowance is not recorded as finished
Decision: the pass writes its completion to the idempotency ledger — the record of paid work already done, which a redelivered job reads instead of re-running — only when it actually reached the end of the shortlist. Stopping because the store's daily allowance of analyses ran out mid-walk records nothing.
Why: a completion record is a promise that there is nothing left to do for that store on that date, and after a part-way stop that is untrue. Recording it would mean the pages below the cut-off are never reached, not even once the allowance resets, because every later attempt for the same date would short-circuit on the record. `T6.1` already decided the remainder is left rather than squeezed in; this is what makes "left" mean "still available" rather than "dropped". The cost is that a retry re-walks the pages that did get compared, which is four indexed reads apiece and no money.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 (per-type cap), §14.3.1; invariants 17 and 18.

## 2026-09-04 — R-INTENTGAP-JOB — The pass runs for a store whose subscription has lapsed, exactly as the weekly scan does
Decision: no billing, vacation or deletion check was added. The pass is stopped by the operator kill switches and by the store's own daily allowance for this call type, and by nothing else.
Why: this is detection, not generation, and invariant 16 is explicit that billing state gates generation and publishing only. The weekly signal scan — the sibling job, which also buys results pages — reads the kill switches and no lifecycle gate, so adding one here would make two jobs doing the same kind of work answer to different rules with nothing in the specs asking for the difference. **Flagged rather than hidden:** the practical effect is that a lapsed store still costs money on Sundays. It is the posture the product already has, not one this card introduced, and changing it is a decision about both jobs at once.
Nearest spec: main §4.2, §14.4, §14.5; invariant 16.

## 2026-09-04 — R-INTENTGAP-JOB — FLAGGED: nothing schedules the pass and nothing registers it, and both are deliberate
Decision: **none taken.** `registerIntentGapTasks` (`packages/jobs/src/optimize/intent-gap-tasks.ts`) is built, typed, exported and tested, and no code path calls it. `intent_gap_scan_weekly` is not in `packages/jobs/src/runtime/crontab.ts`. Until both land, this pass never runs in production.
Why: two separate holds, both instructed. The schedule is switched off by the founder's decision (open question 4) until the last handlerless crontab entries land, and this card was told in as many words not to be the thing that quietly turns paid work on. The composition root, `apps/web/instrumentation-node.ts`, is reserved for the integrator by the build plan, and `T6.2`'s `registerOptimizeTasks` is already waiting there for the same reason — this is the second entry of the same shape, not a new kind of gap.
What the wiring needs, precisely. In `startServerRuntime()`, alongside the other `register*Tasks` calls: `registerIntentGapTasks({ getDb: db, getPool: dbPool, seo: seoProvider(), pageFetcher: new GuardedPageFetcher(), llm: <the process's Anthropic client>, prompt: loadPrompt('intent-gap', 1) })`. The LLM client is the one thing that is not already built in that file — the generation lane memoises its own inside `apps/web/app/api/articles/_lib/config.ts` and does not export it, so either that factory grows an export or the composition root builds the process's one client itself and hands it to both. And in `crontab.ts`, one entry: `{ task: 'intent_gap_scan_weekly', schedule: '0 * * * *', why: … }` — hourly for the local-Sunday reason above, and only when the founder switches the schedule back on. A crontab entry naming a task nobody registered is refused at worker start, so the two have to land together or the registration first.
Nearest spec: build plan §3 (lane ownership), §6 `R-INTENTGAP-JOB`; DECISIONS 2026-09-04 T6.2 (nothing calls the OPTIMIZE task registration).
## 2026-09-04 — T5.1 — The day an article belongs to is read at the publish moment, not at the writing moment
Decision: the daily generation cycle now derives "which day's topic is this" from the store's local clock at the moment the article is due out — `now` plus the writing lead — rather than from the clock when the writing starts. `publishDayFor(now, timezone, leadHours)` in `packages/core/src/generation/cycle.ts` is that derivation, and both the hourly sweep that queues the job and the run itself use it.
Why: authorised by the founder ahead of this card, from a MEDIUM audit finding. Writing starts a fixed six hours before each store's publish hour and wraps backwards over midnight, so a store publishing at 02:00 starts at 20:00 the previous evening. Reading the date at the writing moment took Tuesday evening's date and published the result on Wednesday, leaving the calendar and the shop permanently one day apart — every planned day delivered late, for ever, on every store whose publish hour is earlier than its lead. The article belongs to the day it appears. Real hours are added and the sum then formatted in the store's zone, rather than a date string being shifted, so a daylight-saving change does not move the answer.
Consequence, stated: for the default 09:00 store nothing changes at all (03:00 + 6h is the same date). It changes behaviour only for a publish hour earlier than the lead — 00:00 to 05:00 — which is precisely the case that was broken.
Nearest spec: main §9.1 (one pass per account per day, anchored to the persona timezone), §9.4 (the publish hour); CLAUDE.md invariant 14.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — T5.1 — One article is handed over per publish hour, oldest first
Decision: the delivery run publishes at most one article per pass, choosing the oldest of those cleared. A merchant with three approved drafts gets one a day for three days.
Why: main §9.4 gives the reason for a fixed hour as "predictable for the merchant, and naturally spaced at ≤1/day so publishing never looks like an algorithmic burst". Handing over everything that happens to be ready would produce exactly that burst the first morning after a merchant worked through a review backlog. Oldest first so nothing waits for ever behind newer work. Nothing in the specs states this explicitly, which is why it is journalled: it is a reading of the pacing sentence rather than a rule quoted from one.
Consequence: a store that accumulates finished drafts drains them at one a day. The count is never shown to the merchant as a backlog figure — invariant 23 forbids denominators — so this is invisible except as articles appearing on consecutive mornings.
Nearest spec: main §9.4; CLAUDE.md invariants 14, 23.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — T5.1 — "The live store" means our synced catalogue rows, read at build time — not a Shopify API call
Decision: the founder's rule that every product reference is resolved against the live store at the moment the bundle is built is implemented as a read of `products` and `store_pages` — our own mirror of the shop, kept current by the daily sync and the product webhooks — at the moment the merchant presses download. No Shopify request is made during a download.
Why: three reasons, and the card's own done-when settles it. The done-when is "a bundle built **after a fixture price change** carries the new price", which is a change to our rows. A download is a request path, and a vendor call inside one makes the download as slow and as failable as Shopify is that minute. And an export-mode account has read scopes only and may have been disconnected entirely, in which case a live call has nothing to ask — while the mirror still holds what the store last said. The distinction that matters is preserved either way: the values come from the store's *current* rows and never from what was written down when the article was drafted.
Consequence, stated plainly: a price changed in Shopify is reflected in a download once the webhook or the nightly sweep has landed it — minutes, usually, and up to a day if a webhook is lost. That is a real window and it is the same window every other part of the product already reads through.
Nearest spec: main §9.4, §9.5; `docs/content-pointers.md` §9; DECISIONS 2026-09-01 (founder, the price-as-reference rule).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — T5.1 — A download is refused outright when a referenced product has gone
Decision: `buildExportBundle` throws rather than rendering a gap, and the route answers 409 with a sentence naming what happened. The same is true of a marker in the body that no reference row explains.
Why: the founder's rule for auto-publish (card `T5.2`) is that a missing product fails the publish and raises a repair rather than publishing a hole. A download is the export-mode equivalent of a publish — it is the moment the article leaves us — so it fails the same way. The alternative, rendering the sentence without the product, hands the merchant prose with a hole in it that they would paste into their own blog without noticing.
Not done here: raising a repair or an action card. That is `T5.3`, which owns the repair queue and the "export accounts get action cards" rule; there is no repairs table in the schema yet.
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (drift and repair), §9.5; `docs/content-pointers.md` §9.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.1 — PARKED, needs a schema wave: the export bundle can carry no images, because nothing stores an image address
Decision: the bundle's image block is built, filtered and tested, and in the running product it is **always empty**. `buildBundleForArticle` passes an empty list with a comment saying why.
Why: `products` has no column for a Shopify image URL and there is no separate table for one. The catalogue sync reads `images` from the Admin API and uses them only inside the content checksum, then drops them (`toProductRow`, `packages/core/src/catalog/products.ts`). `T4.3` recorded the same gap for the evidence pack and the draft. So there is nowhere to read an image address back from, and a feature card may not add a migration.
What it costs: an exported article carries no images, and main §9.2's "product images from the catalog only" is unmet on the export path. The rule the card asks for — only `cdn.shopify.com` addresses, never bytes — is implemented and tested against planted input (`shopifyCdnImagesOnly`), so the day a column exists this needs one line changed.
What it needs: one column or side table holding a product's image addresses and alt text, filled by Lane B's catalogue sync. A schema-wave card.
Nearest spec: main §9.2 (images), tech §2.1 ("the export bundle contains URLs, not image bytes"); DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T4.3.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.1 — A price is printed with its currency only when the store's rows carry one, and today they never do
Decision: `formatPrice` prints `49.99 USD` when a currency is known and a bare `49.99` when it is not, and never guesses one from the merchant's country.
Why: a shop in Germany selling in dollars is ordinary, so a currency inferred from the persona country would be wrong on exactly the stores that care. The `priceRange` type already has an optional `currency` field — and Lane B's `toProductRow` never fills it, so in the running product every export price is a bare number.
What it costs: an exported article's prices read as unlabelled figures. Fixing it is one line in the catalogue mapper (`priceRange: { min, max, currency }` from the shop's own currency) and needs no migration, because `price_range` is `jsonb` — but that file is Lane B's. Flagged rather than done.
Nearest spec: main §9.2; `docs/content-pointers.md` §9.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.1 — The download endpoint is not in the frozen route table, and returns the three files in one response
Decision: `GET /api/articles/{articleId}/export` exists as a route in `apps/web` and has **no entry** in `packages/core/src/api/routes.ts`. It returns `{files: [{filename, mimeType, content}]}` — all three at once.
Why: two separate things. (a) The frozen contract (`T0.7`) names `/api/articles/{articleId}` and `/published-url` but no download route at all, while ui §6.2 requires download buttons for Markdown, HTML and metadata and Lane F's articles screen already calls an `onDownload(article)` callback expecting exactly this list. The route table is in `packages/core/src/api`, which is not this lane's, so the entry could not be added here. (b) All three in one response because they are rendered from a single reading of the store: three requests could straddle a price change and hand the merchant a Markdown file and an HTML file that disagree.
What it needs: an entry in `ROUTES` plus a response schema, and `pnpm contracts:check --write`. Integrator or whoever owns the contract. Until then the OpenAPI document does not describe a route the product serves.
Nearest spec: ui §6.2; tech §3; build plan §3 (lane ownership).

Decision: `packages/jobs/src/runtime/crontab.ts` gains one entry (`publish_delivery_sweep`, hourly) and `apps/web/instrumentation-node.ts` gains one registration block.
Why: a scheduled job with no crontab entry never runs, and a task with no registration is a name the crontab check refuses at worker start. Neither file belongs to a lane in build plan §3; three previous cards from three different lanes have each appended to both, and the additions are one block each at a stable point. The integrator should expect a touch here on merge. The alternative — folding delivery into the existing generation sweep to avoid the edit — would have made one task name mean two unrelated things and made the crontab's own explanation untrue.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.1 — An auto-publish store's finished article waits rather than being exported instead
Decision: the delivery run stops with `auto_publish` when the account's delivery mode is `auto`, and hands over nothing.
Why: auto-publish is a write to the merchant's own shop under a second, explicit consent, through the two-phase intent protocol — card `T5.2`. Exporting instead would deliver in a mode the merchant did not choose, and marking the article `published` with no address would make it look posted when nothing was. Waiting is visible and correct; `T5.2` picks it up from the same `draft` state.
Consequence until `T5.2` lands: an account that switches to auto-publish stops receiving articles entirely. Nothing today can switch it — the settings route that would is not built — so no live store can reach this state.
Nearest spec: main §9.5 (delivery modes), §14.3.7; CLAUDE.md invariant 21.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.1 — The publish hour is configurable in the database and by nothing else
Decision: this card reads `account_settings.publish_hour` and `.timezone` and honours whatever is there. It adds no way for a merchant to change them.
Why: the card's scope says "09:00 persona-timezone publish hour (configurable, IANA)". The columns exist with the right defaults, the frozen contract already carries `PATCH /api/settings` with `publishHour` and `timezone` in its schema, and the Settings screen's strings are already written — but the route itself is unbuilt and `apps/web/app/api/settings` is not this lane's directory. So the hour is configurable in the sense that everything downstream of the setting honours it; a merchant cannot yet set it.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.1 — FLAGGED: the download button points at a route nobody has built, and not at the one this card built
Decision: **none taken.** Stated so it is not discovered later as a bug.
Why: `apps/web/app/(app)/content/articles/ArticlesClient.tsx` (Lane F) implements the download by fetching `GET /api/articles/{id}` — the read-only detail route — and assembling the three files in the browser from its response, with `articleFiles()` in `packages/ui`. That detail route **does not exist**: there is no `route.ts` for it anywhere in `apps/web`, so the fetch fails, the callback returns an empty list, and pressing a download button today saves nothing. The confirm-address button next to it calls `POST /api/articles/{id}/published-url`, which this card built, and works.
Why the browser cannot keep doing it that way: the founder's rule is that every product reference is resolved against the live store at the moment the bundle is built. A browser cannot resolve one — it has no access to the catalogue — so the values would have to be resolved by whatever serves `/api/articles/{id}`, whose response schema is frozen and carries a single rendered `html` string plus a metadata object with no product-reference block. Building the bundle where the store's rows are is what `GET /api/articles/{id}/export` does.
What it needs: the articles screen's `download` callback re-pointed at `/api/articles/{id}/export`, which returns `{files: [{filename, mimeType, content}]}` — exactly the shape `onDownload` already expects, so it is a one-line change in another lane's file. Either that, or the detail route is built and made to resolve references itself, which duplicates the resolver.
Consequence until then: the export bundle is reachable and correct at its own address and **no screen calls it**.
Nearest spec: ui §6.2; main §9.4, §9.5; build plan §3 (lane ownership).

## 2026-09-04 — R-INTENTGAP-SCAN — The scan recomputes the key from the same three things, and reads the paying side's own numbers to do it
Decision: the weekly scan finds a stored page comparison by rebuilding the address it was filed under — the page's change fingerprint (`store_pages.checksum`), the identity of the search whose results page was read, and the moment that results page was read — and by importing the paying side's own key function (`intentGapCacheKey`, `packages/jobs/src/optimize/analyse.ts`) rather than re-spelling the format. All three parts are ordinary rows this scan can read for free, so no new storage of any kind was added. Two smaller consequences of the same rule: the scan reads the intent-gap numbers from the rules **defaults** (search depth, the position band, the per-pass ceiling) rather than from the store's locale layer, because that is what the paying pass reads; and the evidence on a detected gap is dated from when the comparison was made (the cache row's own `created_at`), not from when the scan read it back.
Why: the founder's split only works if the free half can find what the paid half left, and the constitution forbids this lane a migration. Recomputing works because every part of the address is already durable and already account-readable. Re-spelling the key format in a second file would be the way the two halves silently drift apart — a changed prefix or separator on one side and the signal simply stops appearing, with nothing failing. The same argument covers the numbers: locale layers today override only one gate threshold, so the two sides agree either way, but a future locale override of `serp_top_n` would change the search depth on one side only and the scan would look under a key nothing was ever written under. **The one duplication that remains, stated plainly:** the four-line helper that stands in for the ranking pages when replaying a cached answer (their addresses are known, their contents were never fetched) exists in both files, because the paying side keeps it private and that file belongs to another lane. If the grounding rule ever changes, both copies have to change.
Nearest spec: main §10.3 step 2, §7.3 (the Intent Gap row), §14.3.6; DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T6.1 (the cache-key entry), 2026-09-03 FOUNDER (the comparison runs as its own job).

## 2026-09-04 — R-INTENTGAP-SCAN — A page nobody compared keeps its opportunity, because "we did not look" is not "the gap closed"
Decision: the scan's expiry sweep — which closes an open opportunity whose evidence this pass no longer finds — skips an intent-gap row for any page this pass read no stored comparison for. Only a page whose comparison was actually read, and which turned out to have no gap left, loses its opportunity.
Why: every other signal in this scan is measured from scratch on every run, so "not detected this week" genuinely means the evidence stopped holding. This one is not: a comparison can be absent because the paying pass was stopped by the store's daily allowance, because the merchant edited the page, or because the results page was bought again — none of which says anything about whether the page still answers less than the pages above it. Expiring on absence would also make the opportunity flicker: the dedupe index covers open rows only, so an expired row plus a re-detection next week is a *new* row and a second "we found something" event for the same page. The card's own wording for the no-comparison case is "skipped", and a skipped page cannot also be a page whose evidence was re-checked. **This is the one place this card reads past the literal instruction** ("add it to `GSC_SIGNAL_TYPES`", the list that drives both the Limited Intelligence branch and the expiry sweep); reversing it is deleting one line in `runSignalScanLocked`.
Nearest spec: main §7.9 (lifecycle, expiry, dedupe), §10.3; invariant 10 (open rows dedupe on `(account_id, signal_type, entity_ref)`; expiry never deletes).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — R-INTENTGAP-SCAN — FLAGGED: the signal is evaluated only for stores with Search Console, but the rules file says it needs no Search Console
Decision: as the card instructs, the intent-gap read runs inside the same branch as the four Search Console detectors, so a store in Limited Intelligence mode — no Search Console connection — evaluates none of it. `signals.config.yaml` was **not** changed: `existing_page_intent_gap` still carries `needs_gsc: false`.
Why: the branch is right in practice. A page reaches the shortlist by sitting between positions 4 and 20 for one of the store's query clusters, which is Google's own record of what it showed; and the pass that buys the comparisons only sweeps stores that have Search Console connected, so a Limited Intelligence store has nothing stored to read anyway. But it leaves two statements about the same signal disagreeing, and both are visible: `needs_gsc` is what the Opportunities screen's *Limited Intelligence* badge reads to name which signals are unavailable, so today the badge does not name this one while the scan does not evaluate it. Main §7.11's own list of what stops without Search Console also omits Intent Gap, while main §7.3 gives it a second qualifying route — being the store's existing target for a keyword competitors rank for — which needs no Search Console at all and which neither half implements today (the paying pass passes no competitor-gap targets). **Not resolved here** because flipping `needs_gsc` changes `rules_version` for every lane and changes what a merchant is told, and building the second route is new work the card does not ask for. Integrator/founder call: either flip the flag so the badge tells the truth, or wire the competitor-gap route on both sides so the flag stays true.
Nearest spec: main §7.3 (the Intent Gap row's two qualifying routes), §7.11 (Limited Intelligence), Appendix A (the badge copy); `packages/core/src/signals/limited-intelligence.ts`; invariant 9.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.2 — The marker we write onto a merchant's shop names the article, not the revision
Decision: every post Sortiva makes carries `sortiva-<article id>` in a Shopify metafield **and** as a tag, and that string never changes when the article is later revised. Our own claim rows (`publish_intents.article_external_id`) do carry a revision suffix — `sortiva-<id>#r2` — and that suffix never leaves the database.
Why: the marker exists so that a worker which died mid-publish can ask the shop "is my post already there?" instead of guessing. That question is about the *article*, so a marker that changed per revision would make the shop unsearchable for exactly the thing we needed to find. The revision still needs a name of its own, because the table's unique index is what stops two workers publishing the same thing and a confirmed first claim would otherwise block every later repair — so the revision lives in the claim's name, where only we read it. The tag duplicates the metafield because a tag travels inside the ordinary article-list response, and finding the same value in a metafield would cost one extra request per article on a blog that may hold hundreds; it is also the fallback for a store where the metafield write is refused.
Consequence: the tag is visible to the merchant in Shopify's admin, on every article we post.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.7 steps 2 and 5; CLAUDE.md invariant 19.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.2 — How many times recovery has been tried is counted from the claim's age, not from a column
Decision: `recoveryDecision` in `packages/core/src/publish/intent.ts` decides "wait / adopt / re-send / give up" from two facts only — how old the unconfirmed claim is, and whether the shop already carries our marker. Giving up happens at 25 minutes.
Why: main §14.3.7 says the sweep runs every 5 minutes, first looks at a claim 10 minutes old, and abandons after 3 failed recoveries. Under that cadence "three failures" and "twenty-five minutes old" are the same moment, and `publish_intents` has no column for an attempt counter — a feature card may not add a migration. Deriving it also has a property a counter does not: it cannot drift, because there is no write to lose.
What it costs: if the sweep's cadence is ever changed, the number of attempts before giving up changes with it. The three constants are named together in one file so that is visible rather than buried.
What would close it: an `attempts` column on `publish_intents`, in a schema wave.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.7 step 4; main §13 `publish_intents`.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.2 — Auto-publish is refused in the database, not only in the API
Decision: `setDeliveryMode(db, scope, 'auto')` is an `INSERT … SELECT` whose `WHERE` requires the store to have granted `write_content` **and** to have named a target blog. With neither, no row is inserted and no conflict update runs, so the switch does not move. Switching back to export is never guarded.
Why: main §9.5 says auto-publish cannot be enabled without a target blog resolved, and invariant 21 makes read and write two separate consents. A store left in auto-publish with nothing to post to would fail silently every morning at its publish hour with nothing the merchant could do about it, so this is a property worth holding in the data rather than in one code path — the same shape invariant 5 uses for the competitor cap. Turning it *off* is unguarded because withdrawing consent has to work under every condition, including a connection that is already broken.
Nearest spec: main §9.5; CLAUDE.md invariant 21.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.2 — An article is recorded as published only after the shop confirms the post
Decision: the two-phase publish writes the claim row first and leaves the article in `draft`; the article moves to `published` in step 3, once Shopify has answered with an article id. A crash between the post and the record leaves an article that still reads as undelivered, and the five-minute recovery sweep moves it.
Why: main §14.3.7 step 1 says the claim is inserted "in the same transaction that marks the article publishing" — and there is no `publishing` value in the `article_state` enum, nor may a feature card add one. Of the two available readings, marking it `published` before the post would tell the merchant something is on their site during exactly the window when a crash may have left it nowhere. The claim row is itself the durable "this publication is in flight" marker that a second worker collides with, so nothing is lost by leaving the article alone until there is something true to record.
Consequence: for up to fifteen minutes after a crash (ten minutes' grace plus one sweep) a published article shows in the app as still waiting. It is never the other way round.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.7; main §13 `articles`, `publish_intents`; CLAUDE.md invariant 19.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.2 — A republication is re-sent rather than looked up, and a first publication is looked up rather than re-sent
Decision: the recovery sweep asks the shop "is my post already there?" only for a **first** publication. For a revision it simply re-issues the update, because replacing an article's contents with the same contents produces the same article. Neither path can create: an update carries the remote id or does not happen.
Why: main §14.3.7 step 4 describes the marker lookup for the create case, and step 5 says updates use "the same protocol" conditional on the stored remote id. Taken literally, a marker lookup on an update answers a question that does not decide anything — the article is found either way, because it was found when it was first published — so the decision has to come from the stored id, which is what step 5 actually says. Re-issuing is safe in a way re-creating never is.
Consequence: a revision that fails repeatedly is retried by each sweep until it ages out at 25 minutes, where a first publication is retried at most three times. Both end in the dead-letter queue.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.7 steps 4 and 5; CLAUDE.md invariant 19.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — T5.2 — A publish blocked by a missing product raises the bell, because the repair queue does not exist yet
Decision: when an article names a product the store no longer has, the publish fails, nothing is claimed, nothing is posted, and a `repair_needed` notification is emitted, deduplicated on the article and the reason.
Why: `docs/content-pointers.md` §9 and the card both say the publish fails and "a repair is raised". There is no repairs table anywhere in the schema — the word appears only as `accounts.auto_repair` and two enum values — and it arrives with `T5.3`, which owns the repair queue and the export-account action card. The notification type, its copy and its email template all exist and are tested. So the merchant is told; what to *do* about it is `T5.3`'s. Doing nothing at all would leave an article that silently never appears, which is the worse of the two.
What it needs: `T5.3` to route this into the repair queue rather than only the bell.
Nearest spec: `docs/content-pointers.md` §9; main §14.1; tech §1 (notifications are append-only, unique on `(account_id, type, dedupe_key)`).

## 2026-09-04 — T5.2 — The write grant and the blog picker are served at `/api/publish/*`, not at the addresses the frozen contract names for them
Decision: the four routes that turn auto-publish on live under `apps/web/app/api/publish/` — this lane's own directory — as `POST /api/publish/grant/start`, `GET /api/publish/grant/callback`, `GET /api/publish/blogs`, `POST /api/publish/target` and `POST /api/publish/mode`. None of them appears in `packages/core/src/api/routes.ts`.
Why: the frozen route table (`T0.7`) names `GET /api/settings/blogs`, `POST /api/settings/blog`, and puts the auto-publish switch on `PATCH /api/settings` with the two conflict codes this card enforces (`write_scope_required`, `target_blog_unresolved`). Those live under `apps/web/app/api/settings`, which **does not exist**, is owned by no lane in build plan §3, and is built by no card. Building it would have meant writing the entire settings surface — publish hour, timezone, draft review, vacation mode, e-mail preferences, UI language — which spans four other lanes' semantics and is a card in its own right; a `PATCH /api/settings` that silently ignored six of its ten fields would be worse than none. So the routes were built where this lane owns the ground, at addresses that say what they are.
What it costs, plainly: **Lane F's Settings screen calls the contract's addresses and will get a 404 from all of them.** `phaseAfterDeliveryConflict` in `packages/ui/src/settings/settings.ts` already knows both conflict codes and both are returned unchanged, so the flow works the moment the addresses agree.
What would close it: either the Settings screen's three calls are re-pointed at `/api/publish/*` and the three contract rows are amended, or `/api/settings` is built and its `delivery` field calls `autoPublishReadiness` and `setDeliveryMode` — the same two functions these routes call. Integrator's choice; both are small.
Also unresolved, and unchanged by this card: `settingsSchema` still carries no field naming *which* blog is the current target, so the Settings screen can offer to change it but cannot state it (recorded at 2026-09-03 T9.7). `readPublishTarget` returns it; nothing renders it.
Nearest spec: main §9.5; ui §9.1; tech §3; build plan §3 (lane ownership), §4 (frozen contracts).

## 2026-09-04 — T5.2 — The publishing grant comes back to its own callback, and Lane B's install callback is untouched
Decision: the second Shopify pass starts at `POST /api/publish/grant/start` and returns to `GET /api/publish/grant/callback`, both in this lane. `apps/web/app/api/shopify` — the install start, the install callback, and the `assertReadOnlyGrant` that throws a write grant away — is not edited at all. The state value carried through the consent screen is signed by `signPublishGrantState` in `packages/core/src/publish/grant.ts`, which is this card's own and independent of Lane B's `_lib/state.ts`.
Why: three reasons, in order of weight. The two flows end in different places — the install finishes on the dashboard with onboarding resuming behind it, this one finishes in Settings with a blog still to choose — so one shared callback would have to branch on purpose anyway. `apps/web/app/api/shopify` is Lane B's directory and a `T2.1` test deliberately holds the install callback's discard-a-write-grant behaviour in place; changing it is a review failure and arguably a security regression. And a separate purpose stamped inside the signed state means an install redirect cannot be replayed at the publishing callback to record a read-only grant as a publishing one.
What this needs from outside the repository: `https://<APP_URL>/api/publish/grant/callback` must be registered as an allowed redirect URI on the Shopify app, and the app's configured scopes must include `write_content`. Both live in the Shopify Partner dashboard. Until they are, the second grant cannot complete against a real store; it is proven here against a fake shop.
Nearest spec: main §9.5; CLAUDE.md invariant 21; build plan §3; DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T9.7 (which flagged this gap and declined to guess at it).

## 2026-09-04 — T5.2 — NOT DONE, and the exact line it needs: the recovery sweep is registered and nothing schedules it
Decision: **none taken, deliberately.** The five-minute sweep that settles a publication a crash left unanswered is built, tested and registered as the task `publish_recovery_sweep` — and `packages/jobs/src/runtime/crontab.ts`, which is integrator-resolved and which this session was told not to edit, has no entry for it. Nothing in production will ever run it.
The line, verbatim, for whoever adds it — one more object in `CRON_ENTRIES`:

    {
      task: 'publish_recovery_sweep',
      schedule: '*/5 * * * *',
      why:
        'Settles publications a crash left unanswered. Between putting an article on a merchant\'s ' +
        'shop and recording that we did there is an instant where the post exists and nothing of ' +
        'ours knows it; a worker that dies there leaves a claim with no answer, and the naive ' +
        'repair — try again — is how a merchant ends up with the same article posted twice. Every ' +
        'five minutes, because the window it closes is minutes wide: the app believes the article ' +
        'never went out for as long as it stays open. A claim younger than ten minutes is left ' +
        'alone in case a worker still holds it, and each pass asks the shop whether our marker is ' +
        'already there before it ever considers sending again.',
    },

No registration is needed in `apps/web/instrumentation-node.ts`: `registerPublishTasks(publishTaskDeps())` is already called there from `T5.1`, and this task registers inside it. A registered task with no crontab entry is not caught by `assertCrontabTasksExist`, which only fails the other way round — so nothing goes red; the sweep simply never runs.
Consequence until the entry lands: a publish interrupted between posting and recording leaves the article showing as unpublished in the app for ever, with the post live on the merchant's shop. The next day's run tries the same article, collides with the claim, and stops — so it never double-posts, but it also never recovers.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.7 step 4; build plan §3 ("Files no lane owns").

## 2026-09-04 — T5.2 — Every publishing behaviour in this card is proven against a fake shop, not a Shopify store
Decision: `FakeShopifyPublishClient` (`packages/providers/src/shopify/publish-double.ts`) stands in for Shopify everywhere this card is tested, including the chaos case. No test in this card has ever talked to a Shopify store.
Why: there is no Shopify development store credential in this environment, and the card's first done-when — "dev-store smoke publishes once" — cannot be run. What the fake buys is something a real store could not: it counts creates, so "exactly one article after a kill" is a measurement rather than an inspection, and it survives repeated worker deaths reproducibly.
What is therefore **not** proven: that Shopify accepts the article payload as written — the metafield shape, `summary_html`, `published: false` for a draft post, the `handle` collision behaviour when a slug is already taken on that blog, and whether the article URL this card builds (`https://<shop>.myshopify.com/blogs/<blogId>/<handle>`) is the address a reader actually gets, since Shopify's own blog URLs use the blog *handle* rather than its id. That last one is the most likely thing to be wrong on first contact with a real store.
What it needs: a development-store credential and one live publish, before this is relied on. Recorded rather than worked around.
Nearest spec: main §9.5, §14.3.9; build plan T5.2 done-when 1.

Decision: `republishArticleToShopify` (`packages/jobs/src/publish/republish.ts`) implements main §14.3.7 step 5 in full — an update per revision, conditional on the stored remote id, that never falls back to create — and no code path in the running product reaches it.

Consequence, stated: an article whose product data drifts after publication is not updated on the merchant's shop today. The recovery sweep reaches this function only for a revision claim, and no revision claim can exist until something opens one.
What closes it: `T5.3`'s repair queue calling it with the next revision number.

## 2026-09-04 — T5.2 — One integrator-resolved file was edited: the lint exemption list
Decision: `eslint.config.mjs` gains one entry, `'apps/web/app/api/publish/_lib/config.ts'`, appended to the existing composition-root list that exempts a file from `sortiva/no-raw-db-access`.
Why: that rule stops any code outside `packages/db` importing the raw database handle, so no query can reach a table without naming whose account it belongs to. A composition root does not query — it hands `db()` to something that later scopes it — and the rule cannot tell the two apart. Six previous cards from four lanes have appended to this exact array for the identical reason, each with its own note. Without it `pnpm lint` is red, which is a gate this card has to pass.
The file is integrator-resolved rather than union-merged, so a merge touches it. Flagged here and in the session report so that is expected rather than discovered.
Nearest spec: build plan §3 ("Ordered code … stays integrator-resolved. If a lane needs to change one, say so in the session report").
