## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The recovery sweep is renamed, and the recurring job schedule switches on
Decision: the handler the `T5.2` card registered as `publish_recovery_sweep` is renamed to `publish_intent_recovery_sweep`, the name the schedule has used since M0. Carded as `R-SCHEDULE`, Lane D. This reverses the "wait" of 2026-09-03 by satisfying its own condition rather than by relaxing anything: the worker enables recurring jobs only when every scheduled entry has code registered under exactly that name, three of the four then-missing handlers have since landed, and this rename supplies the fourth. Nothing else changes — the all-handlers-present rule is untouched.
Why: the integrator re-derived the mismatch independently before putting it to the founder, resolving every registered task-name constant in the tree and matching it against all seventeen scheduled entries by hand; there is exactly one mismatch and this is it. The audit's "only one" claim is correct, including its own correction of a false second candidate (`subscription_reconciliation_nightly`, registered from outside the jobs package). The handler moves rather than the schedule because `crontab.ts` is integrator-resolved and its name is the older, published one. What the founder was told this buys, and costs: every one of the seventeen recurring jobs becomes live wherever the app starts — the daily article generation cycle, the weekly signal scan, monthly replenishment, the nightly sweep that actually erases deleted accounts and prunes tables, the automatic spend brakes, and all scheduled mail — several of which have never run outside tests. Mitigating: the product has never been deployed (`railway.toml`'s start command still cannot find the build), so "on" today means on in a local process with a real database, not on a live server.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 (kill switches and auto-trips), §14.3.7 (two-phase external writes); `docs/overnight-state.md` "Audit findings, unactioned", the `T5.2` CRITICAL.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — A scheduled name with no handler must fail rather than silently disable the schedule
Decision: authorised as work, carded to `T10.2`, and **not built now**. Today a single name mismatch turns off all seventeen recurring jobs and reports it as one log line among many; it should stop the worker loudly instead. No file changes from this entry.
Why: this is the fourth instance in two days of the same class — the mechanism that tells you whether something is finished is itself the broken thing, and it fails reassuringly. The others: `contracts:check` never compares the contract to the routes on disk (ten endpoints diverged, it passed every time); the stub report only covers class-shaped stand-ins; the chaos suite discards `result.kills`, so a scenario whose kill never fires is indistinguishable from one that passes. `T10.2`'s done-when is already "zero invariants without teeth", so it has a home and needs no new card.
Nearest spec: build plan §6 (`T10.2`); `docs/overnight-state.md` "The pattern that keeps recurring: reporters fail towards 'fine'".

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — Wiring the change-stream reader is authorised, and turned out to be more than the one line it was recorded as
Decision: the founder authorised wiring the reader that turns "the merchant changed something in their store" into "re-read exactly that page" — the input `T5.3` is built on. **Not yet built**, because verifying it before building showed the recorded description was wrong in a way that changes the work, and the remaining part is a founder call rather than a coding one.
Why: this file and the state file both described the reader as unwired and one line from working — register `registerCatalogEventTasks` in the composition root. That registration is genuinely missing and is one line. But `enqueueCatalogEventDrain`, the call that starts a pass, is made from exactly one place: the drain re-queueing *itself* when a pass found something. Nothing ever starts the first pass, so registering the handler alone would leave the stream still never read, and the wiring would look done. What triggers the first pass is undecided and is put to the founder separately. **Two producers are real and registered** — the Shopify webhook handler and the nightly catalogue sweep both call `recordCatalogChanges` — which also means the comments in `packages/jobs/src/inventory/drain.ts` claiming the producing half "does not exist yet" are stale and will mislead the next reader; not fixed here, since that is another lane's file and not this decision's business.
Nearest spec: main §14.1 (drift and repair), §7.5 (event-driven cadence); build plan §6 (`T5.3`).

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — A merchant's edit is read when Shopify announces it, and it triggers a full scan
Decision: two answers, completing the authorisation above. **(1) The trigger is the webhook.** When Shopify tells us a merchant changed something, the handler records it as it does today and then queues a pass over the change stream, so the changed pages are re-read within minutes instead of at the next nightly walk. The nightly catalogue sweep is left as the backstop it already is and does not also queue a pass. **(2) The pass triggers a full signal scan**, the same paid market analysis the weekly scan runs — so a merchant who fixes a missing page title or fills out a bare product family sees the product notice within minutes rather than by Monday. Carded as `R-STREAM-WIRE`.
Why: the nightly-sweep option was rejected as making the reader barely better than the nightly walk it exists to improve on; a schedule of its own was rejected as waking for every account whether or not anything changed, and as a new recurring job on a schedule switched on the same morning. On the paid half, the founder was told the cost plainly: a store that edits its catalogue often buys more search-results data. Two things bound it — repeat passes at the same point in the stream share an idempotency key and are de-duplicated, and the automatic spend brakes went live with the schedule this morning — with the caveat, stated before the choice, that the brake trips for the whole store rather than for this path alone.
Nearest spec: main §7.5 (event-driven cadence), §14.1 (drift), §14.5 (spend caps).
## 2026-09-04 — R-SCHEDULE — The recovery sweep's constant is renamed with its value, and its log line is left alone
Decision: two choices the card left open. (1) The exported constant that names the five-minute recovery sweep was renamed `PUBLISH_RECOVERY_SWEEP_TASK` → `PUBLISH_INTENT_RECOVERY_SWEEP_TASK` alongside the string it holds, rather than keeping the old identifier over the new value. (2) The log line the sweep writes when it finishes, `publish_recovery_sweep_complete` (`packages/jobs/src/publish/recovery.ts`), was **not** renamed and keeps its wording.
Why: on (1) — the bug this card fixes is a name that did not match, and leaving a constant called `..._RECOVERY_SWEEP_TASK` holding `publish_intent_recovery_sweep` would leave a second, smaller mismatch in the same file, inviting the next reader to "correct" the string back. The symbol is exported from `@sortiva/jobs` but has exactly three uses in the whole tree, all in the publishing lane's own two files, so the rename costs nothing outside it. On (2) — that string is a label in a log record, not a task name; nothing dispatches on it, no test or dashboard config mentions it, and the tree already treats the two as separate vocabularies (the weekly scan's task is `signal_scan_weekly` while its finishing line is `weekly_scan_sweep_complete`). Renaming it would break any saved log search for no gain.
Nearest spec: tech §2 (the schedule and its handlers); main §14.3.7 (what the sweep does).

Decision: the entry `2026-09-04 — T5.2 — NOT DONE, and the exact line it needs` is **wrong in both halves and must not be acted on.** It says the schedule has no entry for the five-minute sweep that settles a publication a crash left unanswered, and supplies a verbatim block to paste into `CRON_ENTRIES`. The entry has existed since M0 under the name `publish_intent_recovery_sweep`; it was the *handler* that was misnamed, and `R-SCHEDULE` has now renamed it. **Pasting the suggested block would create a second five-minute schedule for the same sweep, under a name with no handler — which would disable all seventeen recurring jobs again**, the exact failure this morning's work removed. The journal is append-only, so the original entry stays where it is; this supersedes it.

## 2026-09-04 — R-STREAM-WIRE — A burst of edits collapses in the queue, not in the handler
Decision: the webhook handler asks for a pass over the change record every time it records a change, and does nothing to combine those asks. Nothing counts, debounces, or checks whether a pass is already waiting.
Why: the ask (`enqueueCatalogEventDrain`, `packages/jobs/src/inventory/queue.ts`) already carries a job key naming the account and nothing else, and the queue treats a second ask under an existing key as a replacement rather than an addition. A merchant re-tagging forty products therefore leaves one waiting pass, not forty. Adding a second guard in the handler would be a second answer to a question already answered, and the two would disagree the first time either changed.
Proved rather than asserted: `apps/web/app/api/webhooks/shopify/[topic]/_lib/stream-wire.test.ts` delivers five edits through the receiver, checks all five were recorded as separate changes, and asserts exactly one pass is waiting with that account's key. Confirmed non-vacuous by making the key unique per ask and watching the test go red with five.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.1 (a worker is effectively-once), §14.1.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — R-STREAM-WIRE — The ask names no place to resume from, and can cost a waiting pass its place
Decision: the ask carries only the account. It never carries a cursor — the marker saying how far through the change record the last pass got.
Why: the webhook handler has no way to know one. The marker lives in the waiting pass's own payload, which is where a previous card deliberately put it to avoid a schema change.
Consequence, stated: when a pass is already waiting *and* it carried a marker (which happens only in the seconds between a pass finding changes and re-running itself), the new ask replaces it and the marker is lost. That pass then re-reads the store's changes from the beginning and re-requests a read of every page ever changed. Wasteful, never wrong — a re-read writes the same rows — and self-correcting on the next pass. The window is small because a pass that asks for itself again runs immediately.
What would close it: asking under a job-key mode that leaves an already-waiting job alone instead of replacing it (`unsafe_dedupe`), which is correct here because the waiting pass will read the new change anyway. That is a change to `packages/jobs/src/inventory/queue.ts`, which is Lane C's and outside this card's authorised edit. Flagged in the session report instead of taken.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.1; DECISIONS 2026-09-02 T3.2 (the marker travels in the payload).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — R-STREAM-WIRE — A delivery that changed nothing asks for nothing
Decision: a `products/update` whose words, prices and stock all match what we already hold records no change and queues no pass. A duplicate or reordered delivery is likewise silent, because the handler stops before recording. Every delivery that *does* record a change queues a pass, even if that exact change was already in the record.
Why: the first half is the point — Shopify sends `products/update` for edits to fields we do not track at all, and a pass per touch would be a paid market analysis per touch. The second half is deliberate slack in the safe direction: refusing to ask when the change record already held the row would leave a change the nightly sweep found (the sweep is deliberately not a trigger) waiting for a trigger that never comes. The cost of asking anyway is one job that collapses into the one already waiting.
Nearest spec: main §14.1, §14.3.1.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The OPTIMIZE stuck states are fixed
Decision: "well fix it" and "come up with a fix, let me know what you did." Two findings, one card: pressing the improve-this-page button marks the opportunity busy before queueing, so with nothing registered to pick the work up two presses consume a store's daily allowance for ever and the state guard refuses to retry them; and separately there is no way out of the busy state when the work is refused or crashes — the job returns the row on exactly two paths and no sweeper, timeout or reaper exists anywhere. ## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The spend brake stops switching OPTIMIZE off for ordinary use
Decision: "fix it." The nightly sweep counts model calls rather than generations, trips at exactly the cap rather than above it, and the flag is sticky until an operator clears it — so a merchant who uses both of their two daily recommendations, or one that needed its single automatic retry, has the feature switched off permanently. The same defect applies to the intent-gap call type. Carded as `R-SPEND`, Lane G (its file).
Why: worse than an accounting bug, because of what the merchant is then shown — the outage copy, "we paused this action rather than continue with lower-quality or stale data", which tells them we protected their quality when they in fact hit our arithmetic. The operator-facing incident text also states a count that is wrong.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 (spend caps), §14.4 and Appendix A (the outage sentence); invariant 22.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The publishing path's four defects are fixed together
Decision: "fix it" on three separate findings, plus "no sortiva tag anywhere that's publicly visible." All four live in the same publishing code and are carded together as `R-PUBLISH`, Lane D, because separate cards would collide in one file. (a) The did-my-post-land check reads one page of up to 250 recent articles with no paging, so an established blog can answer "no" when the answer is yes and get a second copy posted. (b) A transient Shopify failure at the moment of posting strands that article for ever and a rejected token is never reported to the merchant, though the machinery to tell them exists and drives the reconnect banner. (c) The address recorded for a published article uses the blog's numeric id where Shopify uses its name, so Search Console attribution can never match and a working article appears to earn nothing for ever; the correct value is already stored and unused. (d) **The visible tag goes.**
Why on (d) specifically: every published article currently carries a `sortiva-<id>` tag in the merchant's Shopify admin, which can surface in storefront tag lists their own shoppers see. The tag is a *duplicate* marker — the real one is a metafield, invisible to the merchant and Shopify's own place for app-owned data. The tag was written every time because the recovery sweep reads it out of a plain list response, and a metafield-only marker costs one extra request per article to look for. **That cost is now the price of the founder's answer, and it lands in the same card as (a), which changes that same lookup anyway.**
Nearest spec: main §9.5, §14.3.7, §12.2 (attribution); invariant 19, 22.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — A signal with no search term stops buying search results for a web address
Decision: "detail what the fix would be and do it." Three of the four signals that produce an improve-this-page suggestion record a search term; the missing-or-weak-metadata signal records none, and the pipeline falls back to the page's own web address as the search. It then pays the search vendor for results for `https://store.example/collections/boots` as though a person had typed it, tells the model "the search: <that address>", and measures keyword stuffing against the words in a URL. Carded as `R-NOQUERY`, Lane E, placed there by the integrator because it spans two lanes.
Why: the likely end state is a failed recommendation after spending both the merchant's daily allowance and our vendor money, and nothing logs it. **The fix detailed for the founder, and what the card specifies:** the pipeline must refuse to run rather than substitute — a signal that carries no search term either resolves one from the store's existing query clusters for that page, or the recommendation is not offered at all and says why. Substituting a URL for a search is the failure; a narrower fallback would repeat it more quietly.
Nearest spec: main §7.3, §10.3, §14.5 (per-type cap); invariant 7.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The quality judge's model cannot be changed by configuration
Decision: "yes no one should touch it." The judge tier stops honouring the `ANTHROPIC_MODEL_*` environment override. Today an operator can point the judge at the cheap model with one variable that ships in `.env.example`, and neither the code nor CI would notice — and the spend would be misreported, since the expensive price list is kept.
Why: the rule is that a smaller model is never substituted for the judge, and the code honours it everywhere except this one configuration route. The override mechanism stays for every other tier, where pinning a model per environment without a code change is legitimate.
Nearest spec: main §8.4, §14.4; invariant 11.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — A quality failure of ours never costs the merchant a day, and we must be able to see when it does
Decision: "our own failure to write good should never count toward the article quota. BUT we must have very solid foundations in place to track if this is happening." Two halves: a draft that fails our own gate does not consume the store's daily allowance, and the rate at which that happens is measured and visible rather than inferred.
Why: the second half is the load-bearing one and the founder said so — a quota that silently absorbs our failures looks identical to a quota the merchant used, and the only way to know which is happening is to have counted. The measurement is not optional dressing on the fix.
Nearest spec: main §4.2 (the cap), §8.4, §9.6; invariant 23 (no denominators shown to the merchant — the measurement is operator-facing).

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The judge's own sentences may reach a merchant, and are always in English
Decision: "yes, but always in English no matter what language store is in." This settles a contradiction between the constitution (every user-facing explanation renders from a template, never from a model) and the spec (the rejection card restates the judge's failing criteria in plain language). The spec wins, with a constraint the spec did not state: the judge's justification is always English, even for a store publishing in another language.
Why: the contradiction was real and needed a ruling either way rather than a quiet resolution. The English constraint answers the specific hazard flagged by the audit — a justification coming back in the store's own language inside an otherwise-English sentence, which is a worse artefact than either language alone.
Nearest spec: main §8.4, §8.6; invariant 8; Appendix A.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — An overridden article gets its own state, and is never reviewed again
Decision: "add a fifth state and something we overridden does not be reviewed again imho." An article a merchant publishes over a quality rejection gets a fifth state meaning "cleared to deliver", rather than returning to `draft` — which today covers three different situations at once — and it does **not** pass through draft review on accounts that have review turned on. This closes the second half of question 13.
Why: `draft` currently means "written, not yet graded", and an overridden article is neither; the only thing separating them is a flag whose meaning is "excluded from learning", not "cleared to publish", so any query asking "which drafts go out today" gets un-graded articles unless it remembers to join the decision table. On the review half: a merchant who has just overruled the quality gate has already made the decision review exists to ask for.
**Blocked on a migration.** A fifth state is a new enum value and all four schema waves are closed, so this needs a mini-wave before it can be built.
Nearest spec: main §8.6, §9.3.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — Sessions become revocable, and signing out revokes
Decision: "yes it should and it should be revoked on sign out." A signed-in session becomes revocable before it expires, and signing out revokes it rather than only clearing the cookie in that browser. This buys "sign out everywhere" and instant lockout when an account is deleted or a credential is compromised.
Why: the founder was told the cost before choosing — a database read on every signed-in request, on a platform chosen for being cheap — and took it. The 24-hour session lifetime should be revisited in the same card: it is short *because* revocation was impossible, so the reason for the number goes with this change.
**Blocked on a migration.** There is no `sessions` table anywhere in the schema and a session must exist somewhere before anything can delete it; all four schema waves are closed, so this needs a mini-wave.
Nearest spec: main §4.1; tech §3.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The empty-state scan line becomes relative
Decision: "yes reword it and it should be relative (next scan in x days)." The canonical sentence telling a merchant with no open opportunities when the next scan runs stops naming Monday and names a relative interval instead. This is quoted copy, so only the founder could change it, and they have.
Why: the screen's header already shows the real next-scan date, taken from the API, because a store whose scan lands on a Tuesday would otherwise be told something untrue every week. So the header could say Tuesday while the line below promised Monday. A relative interval is true whichever day the scan falls on, which removes the contradiction rather than papering over it.
Nearest spec: main Appendix A (canonical strings); ui §5; invariant 24 (the snapshot test that holds these strings moves with the copy).

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — Discarding a draft still does not retire the subject
Decision: "no, a bad draft never means bad subject." No change: throwing away a draft closes the calendar day and leaves the subject available for future planning. Recorded because the behaviour was previously an undictated choice made by a card and journalled rather than asked.
Nearest spec: main §8.7, §9.3.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — Auto-published articles ship without images, for now
Decision: "for now yes." Articles published automatically carry no images, against what main §9.2 asks for. Accepted deliberately rather than by omission, and marked as a known departure to revisit rather than a settled design.
Nearest spec: main §9.2.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The Shopify development store waits until everything else is done
Decision: "this is the last piece and blocks nothing, leave it for [when] the rest of the product is 100% done." No development store is procured yet.
Why, and what it costs, stated so the consequence is not rediscovered: `T10.4` cannot run, one of `T5.2`'s done-whens stays **not met**, and every claim about how the product behaves against Shopify rests on a fake that cannot rate-limit, cannot reject a token, cannot rewrite a colliding address, cannot paginate and cannot refuse a metafield — and the fall-back-to-a-tag design existed for that last case. The `R-PUBLISH` card now removes the tag, so the metafield path becomes the only marker and is still untested against a real store.
Nearest spec: build plan §6 `T10.4`, §10 (kickoff checklist).

## 2026-09-04 — R-OPTIMIZE-STUCK — The daily allowance counts recommendations written, and the cap is enforced where the work is picked up
Decision: `countOptimizeGenerationsSince` (`packages/db/src/repositories/optimize.ts`) no longer adds the opportunities sitting in `executing` to the count. It counts only recommendations written since the start of the day. The thing that count was protecting against — several presses in the same instant all getting past the cap before any has produced anything — moves to the background job, which re-reads the same meter under the account's lock immediately before it spends anything, and refuses with a new outcome, `paused` / `daily_cap_reached`, if the day is already spent.
Why: a store's allowance is two a day. Counting a *marked* page as a spent generation meant anything that marked a page and then never did the work took a day's allowance away permanently — and with the job registered nowhere, that was every press. Two presses cost a store its entire allowance for ever. The count had to stop reading marks; but simply deleting the running count would have left the cap unenforced against a burst of presses, which is a money control, so it is re-established at dequeue instead. The spec's own wording puts spend caps at dequeue, checked from our own counters, which is exactly where it now is.
Consequence worth stating: a press accepted in a burst that the job then refuses for the cap is answered `generating` at the time and quietly hands the page back a moment later, with nothing shown to the merchant. That is only reachable when presses arrive faster than a generation completes; the ordinary serial case is still refused in the request with the cap message. No new merchant-facing copy was invented for it.
Nearest spec: main §10.2 (the daily cap), §14.5 (caps enforced from our counters at dequeue); invariant 17.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — R-OPTIMIZE-STUCK — A second press while a generation is genuinely running is answered "still going", not refused
Decision: `POST /api/recommendations` on an opportunity already in `executing` returns 200 `{ state: 'generating', generated: false }` instead of 409 `opportunity_already_updated`.
Why: this is a change to what a caller sees, so it is flagged rather than buried. The card's done-when is that two presses in a row leave the page retryable rather than permanently refused, and the second press has to answer *something*. The 409 it used to give said "This opportunity was updated by the latest scan" — a sentence describing something that did not happen — and, because nothing ever moved the page back out of `executing`, it was the answer for ever. The state it now returns is the one the drawer's own read (`GET`) already returns for exactly this situation, so the two agree. No consumer is pinned to the old answer: the frozen route table does not declare these four endpoints at all (see `R-CONTRACT`), so no generated fake server and no screen is built against them yet.
Nearest spec: main §10.2; tech §3 (guarded transitions answer 409 — still true of the transition itself, which is untouched); invariant 15.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — R-OPTIMIZE-STUCK — An abandoned page is handed back the next time the merchant looks, and there is no background sweeper
Decision: `releaseAbandonedOptimizeGenerations` hands back every page of the account that has been marked "we are working on this" for longer than `gates.optimize_recommendation.abandoned_after_minutes` (30). It is called at the top of both `/api/recommendations` reads — the button and the drawer — and by nothing else. No scheduled sweep was built.
Why: recovery has to reach the merchant, and both of the places a stuck page hurts them are requests they make: the drawer showing a spinner that never resolves, and the button refusing a page nothing is working on. Healing on those two paths fixes both at the moment they are felt, needs nothing wired, and cannot itself be the thing that silently never runs. A scheduled sweeper would need a crontab entry and a composition-root registration, both integrator-resolved and both currently held, so it would have been code that runs nowhere — the exact shape of the defect this card exists to remove. What it costs, stated plainly: a page nobody ever looks at again stays marked. It costs the store nothing now that the allowance no longer reads marks, and no merchant is shown it, but it is not zero — the row is untidy and an operator counting `executing` rows would over-count. If a background sweep is wanted later it is a small job over the same function.
Nearest spec: main §7.9 (lifecycle), §10.2; invariant 15 (the hand-back is a guarded update and a zero-row result means somebody else already moved it).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — R-OPTIMIZE-STUCK — Releasing the page lives outside the generation, so paths nobody thought of are covered too
Decision: `generateOptimizeRecommendation` is now a wrapper: it runs the generation and, on every outcome except a finished recommendation and on any thrown error, hands the page back with a guarded update. The hand-back that used to sit inside the validation-failure path was removed, so there is one place rather than one per return.
Why: the defect was not that a particular path forgot to release; it was that the release was written per-return, so a path added later — or a throw — was released by nobody. Doing it at the boundary means the next outcome anyone adds is covered without being remembered. The finished path still moves the page on itself, because it moves it on *with the recommendation attached* and that has to happen in the same step.
Nearest spec: main §7.9, §10.1–10.4; invariant 15.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — R-OPTIMIZE-STUCK — Edits outside the lane's own directories, and one graph left alone
Decision: three files outside `packages/core/optimize`, `packages/jobs/optimize` and `apps/web/app/api/recommendations` were changed. `packages/db/src/repositories/optimize.ts` — the OPTIMIZE lane's own repository, in a directory the lane table assigns to nobody — carries the corrected counter and the new hand-back query. `packages/rules` gains one threshold, which moves `rules_version` for every lane. Not touched: `packages/core/src/opportunities/lifecycle.ts` (Lane C's written state graph), which still forbids `executing` → `accepted`; nothing consults it, and `R-GRAPH` is the card that corrects it.
Why: the counter and the query are the fix. This card introduces no transition the OPTIMIZE path did not already perform — `executing` → `accepted` is the same move the success and validation-failure paths have always made — so `R-GRAPH` needs no widening on account of this card.
Nearest spec: build plan §3 (lane ownership, schema ownership); main §7.10 (thresholds live in the config layer).
Class (filled by audit):
## 2026-09-04 — R-PUBLISH — "Did my post land?" is narrowed by time, paged to the end, and confirmed one article at a time
Decision: the question we ask a merchant's shop before ever re-sending an article is now three things instead of one page of guessing. It asks only for posts created since we claimed the publication (a server-side filter Shopify applies, so an established blog of thousands of posts comes back as the handful written in the last few minutes); it follows Shopify's own "next page" pointer to the end of that narrowed list instead of reading the first response and concluding; and because the marker that identifies a post as ours now lives only in a metafield — a hidden field, not in the list response — each candidate costs one further request to check.
Why: reading one page of up to 250 posts and calling that "the blog" is the defect this card exists to fix, and paging alone would not have been affordable: with the visible tag gone, an unnarrowed search would cost one request per article on the whole blog, which on a five-minute sweep would spend a store's entire Shopify request budget. Filtering by creation time is what makes the metafield-only marker cheap, and it degrades safely — a shop that ignored the filter would return everything and the paging would still find the article, only slower.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.7 (two-phase publish, recovery asks the remote before re-executing); invariant 19.

## 2026-09-04 — R-PUBLISH — A search that ran out of pages refuses to answer rather than answering "no"
Decision: the marker search now throws (`MarkerLookupIncomplete`) if it hits its ceiling of pages, and the recovery sweep treats that as "ask again in five minutes" — leaving the claim on the publication open — rather than as an answer.
Why: "not there" is the only answer that authorises posting an article to somebody's blog a second time. An incomplete search that reported "not there" would be the original defect wearing a new shape. The ceiling itself exists so that a filter the shop declines to honour cannot turn one question into an unbounded walk of a merchant's entire blog; in normal operation the search ends on its first page.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.7; invariant 19.

## 2026-09-04 — R-PUBLISH — A five-minute allowance for our clock and Shopify's disagreeing
Decision: the creation-time filter reaches five minutes further back than the moment the claim was opened.
Why: the filter is only safe if it cannot step over the very post it is looking for. Our server's clock and Shopify's are not the same clock, and a post stamped fractionally before our claim would be invisible to an exact filter — and invisible means "not found" means "post it again". Five minutes is far more disagreement than either clock will ever have, and the only cost is that the search also sees whatever the merchant themselves posted in those five minutes, which is almost always nothing. It lives beside the other recovery timings in `packages/core/src/publish`, not in the rules package, which holds the numbers that decide what the product recommends rather than how a retry behaves.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.7.

## 2026-09-04 — R-PUBLISH — A failed post is judged by whether it could have landed, and only then is the claim given back
Decision: when the shop throws while we are posting, the failure is sorted into one of two cases. **Refused** — a rejected token, a rate limit, a request Shopify would not accept — means nothing was written, so the claim on the publication is deleted and the article is simply due again on the next run. **Unknown** — the connection broke, or Shopify answered with a fault of its own — means the post may be sitting on the merchant's blog already, so the claim is kept exactly as it is and the recovery sweep settles it by asking the shop. A new repository function, `releasePublishIntent`, deletes the claim row (rather than marking it), because a row left behind under any state would block every future attempt at the same publication for ever.
Why: before this, any hiccup at the moment of posting left the claim open and threw; every later attempt collided with that claim, reported "already claimed", and stopped — so a momentary Shopify blip stranded that article permanently and silently. Releasing the claim unconditionally would have been the opposite mistake and a far worse one: it is exactly how a merchant gets the same article posted twice. The split is the whole safety property, so it is a named function in `packages/core` with its own tests rather than a condition inside the job.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.7, §14.4 (degrade to a visible pause); invariants 19, 22.

## 2026-09-04 — R-PUBLISH — Publishing raises the reconnect the rest of the product already raises
Decision: when Shopify rejects our token during a publish, a republish or a recovery sweep, the publishing path now calls `recordConnectionLost` — the same function the onboarding path has always called — which stamps the connection as broken, moves the store to the reconnect screen and emits the notification that raises the banner and the 24-hour email. The database-backed adapter it needs is built inside `packages/jobs/src/publish/reconnect.ts` from repositories the publishing jobs already depend on, rather than being injected from the web app's composition root.
Why: the merchant is the only person who can fix a dead token, and silence guarantees it stays broken — the product's rule is to degrade to a *visible* pause. Building the adapter in the jobs package rather than passing a port down means every process that can publish also raises the reconnect, with no wiring line for a future deployment to forget; the alternative was a new optional dependency on the publish jobs, wired in one composition root, which would be silently absent anywhere it was not added. It duplicates about thirty lines that the Shopify lane's own bindings file also has; importing across that lane's directory was the other option and was judged worse.
Nearest spec: main §6.2, §14.4, Appendix A; invariants 21, 22.

## 2026-09-04 — R-PUBLISH — One store's broken connection no longer ends the recovery pass
Decision: the recovery sweep handles each claim inside its own try/catch. A rejected token raises the reconnect and moves on; any other failure is logged and the claim is left pending. The pass continues to every other store.
Why: found while adding the reconnect. The sweep walks claims across every account, and any exception thrown while asking one store's shop propagated out of the loop and ended the pass — so one merchant with a dead token stopped interrupted publications from being settled for everybody else, indefinitely. Adjacent to the card's own fix and in the same code path, so mended here rather than left as a second defect in a path this card is meant to make safe.
Nearest spec: main §14.3.7.

## 2026-09-04 — R-PUBLISH — Removing the tag also stops a repair overwriting the merchant's own tags
Decision: the article we send Shopify has no `tags` key at all, rather than an empty one.
Why: this needs saying because there is an open founder question about what a republication is allowed to overwrite, and this card was told not to widen or narrow it. Sending an empty tag value would have satisfied "no tag of ours" while wiping any tags the merchant had put on the post themselves — a strictly worse outcome and a change to what a republish overwrites. Omitting the key means Shopify leaves the post's tags untouched. So the answer to the open question is unaffected for the article's address and its published/unpublished state, and for tags the republish now leaves the merchant's own alone, which is a narrowing that (d) forces rather than one this card chose.
Nearest spec: main §9.5, §14.1; invariant 21.

## 2026-09-04 — R-PUBLISH — The recorded address names the blog correctly, and still names the wrong host
Decision: an article's recorded address is now built from the blog's name-slug (`/blogs/news/…`) instead of its numeric id, using the value already stored beside the id when the merchant picked the blog. The host is unchanged and remains the store's `*.myshopify.com` address.
Why, stated plainly because it means a done-when is only partly met: the numeric id was the defect the card names, and it is fixed. But Search Console reports a store's traffic under the domain the shopper actually visited, which for almost every real store is their own domain, not `acme.myshopify.com`. So the recorded address is now correct in its path and still wrong in its host, and attribution would still fail to match. Fixing that needs a decision this card does not carry — which of the store's addresses is canonical, and where the publishing client (one per process) would get a per-account domain from. Flagged rather than chosen. The same address is also what an article's internal links to earlier articles are built from, so the path fix corrects live links in published articles as well as attribution.
Nearest spec: main §12.2 (attribution), §9.2 (internal links).

## 2026-09-04 — R-PUBLISH — The fake shop can now fail the way a real shop fails
Decision: the stand-in Shopify used by every publishing test hands back one page of articles at a time, of a size a test can shrink; keeps the marker in a per-article field the list response does not carry, so each candidate costs a separate look; honours the creation-time filter; and refuses to answer "not there" when it ran out of pages before it ran out of articles. Every page read and every marker read is recorded, so a test can assert that a search really paged rather than peeked.
Why: the reason no test caught the duplicate-post defect is that the old fake searched its entire memory in one call — it could not reproduce the failure a real blog produces, so the code passed. A double that cannot fail the way the real thing fails is not evidence. This is the card's own done-when and not a nicety.
Nearest spec: build plan §4 (seams have test doubles); main §14.3.7.
## 2026-09-04 — R-SPEND — The daily-allowance brake counts work the merchant asked for, and no longer counts it twice
Decision: the nightly sweep's two per-store ceilings stop counting rows in the spend ledger as though each one were a piece of work the merchant asked for.
- **OPTIMIZE**: it now counts recommendations produced today — the same number the "Generate recommendations" button itself checks before it spends anything (`countOptimizeGenerationsSince`), so the brake and the button can never disagree about how much of the day is gone. One recommendation is one recommendation whether the model had to be asked once or four times.
- **Intent-gap**: there is no row anywhere that records one analysis, so it still counts paid model calls — but against a ceiling that leaves room for the one extra call our own wrapper makes when an answer comes back in a shape it cannot read (`MODEL_CALLS_PER_PAID_ANALYSIS_MAX`, `packages/core/src/ops/auto-trips.ts`). The operator-facing incident says which of the two it counted and how that relates to the merchant's allowance, rather than reporting calls as though they were analyses.
- Both now trip **above** the allowance rather than **at** it. Using an allowance in full is what an allowance is for, and the next click is already refused by the button; this brake is the backstop for the case that refusal missed.
Why the two halves differ: `packages/jobs/src/optimize/analyse.ts` is the only place that could write a per-analysis record and it is Lane E's file, so this card cannot add one. The alternative that would make intent-gap exact is a `spend_events` column marking a repair attempt (a migration; all four waves are closed) or having the model wrapper record the re-ask under its own call type — a change to a vocabulary every lane's spend rows share, which is a founder question rather than a lane's choice. The cost of the loose ceiling as built: a store could make up to twice its allowance of intent-gap analyses without the brake firing. Nothing else changes, because the real limit on that call type is the shortlist the daily pass builds, which is already capped at the allowance.
Nearest spec: main §14.5; invariant 17.

## 2026-09-04 — R-SPEND — Departure from the spec: the two daily allowances lower their own switch
Decision: the auto-trip sweep now takes back down the two per-store daily-allowance switches (`account.pause_intent_gap`, `account.pause_optimize`) once the count they complained about is back inside the ceiling — which the turn of the UTC day guarantees. It lowers only switches it raised itself (`tripped_by = 'auto'` and its own actor name), only those two flag names, and records `reset_by` as the sweep so an incident list can tell an automatic close from a person's.
**This departs from main §14.5, which says auto-trips never auto-reset.** The founder's card requires it in as many words ("the trip clears on its own cadence rather than needing an operator"), and the two are irreconcilable, so the card wins and the departure is written down here rather than resolved quietly. The reasoning that makes it safe: every other automatic trip reports something nobody expected and genuinely needs a person to decide it is over. A day's allowance reports nothing unexpected at all — it is a condition that is true today and false tomorrow, and nobody has to decide that a new day has started. Every other switch, including the account-wide spend pause, still waits for a person, and a switch a person raised is never lowered here.
A second consequence, stated because it is the reason the live defect ends today rather than tomorrow: switches the old arithmetic raised this morning are lowered by the first sweep after this ships, because the corrected count for those stores is inside the ceiling.
Nearest spec: main §14.5; build plan §6 `R-SPEND`.

## 2026-09-04 — R-SPEND — Authorised cross-lane edit: two of Lane E's test files plant more usage
Decision: `packages/jobs/src/optimize/intent-gap.test.ts` and `intent-gap-pass.test.ts` (Lane E's) set up "this call type is paused for this store" by planting usage and letting the real sweep raise the flag. With the corrected arithmetic the amount they planted no longer trips anything, so they plant past the ceiling instead. No assertion changed and no production code in Lane E was touched; one `describe` title that said "has used its allowance" now says "has been paused for", because using the allowance is precisely what no longer pauses anything.
Nearest spec: build plan §3 (lane ownership).

## 2026-09-04 — R-SPEND — Not fixed, and deliberately: the honest allowance sentence is inline in Lane E's route
Decision: left alone. The sentence a merchant now sees when they have used the day's recommendations — "2 per day — available tomorrow." — is written directly into `apps/web/app/api/recommendations/_lib/handlers.ts` rather than living in `packages/ui/strings/en.json`, which the constitution says is the only home for copy. So is the canonical outage sentence two lines below it. Both are Lane E's file and neither needed to change for this card, so moving them is a separate, mechanical piece of work for whoever owns that route next.
Consequence, stated: the canonical outage sentence ("Delayed — we paused this action rather than continue with lower-quality or stale data.") remains word for word where it is and is still the right sentence for the case it now actually covers — a person deliberately pausing one store's recommendations. It is no longer shown for "you have used today's allowance", because after this card the brake cannot fire while the button's own count is still inside the allowance, and the button's honest sentence is checked first.
Nearest spec: main Appendix A; invariant 24; the code-structure rule on copy.

## 2026-09-04 — R-SPEND — Flagged, not fixed: a store's intent-gap allowance can be spent twice over by legitimate paths
Decision: not addressed by this card. The daily intent-gap allowance is 10 analyses. The scheduled comparison pass shortlists up to 10 pages in one run, and separately each OPTIMIZE generation may make one more intent-gap analysis while assembling its evidence — so a store using everything it is entitled to can reach 12 analyses in a day against an allowance of 10, without anything having gone wrong. The two spenders were built to the same number without either knowing about the other.
Why it is not fixed here: deciding whether the allowance should rise, whether the OPTIMIZE path should draw on the same budget, or whether the pass's shortlist should shrink is a product decision about what a merchant is entitled to, not an arithmetic error. Flagged so it is a choice rather than a surprise.
Nearest spec: main §10.3, §14.5.

## 2026-09-04 — R-OPTIMIZE-WIRE — The one model client is exported rather than rebuilt, and it is named for the process
Decision: `generationLlm()` in `apps/web/app/api/articles/_lib/config.ts` is renamed `processLlm()` and exported. That is the only change made to that file. The recommendations lane's two background jobs import it rather than constructing an Anthropic client of their own.
Why: the integrator took this call on 2026-09-04 and the card carries it. A model client holds the request cache that makes a retried step free and the spend ledger the daily ceilings are computed from; a second one is a second set of cached answers and a second place a merchant's spending is recorded, so the two would each believe they were the only spender. Two earlier cards refused to build a second and left their job unregistered instead, which is why the improve-this-page button reached nothing. The alternative — moving construction into `apps/web/instrumentation-node.ts` — was rejected by the integrator because that file imports its dependency bundles *from* these config files, so building the client there would invert the direction and force a client argument through every deps factory for no change in behaviour. The rename is part of the export: a name saying "generation" invites the next lane to build its own.
Nearest spec: main §14.2 (one instrumented wrapper), §14.3.6 (billable calls cached, written before processing); invariants 20 and 25.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — R-OPTIMIZE-WIRE — FLAGGED: the process still builds more than one model client, and this card could not fix that
Decision: **none taken.** The done-when asks that the process construct exactly one Anthropic client. After this card the *content engine* does — the daily article, the calendar top-up, the improve-this-page generation and the weekly page comparison all share one, proved by a counting test. But three further memoised clients exist in other lanes' composition roots and are untouched: `ingestionLlm()` (`apps/web/app/api/shopify/_lib/config.ts`), `calendarLlm()` (`apps/web/app/api/calendar/topics/_lib/config.ts`) and `previewLlm()` (`apps/web/app/api/preview/_lib/config.ts`). The server's start-up hook builds two of these (ingestion and generation) before it has finished registering tasks, so the literal claim was already false before this card and is still false after it.
Why it was not fixed: the card authorises exactly one edit outside this lane — the export above — and forbids any other change to the articles config. Consolidating the other three means editing three more lanes' files. Each also duplicates a search-vendor selection and a PostHog capture in the same way, so the sensible version of this is one small card that gives the process a single shared bundle for those three services, not three separate reaches.
What it costs today, plainly: a merchant's model spending is recorded from up to four places rather than one, and an answer cached by one of them is not found by the others — so onboarding and generation can pay separately for the same question. Neither breaks a ceiling: both the cache and the ledger are database tables, so the caps still see every call. It is waste and untidiness, not a correctness fault.
Nearest spec: main §14.2, §14.3.6; invariant 25.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — R-OPTIMIZE-WIRE — The search vendor is duplicated in this lane rather than shared, and that is deliberate
Decision: `optimizeSeoProvider()` in `apps/web/app/api/recommendations/_lib/config.ts` repeats the small "mock or DataForSEO, decided by `SEO_PROVIDER_MODE`" selection that the Shopify, calendar-topics and articles configs each already make for themselves. It was not imported from another lane, and no new export was added anywhere to share one.
Why: the model client was shared because sharing it is the whole point of the integrator's decision. The search vendor is a different case on the facts. Nothing that matters is held inside the object: the cache that stops a repeated search being bought twice and the ledger the daily ceiling is read from are both database tables handed in, so a second instance cannot double-spend or lose a cached answer. Against that, importing it would be a reach into a directory this lane does not own, which every lane so far has declined to do (DECISIONS 2026-09-03 T4.2), and the card authorises exactly one cross-lane edit.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 (caps from our own counters), §14.3.6; invariant 25.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — R-OPTIMIZE-WIRE — Registering the two jobs is proved to touch no database, by pointing the address at nothing
Decision: `optimizeTaskDeps()` and `intentGapTaskDeps()` hand over the connection pool as a factory and never query. The test that proves it sets the database address to a port nothing listens on, builds both bundles, registers both tasks, and then reads the pool's own count of connections it has made, which is zero.
Why: the composition root runs while the web server is still starting, and a registration that queried would make the whole server fail to start because a background job's database was briefly unreachable. Measuring the pool's connection count says "no connection was opened" rather than "no connection was opened as far as we can tell from reading the code" — and the unreachable address means any query at all would throw rather than pass silently. Note what is *not* claimed: `db()` is called, so the pool object is created. Creating it opens nothing; it connects on its first query, and every one of those happens inside a running job.
Nearest spec: tech §2 (the worker starts in the web server's process); main §14.3.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — R-OPTIMIZE-WIRE — The end-to-end proof runs the press twice, against a worker that has not been told and one that has
Decision: the wiring test presses the button through the real route handler, then starts a real Graphile worker with the job **not** registered and shows nothing happens, then registers it and shows the work runs. Both halves are asserted on the queue and on the opportunity's own row, not on a stand-in.
Why: a test that registers the job and then watches it run proves the job works, which was never in doubt — the missing piece was the registration, and a test that supplies it cannot fail for want of it. Running the unregistered case first is what makes the second half mean something: it reproduces exactly what shipped, a press that queues work no running code answers to, and shows the page left marked "we are working on this" for ever. The case drives a page the store's inventory has no record of, so the real generation refuses before buying anything and hands the page back; the search vendor, page fetcher, model client and grader are all stubs that throw, so any of them being reached would fail the test rather than spend.
Nearest spec: main §10.2; main §14.3.1 (guarded transitions); invariant 15.
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — R-OPTIMIZE-WIRE — The weekly page comparison is wired in the same card, and still has no schedule
Decision: `intentGapTaskDeps()` is built alongside `optimizeTaskDeps()` and the registration line for `registerIntentGapTasks` is in this card's report next to the other one. **No crontab entry was added**, so the pass still runs only if something queues it, which nothing does.
Why: `R-INTENTGAP-JOB` left its registration waiting on the same client question this card answers, and its dependencies are the same four things — the shared model client, the search vendor, the guarded page fetcher and the intent-gap prompt — so wiring it was a second call with no new decisions in it. The schedule is a different matter and stays off: the recurring schedule is switched off by founder decision until the last handlerless entries land, and a crontab entry naming a task nobody has registered makes the worker refuse to enable cron at all, which silently disables every recurring job in the product.
Nearest spec: main §7.5, §10.3; build plan §6 `R-INTENTGAP-JOB`; DECISIONS 2026-09-04 R-INTENTGAP-JOB (nothing schedules the pass).
Class (filled by audit):

## 2026-09-04 — INTEGRATOR — Correcting my own rationale: the process had four model clients, not two
Decision: the shared-client decision recorded earlier today ("export the existing one; what matters is that the process has exactly one client, and exporting achieves that") **was right in its direction and wrong in its stated reason.** Exporting was correct and added no new client. But the claim that it produced one client per process was false when I wrote it: there are **four** production Anthropic clients — in the article generation, Shopify onboarding, calendar-topic and preview composition roots — each memoised separately and **each documented in its own file as "the one client"**. I had checked only the two the card named. The `R-OPTIMIZE-WIRE` lane found the other two, stated plainly that the done-when could not be met, and did not quietly redefine it.
What it costs, so it is not rediscovered as a surprise: model spending is recorded from up to four places rather than one, and an answer cached by one is not found by the others — so onboarding and generation can pay separately for the same question. **It breaks no ceiling**: the cache and the spend ledger are database tables, so every call is still visible to the caps. It is waste, not a correctness fault.
Why not fixed here: the three other clients live in three files that card explicitly forbade the lane to touch. The fix is its own small card giving the process one shared bundle — the model client, the search vendor and the analytics capture together, since the search vendor is duplicated four ways for the same reason.
Nearest spec: main §14.2, §14.7; invariant 25 (one instrumented wrapper); tech §1.4.

## 2026-09-04 — INTEGRATOR — Both recommendation jobs are registered, and neither is scheduled
Decision: the composition root now registers the improve-this-page generation and the weekly page-comparison pass. **Neither gets a crontab entry.** The first is queued by the merchant pressing a button; the second stays scheduleless until that is decided separately, which is the founder's standing position.
Why: the improve-this-page job had been deliberately unregistered since `T6.2`, because wiring it while the stuck-state defects existed would have made a CRITICAL reachable rather than theoretical. `R-OPTIMIZE-STUCK` removed those, so the hold expired. Verified on the merged tree by starting the built application and reading the worker's own line: `started with 35 task(s), cron enabled`, from 32 before. A separate observation worth writing down: **`pnpm smoke:dev` replaces the production build**, so any check that starts the built server must run after `pnpm build`, not after the dev smoke.
Nearest spec: main §10.1–10.4, §14.5; build plan §3 (integrator-resolved files).

## 2026-09-04 evening — FOUNDER — Thirteen decisions taken in one pass
Decision: the founder worked through every remaining open item. Each is recorded separately below. Two were answered only after the integrator produced concrete examples from the code, and **one of the integrator's own recommendations was withdrawn during that exchange as already built** — see the grounding entry.
Nearest spec: none — process.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — A repair sends only the words we wrote
Decision: when we update an article we already posted, we send the title, body and summary and **omit the article's address, its tags and its published state**. Shopify's update is partial, so anything we do not send is left alone: a merchant's rename, their own tags and their decision to unpublish all survive a repair untouched. This settles the open republish-overwrite question and changes the stance from "we own this post once we make it" to "we own only the words we wrote".
Why: `T5.3` made this reachable without the merchant clicking anything — a store with automatic repair on can have its post rewritten by a scheduled job. One third was already closed by the no-visible-tag answer, which forced the tags key to be omitted rather than emptied. The account's live-or-draft setting now applies when an article is **created** and not on later updates, which is right: it is a publishing preference, not a standing instruction to re-publish.
Nearest spec: main §9.5, §14.3.7; invariant 21.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — A store's own claimed domain is the address we record
Decision: the address recorded for a published article uses **the merchant's own claimed domain** — the one their shoppers visit — not the `myshopify.com` host. This completes the half-fix `R-PUBLISH` landed.
Why: Search Console reports a store's traffic under the domain shoppers actually visit, so an article recorded under the `myshopify` host can never be matched to the traffic it earns — a working, ranking article would appear to have earned nothing, permanently, and the damage lands months later. We already hold the claimed domain per account. The engineering consequence, stated: the publishing client is one per process, so it needs somewhere to look up a per-account domain rather than holding one.
Nearest spec: main §12.2 (attribution), §9.5; main §2 (the claimed domain).

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — A stranded generation run is swept, finished or abandoned loudly
Decision: a sweeper finds runs stranded past their own day, finishes the one furthest along, and gives up loudly on the rest. This resolves the deliberately-red chaos scenario.
Why: today a run killed before the store's local midnight is never picked up once the retry lands the next day — the article is written, never graded, the calendar day sticks on "generating" for ever, and **the queue records the retry as a success**: no alert, no dead letter, no visible error, just a paid-for draft that never appears. The two alternatives were rejected on their own terms: finishing yesterday's draft today puts an article on a day the calendar never scheduled, brushing the rule that a gap stays a gap; abandoning throws away work already paid for. The sweeper's own cadence is an implementation choice for its card.
Nearest spec: main §8.7, §9.1, §14.3; invariant 14.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The refresh pool comes forward out of the deferred milestone
Decision: `T7.2`'s refresh pool is **un-deferred**. A suggestion landing on one of our own published articles is currently refused with nowhere to go, and the merchant sees a card they can do nothing with.
Why: `T6.3` built the refusal correctly — the product must not answer "improve this page" with a list of edits when the page is one we wrote; that is ours to rewrite. But the place the work should go instead lives in the learning milestone the founder deferred on 2026-09-02, so the product has stopped doing the wrong thing and has nowhere to put the right thing. The founder chose to bring the pool forward rather than accept the dead end for launch. **This is the largest item decided tonight** and reopens part of a milestone previously out of v1. The rest of `M7` — labels, patterns, per-opportunity outcomes — stays deferred.
Nearest spec: main §9.6.5 (refresh candidates), §10.5; build plan §6 `T7.2`.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The scheduled comparison shortlist shrinks below the allowance
Decision: the weekly page-comparison pass shortlists **fewer** pages than the store's daily allowance of paid comparisons, so the merchant-pressed path always has room. The allowance itself does not move, and the two spenders do not share one counter.
Why: the allowance is 10 a day; the scheduled pass alone shortlists up to 10 and each improve-this-page generation may make one more, so wholly ordinary use reaches 12 — two spenders built to the same number without either knowing the other existed. Raising the allowance was rejected as changing a deliberate number to fit an accident; one shared counter was rejected because it makes the button's availability depend on what a background job did earlier, which cannot be explained to a merchant who has just been refused. The shortlist loses its least promising candidates, which are the ones least worth paying for.
Nearest spec: main §14.5 (per-type caps), §10.3.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — Grounding in the merchant's own facts is asked for in the prompt, not enforced, and not counted
Decision: the writing prompt asks for at least one fact from the merchant's own catalogue per suggested section where one exists. **No lint, no hard failure, and no measurement** — the founder was offered a report-only count and declined it explicitly ("don't even count it, there is no use").
Why, and a correction: the integrator first recommended "allow it but say so on screen" and **withdrew that recommendation on checking the code — it is already built.** A fact drawn from the pages ranking above the merchant is already labelled to them as "Covered by the pages above you: <subtopic> (<competitor urls>)", against "Trailblazer GTX — waterproof rating" for one of their own. So the merchant can already tell where a suggestion comes from, which is what the proposal was for. Requiring an own-store fact was rejected because it silently drops suggestions about subjects the store has nothing recorded for yet — often the real gap. The founder's stance: put it in the prompt and accept that a model will not always obey, as part of using one.
Nearest spec: main §10.3; invariant 7.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The recommendation's rationale is explained in the prompt, kept as prose, and labelled
Decision: the `rationale_key` field is explained in the prompt (it is currently **required by the schema and never mentioned in the prompt at all**), stays free prose rather than becoming a key into the copy catalogue, and is **labelled on screen as model-written**.
Why: the spec asks for a plain-language rationale, and a fixed list of reasons loses the specificity that makes it worth showing. The founder accepted the cost, which was stated: an unreviewed sentence reaches a merchant, and this is the exact boundary the "every explanation renders from a template" rule exists to hold — so the label is what keeps it honest. The field's name should stop implying it is a catalogue key.
Nearest spec: main §10.4; invariant 8; Appendix A.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The citation word lists go, in every language, and the instruction moves to the prompt
Decision: the English superlative/absolute/attribution/comparison lists are **removed**, not extended per language. The writing prompt instead asks for a citation on every superlative, absolute, attributed statement and comparison, in whatever language the store publishes in. **Shape-based detection stays** — numbers, measurements, percentages and durations are found by pattern and remain deterministic in every language.
Why: the founder chose consistency over a stronger guarantee in one language, matching the grounding decision above. The cost was stated plainly before the choice and is real: for these four claim kinds nothing deterministic stands behind the model any more — it both writes the claims and decides which need evidence, in the check whose whole purpose was not trusting the writer. What this buys is that a Danish store and an English one are held to the same bar, where today "den mest vandtætte støvle" passes ungrounded and "the most waterproof boot" does not.
Nearest spec: main §8.3, §8.4; invariant 11.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — A merchant may dismiss work already running
Decision: the "not interested" button works on a suggestion the product is in the middle of working on, and the work is abandoned. The state graph gains that edge.
Why: the button already does this; only the graph forbade it. A merchant who has decided against something should not have to wait to say so, and a model call we have already paid for is our cost to absorb rather than their reason to be blocked.
Nearest spec: main §7.9, §8.7.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The seven-day domain hold stays, and becomes reliable
Decision: a deleted account's domain stays unclaimable for seven days, and the release happens on the deadline **whether or not the cleanup job ran**.
Why: today the hold is delivered by a cleanup job deleting the row, so if that job never runs the domain is blocked for ever — safe, but not what was promised. Immediate release was rejected: a merchant who deletes in haste, or whose account is taken from them, could lose their domain to a squatter the same hour. Note the earlier journal recommendation of a database rule was already struck — it would free the domain the *instant* the account is deleted, because the deadline cannot live inside the rule.
Nearest spec: main §2, §5, §14.6; invariant 1.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The generation runway widens from six hours to ten
Decision: writing starts **ten** hours before the store's publish hour, not six. For a 09:00 publish that is 23:00 the previous evening in the store's own timezone.
Why: six was specified by nobody, was a first guess, and has never been measured against a real run. Ten nearly doubles the room for the writer, the checks, the judge and the single repair attempt while still finishing the same night for most timezones — which matters because a run crossing the store's local midnight is exactly the case the stranded-run sweeper exists to catch. Twelve was rejected for leaning hardest on that sweeper and putting the most distance between reading a product's facts and the article appearing.
Nearest spec: main §9.1, §9.4.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — The opportunity state graph starts refusing moves it does not list
Decision: every status write on an opportunity consults the graph first, and an unlisted move fails loudly instead of happening silently. **Its own card, with every path exercised before it goes live.**
Why: today the graph is a document nothing consults, so it informs code that chooses to ask and cannot stop anything. Two disagreements between the graph and the running code were found on 2026-09-04 alone, both by a person going looking, and one of them was **the most common completion in the whole improve-this-page feature** — a merchant pressing "I applied this" — forbidden on paper and performed every time. The risk is the mirror image and was stated: a wrongly-drawn graph now breaks working features in production rather than being a stale document, which is why it is carded separately rather than folded into another change.
Nearest spec: main §7.9, §14.3.1; invariant 15.

## 2026-09-04 — FOUNDER — Deployment stays out for now
Decision: the deployed start command is not fixed and nothing is deployed. It remains a known blocker for the first attempt to ship, alongside the deprecated platform config format and the deleted service.
Why: nothing else is blocked by it, no merchant exists, and which of the two one-line fixes is right depends on the working directory the platform hands the service — which nobody knows, because the project has never been deployed. The founder chose not to spend the evening on it.
Nearest spec: tech §2, §5; `docs/overnight-state.md` "BLOCKER — the deployed start command".
